### **Introduction**

The U.S. Department of Agriculture proposes to rescind the 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule (2001 Roadless Rule) (66 FR 3244, 36 CFR Subpart B (2001)), which prohibits road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvesting in inventoried roadless areas, with limited exceptions. Rescinding the 2001 Roadless Rule is intended to return decision-making for these activities to local officials, in conjunction with forest-level land management planning. The 2001 Roadless Rule applies to approximately 44.7 million acres of National Forest System lands, including in Alaska. The 2001 Roadless Rule is not in effect in Colorado or Idaho because those states have state-specific roadless rules.

Accompanying the proposed rule, which is published in the Federal Register, the Forest Service has prepared this draft environmental impact statement (EIS) in compliance with the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) and other relevant laws and regulations. The draft EIS applies USDA’s interim final NEPA regulations of July 3, 2025, and all references to 7 CFR 1b within this document refer to the interim rule<sup>1</sup> . This draft EIS discloses the environmental impacts that would result from the proposed rule and alternatives. All documents, including the proposed rule and other supporting information, may be found on the project website.

This draft EIS is also being published to facilitate comments on the alternatives and environmental impacts in accordance with USDA regulations (7 CFR 1b.7(n)). USDA invites written electronic comments on the proposed rule and draft EIS (see Additional Opportunity to Comment).

#### Background

##### **Roadless Rule History**

Throughout its history, the Forest Service has managed undeveloped areas to conserve their unique characteristics. After passage of the Wilderness Act of 1964 (Pub. L. 88-577), management of these areas, commonly known as “roadless areas,” became the focus of national attention when the Forest Service initiated a review to determine their suitability for inclusion in the National Wilderness Preservation System, known as the Roadless Area Review and Evaluation (RARE) I (1973) and RARE II (1977) (USDA FS 1978 and USDA FS 1979). In the decades following the inventory, roadless area management continued to be controversial in land management planning and project proposals to harvest timber, build roads, or otherwise develop these areas. In 1999, President Clinton directed the Forest Service to undertake a public process to “provide appropriate long-term protection for most or all of these currently inventoried ‘roadless’ areas...,” initiating the rule-making process. In 2001, the Forest Service issued the Roadless Area Conservation Rule (36 CFR Part 294, Subpart B (2001) (66 FR 3272) (2001 Roadless Rule)) establishing a national-level rule to maintain the values of inventoried roadless areas, limit their development, and address the history of controversy.

1The July 3, 2025, USDA interim final NEPA regulations are available at: https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/07/03/2025-12326/national-environmental-policy-act The 2001 Roadless Rule prohibited road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvesting on inventoried roadless areas (IRAs) on 58.5 million acres, or 31 percent, of National Forest System lands (defined in 36 CFR 294.11 (2001) identified in the set of maps contained in the 2001 Final EIS). The 2001 Roadless Rule provides for certain limited exceptions, including public health and safety, reserved or outstanding rights, existing or renewed mineral leases, to prevent resource damage or address safety hazards, and stewardship<sup>2</sup> purposes (36 CFR 294.12 and 294.13 (2001)).

Despite the intention to resolve the controversy with respect to the management of IRAs, the Roadless Rule has been the subject of nine lawsuits in Federal district courts in Idaho, Utah, North Dakota, Wyoming, Alaska, and the District of Columbia. In the years following the issuance, the rule was both enjoined and reinstated multiple times. Before the Roadless Rule’s effective date, a Federal district court in Idaho granted an injunction blocking implementation, which was lifted a year later by the Ninth Circuit. In 2003, and again in 2008, the Federal District Court of Wyoming issued a nationwide injunction finding the rule was substantively and procedurally flawed. On appeal in 2011, the 10th Circuit vacated the district court decision, reinstating the 2001 Roadless Rule nationwide.

During the years of legal challenges, in 2005, under the George W. Bush Administration, USDA replaced the 2001 Roadless Rule with a State Petitions Rule (70 FR 25,654 (May 13, 2005)), which allowed governors to submit petitions to the Secretary of Agriculture to request adoption of special rules for inventoried roadless area management in their respective states. In 2006, the Federal District Court of Northern California set aside the 2005 State Petitions Rule and reinstated the 2001 Roadless Rule, in response to legal challenges by a group of states and environmental organizations. The U.S. Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit affirmed this decision in _California ex rel. Lockyer v. U.S. Department of Agriculture_ , 575 F.3d 999 (9th Cir. 2009). Numerous states filed petitions for state-specific rules or have sought specific roadless policies through legal challenges, including Alaska, California, Colorado, Idaho, Maine, New Mexico, Utah, Virginia, North Carolina, South Carolina, Oregon, Washington, and Wyoming. Ultimately, Idaho and Colorado Roadless Rules (36 CFR 294 Subparts C and D) addressed road building and timber harvest, along with other uses important in these States.<sup>3</sup> These state-specific rules replaced the 2001 Roadless Rule for National Forest System lands in those states and remain in effect today.

Roadless area conservation has also been of interest in Congress. Over the years, considerable congressional interest resulted in the introduction of competing legislative bills designed to either facilitate or prohibit various multiple use management proposals in inventoried roadless areas. These legislative proposals perfectly illustrate the diverse and opposing views regarding roadless

> 2 The 2000 FEIS for the 2001 Roadless Rule refers to exceptions for cutting and removal of generally small diameter trees as ‘timber harvest for stewardship purposes;’ this term is also used here to refer to the exceptions in 36 CFR 294.13(b)(2001). See Appendix 2 – Rule Language by Alternative for the full text of the 2001 Roadless Rule. 3 These proceeded as general petitions under the Administrative Procedure Act and not under the 2005 State Petitions Rule.

area conservation and included attempts to legislate an outcome, but none of these bills have been enacted into law.

Additional information, including a historical archive, is available at the <u>Forest Service Roadless Area website.</u>

###### Tongass National Forest

The application of the 2001 Roadless Rule to Alaska’s Tongass National Forest has also been subject to ongoing litigation, policy reversals, and rulemaking petitions. The Forest Service manages approximately 22 million acres of Federal lands in Alaska, which are distributed across two national forests – the Tongass and Chugach National Forests, including over 14 million acres of inventoried roadless areas (9.3 million acres on the Tongass National Forest alone). Since the 2001 Roadless Rule, the management of roadless areas on the Tongass National Forest has been subject to multiple legal and policy shifts. The litigation history and rulemaking history are set forth in detail in prior notices (for example, 84 FR 55522 and 85 FR 68688), as well as the Congressional Research Service Report: “The Alaska Roadless Rule: Eliminating Inventoried Roadless Areas (IRAs) in the Tongass National Forest.” (R46505 February 3, 2021). The history contained in these notices and reports will not be repeated here. In response to a petition from the State of Alaska and after extensive engagement with the public, Alaska Native Tribes, Alaska Native Corporations, and others, in 2020, the USDA issued the final rule entitled “Special Areas; Roadless Area Conservation; National Forest System Lands in Alaska,” (2020 Alaska Roadless Rule) (85 FR 68688). It fully exempted the Tongass from the 2001 Roadless Rule. However, in 2021, the Department initiated a new rulemaking process to reconsider the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule, and the exemption rule was subsequently repealed in 2023. Now, Executive Order 14153: Unleashing Alaska’s Extraordinary Resource Potential, directs that the Secretary of Agriculture “shall reinstate” the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule, exempting the Tongass National Forest from the 2001 Roadless Rule.

Consistent with past rulemaking, USDA puts additional focus on the Tongass National Forest throughout this EIS. The analysis in this EIS relies on and incorporates by reference the 2020 Final Environmental Impact Statement (2020 FEIS) for the Alaska Roadless Rule, analysis supporting the 2023 rulemaking, and associated records, as outlined in 7 CFR 1b.9 on efficient and effective environmental reviews (see Environmental Impacts within the Tongass National Forest and Appendix 4 – Review of the Final Environmental Impact Statement for the 2020 Alaska ). These supporting documents are available on the project website.

##### **Inventoried Roadless Areas**

Today, roadless areas total approximately 58.2 million<sup>4</sup> acres, or about 30 percent of all National Forest System lands. The 2001 Roadless Rule applies to approximately 44.7 million acres of National Forest System lands, including about 9.3 million acres within the Tongass National Forest. The 44.7 million acres exclude approximately 9 million acres of Idaho Roadless Areas

> 4 While the original rule listed 58.5 million acres, mapping modernization and technical corrections reduced that to 58.2 million acres.

and 4 million acres of Colorado Roadless Areas, which are managed under state-specific roadless rules. Current areas are reflected in national maps at the Forest Service Roadless Area website.<sup>5</sup>

> 5 To view the national maps in an interactive map viewer select ‘Map Service for inventoried roadless areas designated by the 2001 Roadless Rule’, then select ArcGIS Online Map Viewer.

**Figure 1. National Forest System lands and 2001 Roadless Rule Inventoried Roadless Areas**

More than 95 percent of IRAs managed under the 2001 Roadless Rule (outside of Colorado and Idaho) are in ten Western states (from largest inventoried roadless area acreage to smallest):

Alaska, Montana, California, Utah, Wyoming, Nevada, Washington, Oregon, New Mexico, and Arizona (see Volume II Maps). In the West, IRAs were typically 5,000 acres or greater. Some roadless areas are less than 5,000 acres—either because they are adjacent to Congressionally designated wilderness or in the eastern US (east of the 100th meridian). While they are referred to as “roadless”, because of the decades between inventory and designation, there are thousands of miles of existing roads within lands subject to the 2001 Roadless Rule, including National Forest System roads as well as roads of other ownerships (see National Forest System Roads section). Additionally, decommissioned roads and unauthorized routes that are not part of the official system or records may exist.

The 2001 Roadless Rule identified the following features that often characterize or are present within inventoried roadless areas: (1) high quality or undisturbed soil, water, and air; (2) sources of public drinking water; (3) diversity of plant and animal communities; (4) habitat for threatened, endangered, proposed, candidate, and sensitive species and for those species dependent on large, undisturbed areas of land; (5) primitive, semi-primitive nonmotorized and semi-primitive motorized classes of dispersed recreation; (6) reference landscapes; (7) natural appearing landscapes with high scenic quality; (8) traditional cultural properties and sacred sites; and (9) other locally identified unique characteristics (36 CFR 294.11 (2001); 66 FR 3272).

##### **2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule Implementation**

The 2001 Roadless Rule provided that the responsible official may grant certain limited exceptions to the road construction prohibitions, including public health and safety, reserved or outstanding rights, existing or renewed mineral leases, and realignment of roads to prevent resource damage or address safety hazards (36 CFR 294.12(b) (2001)). It also provides exceptions to the timber harvest prohibitions for generally small diameter tree cutting to improve habitat for threatened, endangered, or sensitive species or to maintain or restore ecosystem characteristics and where it maintains or enhances one or more of the roadless characteristics, or when the activity is incidental to activities not otherwise prohibited (36 CFR 294.13(b) (2001)) (see also Table 1 in Proposed Action and Alternatives). The rule states that use of the timber harvest exceptions would be expected to be infrequent. Amid legal challenges to the 2001 Roadless Rule and to provide for consistent application, exceptions for specific activities in IRAs were reviewed and authorized by the Secretary and Forest Service Chief until 2012. In 2012, certain exceptions were delegated to the applicable regional forester. In 2018 all exceptions were delegated to the regional foresters, and some exceptions for public health and safety or small diameter tree cutting<sup>6</sup> may be further delegated to responsible officials. Approval of exceptions generally requires review documentation, including maps and justification. The activities are subject to the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA), as applicable.

Regional forester approval of exceptions under the 2001 Roadless Rule has been limited, and the majority have been for timber harvest for stewardship purposes. There is no national-level tracking of the review, approval, or implementation of 2001 Roadless Rule exceptions, though the Washington Office has recommended that Responsible Officials document reviews and

> 6 Regional foresters and deputy regional foresters must retain sole authority for exceptions under §294.12(b)(3)-(7) and §294.13(b)(4) (2001).

approvals. For the purpose of this analysis, existing information was compiled from Regional level tracking of review and approvals between 2018 and 2025. This information shows about 188 documented proposals, about 10 percent involved road related activities and 96 percent involved tree cutting (some involved both road and tree cutting activities). This information does not include responsible official reviews or approvals below the regional forester level<sup>7</sup> , so it does not include all exceptions that were granted. In addition, the information does not include if the final decisions were made for these proposed activities or if they have been implemented.

The best available information on activities that have been implemented within IRAs over the last decade comes from the Forest Service Activity Tracking System. This data shows an average of 500 acres per year of timber harvest and a total of 1.8 million acres of hazardous fuels reduction within IRAs managed under the 2001 Roadless Rule. More than half of the hazardous fuels reduction was from wildfire use, which is reported when acres burned by wildfire are determined to meet land management planning objectives in the post-fire assessment. An additional 25 percent of the hazardous fuels reduction was from prescribed burning activities for reducing fuel hazards and as resource management treatments. Best available records of permanent road construction indicate that approximately 60 miles of roads were built within inventoried roadless areas after 2013. See National Forest Transportation System, Vegetation and Forest Management, and Fire and Fuels Management sections for more detail about past and ongoing activities.

Overall, use of the available exceptions has been limited in scope and inconsistent. This is due in part to rigorous project-level analysis requirements and associated reviews and approvals needed to demonstrate consistency with Roadless Rule requirements, as well the rule’s intent for infrequent use.

##### **Relationship of the 2001 Roadless Rule to Land Management Planning**

Administration of each national forest and grassland is guided by a land management plan, along with numerous land management laws, regulations, policies, and agency directives. A land management plan provides a framework for future site-specific project analysis and decision making. Land management plans are developed, amended, and revised under the National Forest Management Act and the land management planning regulations (2012 Planning Rule at 36 CFR Part 219). Plans must provide for or contribute to ecological integrity and social and economic sustainability; conform to existing laws and regulations; and are tailored to the unique local conditions of the national forest or grassland. They are generally available for review on the website for each national forest or grassland.

The 2001 Roadless Rule did not amend, revise, or compel amendment or revision of any land management plans (36 CFR 294.14(b) (2001); 66 FR 3273); however, the Roadless Rule supersedes individual land management plans where a provision of the rule is more restrictive than a plan. Similarly, for some areas, the plans may contain plan components that are more restrictive than the 2001 Roadless Rule. Under the 2012 Planning Rule, inventoried roadless

> 7 Similarly, the Agency does not track when requests for exceptions were abandoned or not approved.

areas are treated as a type of designated area,<sup>8</sup> with the option to also identify these areas as unique geographic areas or management areas in the plans. These 2012 Rule plans may also include additional plan components for inventoried roadless areas if they are compatible with the restrictions of the applicable roadless rule. Within all land management plans (under 2012 or 1982 rule), inventoried roadless areas currently overlap other management areas or geographic areas, such as core habitats, research natural areas, alpine areas, or areas managed for backcountry recreation where there are expected to be low levels of development, including timber harvest and road construction.

There is a great variation in how existing land management plans address inventoried roadless areas and the 2001 Roadless Rule. The majority of the current land management plans across the National Forest System (108 out of 126) were developed or revised under the 1982 Planning Regulations. A total of 77 of these plans were developed or revised after the 2001 Roadless Rule was issued; however, 45 of those were revised between 2001 to 2011 when the status of the Roadless Rule was uncertain due to litigation. Therefore, these plans are less likely to have incorporated IRAs into their management areas or provided specific direction for these areas. Newer plans, especially the 19 revised under the 2012 Planning Rule and directives, are more likely to identify IRAs as a type of designated areas and may have additional plan components for these areas. The content of current land management plans has been evaluated and relevant management direction summarized for analysis purposes in this EIS (see Potentially Affected Environment and Appendix 1 – IRA Acreage Summarized by Region, State, and Forest).

#### Agencies and Persons Consulted

On June 23, 2025, U.S. Department of Agriculture Secretary Brooke L. Rollins publicly <u>announced</u> the intent to rescind the 2001 Roadless Rule. The Forest Service began notifications to Tribes in July 2025. On August 29, 2025, the agency announced a public comment period to provide the opportunity for Federal and State agencies, local governments, businesses, organizations, and individuals to provide comments (90 FR 42179).

> 8 36 CFR 219.7(d) Management areas or geographic areas. Every plan must have management areas or geographic areas or both. The plan may identify designated or recommended designated areas as management areas or geographic areas.

> 36 CFR 219.9 Designated area. An area or feature identified and managed to maintain its unique special character or purpose. Some categories of designated areas may be designated only by statute and some categories may be established administratively in the land management planning process or by other administrative processes of the Federal executive branch. Examples of statutorily designated areas are national heritage areas, national recreational areas, national scenic trails, wild and scenic rivers, wilderness areas, and wilderness study areas. Examples of administratively designated areas are experimental forests, research natural areas, scenic byways, botanical areas, and significant caves.

Geographic area. A spatially contiguous land area identified within the planning area. A geographic area may overlap with a management area.

Management area. A land area identified within the planning area that has the same set of applicable plan components. A management area does not have to be spatially contiguous

##### **Consultation with Federally Recognized Tribes and Alaska Native Corporations**

On July 22, 2025, the Forest Service notified Tribes and Alaska Native Corporations of the opportunity to consult on the proposed rulemaking to rescind the 2001 Roadless Rule, with a summary analysis of proposed changes and an invitation to a Tribal forum engagement session. This information was also emailed to regional foresters on July 25, 2025. The Office of Tribal Relations held a Tribal forum engagement session on August 5, 2025.

The Forest Service received 64 requests for consultation, 29 consultations have been held, and an additional 5 scheduled. Tribal consultations were held either in person or virtually with local or regional Forest Service line officers serving as consulting officials. Some tribes submitted letters in lieu of, or in addition to, consultations. Consultations will be ongoing throughout the rulemaking process and a final Tribal Impact Summary Statement will be included with the final rule. In addition to government-to-government consultations, many comment letters were received from Tribes during the public comment period on the notice of intent published on August 29, 2025. The comment letters received from Tribes or Tribal organizations during the comment period (via hardcopy or the via regulations.gov comment portal) were included in the public comment summary (Appendix – Notice of Intent Comment Summary) and considered in the development of the draft EIS and proposed rule. See also Proposed Action and Alternatives and Tribal Rights and Interests.

##### **Notice of Intent**

On August 29, 2025, the Department published a NOI in _the Federal Register_ to inform interested parties that it would prepare an EIS, initiating a 21-day comment period (90 FR 42179). The NOI requested public comments on alternatives or effects and on relevant information, studies, or analyses with respect to the proposal (7 CFR 1.7(b)). Comments received in response to the NOI were used to inform the range of alternatives and environmental impact analyses. Over 220,000 comment letters were received during the NOI comment period. These comments came from Federally Recognized Tribes, Alaska Native Corporations, and Tribal members; members of Congress and other elected officials; Federal, State, and local governments; and businesses, organizations, and individuals. Counting all signatures submitted with the petitions as individual submissions, the letters represent over 625,000 submissions. The comments are posted electronically at Regulations.gov.

More than 90 percent of the letters received contained brief sentiments or opinions on the proposed action. Of the comment letters received, approximately 99 percent were generally opposed to the proposed rule recission, while approximately 1 percent were generally in support. Those containing substantive comments related to the NEPA process, potential alternatives, and/or specific issues to be addressed in the EIS are summarized in Appendix 3 – Notice of Intent Comment Summary. Major comment themes include environmental review and compliance, purpose and need, alternatives, infrastructure and transportation, cultural and Tribal resources, biodiversity, water quality, geology and soils, minerals and mining, climate change, carbon, air quality, public health and safety, recreation and access, socioeconomic effects, and impacts on specific locations such as the Tongass National Forest.

After considering comments received in response to the notice of intent, the Department responded by: clarifying the proposed action; analyzing an additional alternative in detail; using the information to inform analysis (including consideration of various literature and references cited); and making corrections or updating content to read more clearly and be more readily understood. Other comments were considered but no change was made because the concern, recommendation, or suggested alternative was already accounted for, outside the scope of the rulemaking and purpose and need, and/or outside the authority of the Department or agency.

##### **Additional Opportunity to Comment**

An additional opportunity to comment is available following publication of the proposed rule in the federal register.  Comments should be sent via one of the following methods:

- Electronically (preferred): Through the Federal eRulemaking Portal, https://www.regulations.gov, identified by docket number FS-2025-0001 or RIN 0596AD66; or

- Mail: Hardcopy letters must be submitted to the Director, Ecosystem Management Coordination, 201 14th Street SW, Mailstop 1108, Washington, DC 20250-1124.

See the project website, Federal Register Notice of the Proposed Rule and/or www.regulations.gov for instructions on how to submit your comments. Substantive comments received will be published electronically.

##### **Schedule**

A Final Rule and Final Environmental Impact Statement are anticipated to be completed in 2027.

### **Purpose and Need for Action**

The Department is proposing this rule rescission to reduce regulatory burden and return land management decision-making for inventoried roadless areas to local Forest Service officials. In 2001, the Roadless Area Conservation Rule established a single, nationwide set of prohibitions intended to provide lasting protection for inventoried roadless areas within the National Forest System in the context of multiple-use management. Today, the Department believes increased management flexibility in the administration of these lands is needed to better meet the multipleuse mission and provide benefits to the American people. This approach prioritizes decisionmaking by local Forest Service officials informed by Tribes, State, and local communities. As explained below, there is a need to increase local management flexibility and, where necessary, allow for active management to respond to changing and localized conditions, such as increasing wildfire risk, the spread of insect and disease infestations, and the need for community protection in the wildland-urban interface.

Under the current Administration, the Department has refocused policies, programs, and resources on increasing rural economic opportunity, decreasing Federal regulation, and streamlining Federal Government services. Specifically, this deregulatory action advances the policy objectives of Executive Order 14192, _Unleashing Prosperity Through Deregulation_ , to alleviate unnecessary regulatory burdens. In Executive Order 14225, _Immediate Expansion of American Timber Production_ , the President declared that “the United States has an abundance of timber resources that are more than adequate to meet our domestic timber production needs, but heavy-handed Federal policies have prevented full utilization of these resources.” In Executive <u>Order 14154,</u> _Unleashing American Energy_ , the President likewise declared that “it is in the national interest to unleash America's affordable and reliable energy and natural resources.” In <u>Executive Order 14153,</u> _Unleashing Alaska's Extraordinary Resource Potential,_ the President declared that “it is the policy of the United States to fully avail itself of Alaska's vast lands and resources” and “maximize the development and production of the natural resources located on both Federal and State lands within Alaska.” Consistent with this policy, Executive Order 14153 directed the Secretary of Agriculture to reinstate the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule that exempted the Tongass National Forest in Alaska from the 2001 Roadless Rule. This proposed rescission does not mandate timber cutting or road construction but would relieve regulatory burden relative to management of National Forest System lands.

#### Rationale for the Proposed Rule

As resource conditions and national policy have evolved, the Department has determined that a single, national blanket approach to the management of inventoried roadless areas taken in the 2001 Roadless Rule constrains responsible officials from exercising the timely, place-based discretion needed to meet the Forest Service’s multiple-use mission. In addition, evolving national priorities and changed conditions have required more active management approaches. The 2001 Roadless Rule limited the Forest Service’s ability to conduct vegetation management within inventoried roadless areas and has contributed to the lack of active management of the national forests, which has contributed to challenges in addressing forest health concerns. The 2001 Roadless Rule prohibited cutting, sale, or removal of timber—except when one of a limited set of exceptions applied. Per the Rule’s text, the use of these exceptions was “expected to be infrequent” (36 CFR 294.13(b) (2001)). In addition, while exceptions were available, their use was limited and inconsistent due to associated reviews and approvals to demonstrate consistency with the 2001 Roadless Rule. Furthermore, the limited number of roads within inventoried roadless areas and the inability to reconstruct or build new roads to provide the needed access further limit management flexibility. Taken together, the prohibitions have removed important management tools for key areas where there are needs or opportunities to address overgrown and fuel-loaded national forests.

This proposed rescission is intended to return primary authority for determining the appropriate management of inventoried roadless areas at the local level to the land management planning process mandated by the National Forest Management Act of 1976 (16 U.S.C. 1600 et seq.). The National Forest Management Act requires that plans shall assure multiple use and sustained yield of National Forest System products and services and include coordination of outdoor recreation, range, timber, watershed, wildlife and fish, and wilderness (16 U.S.C. 1604(e)(1)). The National Forest Management Act establishes the requirement for the Forest Service to develop land management plans, including direction in 16 U.S.C. 1604(a) and (b) for interdisciplinary planning and consideration of landscape-level conditions. These statutory requirements are implemented through the Agency’s land management planning framework, which require consideration of the plan area in the context of the broader landscape and requires that each plan reflects the unit’s expected distinct roles and contributions to the local area, region, and Nation. The land management planning framework ensures each land management plan addresses similar conservation objectives as the 2001 Roadless Rule was intended to address, such as ecological integrity (including air, soil, and water), sources of public drinking water, diversity of plant and animal communities (including federally listed threatened and endangered species), sustainable recreation, scenic character, and protection of cultural and historic resources. At the same time, this planning approach allows for place-based, collaborative decision-making that is responsive to specific on-the-ground resource conditions, rather than a “one-size-fits-all” national mandate. While national-level considerations are important, land management planning efforts by local decisionmakers at the national forest or regional scale are best positioned to make decisions about inventoried roadless areas because they understand the unique ecological, economic, and social needs of their communities.

In 2001, USDA and the Forest Service asserted that a national prohibition was the best means to reduce conflict and potential for incremental impacts to the ecological and social values of these areas. Since then, conditions across National Forest System lands have changed substantially, especially in the Western United States where many inventoried roadless areas are located, with increasing drought, extreme temperatures, wildfire frequency and severity, and insect and disease outbreaks. Alongside these landscape changes, the Agency’s land management planning framework has become more adaptive, science-based, efficient, and effective. These changes, combined with evolving Department priorities for active forest management and deregulation, diminish the basis for a national prohibition. The proposed rule would return land management decision-making for inventoried roadless areas to local Forest Service officials—giving them the flexibility to address conservation and resource issues informed by input from stakeholders, communities, and state, local, and tribal governments. Local decision making would remain subject to the substantive requirements of the Endangered Species Act, National Forest Management Act, and other related laws and regulations.

The Secretary of Agriculture has broad authority under statutes such as the Organic Administration Act of 1897 and the Multiple-Use Sustained-Yield Act of 1960 to manage the National Forest System for a variety of uses. Just as the establishment of the 2001 Roadless Rule was discretionary, this proposed rule is an exercise of discretion to determine the most appropriate process for balancing competing values and uses in inventoried roadless areas within that legal framework.

### **Proposed Action and Alternatives**

The alternatives presented in detail below represent a reasonable range of rulemaking alternatives that are technically and economically feasible and consistent with the stated purpose and need. For each alternative, the resulting status for the Tongass National Forest is highlighted. In the final decision-making, the Secretary would determine whether to address the Tongass the same as every other national forest (alternative 1 and 2), or to treat the Tongass differently than the National rule (alternatives 3).

Appendix 2 – Rule Language by Alternative provides the regulatory language that would be associated with each alternative.

Alternative 1 – No Action, 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule

- Continues current management, pursuant to the 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule

- Prohibits road construction, reconstruction, and timber harvest in inventoried roadless areas

- Provides limited exceptions for public health and safety, reserved or outstanding rights, existing or renewed mineral leases, and realignment to prevent resource damage or address safety hazards, and stewardship purposes (see 36 CFR 294.12 and 294.13 (2001), Table 1)

- Applies to inventoried roadless areas totaling 44.7 million acres; including 9.3 million acres of the Tongass National Forest

Alternative 1, the no-action alternative, illustrates the consequences of taking no action to contrast the impacts of the proposed action and alternatives with the current condition and expected future condition if the proposed action or alternative were not implemented (7 CFR 1b.7(h)(3)(iv)). It establishes a benchmark, or environmental baseline, against which the effects of the other alternatives are compared. This alternative reflects the continuation of current management, pursuant to the 2001 Roadless Rule (36 CFR Part 294, Subpart B (2001); 66 FR 3272) (see Appendix 2 – Rule Language by Alternative). Discussion of the consequences of taking no action or implementing any of the action alternatives are discussed in the Environmental Impacts section.

Alternative 1 applies to 44.7 million acres of IRAs, as identified in the current national roadless area maps (see Inventoried Roadless Areas). This alternative does not mean that no activities would occur within IRAs; rather it means the projects and activities currently occurring or allowed to occur would continue (see Common to All Alternatives). This alternative would continue nation-wide prohibitions on road construction, reconstruction, and timber harvest with limited exceptions. Exceptions (Table 1), as listed in 36 CFR 294.12(b) and 294.13(b) (2001), may be authorized following review and approval by the appropriate responsible official (as described in 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule Implementation).

Modification of IRA boundaries would require rulemaking, though technical corrections may be approved by the Chief (see Inventoried Roadless Areas).

Under alternative 1, the 9.3 million acres of inventoried roadless area within the Tongass National Forest would remain subject to the 2001 Roadless Rule. This is the same action analyzed in alternative 1 of the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule FEIS and selected in the 2023 Final Rule Repealing the Alaska Roadless Rule.

#### Alternative 2 – 2001 Roadless Rule Recission (Repeal)/No Rule

- Rescinds the 2001 Roadless Rule in its entirety

- Eliminates nation-wide prohibitions on road construction and timber harvest, continues management by applicable land management plan

- Removes IRA designation from 44.7 million acres, including 9.3 million on the Tongass National Forest

- Does not change Idaho and Colorado state-specific Roadless Rules

The 2001 Roadless Rule prohibiting activities in inventoried roadless areas would be rescinded as drafted in the Notice of Proposed Rulemaking and Appendix 2 – Rule Language by Alternative. Alternative 2 would eliminate the national prohibitions on road construction, reconstruction, and timber harvest set forth in the 2001 Roadless Rule and would remove the national “inventoried roadless area” designation from the 44.7 acres million acres of National Forest System lands currently designated as inventoried roadless areas under the 2001 Roadless Rule.

Management of lands formerly managed as inventoried roadless areas would be governed by applicable land management plan direction for each unit of the National Forest System alongside other relevant laws and regulations. Under alternative 2, the governing land management plan could still include restrictions on road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvesting in roadless areas within the management unit, as well as management direction that influences when, where and how these activities may be carried out. These may include direction to meet other management objectives or legal requirements, protect sensitive areas, to provide for primitive recreation opportunities, lands recommended for wilderness, or to provide wildlife habitat. Alternative 2 would not alter existing land management plan direction nor would it compel amendment or revision of any land management plan (see Common to All Alternatives, Potentially Affected Environment).

Alternative 2 would not authorize any specific ground-disturbing projects (see Common to All Alternatives, Potentially Affected Environment).

Under alternative 2, the 9.3 million acres of inventoried roadless area within the Tongass National Forest would be included in the 44.7 million acres no longer managed according to the 2001 Roadless Rule. This is the same action as was analyzed in alternative 6 (the full exemption alternative) of the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule FEIS and selected as the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule – it would have the same results as reinstating the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule.

#### Alternative 3 – Modified Roadless Rule/IRA Boundaries

- Removes roadless rule prohibitions and designation on 31.7 million acres, including:

- ♦ 4.5 million acres of non-NFS lands and congressionally designated areas

- ♦ 17.9 million acres that are within ½ mile on either side of existing roads and/or within wildland-urban interface, outside of the Tongass National Forest, and

- ♦ 9.3 million acres of inventoried roadless area within the Tongass National Forest.

- Continues current management on 13 million acres, pursuant to the 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule;

- ♦ Prohibits road construction, reconstruction, and timber harvest

- ♦ Provides limited exceptions for public health and safety, reserved or outstanding rights, existing or renewed mineral leases, and realignment to prevent resource damage or address safety hazards, and stewardship purposes (see 36 CFR 294.12 and 294.13 (2001)) (same as no action).

- Provides provisions to modify boundaries based on locally led modification proposals

Under alternative 3, the 2001 Roadless Rule would be modified (see Appendix 2 – Rule Language by Alternative). This alternative responds to a wide range of recommendations that suggest removing or modifying inventoried roadless area designations or prohibitions where they have restricted the Forest Service’s ability to maintain key benefits or meet community concerns, especially with respect to forest health, reducing fire risks, and protecting infrastructure and communities. At the same time, it responds to commenters’ concerns of maintaining the roadless rule prohibitions and the associated ecological, social, and economic benefits in core or ‘high value’ areas, including benefits to habitat for big game, fisheries, wildlife connectivity, recreation or opportunities for solitude, and tourism economies. Comments suggested that a modified Rule could meet the purpose and need and provide more flexibility for local decision-makers to propose actions that would protect the wildland urban interface from fire, reduce insect and disease risks, and increase acreages available for potential timber harvest, while also maintaining the benefits of large unroaded areas.

This alternative addresses the issue commenters raised about roaded inventoried roadless areas, sometimes referred to by commenters as ‘inaccurate mapping.’ These roaded areas were included as inventoried roadless areas under the 2001 Roadless Rule because of mapping errors in RARE II and road construction between the time RARE II was completed and the 2001 rule. In addition, some road construction occurred between 2001 and 2010 while the rule was enjoined, or pursuant to the 2001 Roadless Rule’s exceptions. By removing roaded areas, this alternative would provide local decision-makers flexibility to propose road construction and timber harvest to facilitate more active management in areas accessed from existing roads. This alternative similarly responds to comments which recommended providing more flexibility for local decision makers to propose actions that could reduce fire risks to communities, by removing designation of inventoried roadless area in the wildland-urban interface (WUI), the area between undeveloped land and human development, such as homes or other buildings.

Alternative 3 would modify the definition of inventoried roadless areas, referring to an updated map of inventoried roadless boundaries (see Volume II Maps). Under the modified boundaries, similar to alternative 2, 31.7 million acres of existing IRAs would be removed from roadless designation and, therefore, no longer subject to national prohibitions. The revised mapping includes several steps:

- Removing about ½ million acres of non-National Forest System lands from the national inventoried roadless area maps<sup>9</sup> . The Roadless Rule applies only to National Forest System lands (see also Potentially Affected Environment).

- Removing about 4 million acres of Congressionally designated areas, which have more restrictive and more permanent mandates than the Roadless Rule (see also Potentially Affected Environment).

- Removing 17.9 million acres which include 5.1 million acres within the wildland-urban interface and 6.3 million acres of roaded roadless and 4.7 million acres that are both within the wildland-urban interface and within 0.5 miles of existing roads. The remaining acreage was removed as the small, isolated areas did not meet previous mapping criteria.

- ♦ Roaded inventoried roadless areas were defined as the area within 0.5 miles (either side) of existing roads.<sup>10</sup>

- ♦ WUI based on the Healthy Forests Restoration Act’s (HFRA) definition, which includes the areas identified in an existing Community Wildfire Protection Plans or, where a Plan is not in effect, the extent of at-risk communities and up to a 1.5-mi buffer around the communities and areas around evacuation routes for at-risk communities.<sup>11</sup> The mapping process resulted in some small fragments of former IRAs remaining. Under this alternative, USDA would apply past criteria for designating IRAs and the western US (west of the 100th meridian) and Alaska, only retaining areas that are greater than 5,000 acres or share a border with Wilderness or recommended Wilderness. East of the 100th meridian inventoried roadless areas of any size were retained.

Across all Forest Service Regions, the percentage of 2001 Roadless Rule IRA acres that would have prohibitions removed under alternative 3 vary from 60 percent in the Northern Region to 97 percent in Southern Region.

9 There are 44.7 million acres of IRAs managed under 2001 Roadless Rule. Review of the geospatial data found that 44.3 million acres of these are on National Forest System lands, meaning about 0.4 million acres of lands have had land ownership change since the 2001 Roadless Rule or there may be vertical integration problems (mapping errors) between the inventoried roadless data and official land ownership boundary data.

10 Existing roads for this analysis include National Forest System roads and other authorized public roads (see National Forest System Roads)

11 WILDLAND-URBAN INTERFACE.—"The term ‘‘wildland urban interface’’ means—(A) an area within or adjacent to an at-risk community that is identified in recommendations to the Secretary in a community wildfire protection plan; or (B) in the case of any area for which a community wildfire protection plan is not in effect—(i) an area extending 1⁄2-mile from the boundary of an at-risk community; (ii) an area within 11⁄2 miles of the boundary of an at-risk community, including any land that—(I) has a sustained steep slope that creates the potential for wildfire behavior endangering the at-risk community; (II) has a geographic feature that aids in creating an effective fire break, such as a road or ridge top; or (III) is in condition class 3, as documented by the Secretary in the projectspecific environmental analysis; and (iii) an area that is adjacent to an evacuation route for an at-risk community that the Secretary determines, in cooperation with the at-risk community, requires hazardous fuel reduction to provide safer evacuation from the at-risk community”. (Healthy Forests Restoration Action of 2003; 16 U.S.C. 6511 (16))

The inventoried roadless area designation and the following rule prohibitions would continue to apply to the remaining approximately 13 million acres (all outside of the Tongass) (see Volume II Maps):

- Prohibit road construction, reconstruction, and timber harvest, with limited exceptions (same as alternative 1).

- Provides limited exceptions for public health and safety, reserved or outstanding rights, existing or renewed mineral leases, and realignment to prevent resource damage or address safety hazards, and stewardship purposes (see 36 CFR 294.12 and 294.13 (2001)) (same as alternative 1).

This alternative also includes provisions to make modifications and administrative corrections to inventoried roadless area boundaries, responding to comments about issues with inaccurate national mapping. The following procedures are included in this alternative allow site-specific boundary modifications or corrections:

- a. Modifications to boundaries. Responsible officials, working with local government, Tribes, and interested parties, may propose boundary modification to the Chief. Modifications and additions approved by the Chief will be reflected in the set of maps maintained at the national headquarters office of the Forest Service. Modifications of inventoried roadless area boundaries may be made to correspond with geographic features, land management plan management areas, or for other reasons. A boundary modification is not required to perform work done under an exception pursuant to §§ 294.12(b) or 294.13(b). Public notice with a minimum 30-day comment period will be provided for any proposed boundary modifications.

- b. Administrative corrections to boundaries. The Chief of the Forest Service may issue administrative corrections. Administrative corrections to the maps of any inventoried roadless area are adjustments to correct clerical errors, to conform with new statutory or regulatory requirements, or to correspond with changes based on improvements in mapping technology.

Modifications and corrections will be reflected in the set of maps maintained at the national headquarters office of the Forest Service.

Under alternative 3, the 9.3 million acres of inventoried roadless area within the Tongass National Forest would no longer be managed in accordance with the 2001 Roadless Rule. This is the same action as was analyzed in alternative 6 (the full exemption alternative) of the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule FEIS and selected as the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule – it would have the same results as reinstating the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule and is the same as alternative 2.

#### Common to All Alternatives

**_No changes are proposed to state-specific roadless conservation rules for Idaho and Colorado_** (36 CFR Subparts C and D), administration of those areas would continue as follows:

- Idaho Roadless Rule (36 CFR 294, Subpart C) applies to approximately 9 million acres of Idaho Roadless Areas in the national forests within the State of Idaho. Five management themes that provide that assign various permissions and prohibitions regarding road building, timber cutting, sale or removal, and discretionary mineral activities. (https://www.fs.usda.gov/r04/planning/roadless-area-idaho-rule)

- Colorado Roadless Rule (36 CFR 294, Subpart D) applies to approximately 4 million acres of Colorado Roadless Areas. This rule includes an updated inventory of roadless areas; upper tier protection on 1.2 million acres is more restrictive; prohibits tree cutting, sale, or removal, road construction and reconstruction, and the use of linear construction zones, with some exceptions; including exceptions that address state-specific situations at the time of the rule’s promulgation (https://www.fs.usda.gov/r02/natural-resources/forest- <u>management/colorado-roadless-rule)</u>

Any State, Tribe, or other interested entity seeking to establish roadless management provisions tailored to specific geographic or resource conditions may submit a petition for rulemaking under the Administrative Procedure Act (5 U.S.C. 553(e)) and USDA regulations at 7 CFR 1.28. If such petitions are submitted, the Department would consider them consistent with applicable law and policy. If such state-specific roadless rulemaking is proposed and developed in the future, the effects would be attributed to and analyzed as part of the decision-making for that rulemaking. Federally recognized Tribes may separately submit requests for related roadless management provisions under government-to-government consultation. Records of such consultation will be retained and may be referenced in future rulemaking. This process ensures that requests for new or modified roadless management approaches are evaluated transparently and in accordance with established rulemaking procedures.

**_No alternatives would compel the amendment or revision of any land management plan._** While the rulemaking would not require such changes, plans may be amended in any manner whatsoever and are revised periodically (16 U.S.C. 1604(f)). Local responsible officials (forest supervisors and regional foresters) would continue to have discretion to **_amend or revise a_** land management plan as deemed necessary. A determination to amend or revise a land management plan is based on a variety of factors.

- _Plan revisions. A plan revision creates a new plan for the entire plan area, whether the plan revision differs from the prior plan to a small or large extent. A plan must be revised at least every 15 years. But the responsible official has the discretion to determine at any time that conditions on a plan area have changed significantly such that a plan must be revised (16 U.S.C. 1604(f)(5))_ (36 CFR 219.7(a)).

- _Plan amendment. A plan may be amended at any time. Plan amendments may be broad or narrow, depending on the need for change, and should be used to keep plans current and help units adapt to new information or changing conditions. The responsible official has the discretion to determine whether and how to amend the plan and to determine the scope and scale of any amendment. Except as provided by paragraph (c) of this section, a plan amendment is required to add, modify, or remove one or more plan components, or to change how or where one or more plan components apply to all or part of the plan area (including management areas or geographic areas)_ (36 CFR 219.13 (a)).

- _Administrative changes. An administrative change is any change to a plan that is not a plan amendment or plan revision. Administrative changes include corrections of clerical errors to any part of the plan, conformance of the plan to new statutory or regulatory requirements, or changes to other content in the plan_ (36 CFR 219.13(c)).

**_Any future administrative changes, amendments, or revisions to land management plans would comply with NFMA and planning regulations_** (36 CFR 219 or the planning directives at FSM 1920 and FSH 1909.12). No alternatives would modify the planning regulations at 36 CFR 219 or the planning directives at FSM 1920 and FSH 1909.12. (See also ‘Relationship of the 2001 Roadless Rule to Land Management Planning’). The regulations require plans be based on science and meet basic substantive requirements, including providing or contributing to: ecological integrity; air, soil, water and riparian protection; social and economic sustainability; timber; multiple uses and ecosystem services; sustainable recreation, recreation settings, access, and scenery; protection of cultural, and historic resources and management of areas of tribal importance; providing the ecological conditions necessary to contribute to the recovery of threatened and endangered species to maintain viable populations of species of conservation concern; protection and recommendations for designated areas (wilderness, wild and scenic rivers, research natural areas, and others); and contributing to economic sustainability (36 CFR 219.8 through 219.11). They also require public involvement. To the degree that a land management plan could change in the future, the effects of such a change would be attributed to and analyzed as part of the decision-making for that action.

**_No alternatives would compel or authorize site-specific ground disturbing activities._** Future projects in inventoried roadless areas will be proposed, reviewed, and approved by local Forest Service officials.  All future site-specific project decisions, including those to authorize road construction, reconstruction, and timber harvesting, must be consistent with land management plans. Future project decisions must comply with applicable Federal and State laws, such as the National Environmental Policy Act, Clean Water Act, the Clean Air Act, Endangered Species Act, National Historic Preservation Act, and the Wilderness Act, as well as provisions for government-to-government consultation with Tribes and Alaska Native Corporations.

**_The reasonable exercise of reserved or outstanding rights for access, occupancy, and use_** of National Forest System lands within inventoried roadless areas would not be affected. The rights include those that exist by law or by treaty. They include, but are not limited to, the right to provide reasonable access across National Forest System lands to private property, mining claims for locatable minerals under the 1872 Mining Law, land uses protected by Native American treaty rights, and uses under the Alaska National Interest Lands Conservation Act.

All alternatives would allow for the **_continuation, transfer, or renewal of existing land-use authorizations_** for activities in roadless areas. “Authorizations” refer to land uses allowed under a special use permit, contract, or similar legal instrument. Numerous types of lands and recreation-related authorizations are issued for occupancy and use of National Forest System lands.

**_Other Forest Activities that are not otherwise prohibited_** are permissible in inventoried roadless areas under all alternatives, if not restricted by land management plans or other law, regulations, and policies. These activities include, but are not limited to, the following:

- Maintaining existing roads;

- Constructing or maintaining motorized and non-motorized trails;

- Engaging in hunting, fishing, hiking, camping, and other recreational uses;

- Using a motorized vehicle, such as an off-highway vehicle, on a trail open to motorized use;

- Riding a bike on a trail open to mechanized use;

- Implementing prescribed burning; and

- Grazing livestock.

#### Comparison of Alternatives

Table 2 compares the scope and scale of each alternative.

|---|---|---|---|
|National-level<br>Prohibitions|Road construction,<br>reconstruction, and<br>timber harvest, with<br>limited exceptions (36<br>CFR 294.12 and 294.13<br>(2001))|None|Same as alternative 1|
|Inventoried roadless<br>areas (acres) subject to<br>nation-wide prohibitions|44.7 million acres,<br>including Tongass|0 acres|13 million acres|

|Tongass National Forest<br>Inventoried Roadless<br>areas|9.3 million acres|0 acres|0 acres|
|Inventoried roadless area<br>boundary modification|Requires rulemaking;<br>Chief approves technical<br>corrections.|Not applicable|Locally led modifications and<br>additions, approved by the Chief,<br>with public notice and comment.<br>Chief may approve administrative<br>corrections.|
|Potentially affected<br>inventoried roadless<br>areas (IRAs)|Not applicable|40.1 million acres|27.2 million acres|

Under alternatives 2 and 3, for areas removed from IRA designation, where national prohibitions no longer apply, it is reasonably foreseeable that road construction or timber harvest activities could increase in the future. Across the resources evaluated in the DEIS, the potential effects of increases in road construction and timber harvest activities are described in a general, programmatic, and qualitative way (see Table 3). However, the site-specific locations and amounts of activities that could occur in the future are uncertain. To compare alternatives and the potential intensity of effects, the DEIS estimates where activities may be more or less likely to occur in the future based on the direction in current forest and grassland land management plans and the technical and economic feasibility for timber harvest. Estimates of activities and effects are more predictable in the shorter-term.

Alternative 2 and 3 would give more flexibility for local land managers to tailor active forest management to address local land conditions and needs, though benefits would likely be modest and localized. For alternative 2, about 4.8 million acres (16 percent of forested areas in the potentially affected IRAs) are in areas where current land management plans allow timber harvest and are more likely to be operable. Slightly less for alternative 3 (4.3 million acres). Depending on the alternative, these areas could potentially contribute a 4 to 10 percent increase in total sawtimber harvest towards the nationwide increase across NFS lands, providing a resulting opportunity for an increase in revenue for the timber industry, which could benefit local communities. In addition, substantial acreage within potentially affected IRAs has a relatively high likelihood of burning under high-intensity conditions that are difficult to manage or that could expose communities, infrastructure, or drinking-water sources. Alternatives 2 and 3 would provide additional opportunities to expand hazardous fuel treatments, especially those integrated with timber management, in priority firesheds, community wildfire risk reduction zones, and other high-risk areas. Under alternative 3 these hazardous fuel treatments would be more concentrated in the wildland-urban interface and roaded areas. The management flexibility or opportunities could provide resulting benefits, aligned with land management plan desired conditions, such as improving habitat or reducing wildfire risk to resources.

In general, impacts from road construction and timber harvest activities could include: increased fire frequency (human-caused), reduction in primitive or semi-primitive recreational settings; sedimentation and impacts to water quality; landscape fragmentation; adverse impacts to threatened, endangered, or sensitive wildlife and plants; loss of scenic character; impacts to tribally important resources; or economic losses related to recreation or non-commodity values of roadless areas. While these activities and effects could increase under alternatives 2 or 3, the amount (intensity) of these effects depends on the specifics of future decisions (for example, future travel management decisions about if constructed roads would be open to the public). Most effects would occur under both alternatives but affect less area or be slightly reduced under alternative 3. Across all alternatives, potential adverse effects could be reduced or mitigated by existing land management plans and individual project design. They could be further mitigated through future land management planning decisions, consistent with the 2012 Planning Rule.
