#### Alternatives Considered but Eliminated from Detailed Study

Public comments received in response to the Notice of Intent provided recommendations or suggestions for alternatives. The alternatives recommended have been organized into groups or categories of similar recommendations. See also Appendix 3 – Notice of Intent Comment Summary for more detail on recommendations.

Alternatives analyzed in detail must be technically and economically feasible and meet the purpose and need (NEPA section 102 (2 (c)(iii)), 7 CFR 1b.7(h)). Roadless rules are narrowly focused prohibitions and exceptions established by the Secretary concerning whether road construction/reconstruction and timber harvest may be allowed within certain designated inventoried roadless areas. The alternatives evaluated in detail in this DEIS focus on the geographic locations (boundaries) in roadless rulemaking. Some of the recommended alternatives would not have substantially different effects or are sufficiently duplicative of the alternatives considered in detail.

Some of the alternatives recommended are outside the scope of the proposed rulemaking. These include, but are not limited to, recommending legislative solutions (such as codifying the existing Rule or providing permanent protection through designation as wilderness), alternatives to rulemaking, or changes to land management plan rules and processes (such as recommended wilderness process). General guidance considering other discretionary or non-discretionary aspects of management are better addressed through land management planning or site-specific project level planning.

##### **Alternatives With a Different Geographic Scope of the Rule**

Recommendations included additional suggestions to re-inventory roadless areas to add or remove additional areas, provide for local review of boundaries, address errors with historic roadless area mapping, remove roadless areas that are already ‘roaded’ or substantially altered, or develop an approach that allows for changes to roadless area designations, but maintains a consistent amount of roadless over time.

An alternative that increases the acreages covered by the rule would not be responsive to the deregulatory executive orders or to the fire and forest health-related concerns associated with maintaining existing prohibitions and exceptions, nor would it meet the purpose and need to restore decision making to local responsible officials. It is not the intent that management of areas currently un-inventoried be subject to a national prohibition.

Alternative 3, which is being considered and analyzed in detail, includes modifying the IRA boundaries to address comments suggesting reduced prohibitions or increased flexibility for “roaded inventoried roadless” areas and within the wildland urban interface. It also provides a provision to correct or modify boundaries. Alongside alternative 3, an alternative that uses a different wildland-urban interface boundary to delineate new geographic boundaries of inventoried roadless areas was considered. A sensitivity analysis associated with HFRA WUI boundaries and the census-based wildland-urban interface approach (Radeloff et. al. 2023) to delineating the wildland urban interface, along with applying the 0.5-mile buffer from existing roads, showed that the differences in the two wildland urban interface delineations while buffering the 0.5 mile from existing roads revealed about a 12 percent difference of remaining inventoried roadless acreages. The Healthy Forest Restoration Act definition of wildland-urban interface was included in the development for alternative 3 and considered in detail, as it aligns more closely with the purpose and need relative to local decision-making.

Additional recommendations suggested different approaches to modifying boundaries, including treating restoration areas, priority watersheds for treatment, insect and disease areas, or wildfire crisis strategy landscapes differently. The effects of these different approaches would not be substantially different than alternative 3. Management opportunities relevant to these suggestions are currently limited by the Rule’s prohibitions and their proximity to roads, and alternative 3 was specifically developed to address management opportunities near existing roads and within the WUI.  As shown in the analysis (see Vegetation and Forest Management and Fire and Fuels Management) alternative 3 would provide for slightly less management flexibility overall when compared to alternative 2 but does provide for more management opportunity in key areas (for example, priority firesheds) than the no action alternative.  In addition, alternative 3 would continue the existing Rule’s exceptions and provides for locally led boundary modifications which could enable local units to propose adjustments where the nationally derived boundaries do not align with local data or conditions.

##### **Alternatives That Would Modify the Rule Prohibitions or Exceptions**

Recommendations suggested that the prohibitions and exceptions in the rule be modified to provide more restrictions on activities or reduced exceptions. Other recommendations suggested the rule could be modified, similar to the Idaho and Colorado Rules, to include additional flexibility in the exceptions or different ‘tiers’ of roadless management, especially to meet needs related to the wildland-urban interface or for management within already roaded areas. Alternative 3 is being considered and analyzed in detail. It takes a different approach to address recommendations to modifying where existing rule prohibitions and exceptions apply, especially related to wildland-urban interface and roaded inventoried roadless areas.

An alternative that considered maintaining existing inventoried roadless boundaries and prohibitions on permanent road construction while allowing temporary road construction and timber harvest was considered but eliminated from detailed study as not being responsive to the purpose and need as it would continue to restrict local decision-making discretion. In addition, maintaining national roadless boundaries would require project-level analysis considerations of roadless values which have been, and would continue to be an administrative and legal burden for the agency.

Adding restrictions to existing rule prohibitions would not meet the purpose and need to restore decision making to local responsible officials, nor would it serve to address the increasing fire and forest health related concerns associated with maintaining the existing prohibitions and exceptions.

##### **State-Specific Rulemaking**

Several comments recommended taking a state-specific rule-making approach, similar to those used in Idaho and Colorado Roadless Rules. Any State, Tribe, or other interested entity seeking to establish roadless management provisions tailored to specific geographic or resource conditions may submit a petition for rulemaking under the Administrative Procedure Act (5 U.S.C. 553(e)) and USDA regulations at 7 CFR 1.28) (see also Common to All Alternatives). The options for state petitions exist regardless of the national roadless rule – Idaho, Colorado, and Alaska state-specific rules were all developed through these procedures in time periods where the 2001 Roadless Rule was not in effect and when it was in effect.   Some comments suggested retaining the 2001 Roadless Rule while allowing for potential state-specific rulemaking – this is not considered in detail separately, as this would have the same effect as Alternative 1. Others suggested rescinding the Rule and instead providing for State-specific Rulemaking (akin to the 2005 State Petitions Rule), this was not considered in detail as this would have the same effects as alternative 2.  The impacts of future state-specific rules are unknown and not predictable. If state-specific roadless rules are proposed in the future, the effects would be analyzed as part of that decision-making process.

Under all alternatives, during national forest or grassland land management planning, local Responsible Officials must engage States and local governments in the planning process and, where appropriate, shall encourage them to request cooperating agency status (36 CFR 219.4(a)).

##### **Tribal-Specific Rulemaking**

Tribal letters and consultations suggested this rulemaking prioritize Tribal conservation priorities and interests, for example including a mechanism to allow Tribes to request specific management approaches for specific IRAs or to manage specific characteristics of IRAs important to Tribal interests. Other recommendations included a non-rescission alternative that directs or incentivizes cooperative agreements between the Forest Service and Tribes for the management of IRAs and incorporating Indigenous knowledge as a source of best available science.

General or broad discretionary or non-discretionary aspects of national forest and grassland management, such as co-stewardship with Tribes, are better addressed through local land management planning or site-specific project level planning than through National Rulemaking. During the land management planning process, Tribal consultation is required (36 CFR 219.4(a)(2)) and, where appropriate, the responsible official shall encourage federally recognized Tribes to seek cooperating agency status (36 CFR 219.4(a)(1)(v)). The Responsible Official could consider information about Indigenous Knowledge, land ethics, cultural issues, and sacred and culturally significant sites (36 CFR 219.4(3)). In addition, the Forest Service can enter into cooperative agreements or contracts with Tribes to facilitate cooperative forest management, through mechanisms such as the Good Neighbor Authority and the Tribal Forest Protection Act (TFPA) (see Tribal Rights and Interests).

##### **Tongass-Specific Alternatives**

Four additional alternatives for managing roadless area conservation specific to the Tongass National Forest were considered in detail in the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule FEIS (see Appendix 4 – Review of the Final Environmental Impact Statement for the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule and Chapter 2 of the 2020 Alaska Rule FEIS for a description of each of these alternatives). Taken together, the six alternatives considered for the Tongass in 2020 represented the spectrum of management regimes identified to the Forest Service through interdisciplinary review, public comments, public meetings, Tribal and Alaska Native corporation consultations, and cooperating agency input. They were found to be an appropriate range of alternatives for the 2020 and 2023Alaska Rulemaking (2020 Alaska Roadless Rule, 85 FR 68689-68690; 2023 Final Rule 88 FR 5254), which selected alternative 6 and alternative 1, respectively. Alternatives 2 through 5 of the 2020 FEIS were re-considered for this rule-making. However, these alternatives do not meet the purpose and need for the current proposed rulemaking, as they would not comply with the policy direction in Executive Order 14153.

See also several additional alternatives considered but eliminated from study in the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule FEIS (page 2-17), including management or co-management of the Tongass with Tribes, congressional changes to the 2001 Roadless Rule, and use of 2003 and 2008 inventories.

### **Potentially Affected Environment**

This section briefly describes the status of inventoried roadless areas that may be affected. See also Inventoried Roadless Areas above. Potentially affected environments are also included in the environmental impacts section. See Appendix 1 – IRA Acreage Summarized by Region and Volume II Maps.

There are 44.7 million acres of IRAs subject to the 2001 Roadless Rule<sup>12</sup> . Review of the geospatial data found that 44.3 million acres of these are on National Forest System lands, meaning about 0.4 million acres of lands have had land ownership change since the 2001 Roadless Rule or there may be vertical integration problems (mapping errors) between the inventoried roadless data and official land ownership boundary data<sup>13</sup> .

Over 4 million acres are within Congressionally-designated wilderness<sup>14</sup> , wilderness study areas<sup>15</sup> , and wild segments of wild and scenic rivers<sup>16</sup> . These statutory designations have more restrictive and more permanent mandates than the 2001 Roadless Rule. Therefore, changes in management activities in these areas under any alternative are highly unlikely and not reasonably foreseeable and these 4 million acres are not included in the potentially affected environment (see Wilderness and Other Designated Areas).

As described in the Relationship of the 2001 Roadless Rule to Land Management Planning section, the rule and land management plans provide overlapping layers of direction within IRAs that any activities must comply with. Current land management-plans often include plan direction and components that identify where timber management and road construction may or may not be allowed. Example management areas where timber management or road construction may be limited include recommended wilderness, research natural areas, or backcountry management areas. Figure 2 shows the primary management emphasis of management and geographic areas which overlap with the potentially affected IRAs. Some plans include plan components with direction specific to IRAs.

12 Current areas are reflected on national maps at the Forest Service Roadless Area website: https://www.fs.usda.gov/managing-land/planning/roadless

13 Vertical integration is a term used in the Geographic Information System (GIS) community to describe the process of aligning datasets from different sources. This includes the process of aligning the 2001 IRA boundary lines with National Forest System (NFS) boundary lines. NFS boundaries include, but are not limited to NFS property boundaries, national forest/national grassland boundaries, and special designated area boundaries (such as wilderness). Misalignments occur over time due to new surveys, improved technologies, and datum shirts (USDA Forest Service 2024 – Memo Procedure for Aligning and Making Technical Corrections). Very slight misalignment of the coverages can result in polygon slivers between the coverages, which can produce acreage differences initially. These differences can amount to tens or hundreds of acres or more, especially because of the large area under analysis.

14 Congress has included approximately 1.3 million acres (or 2.8 percent) of inventoried roadless areas managed under the 2001 Roadless Rule in the National Wilderness Preservation System.

15 2.8 million acres are Congressionally-designated wilderness study areas. Agency policy requires that “[a]ny area recommended for wilderness or wilderness study designation is not available for any use or activity that may reduce the wilderness potential of an area” (FSM 1923.03(3)).

16 85,000 acres are Wild segments of designated Wild and Scenic Rivers. Agency policy states that "[g]enerally, a wild river is accessible only by trail" (FSM 2354.42g) and that we must "[e]nsure trees are not sold, cut, or otherwise harvested in a designated wild river area except under emergency conditions, such as for insect or disease control, fire, natural catastrophe, disaster, public safety, or under specified conditions on valid mining claims" (FSM 2354.42d).

**Figure 2. Proportion of land management plan emphasis categories across a flattened layer of all management, geographic, and designated areas within the potentially affected environment**

A national review of plan components in management areas overlapping the potentially affected environment was completed to identify where plans do or do not further restrict timber harvest and road construction activities. There is much variation between individual land management plans and Regions reflecting differences in local conditions, age of the plans, and changes in planning policies over time (see Appendix 1 – IRA Acreage Summarized by Region for more detail and examples). Overall, within potentially affected IRAs, land management plan components:

- Further restrict (do not allow or generally do not allow) road construction on 21.8 million acres (54.5 percent of the potentially affected IRAs), and

- Further restrict (do not allow or generally do not allow) timber harvest on 26.7 million acres (66.5 percent of the potentially affected IRAs).

Note that this information is intended for programmatic analysis purposes for this EIS, actual implementation of any activities would be subject to local interpretation of the land management plans based on unique local conditions.

Within the Tongass National Forest 9.3 million acres of inventoried roadless area managed under the 2001 Roadless Rule, or about 56 percent of the Forest. Approximately 9.2 million acres of these are included in the affected environment (on National Forest System lands and outside of wilderness, wilderness study area and wild river segments).

The potentially affected environment does not include any lands in Colorado or Idaho because roadless areas in those states, managed under state-specific roadless rules. There would be no changes to those rules and no effects within these states.

### **Scope of the Analysis**

The Environmental Impacts section provides the scientific and analytic basis to compare the alternatives. The scope of the impacts covered in this draft EIS are informed by the preliminary issues listed in the Notice of Intent, substantive information received on the notice (see Agencies and Persons Consulted and Appendix 3 – Notice of Intent Comment Summary), the history of roadless rulemaking analyses and its implementation, and the interdisciplinary team’s review. Effects that could have potential for significant impacts are discussed in proportion to their reasonably foreseeable significance (as defined in 7 CFR 1b.2(f)(3)(ii) and (iii)). This analysis is a programmatic review (42 U.S.C. § 4336e (11)), analyzing a policy or program and its environmental effects. The environmental impact analysis provides a programmatic or high-level review of the potentially significant effects that may result from proposed rulemaking and alternatives, considering the policy and legal framework that guides National Forest System land management.

The draft EIS presents the reasonably foreseeable impacts of the rulemaking alternatives and potential adverse effects that cannot be avoided, including from the no-action alternative. An irreversible or irretrievable commitment of resources will not be made by the rulemaking because none of the rulemaking alternatives propose specific actions that would cause irreversible or irretrievable commitments of resources<sup>17</sup> .

In general, it is reasonably foreseeable that activities with potential for beneficial or adverse environmental effects could occur within or near inventoried roadless areas under all alternatives, and that more road construction and timber harvest activities are reasonably foreseeable under alternatives 2 and 3. However, the rulemaking would not compel, require, or authorize any sitespecific activities. Therefore, site-specific locations and amounts of activities that could occur in the future under the alternatives are uncertain. Any future decisions on these activities are greatly influenced by factors beyond the proposed rulemaking such as local decision-making through

> 17 Irreversible commitments of resources are those that cannot be regained, such as the extinction of a species or the removal of mined ore. Irretrievable commitments are those that are lost for a period of time such as the temporary loss of timber productivity in forested areas that are kept clear for use as a power line rights-of-way or road. The 2001 Roadless Rule FEIS stated that the rule prohibitions could reduce road-caused irretrievable commitments to watersheds, soils, critical habitat, and dispersed recreation activities compared to no action (not issuing the rule) (USDA FS 2000a). Although road construction and/or timber harvest could potentially increase within some roadless areas under alternatives 2 and 3, commitment of resources would be based on site-specific proposals, which are currently unknown, and would be addressed in subsequent land management plan or project environmental analyses.

land management plans and projects, informed by the local community, local industry capacity, agency budget allocations and resources, and other regulations and policies.

Potential adverse effects described in this draft EIS could be reduced or mitigated at the project level through compliance with existing land management plan direction, implementing best management practices, or employing project-specific design criteria; or through future land management planning. Site-specific effects can be meaningfully measured and evaluated at the project and activity stage and are evaluated during the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) process, as appropriate. Common requirements or practices that reduce impacts are noted throughout the Environmental Impacts discussion.

To aid in comparing alternatives, the analysis in the EIS focuses on the difference where (what locations) reasonably foreseeable activities, particularly road construction and timber harvest, are more or less likely to occur within inventoried roadless areas under the alternatives, and the resulting difference in ecological and social effects. Estimates of areas where activities are more likely to occur, were prepared for effects analysis purposes (see National Forest System Roads, Vegetation and Forest Management and other sections) – they do not reflect proposed or planned activity areas and activities would not occur in all of these areas.

The following describes how the agency drew a reasonable and manageable line in consideration of the reasonably foreseeable environmental effects of the proposed action and action alternatives, and those that may extend outside the geographical territory of the proposal or might materialize later in time<sup>18</sup> . The geographic extent of the environmental effects analysis focuses on activities with a close causal relationship to the rulemaking alternatives. The locations, geographic extent, and duration of ecological or social impacts depend on the resource or impact being measured, the activity type or intensity (such as road type or traffic level), and topography and will be generally described in the following sections. Changes to regulations remain in place until additional rulemaking or Congressional action. Short- and long-term reasonably foreseeable environmental impacts of the rulemaking alternatives are considered. The analyses also discuss short and long-term effects that may result from potential future activities, if/when they occur.

The short-term potential for road construction and timber harvest or related activities and associated effects is more predictable and reasonably foreseeable. Within the potentially affected environment the following areas are more likely to have activities or impacts, in the short-term (5 years):

- areas where activities are allowed under existing land management plans (see Potentially Affected Environment);

- areas that are more feasible or economical for equipment and workers to access (more operable) (see Vegetation and Forest Management); and,

- areas with a greater management priority or ‘need’ (for example, high risk firesheds and areas affected by insect and disease or other disturbances);

18 7 CFR 1b.11(a)(12)

- areas with high product potential/economic viability for timber production to support management objectives.

In the longer term (up to 20 years and beyond), the extent of activities is more uncertain. Still, it is reasonable to expect a greater proportion of the potentially effected environment could be impacted by activities over time. Factors affecting long term change include natural events such as storms and wildfires, local decision-making through projects and land management plan amendments and revisions, national policy changes, state-level rulemaking proposals, technological changes, industry and economic changes, etc. Areas that are least likely for development in the short term (currently restricted, less developed, etc.) could be impacted by change, in the longer term. However, given the long causal chain and distance from the action proposed, predicting the specific amounts is too speculative.

There are also other ongoing or reasonably foreseeable actions with relevance to impacts of the proposed action and alternatives. Forest Service policy decisions and local plan and project decisions would determine how much and where actions would occur.

### **Environmental Impacts**

#### National Forest System Roads

##### **Background**

###### National Forest Transportation System

The Forest Service maintains and administers approximately 368,000 miles of roads on National Forest System lands, referred to collectively as National Forest System roads (NFS roads). The objective of the NFS transportation system is to provide the minimal mileage road system needed for safe and efficient travel, and for administration, utilization and protection of NFS lands.<sup>19</sup> The roads under Forest Service jurisdiction are necessary for administering, managing, protecting, and using national forests. They are commonly called “system roads” and they are maintained and managed by national forests, including collecting and inventorying physical road characteristics within data preserved in the Forest Service Natural Resource Manager (NRM) database<sup>20</sup> .

19 The National Forest Roads and Trails Act of October 13, 1964, as amended (16 U.S.C. 1608). Authorizes construction and maintenance of adequate system of roads and trails for the national forests carried forward on an economical and environmentally sound basis. The Travel Management Rule (Title 36, Code of Federal Regulations, <u>Part 212 (36 CFR Part 212)). Establishes requirements for the administration of the Forest transportation system.</u>

20 Official Forest Service records as found in the Natural Resource Management (NRM) database were used to provide information on NFS roads (open, closed, administrative use only, etc.). Database records are dynamic; the roads data of September 2025 is used for analyses. Data, methods and results of analyses related to roads are provided in the project file. The Forest Service does not have a national database of temporary road information and any temporary road data is tracked at the local level during project implementation.

The FEIS for the 2001 Roadless Rule includes NFS road system history (page 3-21). Information includes annual mileage increases to the NFS roads system from the 1940s to its peak of 386,000 miles in 2000 (USDA Forest Service 2000b). More recent data shows that in the last 23 years, there has since been a decline in the system of 18,000 miles, as illustrated in Figure 3.  Figure 4 shows the distribution of NFS road miles by vehicle use. Approximately 65,000 miles, or close to 18 percent, are managed to accommodate standard passenger cars; 200,000 miles, or 54 percent, of NFS roads, are managed to accommodate high-clearance vehicles; and the remaining 103,000 miles, or 28 percent, are currently in storage, temporarily restricting roads from standard vehicle use but preserving them for future operation.

**Figure 3. NFS road system mileage has declined by 18,000 miles over the last 23 years.**

**Figure 4. Miles of NFS Road by typical vehicle use. A total of 65,000 miles of National Forest System roads are managed to accommodate passenger car use.**

The downward trend in National Forest System road system mileage was spurred by increased emphasis on transportation system management, consideration for ecosystem protection and wildlife habitat connectivity, funding available for decommissioning unneeded roads, and recognition of declining funding for maintaining the road system. Two major transportation system management policies were implemented in the last two decades for planning purposes to meet road users’ needs while at the same time being fiscally responsible.

One policy was the Roads Analysis Policy implemented in August 1999. The objective was to produce a road system that was safe and responsive to public needs and desires, was affordable and efficiently managed, had minimal negative ecological effects on the land, and would be in balance with available funding for needed management actions. Further information on the Roads Analysis Policy is available in the FEIS for the 2001 Roadless Rule, pages 3-28 and 3-29 (USDA Forest Service 2000a).

The Forest Service further refined transportation management strategies with the implementation of the Travel Management Rule (36 CFR Part 212) (TMR) in 2005. The goal of the rule was the identification of an environmentally and financially sustainable transportation system while meeting public needs. Under the TMR, each National Forest System unit was required to identify the minimum road system needed for safe and efficient travel and for administration, utilization, and protection of NFS lands. In determining the minimum road system, the units were required to incorporate a science-based roads analysis at the appropriate scale to identify NFS roads that were no longer needed to meet forest resource management objectives.

Today, the NFS road system serves a wide variety of forest users and administrative purposes, joining with county, state, and national highways to connect rural communities and urban centers with NFS lands, resources and activities. Access for administrative purposes includes fire management, law enforcement, natural resource management such as timber and watershed, and facilities management. Permitted operations such as ranching, special-use recreation events, utility and telecommunications, and energy exploration and mining rely on NFS roads. Additionally, NFS roads are used for recreation purposes and access including hiking, sightseeing, fishing, hunting, camping, and off-highway vehicle use. Finally, NFS roads may connect landowners with their private property and provide access to administrative and developed recreation sites.

While not part of the NFS road system, temporary roads are also developed and used on NFS lands. Temporary roads are authorized under contracts and permits, such as timber sale contracts, special use permits, oil and gas exploration permits, or facility construction contracts; or they may be constructed by the Forest Service for administrative access. These roads are needed for a short time to meet a one-time access need, usually for one year and not more than ten years. The Forest and Rangeland Renewable Resources Planning Act of 1974 (as amended) generally requires temporary roads to be closed and revegetated within ten years after use.

###### _Road System Classification and Definitions_

National Forest System roads are managed and maintained to provide differing levels of service based on intended purpose, management area direction, and type and extent of resource activities. The roads are classified by five different maintenance levels: 1, 2, 3, 4, and 5. Maintenance level 1 roads are roads that have been placed in storage between intermittent use restricting the road from standard vehicle use while still allowing for certain activities such as pedestrian access and motorized trail access if such use is designated. Maintenance level 2 roads are maintained for high-clearance vehicles. Maintenance level 3, 4, and 5 roads are maintained for passage by standard passenger cars during the normal season of use. Typical NFS roads include native surface roads maintained for high-clearance vehicles and slower travel, and gravel and paved surface roads allowing for somewhat higher speeds and travel by standard passenger cars. _Guidelines for Road Maintenance Levels_ found at https://www.fs.usda.gov/t- <u>d/pubs/pdf/11771811.pdf</u> (FSH 7709.59) provides the following definitions for NFS road maintenance levels:

- Maintenance level 1. These are roads that have been placed in storage between intermittent uses. The period of storage must exceed one year. Basic custodial maintenance is performed to prevent damage to adjacent resources and to perpetuate the road for future resource management needs. Emphasis is normally given to maintaining drainage facilities and runoff patterns. Planned road deterioration may occur at this level.

- Maintenance level 2. Assigned to roads open for use by high clearance vehicles. Passenger car traffic, user comfort, and user convenience are not considerations. Warning signs and traffic control devices are not provided with the exception that some signing, such as W-18-1 “No Traffic Signs”, may be posted at intersections. Motorists should have no expectations of being alerted to potential hazards while driving these roads. Traffic is normally minor, usually consisting of one or a combination of administrative, permitted, dispersed recreation, or other specialized uses. Log haul may occur at this level.

- Maintenance level 3. Assigned to roads open and maintained for travel by a prudent driver in a standard passenger car. User comfort and convenience are not considered priorities. The Manual on Uniform Traffic Control Devices (MUTCD) is applicable. Warning signs and traffic control devices are provided to alert motorists of situations that may violate expectations. Roads in this maintenance level are typically low speed with single lanes and turnouts.

- Maintenance level 4. Assigned to roads that provide a moderate degree of user comfort and convenience at moderate travel speeds. Most roads are double lane and aggregate surfaced. However, some roads may be single lane. Some roads may be paved and/or dust abated. Manual on Uniform Traffic Control Devices is applicable.

- Maintenance level 5. Assigned to roads that provide a high degree of user comfort and convenience. These roads are normally double lane, paved facilities. Some may be aggregate surfaced and dust abated. Manual on Uniform Traffic Control Devices is applicable.

Roads on National Forest System lands are also identified and defined by jurisdiction and type. Current policy found in Forest Service Manual 7700 uses the following terms:

- _Forest Road-_ road wholly or partly within or adjacent to and serving the NFS that the Forest Service determines is necessary for the protection, administration, and utilization of the NFS and the use and development of its resources (36 CFR 212.1).

- _National Forest System Road (NFS road)-_ A forest road other than a road which has been authorized by a legally documented right-of-way held by a State, county, or other local public road authority (36 CFR 212). (An NFS road is identified in Forest Service NRM database as being under FS jurisdiction, having a status of ‘existing’ and is part of the NFS roads system.)

- _Public Road-_ A road under the jurisdiction of and maintained by a public road authority and open to public travel (23 U.S.C. 101(a)).<sup>21</sup>

- _Temporary Road_ -road necessary for emergency operations or authorized by contract, permit, lease, or other written authorization that is not a system road and that is not included in a forest transportation atlas.

- _Decommissioned Road_ -road no longer needed wherein actions have been performed resulting in restoration of unneeded road to a more natural state.

- _Unauthorized Road_ -road that is not a Forest road or a temporary road and is not included in a Forest transportation atlas (such as user-created routes and off-road vehicle tracks not designated and managed as a trail).

> 21 NFS roads may be open to public use or identified as administrative use only. An administrative NFS road is any NFS road that is not a public road. NFS roads open to the public are identified on a unit’s motor vehicle use map.

###### _Road Maintenance_

The Forest Service is responsible for maintaining NFS roads resulting from traffic associated with administration of NFS lands, noncommercial uses and activities, incidental noncommercial use related to ownership or occupancy of isolated parcels of private land served by an NFS road, commercial road use that is not subject to cost recovery, and incidental public use (Forest Service Manual 7730).

NFS roads are maintained to accommodate standard passenger cars and high-clearance vehicles such as sport-utility vehicles, pickups, and jeeps. Some NFS roads are stored for future use. These roads are typically single-use roads (for example, timber sale access) that are generally closed after their initial use, kept closed between uses and may receive basic custodial maintenance to prevent damage to adjacent resources and perpetuate the road for future resource management needs. Across all lands, NFS roads maintained for passenger cars include approximately 9,500 miles of paved roads, 36,500 miles of gravel roads, and 19,000 miles of native material (dirt) roads. As illustrated in Figure 4 and Table 5, less than 10 percent of paved roads and less than 30 percent of gravel roads are in good condition.<sup>22</sup>

**Figure 5. Less than 20 percent of NFS paved/gravel surfaced passenger car (PC) roads are in good condition.**

**Table 5. Miles of passenger car roads by surface type on NFS lands**

|**Surface Type – Passenger**<br>**Car Roads**|**Miles**|
|---|---|
|Paved|9,594|
|Gravel|36,504|
|Native Material|18,709|

Road maintenance activities vary by road surface and anticipated traffic characteristics. All roads, except those stored, have some level of signage maintenance, hazard removal, and vegetation management to maintain sight distance. Typical maintenance for native surface (dirt) roads includes bulldozer or grader work to maintain grades and drainage, and maintenance of drainage ditches to prevent erosion and environmental damage. Gravel road maintenance

> 22 Information from the 2024 Roads Program Fact Sheet summarizing various transportation program facts and data as tracked by the USDA Forest Service engineering program. Good, fair, and poor ratings based on four pavement performance measures: roughness, cracking, rutting, and faulting as defined and described in 23 CFR, Part 490.

typically includes grading to maintain crown and shoulder slopes, spot surface replacement to repair sections with new gravel, and maintenance of drainage ditches, culverts and other drainage structures. Asphalt roads similarly include shoulder and drainage structure maintenance and additionally include pothole patching and pavement resurfacing. Road maintenance also includes repair and preservation of major drainage structures such as bridges.

Road maintenance is performed in accordance with best management practices to reduce the concentration of subsurface and surface water runoff, minimize road surface erosion, filter ditch water before entering streams, and decrease risk of culvert failures during peak runoff events. Maintenance may include culvert installation, replacement of existing culverts, installation of drainage dips and surface water deflectors, placement of riprap to armor drainage structures, aggregate surface replacement, aggregate placement to reinforce wet surface areas, ditch construction and cleaning where needed, and surface blading to restore drainage efficiency of the road surface.

###### _Road Decommissioning_

Road decommissioning is part of NFS road system management. It includes applying various treatments to restore unneeded roads to a more natural state, mitigate environmental damage, and restore hydrologic function. Treatments may include one or more of the following:

- Reestablishing former drainage patterns, stabilizing slopes, and restoring vegetation;

- Blocking the entrance to a road or installing water bars;

- Removing culverts, reestablishing drainages, removing unstable fills, pulling back road shoulders, and scattering slash on the roadbed;

- Completely eliminating the roadbed by restoring natural contours and slopes; and

- Other methods designed to meet the specific conditions associated with the unneeded road.

Road maintenance and decommissioning costs can vary greatly based on treatment, location, material needs, terrain, and drainage. Based on current agency data, typical costs for annual road maintenance can range from $5,000 to $50,000 per mile. A review of route decommissioning projects from various Forest Service regions for fiscal years 2022 to 2024 indicated decommissioning costs from as low as $2,000 per mile for minor treatments such as blocking entrances or installing water bars to an excess of $50,000 per mile for more complex treatments such as removing culverts and restoring drainages.

###### _Road Construction or Reconstruction_

Construction or reconstruction of NFS roads may be required to provide needed access for implementing land management plans goals and objectives. Construction and reconstruction are typically paid for by the use that most benefits from the access. Examples include timber harvest by timber purchasers, mining operations by mining claimants, and special use permit access by permittees. However, some roads are built using congressionally appropriated dollars such as roads for recreation, administrative access, and ecosystem restoration. The Forest Service is responsible for planning, design, and construction oversight and often retains long-term jurisdiction, including maintenance and operational responsibilities, for roads constructed on NFS lands.

An informal Forest Service survey related to average cost for new construction indicates that NFS road construction cost in the lower 48 states varies between $80,000 to $100,000 per mile for native surface (dirt) roads. Adding gravel surfacing is an additional $300,000 to $500,000 per lane mile, and approximately $1,000,000 to $2,000,000 per lane mile for asphalt paved roads. In Forest Service Region 10, Alaska, road costs are much higher, typically four to five times that of the lower 48 states. These costs do not include significant drainage structures such as bridges, retaining walls, or other substantial slope stabilization measures.

Road System Management Costs and Funding

Each new mile of road competes for limited road maintenance funding. Road investment planning for capital improvement and maintenance is implemented through a multi-faceted approach at the national and local levels. At the national level, funds are set aside for higher-cost projects and project proposals are submitted for consideration and selected based on agency priorities through the agency’s National Asset Management Program. At the local level, annual funding is allocated and distributed to units based on factors such as national visitor use monitoring figures, National Forest System land area managed, and forest product outputs. Road maintenance plans and future investment plans are prepared at the local level based on their individual needs.

A fiscal year 2023 Service-wide Asset Summary for the Forest Service estimated current replacement value for NFS roads and bridges at $39 billion<sup>23</sup> . Using the industry standard 4 percent of current replacement value to estimate annual funding needs to maintain the existing NFS road network in a state of good repair, maintenance funding needed is calculated out to an estimated $1.6 billion annually. This figure includes reoccurring maintenance and cyclic replacement and repair of major assets such as bridges, drainage structures, pavements, and gravel surfacing. Per a budget review of annual funding, the Forest Service received approximately $270 million in fiscal year 2023, or less than 20 percent of the estimated funding needed to maintain its existing road infrastructure.

Approximately 31,000 miles of passenger car and high-clearance roads were maintained, improved or reconstructed in fiscal year 2024. This equates to approximately 8 percent of the entire NFS road system or approximately 12 percent of open road mileage (mileage not identified as maintenance level 1 or “in storage”) (gPAS<sup>24</sup> ).

Funding received for NFS roads includes annual appropriations, special program funding, collections from commercial users, and highway funds. For example, in fiscal year 2024, transportation projects were partially funded from Great American Outdoors Act (GAOA, 20212025), Legacy Road and Trail Remediation Program as part of the Infrastructure Investment and

> 23 Information from a 2023 Service-wide Asset Summary report as generated from the Forest Service’s database of record, Natural Resource Management (NRM).

> 24 Information from the Forest Service's source for performance data, the Geo-Enabled Performance Accountability System.

Jobs Act (IIJA, 2021-2026), and Federal Lands Transportation Program. Annual appropriations to the Forest Service for roads capital improvement and maintenance funding in fiscal year 2024 was $73 million. The other programs mentioned above provided the remaining $197 million for 2024; however, this funding is outside of typical annual appropriations and not guaranteed. For example, GAOA expired at the end of fiscal year 2025 and IIJA is set to expire on September 30, 2026.

Annual appropriations for construction, improvement and maintenance of the road system have drastically declined in the last 20 years. Annual appropriation funding reached a peak of $234 million in 2004 and dropped to $73 million 2024. The overall trend of decline in appropriated funding over the last 20-years is illustrated in Figure 6.

**Figure 6. Appropriated funding for NFS road system has declined by almost 70 percent over the last twenty years.**

The difference in funding available for roads and that needed to maintain the roads system has resulted in a deferred maintenance backlog.<sup>25</sup> In 2024, the Forest Service estimated a deferred maintenance backlog of $6.9 billion for roads and bridges. This estimate is based on passenger car roads only (maintenance levels 3 - 5). In 2001, the deferred maintenance backlog was estimated at $8.4 billion for all maintenance levels of roads. The 2024 estimate does not include maintenance level 1 or 2 roads as there is no current tracking mechanism for these lower maintenance level roads. (In 2001, resources were available to survey 100 percent of NFS roads to determine deferred maintenance. Currently, the Forest Service uses a random sample method for determining deferred maintenance at a national scale and it includes only passenger car roads.) The growth in deferred maintenance over the last ten years is illustrated in Figure 7.

> 25 Deferred maintenance is the practice of delaying or postponing necessary repairs, upkeep, or replacements for infrastructure to save costs or because of a lack of resources.

**Figure 7. NFS Road System Deferred Maintenance**

##### **Common to All Alternatives**

The objective of the NFS transportation system is to provide the minimal mileage road system needed for safe and efficient travel, and for administration, utilization and protection of NFS lands. Road construction and reconstruction would be designed for the level appropriate for intended use. Any new roads would be designed and maintained in a way that would minimize negative effects on water quality, soil stability, and wildlife habitat. Trails open to motor vehicles are managed for recreation access, including full size vehicles, and are addressed in the recreation section.

The decision to construct a new, permanent road that would become part of the NFS road system would be made as part of transportation planning associated with planned resource management activities and use of NFS lands. Proposals for new roads related to development and the associated environmental effects would be considered through project-level planning.

If future decisions authorize it, modifications to the transportation network would follow law, regulation and policy found at 16 USC 1608, FSM/FSH 7700, relevant State and Federal statutes such as the Clean Water Act, NEPA, and Endangered Species Act, and national forest land management plans. Implementation of these policies can minimize, but not eliminate, some adverse environmental effects.

Potential road system management activities could include construction, reconstruction, maintenance, and decommissioning of NFS roads as well as temporary roads. Road construction and reconstruction could include preliminary site investigations, actual building, and any activities incidental to the construction or reconstruction of a road. Road maintenance would include upkeep of the entire road facility including surface and shoulders, parking and side areas, structures, and such traffic-control devices as are necessary for its safe and efficient utilization. Road decommissioning would include activities that result in the stabilization and restoration of unneeded, temporary or unauthorized roads to a more natural state.

Timber harvest activities would likely generate the highest need for permanent and temporary roads on the landscape. Other activities typically requiring road construction include private or special uses, existing rights, minerals and energy exploration, fuels reduction, pre-commercial thinning, watershed restoration, habitat improvement, and recreation development.

Some timber activities would not require any new roads. For example, it’s assumed timber related activities within 0.5 miles of existing roads where terrain slopes are 40 percent or less, would less likely require the development of new, permanent roads. Conversely, where timber activities are occurring beyond 0.5 miles of existing roads, some construction of new temporary and permanent roads would be more likely.<sup>26</sup>

Financing for construction of roads could be accomplished by appropriated funds, requirements on purchasers of national forest timber and other products, by cooperative financing with other public agencies, and with private agencies or persons.

The Forest Service has a significant road maintenance backlog; however, it is assumed that some new road construction and additional maintenance would result from any action alternative. It is further assumed that revenue generated by timber sales or other resource extraction activities would be used for some road-related system management but not be sufficient to cover the costs of constructing and maintaining all new roads related to a project.

##### **Potentially Affected Environment**

###### National Forest Transportation System

The 2001 Roadless Rule EIS identified approximately 9,660 miles of existing roads within all inventoried roadless areas at that time (including Idaho and Colorado roadless areas).<sup>27</sup> According to Forest Service roads datasets, there are currently approximately 9,500 miles of roads within the potentially affected IRAs (which do not include Idaho and Colorado roadless areas). This information is approximate as precision is limited by spatial analysis methods and use of multiple datasets. The approximately 9,500 miles of roads include approximately 9,400 miles of NFS roads and approximately 100 miles of private roads. Available information in Forest Service database records indicate that approximately 60 miles of roads were built within inventoried roadless areas after 2013. The mileage does not include any temporary, unauthorized, or decommissioned roads that may exist or had previously existed on the ground but are not part

###### Deferred Maintenance

Deferred maintenance is tracked and reported each year for passenger car roads (maintenance levels 3, 4 and 5 only) and bridges (see Road System Management Costs and Funding). Deferred maintenance is calculated on a national scale using a random road sample so there is no means to determine deferred maintenance for only roads within the potentially affected environment.

###### Transportation System Management Costs

Each mile of NFS road within inventoried roadless areas competes for limited road maintenance funding as part of the overall NFS road system. Annual allocations for road construction, reconstruction and maintenance are distributed nationally utilizing a formula. The formula does not consider any factor related to land area or designation managed by each region.

Road maintenance activities and maintenance costs within IRAs is typical of that for the road system outside of IRAs as discussed previously.

Road construction costs vary greatly based on terrain, level of service required for intended use, and remoteness. Costs for construction of permanent roads within IRAs are more likely to be much greater than typical road construction because of steep and difficult terrain, extensive earthwork, geotechnical challenges, costly infrastructure such as bridges or tunnels, and accessibility due to isolated locale. Consequently, it is not feasible to provide a typical cost per mile for road construction within the inventoried roadless areas. In general terms, however, Federal Highways Administration describes mountainous terrain as terrain frequently sloping over 33 percent and recognizes the construction of roads in mountainous terrain as being of “unique difficulty and expense.” Further, some data sources indicate construction costs in mountainous terrain can be 3 to 10 times more per mile than flat terrain.<sup>28</sup>

##### **Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action**

###### National Forest Transportation System

Continued management pursuant to the 2001 Roadless Rule would prohibit road construction or reconstruction with limited exceptions. New permanent road construction resulting in a transportation network change unlikely. Road construction or reconstruction would be based on exceptions. For example, since 2018, agency records show regional foresters reviewed and approved 20 exceptions under the 2001 Roadless Rule for proposed roads related activity (see 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule Implementation). The excepted road activities in these records included road construction for resource protection, road realignment, road safety, road construction pursuant to reserved or outstanding rights, and one exception for road construction in connection with a Secretary of Agriculture determination related to a Federal Highway reroute.

The extent of road construction and reconstruction that occurred in the last 10 years pursuant to the rule’s exceptions is not a reliable predictor of future road construction or reconstruction. An increase in NFS transportation system network miles under alternative 1 would be less likely

> 28 Scale of cost inferred from information provided by Federal Highway Administration’s Status of the Nation’s Highways, Bridges, and Transit Condition & Performance 23<sup>rd</sup> Edition (2019) and video file of mountainous road design process example in Colorado (2024).

than under alternatives 2 and 3. Additional access needs and associated roads would be limited to those that fit within the rule’s exceptions.

###### Deferred Maintenance

Ongoing maintenance activities on roads within IRAs would continue, where consistent with land management plan direction. New permanent road construction resulting in increased deferred maintenance, if similar to the past ten years (approximately 60 miles of road constructed), would be inconsequential at the national scale. Therefore, an identifiable change in annual deferred maintenance reported is less likely under alternative 1 than under alternatives 2 and 3.

###### Transportation System Management Costs

New road construction or reconstruction using exceptions could be authorized, which could result in additional needs for maintenance and possible redistribution of limited maintenance funding. This could impact the existing network both inside and outside of IRAs. A major impact on overall transportation system management costs is less likely under alternative 1 than other alternatives considering new road construction would be by exception only and extremely small compared to the broader road network.

##### **Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action**

###### National Forest Transportation System

Elimination of the national prohibitions set forth in the 2001 Roadless Rule for road construction and reconstruction within inventoried roadless areas could result in new road construction and an increase of NFS transportation system mileage overall<sup>29</sup> . Temporary road construction would be more likely on lands within 0.5 miles of existing roads (11.3 million acres or 28.3 percent of the affected environment). Permanent road construction within 0.5 miles of existing roads would be less likely, hence, less likely to result in increase in NFS road system mileage. Activities could be proposed further than 0.5 miles from an existing road, wherein access to the area by new permanent roads would be more likely. In the short term, any new permanent road construction would be limited to areas where it is currently allowed by land management plans (18.2 million acres or 45.5 percent of the affected environment).

###### Deferred Maintenance

New permanent roads would increase future maintenance needs. Similar to alternatives 1 and 3, ongoing maintenance activities on existing roads would likely remain unchanged unless there is a modification at land management direction levels. Under alternative 2, there is more likely to

> 29 New road construction would be accordance with 16 U.S.C. 1608 which states ”Unless the necessity for a permanent road is set forth in the forest development road system plan, any road constructed on land of the National Forest System in connection with a timber contract or other permit or lease shall be designed with the goal of reestablishing vegetative cover on the roadway and areas where the vegetative cover has been disturbed by the construction of the road, within ten years after the termination of the contract, permit, or lease either through artificial or natural means. Such action shall be taken unless it is later determined that the road is needed for use as a part of the National Forest Transportation System.”

be a measurable increase in overall reported NFS road system deferred maintenance for the NFS road system than under alternatives 1 and 3.

###### Transportation System Management Costs

New permanent road construction or reconstruction would be more likely under alternative 2 and would require additional maintenance planning and possible redistribution of limited maintenance funding. This could impact the existing road system both inside and outside of inventoried roadless areas. It’s more likely that there would be an impact to overall transportation system management costs and maintenance fund redistribution considering new road construction and increased maintenance needs under alternative 2 than under alternatives 1 and 3. Budget and resource constraints could limit potential of new permanent road construction.

##### **Effects of Alternative 3 – Modified Rule**

###### National Forest Transportation System

Like alternative 2, removing 2001 Roadless Rule prohibitions on lands within 0.5 miles of existing roads would more likely result in temporary road construction for access; however, permanent road construction would be less likely within 0.5 miles of existing roads and would not likely result in substantial growth in transportation network mileage overall.

Permanent road construction would be more likely in the wildland-urban interface areas that extend further out than 0.5 miles of existing roads. Like alternative 2, as more activities are proposed further than 0.5 miles from existing roads, access to the area by new roads becomes more likely. In the short term, new permanent road construction would be limited to areas where it is currently allowed by land management plans (14.2 million acres or 52 percent of the alternative 3 potentially affected environment).

On the 13 million acres where current management would continue under the 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule, effects would be similar to alternative 1. Road construction or reconstruction would be authorized only when authorized by exceptions. Potential for new permanent road construction in this area would be less likely.

###### Deferred Maintenance

Permanent road construction could occur in the potentially affected IRAs where the roadless rule prohibitions would be removed under this alternative. New permanent roads would increase maintenance needs. Similar to alternative 2, an increase in deferred maintenance for the NFS road network is more likely, but because road construction would remain prohibited in a portion of the affected environment, there would be less increase than alternative 2.

###### Transportation System Management Costs

Removing Roadless Rule prohibitions on lands within 0.5 miles of existing roads would less likely result in permanent road construction or increase in mileage of the NFS road system. Additional access needs by permanent road for the 13 million acres remaining under the Roadless Rule would require an exception. It’s less likely that there would be an increase to overall transportation system management costs related to new road construction. Like alternative 2, new permanent roads would require maintenance planning and possible redistribution of limited maintenance funding; however, new permanent road construction would be less likely for alternative 3. It’s less likely that there would be an impact to overall transportation system management costs due to new road construction and increased maintenance needs.

##### **Conclusion**

Under alternatives 2 and 3, roads would be constructed or reconstructed only where there is a need and funding source –timber harvest or other active vegetation management activity would generate the greatest need. Temporary roads would be more commonly used within ½ mile of existing roads under all alternatives. Current land management plans would allow or generally allow road construction on about half of the potentially affected environments (18 million acres (45.5 percent) for alternative 2 and 14 million acres (53 percent) for alternative 3).

Across all alternatives, permanent road construction and reconstruction is less likely in the shortterm due to land management plan requirements. In the long term, permanent and temporary road construction would be more likely under alternatives 2 and 3; however, it would be less likely under alternative 3 than alternative 2.

National Forest System road mileage, deferred maintenance and management cost increases are more likely under alternatives 2 and 3; however, increases would be less likely under alternative 3 than alternative 2. In the long-term, under alternatives 2 and 3 there could be some need to shift resources and funding from one region to another to account for more permanent mileage added to the NFS road system in a particular region.
