#### Access to Non-National Forest System Lands and Special Use Authorizations

##### **Background**

The Forest Service authorizes activities on, or occupancy of National Forest System land for a wide range of improvements considered special uses, including water transmission/storage, utilities, research, military training, communication uses, agriculture, pipelines, recreation events, ski areas, road access and much more. Between recreation and lands special uses, there are more than 200 different types of special uses that may be authorized. Special use authorizations are uses allowed under a special use permit, or similar legal instrument. The Forest Service processes approximately 4,000 new authorizations each year across the entire country, including renewals and reissuances of existing uses.

Special use authorizations are typically reactive in nature to respond to public and customer needs. An authorization is issued only after being evaluated and having passed through screening criteria (36 CFR 251.54), including compliance with the applicable land management plan, and environmental analysis processes. Special use authorizations are the legal instrument that the agency issues to allow for the use and occupancy of NFS lands and that enable the holder to operate in otherwise prohibited activities and/or areas. Special use authorization terms range widely based on the use type, applicable legal authorities, and practical need with the term ranging anywhere from 1 day in length (such as recreation events like a bike race) to being an authorization with no stated expiration date (such as an easement for a county road). Utilities, roads for private access, and other infrastructure, are commonly issued for longer terms, such as 5-30 years, and renewed or re-issued to continue the use until no longer needed, provided the use complies with the authorization. The Special Uses Database System (SUDS) is the official Forest Service record system for creating, billing and administering authorized special uses.

All the alternatives allow for the continuation, re-issuance, or renewal of existing special use authorizations for activities in roadless areas.

###### Authorizations to Access Lands of Other Ownership

Landownership within NFS boundaries includes parcels of land owned by states, private individuals, and other Federal and non-Federal entities known as inholdings. The most common special uses authorized generally, but not always, rely on road access to accommodate construction, operation, and maintenance on these inholdings. As such, most existing and anticipated future special uses are either located or reasonably likely to be located where roads currently exist.

Roads constructed to access private lands within or adjacent to NFS lands, are typically the responsibility of the benefiting landowner who secures an authorization. However, when it is in the interest of the Forest Service, the agency may accept or share responsibility for operation and maintenance. Members of the public may use open, unrestricted NFS roads without a special use authorization (36 CFR 251.50(d)). If a party proposes road maintenance or commercial hauling, an authorization is typically required (36 CFR 251.110(d)).

Alaska National Interest Lands Conservation Act (ANILCA), Section 1323 ensures access to non-Federal land in-holdings. Under ANILCA and 36 CFR part 251.110, subpart D, the authorized officer must provide access adequate for the landowner’s reasonable use and enjoyment. Access need not be the most direct or convenient and may be non‑motorized or via alternate routes, including across other ownerships. Alternative routes and modes of access may be considered. If a landowner has an adequate alternative route or mode of access, including access across other land ownerships, the Forest Service is not obligated to authorize roaded access. Reasonable access is determined on a case-by-case basis and may not require year-round motorized access via a road. The Forest Service recognizes valid ANILCA access as a statutory right.

Reserved or Outstanding Rights for Access, Occupancy, and Use

The 2001 Roadless Rule prohibition on road construction and reconstruction in IRAs did not affect already existing access, occupancy, and use rights. The rights include those that exist by law, treaty, or by other authority. They include, but are not limited to, the right to provide reasonable access across NFS lands to private property, mining claims for locatable minerals under the 1872 Mining Law, and land uses protected by Native American treaty rights. These rights include, but are not limited to, rights of access provided in the Alaska National Interest Lands Conservation Act (ANILCA) (Public Law 96-487) and recognized highway rights-of-way granted over NFS lands under Revised Statute 2477 (Public Law 94-579). The most common type of access pursued in conjunction with these two prominent statues is motorized road access.

###### Rights-of-Way

R.S. 2477 provides a means by which rights-of-way were granted for public highways constructed across public domain lands in the late 1800s to early 1900s. A R.S. 2477 highway must have been constructed across public domain lands before the date of the national forest reservation. R.S. 2477 did not require the issuance of any formal authorization to exercise and perfect a right-of-way. The Federal Lands Policy Management Act repealed R.S. 2477 in 1976. However, rights-of-way that predate the establishment of the national forest are still in effect, unless they have been subsequently relinquished. The Forest Service recognizes valid R.S. 2477 right-of-way as outstanding rights.

The Forest Service cooperates with the Department of Transportation’s Federal Highway Administration (FHWA) to grant Counties and States rights-of-ways for highway projects on the Federal aid system or under chapter 2 of the Federal Highway Act (23 U.S.C. 317). Examples include adding a passing lane to an existing highway, assigning road jurisdiction to the State or County for an existing road that requires maintenance, or even the new construction of a highway.

##### **Potentially Affected Environment**

Land use authorizations and rights-of-way existing prior to the 2001 Roadless Rule are ongoing. The Special Uses Database System does not have specific tracking or reporting capabilities to identify the number or location of uses in IRA compared to other NFS lands. Inventoried roadless areas typically have fewer existing roads, improvements, and development, therefore special uses are not a major consideration, relative to other National Forest System lands. Agency records show regional foresters have reviewed and approved few exceptions related to special use authorizations that authorized road construction or reconstruction or tree cutting in IRAs between 2018-2025, including both lands and recreation special uses (see 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule Implementation). The uses included military training, installation of telecommunications, pipeline and powerline work and related vegetation management, facility maintenance and removal of water storage/transmission, communication site, road access, and ski area facilities. In context of the national special uses program, these exceptions account for less than 1 percent of the national special uses’ annual workload.

##### **Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action**

Special use authorizations that existed prior to the 2001 Roadless Rule would continue until their term ends. The 2001 Roadless Rule contains exceptions to the road construction and tree cutting prohibitions for reasonable exercise of reserved or outstanding rights for access, occupancy, and use of NFS lands. These would continue and new requests would be authorized, consistent with relevant statutes. Public roads and highways that would have required additional or new rightsof-ways from the FWHA within IRAs, would continue to require an exception to proceed, though they are very rare.

Most new proposals involving road construction or timber harvest (tree cutting) that are not based on a reserved or outstanding right or provided in statute or treaty within IRAs would typically be rejected in the screening process due to the conflicting nature of the use and land designation. However, other new uses in IRAs that do not require road construction, road reconstruction, or timber harvest (tree cutting) are evaluated on their own merits and case specifics and authorized consistent to other special uses outside of IRAs.

##### **Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action**

Similar to alternative 1, pre-existing uses and those related to reserved or outstanding rights for access, occupancy, and use of NFS lands would continue. It is reasonably foreseeable that there would be an increase in new proposals for uses that benefit the public or provide access to nonFederal lands within or adjacent to IRAs, as well as requests for expansion of existing uses (for example, additional access routes or improved road quality, as well as frequency of use). Under the proposed action, new special use proposals would be evaluated on their own merits and no longer be rejected outright due to prohibitions for road construction or reconstruction.

Requests for and approval of road construction-related authorizations are most likely to occur in areas that are ‘operable’ and where current land management plans allow these activities (see National Forest System Roads and Vegetation and Forest Management sections). Alternative 2 would enable the FHWA and Forest Service to more swiftly accommodate public access needs involving road construction or reconstruction on highways within the potentially affected IRAs.

There is little difference in effect between alternatives with respect to how the Forest Service would respond to an ANILCA access requests, but, under alternatives 2 and 3, it would be less cumbersome to evaluate, grant and administer access that include new road construction that are not categorically prohibited in roadless areas. Under all alternatives, the reasonable exercise of reserved or outstanding rights for access, occupancy, and use of NFS lands within roadless areas would not be affected. There is little to no effect between alternatives with respect to how the Forest Service would respond to an R.S. 2477 claim.

##### **Effects of Alternative 3 – Modified Rule**

These effects would be similar to alternative 1 in areas that continue to be managed under Rule prohibitions and similar to alternative 2 in areas where the roadless designation and prohibitions no longer apply. Requests would be most likely to occur in areas where the prohibitions no longer apply, that are operable, and where current land management plans allow these activities (see National Forest System Roads and Vegetation and Forest Management sections).

#### Mineral Exploration and Development

##### **Background**

On National Forest System lands, minerals are classified according to the law under which they are managed. Minerals are classified as locatable, leasable, and mineral material (common variety or salable). This distinction is important because each classification is subject to different authorities and requirements for exploration and development, and in some cases the Forest Service cannot prohibit exploration and development. Exploration and development of any domestic mineral or energy resources is a function of fluctuating commodity prices, environmental and permitting costs associated with domestic mineral or energy resource development, accessibility to domestic mineral or energy resources, and level of public support for mineral and energy resource development on Federal lands. At present, the administration’s priorities are to remove barriers to development of domestic mineral and energy resources as identified in Executive Order 14154 Unleashing American Energy, Executive Order 14156 Declaring a National Energy Emergency, Executive Order 14241 Immediate Measures to Increase American Mineral Production, and Executive Order 14261 Reinvigorating America's Beautiful Clean Coal Industry and Amending Executive Order 14241.

###### Locatable Minerals

Most valuable mineral deposits on lands open to mineral entry are considered locatable unless otherwise determined to be leasable or a mineral material. Locatable minerals include gold, silver, copper, lead, zinc, barite, gypsum, and certain varieties of limestone, which are subject to appropriation under the General Mining Law of 1872 (17 Stat. 91, as amended). Most of the critical minerals (USGS 2025) are locatable under the mining laws. The Mining Law of 1872 provides United States citizens a right to prospect, explore and develop these minerals on public domain lands. It applies to National Forest System lands by virtue of the Organic Administration Act (16 U.S.C. §§ 482). It also provides for access suitable for conducting these activities. Depending on the stage of exploration or development, reasonable access can range from unimproved temporary roads for prospecting or drilling to more permanent improved roads for full mine development and transportation of ore. The Forest Service cannot prohibit reasonably necessary activities required for the exploration, prospecting, or development of valuable mineral deposits. However, the Forest Service has the authority and the obligation to regulate locatable mineral operations in order to prevent or minimize damage to surface resources (36 CFR Part 228, Subpart A).

###### Leasable Minerals

Leasable minerals are those that can be explored for and developed under one of the several mineral-leasing Acts. They include energy mineral resources such as oil, gas, oil shale, coal, gilsonite, and geothermal. They also include non-energy minerals, such as phosphate, and minerals important for their sodium, potassium, or sulfur content. Moreover, for lands acquired or administered under the Weeks Act (Public Law 61-435) (mostly in the Eastern United States) and the Bankhead-Jones Act (Public Law 75-210), they include deposits of otherwise locatable minerals like gold, copper, lead, barite, and gypsum.

Exploration and development of leasable mineral resources are discretionary activities. This means that leasing may or may not be allowed by the Bureau of Land Management, the agency that has the authority to dispose of leasable mineral resources on National Forest System lands. Most leasable mineral resources, however, may only be leased if the Forest Service provides consent. The Bureau of Land Management and Forest Service evaluate the effects of any future lease exploration and/or development in project specific environmental review documents and ensure compliance with other relevant law, regulation, and policy prior to leasing.

Once issued, the holder of a lease or permit has a contractual agreement with the government that allows reasonable access for exploration and development of the leased commodity. After a lease is issued, it can be modified and adjusted for economic or technical reasons. Often, during mine development areas of mineral will be identified that are not included in the original lease. To promote recovery or prevent environmental damage, these areas may be added to the existing leases. As an example, it may be more environmentally sound to locate ancillary facilities, such as topsoil and overburden stockpiles, in areas outside the boundaries of the issued lease. This would require a modification of the lease and possibly expanding the lease boundary.

Coal and oil and gas exploration and development are the most likely leasable minerals within inventoried roadless areas in the affected environment.

###### _Oil and Gas Leasing and Development_

Approval for leasing, exploration, or development of oil and gas on National Forest System lands is governed by the Mineral Leasing Act of 1920, the Federal Onshore Oil and Gas Leasing Reform Act of 1987. The Bureau of Land Management issues Federal oil and gas leases on National Forest System lands and approves drilling through the application permit to drill (APDs). The Forest Service provides consent to the Bureau of Land Management to lease on National Forest System lands (a forest-wide programmatic analysis is typically completed). The Forest Service approves the site-specific surface use plan of operations (SUPO), applying additional site-specific protections that do not conflict with lease terms. The Forest Service monitors surface disturbing activities, enforces surface use requirements, and ensures reclamation (36 CFR 228 Subpart E).

###### _Coal Leasing and Development_

Approval for leasing, exploration, or development of coal on National Forest System lands is governed by the Mineral Leasing Act of 1920, the Federal Coal Leasing Amendments Act of 1976, codified in 43 CFR 3400. Coal leasing is proponent driven and leasing is typically completed on a site-specific basis. Coal markets are dictated by demand, typically for thermal coal used in electrical power generation. When the Bureau of Land Management receives a coal lease application located on National Forest System lands, the Bureau of Land Management requests Forest Service consent to issue a Federal coal lease and under what conditions it may do so. The Forest Service completes appropriate environmental review prior to making the consent determination with conditions of its consent to protect surface resources, site specific coal lease stipulations. Once a lease is issued, the lessee or operator will be required to submit a Federal mine plan or amendment to an existing mine plan to the Office of Surface Mining Reclamation and Enforcement and/or the state agency with primacy over coal mine permitting in that state. The Forest Service will cooperate on permit reviews and may recommend additional conditions to protect surface resources that do not conflict with the contractual rights of the lessee identified in the coal lease per 30 CFR Part 700.

Exploration for coal occurs on Federal lands with the issuance of a coal exploration license obtained from the Bureau of Land Management on lands where coal has not already been leased. On-lease exploration can occur on lands currently under a Federal coal lease. For either situation, road construction may be necessary for the drilling of bore holes for sampling and coring in the exploration for coal. However, this activity is subject to site specific environmental review, where restrictions to the surface may apply and could also preclude road construction on lands not suitable for that activity.

##### **Potentially Affected Environment**

Exploration, mining, and mineral processes under the mineral disposal authorities occur within IRAs and are not themselves prohibited activities. A lack of existing roads, as they were defined in the criteria for identifying the 2001 inventoried roadless areas, does not necessarily imply that these areas have little or no prospective value for containing mineral or energy deposits. Road construction, reconstruction, as well as incidental tree cutting have continued within inventoried roadless areas, subject to the exceptions in the 2001 Roadless Rule. Roads constructed for mineral or energy prospecting activities are typically temporary in nature and, therefore, not National Forest System roads.

###### Locatable Minerals

Valuable deposits of locatable mineral resources may occur in IRAs, principally west of the 100th meridian. The Forest Service does not track the number of proposals in relation to IRAs, so does not have data to indicate the relative amount of proposed plans of operations that are within or outside IRAs. Where reasonable access involving temporary or permanent road construction or tree cutting is reasonably necessary to support exploration, mining or other locatable mineral operations, these activities are reviewed relative to the 2001 Roadless Rule exception for existing right of access as provided for in the General Mining Law (36 CFR 294.12(b)(3); 36 CFR 294.13(b)(2)). Agency records show that since 2018 regional foresters reviewed and approved 19 exceptions related to locatable mineral projects; the majority of which were exploration operations which required cutting or removing timber rather than road construction (2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule Implementation).

###### Leasable Minerals

Oil and gas and coal resources occur within the potentially affected IRAs. Table 10 shows by region the number of existing Federal oil and gas leases authorized and in production within the potentially affected environment (Minerals & Lands Record System). At present, there are 271 leases with a surface area of approximately 538,300 acres within the IRAs nationwide, excluding Colorado and Idaho, in authorized and producing status.

According to the Minerals Lands Records System, there are 14 authorized Federal coal leases within the potentially affected IRAs, totaling 13,050 acres of land. All of these leases are within the Intermountain Region (Region 4) on the Manti-La Sal National Forest in Utah. Underground methods of extraction are authorized on these leases with limited surface disturbance for exploration, powerlines, vent shafts and pipelines. The federal coal leases are also adjacent to private and state coal tracts that are developed or will be developed during mine development.

##### **Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action**

###### Locatable

Under alternative 1, the 2001 Roadless Rule, road construction and reconstruction or timber cutting for locatable mineral exploration and development would continue to be considered a right of access as provided by the General Mining Law, excepted from the prohibition on road construction or reconstruction, and tree-cutting (36 CFR 294.12(b)(3); 36 CFR 294.13(b)(2)). Therefore, in the long term, it is foreseeable that future exploration, mining, and mineral processing activities would continue to occur in IRAs where valuable deposits exist.

The Forest Service receives an average of 198 plans of operations each year across all NFS lands, ranging from individual operators with small equipment up to large operating mines (USDA FS 2019). The relative amount of these proposals that are within IRAs are not tracked, but a portion of them are assumed to be. Not all plans of operations propose road construction or reconstruction or tree cutting. This same rate of mineral exploration and development is reasonably foreseeable for future decades because we have little information that would lead to higher or lower expectations of development. The Forest Service hears anecdotally and in public comment that there is a perception that the 2001 Roadless Rule prohibitions have affected locatable mineral development, but the reasonable access to locatable minerals, including construction or reconstruction of roads or timber cutting where warranted, is a right of access provided by the General Mining Law, and the Forest Service has continued to approve the construction and reconstruction of roads after the 2001 Roadless Rule was implemented. In fact, of the 11 large-scale mines producing at least one commodity in 2024, six are operating partially or wholly within IRAs (USDA FS 2025c).

All future proposals for locatable mineral exploration or development within inventoried roadless areas would be subject to the planning and design requirements governing locatable minerals in 36 CFR 228, Subpart A<sup>30</sup> . If proposed operations meet the threshold to require a plan of operations, and an analysis of environmental effects would be conducted under NEPA. This plan of operations would be binding on the operator.

###### Leasable Minerals

Under this alternative, management of leasable mineral resources in IRAs would not change the way they are currently managed. Leases issued prior to the 2001 Roadless Rule have valid existing rights and therefore are not subject to the road construction prohibitions in the rule. These leases would continue to be managed under existing laws and regulations governing coal under this alternative without consideration of the 2001 Roadless Rule.

For new or modification of leases Forest Service and Bureau of Land Management, would continue to follow the detailed review process, including environmental impact statements and assessments under the overarching law (see Background). Consistent with the 2001 Roadless Rule prohibitions, a no surface occupancy would be applied to new leases within IRAS, limiting where the lesee could construct roads. The 2001 Roadless Rule would continue to restrict road building associated with new leasable mineral exploration, both on-lease and off-lease exploration, subject to valid existing rights. However, this would not completely preclude exploration from occurring on preexisting roads. The prohibitions could also potentially limit the expansion of existing leases and operations where further identification of leasable mineral reserves or surface developments requiring roads may be needed in the future.

> 30 On February 20, 2026, the Forest Service published a proposed rule in the Federal Register (91 FR 8316) to revise the locatable minerals regulation at 36 CFR Part 228, Subpart A The proposed revisions improve the efficiency and transparency of Forest Service regulation of operations conducted on National Forest System lands under the mining laws, while continuing to minimize, to the fullest extent practicable, adverse impacts on surface resources.

New or modified leases would continue to be managed under existing laws and regulations governing oil and gas and coal under this alternative but would need to consider the 2001 Roadless Rule in leasing decisions.

##### **Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action**

###### Locatable

Under these alternatives, the effects on locatable mineral exploration and development are generally the same as those under alternative 1. Road construction, reconstruction, and tree cutting for locatable mineral exploration and development is a right of access as provided by the General Mining Law. However, the Forest Service hears anecdotally and through public comment that locatable mineral development has been impeded. To the extent that the perception of limitations from the roadless rule has prevented operators from proposing to conduct operations, that perception would be removed.

###### Leasable

New, modified or renewed leases would continue to be managed under existing laws and regulations governing oil and gas and coal under this alternative.

Existing leases would continue under alternative 2. For new leases or modifications of leases, under alternative 2 construction or reconstruction of roads that are reasonable and necessary for development of existing energy or mineral leases, for access to new or existing energy or mineral leases, and for access to associated product conveyance lines could be allowed as necessary in accordance with the terms of the lease, where they are consistent with land management plans or other requirements. In accordance with the terms of the lease, approval of operations involving road construction within potentially affected IRAs would be more likely to be authorized without the no surface occupancy lease stipulation, than alternative 1.

When existing leases expire, renewals would not need to consider the prohibitions associated with the 2001 Roadless Rule. In addition, the IRA boundary would not apply or be considered in any analyses for the modification of existing leases. However, in a site-specific leasing analysis a no surface occupancy stipulation may still be applied to protect surface resources. For example, to prevent excessive erosion on steep slopes, a no surface occupancy may still be applied to the Federal lease.

##### **Effects of Alternative 3 – Modified Rule**

###### Locatable

The effects would be the same as alternative 2.

###### Leasable

Within areas that continue to be managed under the Roadless Rule prohibitions, effects would be similar to alternative 1. New or modified leases would be stipulated with a no surface occupancy stipulation, limiting where the lessee could build roads within the lease boundary. The IRA would continue to restrict road construction associated with new leasable mineral exploration, both on-lease and off-lease exploration, subject to valid existing rights. When existing leases within the retained IRAs (see Table 11) expire, any renewals would have to consider the road building restrictions with respect to the IRA, if a lease was issued prior to the 2001 Roadless Rule. The effect would be the same as the effect in alternative 1.

In areas where the IRA designation and prohibition no longer apply, effects would be similar to alternative 2. Construction or reconstruction of roads that are reasonable and necessary for development of existing energy or mineral leases, for access to new or existing energy or mineral leases, and for access to associated product conveyance lines could be allowed as necessary in accordance with the terms of the lease, where they are consistent with land management plans or other requirements. In accordance with the terms of the lease, approval of operations involving road building within areas previously designated as IRA would be more likely without the no surface occupancy lease stipulation.

According to the Minerals Lands Records System, of the 14 total authorized Federal coal leases in IRAs, all are in Utah, located on the Manti-La Sal National Forest and within the alternative 3 affected environment, totaling 12,727 acres of land. Underground methods of extraction are authorized on these leases with limited surface disturbance for exploration, powerlines, vent shafts and pipelines. The Federal coal leases are also adjacent to private and State coal tracts that are developed or will be developed during the life of the mine and are subject to valid existing rights. The effect would be the same as the effect in alternative 1, unless subject to valid existing right.

##### **Conclusion**

Across all alternatives, mineral and energy development would continue where there are existing rights or permits/leases. The alternatives differ relative to new or expanded authorizations for leasable minerals (primarily oil and gas and coal). Under alternative 1, across all 40 million acres of potentially affected IRAs, new or expanded authorizations for oil and gas or coal exploration and development would be subject to review and include surface use restrictions, including limiting road construction, consistent with the roadless rule prohibitions. This would also apply under alternative 3 in areas that remain IRAs (13 million acres). Under alternative 2, new authorizations and expanded authorizations could include road construction and reconstruction, depending on other legal requirements, such as land management plan components, and resource protection considerations. This would also apply to alternative 3 in areas that are no longer designated IRAs.

#### Vegetation and Forest Management

##### **Background**

This section examines forested and non-forested vegetation within areas managed under the 2001 Roadless Rule, describing the diversity of plant communities, with attention to forest health, carbon, and timber management. It provides background on national trends and emerging issues that shape conditions across the National Forest System and outlines opportunities for forest management. The discussion is intended to frame how these factors interact with land management direction and set the context for comparing effects under the three proposed alternatives.

##### **Potentially Affected Environment**

The potentially affected environment spans approximately 40 million acres with a wide range of vegetation types and conditions (see Table 12). About half of the potentially affected IRAs are forested lands, as defined by the National Landcover Dataset (USGS 2024). These forests are predominantly classified as evergreen forest, with smaller proportions of deciduous forest and mixed forest, reflecting the ecological diversity within roadless areas. Non-forest vegetation ecosystems include extensive shrub/scrub and dwarf scrub cover, grassland; herbaceous, and sedge communities; as well as wetland systems, including woody and emergent herbaceous wetlands. These land cover classes, derived from the National Land Cover Database, provide a foundational understanding of the ecological composition and potential resource sensitivities relevant to evaluating the alternatives. Since the 2001 Roadless Rule, inventoried roadless areas have been managed according to the rule, with the goal of sustaining the public benefits including clean water, biodiversity, recreational opportunities, wildlife habitat, and forest health provided by these areas.

###### Forest Vegetation

About 145 of the 193 million acres, or three-quarters, of the National Forest System lands are forested. Within the potentially affected IRAs, about 21 million acres (52 percent) are forested. Most of the forested area within the potentially affected IRAs (96 percent) is in the western states. The Alaska Region includes 5.79 million acres and the Intermountain Region 5.76 million acres, which make up 46 percent of all forested lands. The Pacific Northwest and Pacific Southwest Regions include 20 percent of the IRA forests (2.9 and 2.1 million acres respectively). The Rocky Mountain and Southwestern Regions include 11 percent of the forests (1.74 and 1.19 million acres respectively). The Southern and Eastern Regions include 4 percent of the IRA forests (each with about a half million acres).

**Figure 8. Forested acres within the potentially affected IRAs by Forest Service region, based on Forest Inventory and Analysis data**

Forest composition, stand age, and structure are important characteristics governing the potential ecosystem services forests deliver and frame their role in providing services to surrounding communities. Composition, structure, and relative abundance of forest types guide local land management planning and decisions to optimize benefits for the public. Forest Inventory and Analysis data on forest composition within the affected environment identifies 26 different forest type groups and their dominant species (Table 13). The predominant forest types within the potentially affected IRAs are the conifer forests of the coastal and Cascade Mountain Ranges of Northern California, Oregon, and Washington; the Rocky Mountains from New Mexico to Montana; and the Alexander Archipelago of Southeast Alaska.

1. Note the total forested acres from Forest Service Forest Inventory and Analysis differs from the forested acres national land cover database classes (Table 12) due to different methodologies in these data sources.

Five conifer groups make up over 75 percent of the forest cover within the affected environment. The most extensive forest type is the fir/spruce/mountain hemlock group of higher elevation forests occurring from northern New Mexico to Alaska and covering nearly 7 million acres. The second most abundant forest type is the pinyon/juniper group covering 3.7 million acres in the Intermountain and Southwestern Regions. Pinyon-juniper forests are typically not harvested for sawtimber but include fuelwood and other potential forest products. Three additional groups each make up 10-11 percent of the total forest cover of the affected environment, including the Douglas-fir group (3 million acres) abundant in the Northern and Pacific Northwest Regions, the Hemlock/Sitka spruce group (2.8 million acres) common in the Alaska Region, and the Lodgepole pine group (2.7 million acres) common in the Northern and Rocky Mountain Regions. Mixed hardwood forests cover 16 percent of the affected environment. They feature oak, hickory, maple, and beech, and are common in the Appalachians and Midwest. Aspen/birch forests and woodland hardwoods are common in the Intermountain Region. In addition to the most abundant forest types within the potentially affected IRAs, relatively unique forest types of lesser extent include the longleaf/slash pine group and the oak/gum/cypress group as identified through Forest Inventory and Analysis data (see Figure 9).

**Figure 9. Number of acres of forest land and forest type within the inventoried roadless areas affected environment by NFS region, based on Forest Inventory Assessment data**

Forests across the potentially affected IRAs include a variety of young, mature, and old growth stands which each provide unique benefits. Across all forest types within the affected environment outside of Alaska,<sup>31</sup> approximately 16-17 percent of the forested areas and Puerto Rico, are old growth, 48-52 percent are mature, and 32-35 percent are young forest.<sup>32</sup> Old growth stands encompass the later stages of stand development that typically differ from earlier stages by tree size, accumulation of large dead woody material, number of canopy layers, species composition, and ecosystem function (USDA FS 1989). They are characterized by multi-layered stands that serve as long-term carbon storage and unique habitat (Woodall et al. 2023). Across all forest types within the affected environment outside of Alaska, approximately 16-17 percent of the forested areas and Puerto Rico, are old growth, 48-52 percent are mature, and 32-35 percent are young forest. Mature forests vary widely and exhibit structural characteristics that are lacking in earlier stages of forest development and may contain some but not all the structural attributes in old-growth forests. Mature forests provide benefits such as biodiversity conservation, wildlife habitat, watershed protection, carbon storage, and opportunities for recreation, timber production, and cultural use. Young forests generally result from disturbance, management, or natural succession and provide benefits for wildlife by increasing habitat diversity and stand initiation for future timber production.

While some of the forested areas have existing roads (see National Forest System Roads section) or had a history of forest management prior to the 2001 Roadless Rule, many of these forests

> 31 See Environmental Impacts Within the Tongass National Forest for discussion of old growth within the Tongass National Forest.

> 32 Based on the working definitions developed for “Mature and Old Growth Forests: Definition, Identification, and Initial Inventory on Lands Managed by the Forest Service and Bureau of Land Management” (Brandt et. al. 2023).

remain largely undisturbed from extensive management activities. Forests are subject to a variety of disturbances that affect their structure, composition, and the delivery of natural benefits, ranging from timber production to carbon sequestration to recreational opportunities (Costanza et al. 2023). These disturbances are frequently categorized as biotic (insects and disease) or abiotic (severe weather, drought, fire). Some areas of the affected environment are at risk of wildfire, drought, and insect and disease epidemics (see also sections that follow). In addition, environmental drivers within and adjacent to IRAs have changed in recent decades, with increasing drought (Zhuang et al. 2024), more extreme temperatures (Alizadeh et al. 2020), and associated changes in wildfire behavior (Balch et al. 2024) and insect and disease infestations.

Carbon sequestration, storage, and emissions are important functions of forests, including in the inventoried roadless areas. Climate, soil productivity, management actions, fire and other natural disturbance processes interact to govern carbon storage. Generally, forests in the United States are a carbon sink and CO2 uptake in the contiguous United States offsets between 12 to 19 percent of total carbon dioxide emissions each year (Janowiak et al. 2017, Ryan et al. 2010). Aboveground biomass in U.S. forests has increased in recent decades, primarily due to increases in biomass per unit area as opposed to increased forest land area (Oswalt et al. 2019). Regional variation in carbon flux in forest land is largely controlled by climate and disturbance. In 2021, forested lands within the national forest system sequestered 43.5 million metric tons (MMT) CO2, which follows national trends of increasing forest biomass in recent decades, however forests in the drier and more fire prone Intermountain, Rocky Mountain, Northern, and Southwestern Forest Service regions had net carbon loss (31.5 MMT CO2) while the remaining regions had a net carbon increase, sequestering 63.5 MMT CO2 (Domke et al. 2023). Stored carbon within forests, including in the inventoried roadless areas, can fluctuate due to forest growth, management, fire, and mortality due to insects and disease. Birdsey et al. (2025) estimate the IRAs contain about 5 percent of the stored forest carbon in the coterminous United States (0.9B Mg C). Carbon loss from insects, disease, and high severity fires can be significant, long lasting, and expected in any of the alternatives. Under all alternatives, the inventoried roadless areas will continue to be managed primarily as forests and continue to sequester and store carbon.

###### Insect and Disease

Commonly observed mortality-causing insects in U.S. forests are native bark beetles of conifers, including fir engraver, spruce beetle, mountain pine beetle, eastern larch beetle, and exotic insects, including balsam woolly adelgid, spongy moth, and emerald ash borer. Tree diseases such as root rots, oak decline, sub-alpine fir decline, and yellow cedar decline also cause tree mortality (based on USDA Forest Service Forest Health Protection data).

Quantifying the effects of insects and diseases on forests, including the amount within potentially affected IRAs, can be challenging due to limitations of data sources (Fitts et al. 2022; Potter et al. 2020). Forest Health Protection detection survey data provides annual snapshots of landscapescale insect and disease impacts on forests. These data are collected predominately via aerial surveys, whose spatial accuracy can be influenced by the scale of the pest event, landscape complexity, and survey conditions; mortality observations collected via aerial detection surveys are more than 70 percent accurate (Coleman et al. 2018, Bright et al. 2020). Between 2000 and 2023, Forest Health Protection detection surveys documented insect and disease-driven tree mortality on 0.8 to 8.4 million acres of all U.S. forests per year (Coleman et al. 2018; USDA 2020, 2022a, 2022b 2023a, 2023b 2024a), including NFS, State, private, and Tribal forests. Some of this annual mortality is observed within IRAs, but due to the nature of the surveys it’s not possible to characterize the mortality observed on IRAs on a national scale.

The presence and severity of insects and disease are influenced by local conditions, which vary across the Nation, including climatic conditions (such as temperature or drought), stand conditions (density, basal area, tree size, and number of canopy layers), landscape context (proximity to outbreak) and pest-host dynamics (mechanism of attack, host defense) (Weed et al. 2013; Costanza et al. 2023; Forest Health Protection 2025; Audley et al. 2020). The National Insect and Disease Risk Map leverages these predictors to model the risk of tree mortality due to insects and diseases over a 15-year period.

According to the 2024 National Insect and Disease Risk Map (Forest Health Protection 2025), approximately 32 million acres, or 22 percent, of all National Forest System forests in the lower 48 states are at risk of tree mortality due to major forest insects and diseases during the 20242038 period. The proportion of forests at risk varies regionally and by management designation. National Forests System forested lands outside of designated areas (wilderness, wild and scenic, and inventoried roadless areas) generally have more active management and are compared to forests in the potentially affected areas, which remain largely undisturbed from extensive management activities. The percent of NFS forests outside of designated areas at risk ranges from 8.9 percent to 12.4 percent in the eastern United States and from 17.5 percent to 50.4 percent in the western United States (Table 14). Insect and disease risk is generally higher in western regions; in western forests, the percent of forests at risk is similar or lower in potentially affected IRAs when compared to NFS forests outside of designated areas.

The areas of potentially affected IRAs that have the greatest amount of insect and disease risk occur within the Northern, Rocky Mountain, Intermountain, and Pacific Northwest regions. In western North America, the mountain pine beetle is the primary tree killing insect. It typically occurs at endemic levels causing low-level scattered tree mortality of less than 2 percent per year, but local biotic and abiotic conditions can trigger mountain pine beetle epidemics that result in widespread tree mortality (USDA FS 2000c). Epidemics have increased in magnitude in recent years (Raffa et al. 2008) and have impacted over 25 million acres in the western United States from 2000-2020 (Audley et al. 2020), including a widespread mortality event in the Central Rockies that impacted millions of acres where stand and weather conditions were favorable to mountain pine beetle (Chapman et al. 2012).

###### Non-Forested Vegetation

Non-forested vegetation varies greatly across the potentially inventoried roadless areas. Alpine meadows and tundra zones, found in the Sierra Nevada and Alaska and other areas, host treeless, high-elevation plant communities of grasses, sedges, and wildflowers. Shrublands and chaparral ecosystems, dominated by drought-tolerant species like sagebrush and manzanita, are widespread in California, the Southwest, and the Intermountain West. Native grasslands and prairies, rich in bunchgrasses and herbaceous plants, stretch across the Great Plains and interior basins. Riparian zones and wetlands, which line streams and rivers, support vegetation such, sedges, and aquatic plants. Approximately 37 percent of the potentially affected environment are non-forested vegetation types (14.9 million acres). For this analysis, non-forested vegetation is defined as plant communities containing a preponderance of herbaceous (grass, grass-like, and forb) or shrub species. These communities will be identified as woody and emergent herbaceous wetlands, grassland/herbaceous and sedges, shrub/scrub/and dwarf scrub, and cultivated crops, pasture/hay (see Table 12). Non-forested vegetation consists of native species and communities, but, in some places, introduction of non-native grasses and forbs has occurred for revegetation or restoration purposes. Plant communities with desirable non-native herbaceous or shrubby species will be considered in the context of non-forested vegetation.

###### Timber and Forest Management

The potentially affected environment is a varied landscape – differences in physical terrain and distance from roads affect potential operability for forest management. Forest management activities in inventoried roadless areas are currently limited to infrequent cutting, sale, or removal of small diameter trees for certain exceptions; and further restricted due to the prohibition on road construction, reconstruction, and temporary road construction which limits access (36 CFR 294.12 and 294.13 (2001)). Since 2018, agency records indicate that regional foresters reviewed and approved 168 exceptions that involved some level of forest management and an additional 14 with tree cutting in association with a roads-related activity (see 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule Implementation). The majority were related to maintaining and restoring ecosystem characteristics, including hazardous fuels reduction. Many were for incidental timber removal to an otherwise not prohibited activity, including minerals activities. To a lesser extent, exceptions approved tree cutting for improving threatened, endangered, or sensitive species habitat and for personal use. Approval of tree cutting exceptions can be delegated to responsible officials below the regional forester level and has been in some regions, so it is likely additional exceptions were granted. In addition, the agency has not tracked if the regionally approved exceptions were eventually implemented.

To understand the timber and non-commercial activities that have been implemented in inventoried roadless areas, the best available information is the Forest Service Activity Tracking System database. Based on that data, from 2014-2024, timber harvest within the potentially affected IRAs was completed on approximately 500 acres per year, on average, with volume harvest levels inconsequential to overall nationwide levels. Non-commercial treatments were conducted on average on about 1,700 acres per year nationwide including hazard tree removal, precommercial thinning, planting, pruning, wildlife habitat treatments, and incidental removal.

##### **Common to All Alternatives (Analysis Assumptions)**

The sale of timber is authorized under the United States Constitution’s Property Clause (article 4, section 3, clause 2) and governed under the Organic Administration Act, Multiple Use Sustained Yield Act (16 U.S.C. 528, as amended), which directs the national forests to manage multiple uses including timber, and ensure sustainable harvest levels of timber; and the National Forest Management Act (16 U.S.C. 1600, as amended), which requires land management plans for national forests and grasslands. Land management plans must ensure:

- Timber harvest is only allowed in areas that will not be irreversibly damaged, where cutting units can be visually blended into the terrain, where streams, wetlands, riparian areas, and water bodies are protected.

- Timber harvest does not exceed sustained yield and is not designated solely to maximize outputs.

- Harvested areas must be regenerated within 5 years.

Any project activity, including timber harvest, must be consistent with land management plans. The 2012 Planning Rule provides further direction for forest management activities including timber harvest and non-commercial treatments. These plans include plan components (desired conditions, goals, objectives, standards, and guidelines) and identify areas suitable and not suitable for timber production to maintain sustained yield levels, identify maximum harvest levels on suitable lands (allowable sale quantity or sustained yield limit), and allow, prohibit, or limit timber harvest to protect resources, and ensure forests are regenerated within 5 years.

Forest management activities include timber harvest<sup>33</sup> and non-commercial treatments<sup>34</sup> and are completed for multiple resource objectives, including but not limited to timber production, fuels

> 33Timber Harvest – Forest management activities that include the removal of sawtimber. Timber sales are often used as a least-cost method (revenue is returned to the Federal treasury to offset the costs of preparing and carrying out the timber harvest while portions of receipts are used for post-sale activities) of managing vegetation to meet multiple resource objectives (Forest Service Manual 2400, Forest Service Handbook 2409.14).

> 34 Non-commercial forest management – Forest management activities that do not include the removal of sawtimber, non-commercial uses of timber, or any forest products removed for personal or incidental use. These activities may be completed with mechanized systems or by hand. Non-commercial forest management are higher cost options for managing vegetation to meet multiple resource objectives than timber harvest (Forest Service Manual 2460, Forest Service Handbook 2409.18).

reduction, public health and safety, forest health improvement, watershed improvement, and wildlife habitat improvement. A range of silvicultural prescriptions are utilized to meet direction within individual land management plans including both even-aged and uneven-aged systems (See Table 15 for most common silviculture prescriptions used by region). Timber sales and other removals of forest products support agency strategic objectives to foster resilient, adaptive ecosystems, mitigate wildfire risk, and strengthen communities (USDA FS 2015). On average per region, NFS harvests less than 1 percent of operable areas annually (Forest Service Cut and Sold Reports 2014-2024).

**Table 15. Common silvicultural prescriptions and harvest levels by region in 2020 through 2024**

|**Region**|**Common Harvest Prescriptions**|
|---|---|
|1 – Northern|Commercial Thin,Clearcut,Seed Cut|
|2 – RockyMountain|Commercial Thin, Removal Cut, Clearcut|
|3 – Southwestern|Commercial Thin, Selection Cut|
|4 – Intermountain|Sanitation, Commercial Thin, Clearcut|
|5 – Pacific Southwest|Commercial Thin, Sanitation, Clearcut|
|6 – Pacific Northwest|Commercial Thin, Sanitation, Selection Cut|
|8 – Southern|Commercial Thin, Clearcut, Seed Cut|
|9 – Eastern|Commercial Thin, Clearcut|
|10 - Alaska|Clearcut, Selection Cut|

Forest management would be implemented within the potentially affected environment at some level across all alternatives. Site-specific activities would be authorized and implemented in accordance with laws, regulations, relevant agency policy and land management plans. Implementation of project level forest management activities would be through contracts, agreements, or permits (timber sale, stewardship or service, agreements, or any other implementation vehicle), following all required measures, including land management plan requirements, best management practices, site-specific project design features, and mitigation measures. Silviculture prescriptions used for forest management would likely correspond with prescriptions currently implemented outside of roadless areas (Table 12) because vegetation types are similar. It is likely that future projects proposing forest management activities would be planned crossing boundaries of potentially affected IRAs, aligned with landscape features or land management plan boundaries.

Timber harvest is set to increase nationally due to urgent challenges to meet domestic lumber demand, reduce wildfire risk, and revitalize rural economies (National Active Forest Management Strategy 2025<sup>35</sup> ). Recent policy and legislation require an increase in timber production. Executive Order 14225 Immediate Expansion of American Timber Production, directs the Forest Service to expand timber production, streamline regulations, and modernize forest management to support national well-being. The One Big Beautiful Bill Act (Section 50301)<sup>36</sup> directs the Forest Service to sell at least 250 million board feet more each year for the next 10 years, subject to land management plan limits. Accordingly, the National Active Management Strategy lays out the goal of an increase in timber production nationally by 25 percent, focusing on sustaining healthy forests and watersheds by completing treatments in the right places and locations and in partnership with States, Tribes, and industry for more efficient product delivery.

It is expected that some level of timber would be harvested from the analysis area under all alternatives (though very limited under alternative 1). Assuming overall timber harvest levels increase on National Forest System lands (as indicated by the goals/requirements above), it is expected that some portion of that increase may occur within the potentially affected environment, and under the action alternatives, there could be more opportunities within the analysis area due to the agency’s emphasis on increased timber harvest. Future actual timber harvest levels in the potentially affected environment would be dependent on factors including land management plans, operability, local decision-making and objectives, local industries, agency budgets, and timber product values.

Under all alternatives, incidental tree cutting would continue to occur when it is incidental to an otherwise permitted purpose; such as, removal of hazard trees adjacent to roads or trails for public health and safety reasons; fire line construction for wildland fire suppression or control of prescribed fire; survey and maintenance of property boundaries; mining operations, maintenance of power or water lines, trail maintenance or construction; and removal for personal or administrative use would continue to occur. Personal use includes activities such as cutting Christmas trees and firewood. Administrative use includes activities such as construction of fences, footbridges, and maintenance of facilities.

> 35 The <u>National Active Forest Management Strategy</u> lays out the goal of an increase in timber production nationally by 25 percent, focusing on sustaining healthy forests and watersheds by completing treatments in the right places and locations and in partnership with States, Tribes, and industry for more efficient product delivery. The agency will maximize use of all tools and authorities that have previously not been used to the extent possible, as well as implementing new tools that can be used to facilitate active forest management, including current and future emergency authorities.

> 36 The One Big Beautiful Bill Act (Section 50301) directs the Forest Service annually, beginning in Fiscal Year 2026 and through Fiscal Year 2034 to sell a quantity of timber on National Forest System lands that is at least 250 million board feet greater than the quantity that was sold in the previous fiscal year, subject to land management plan limits. The Forest Service must annually enter into forty 20-year or longer contracts for the sale of national forest materials for Fiscal Year 2025-Fiscal Year 2034.

###### Timber and Forest Management

Alternative 1 would allow for the least discretion for local land managers to tailor forest management, as appropriate to local land conditions. Forest management activity would remain infrequent, due to the 2001 Roadless Rule’s prohibition on timber harvest. Incidental harvest of small diameter timber could occur, where approved by regional foresters through exceptions (36 CFR 294.13(b)(2001)). The level of assumed forest management activity would continue at approximately the same as the recent levels of 500 acres a year of timber harvest and 1,700 acres a year for non-commercial treatments (see Potentially Affected Environment). Even though overall agency timber harvest may increase nationally, in accordance with current agency direction, timber harvest from IRAs under no action would not increase in either the short or long term. Any timber harvest would be an extremely small portion of the agency’s overall timber production. Incidental and permitted tree cutting would occur at historic levels.

###### Implications for Forest Vegetation, Forest Health, and Carbon

No action would continue trends in vegetation succession governed by natural processes, including disturbances, and management associated with fuels reduction or other permitted management actions. Natural processes and trends differ across the IRAs, but generally include forest biomass accumulation, succession, disturbance and tree mortality related to wildfire, drought, windfall, insects and disease. In the long term, the effects of changing natural processes would likely increase disturbance and tree mortality due to wildfire and pests. Fuels management activity that does not involve road construction or tree cutting (such as prescribed fire, or wildfire use) would continue to be allowed and may offset some increased wildfire risk. Additionally, although they are expected to be infrequent, approved tree cutting exceptions may complement natural processes affecting vegetation structure, composition, and forest health to improve habitat or maintain or restore ecosystem composition and structure.

Under the no action alternative, trends in insect and disease activity would continue; these vary across forest type and are influenced by abiotic and biotic conditions. Infrequent activities permitted under the existing exceptions may support localized management action to mitigate insect and disease caused mortality or damage at minor levels. It is expected that risk of insect and disease activity across National Forest System lands would continue to be highest in western conifer forests where fire, drought, and bark beetles can cause widespread tree mortality.

Under alternative 1 carbon dynamics would be predominantly governed by natural processes versus management actions. These trends may include increasing carbon storage overall with regional variation depending on climate, soils, and disturbance by wildfire, insects and disease. In some regions/areas, carbon loss may increase due to increasing incidence of drought, pests, and wildfires (Seidle et al. 2017, Balch et al. 2024).

###### Non-Forested Vegetation

Under alternative 1, not much change is expected in non-forested vegetation in the short term. In the long term, meadows and hardwood stands would be subject to conifer encroachment which reduces their individual size and distribution across the landscape. These reductions would continue unless some type of mechanical treatment action or natural disturbance such as wildfire occurred. Non-forested vegetation structure, such as shrub density, would continue to move away from management objectives making it susceptible to future natural disturbances, such as insect infestations or large wildfires.

##### **Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action**

###### Forest Management

This alternative would give the most flexibility for local land managers to tailor forest management, as appropriate to local land conditions and consistent with local land management plans. The area where timber harvest would be allowed could increase due to the removal of the 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule prohibitions; however, timber harvest could only occur in areas where it’s allowed under the direction of land management plans, agency policy, and areas where mechanized equipment can safely operate (see below). There would be no nationwide prohibitions and therefore, no required process for exceptions.

The proposed rulemaking does not compel or authorize site specific timber harvest – any harvest that occurs would be the result of future site-specific decisions and local market conditions and the specific locations or amounts of activity are not feasible to predict. Increased timber harvest and non-commercial forest management activities are reasonably foreseeable to occur in the potentially affected IRAs. However, these activities would not be equally reasonably foreseeable across all portions of inventoried roadless areas. The likelihood of timber harvest occurring in any given area is analyzed based on operability categories combined with current land management plan direction (see Potential Timber Harvest Opportunity below). Timber harvest would be more likely to occur in the likely operable and operable but complex areas where costs of implementing treatments is lowest through traditional timber sales, where current land management plans allow it, and where natural resource objectives align such as, but not limited to, fuels reduction in moderate and high-risk areas and addressing insect and disease epidemics.

Non-commercial forest management activities could occur in the same operability categories when equipment is used (for example, non-commercial product removal, thinning with equipment, mechanical site prep) and further used into expanded areas when treatments are implemented by hand (such as hand activities including thinning, pruning, planting, etc.). These treatments would also need to be consistent with current land management plans. Under alternative 2, non-commercial forest management completed by hand (without mechanized/logging equipment) could be feasible in any operability category but may be limited by land management plans, capacity, and funding. Incidental and permitted tree cutting activities would continue to occur and may occur at increased levels if public access increases with opened reconstructed roads or newly opened constructed roads. Generally, these activities would occur more in likely operable areas than likely operable but complex or likely not operable areas.

###### _Potential Timber Harvest Opportunity_

To aid in comparison of alternatives, potential timber harvest operability was estimated based on the capability of mechanized treatment or activities, the existing road system, forest productivity, and slopes and is displayed by region.<sup>37</sup> A nationwide geospatial analysis was used to estimate potential operability, or the capability of mechanized treatment or activities, in each area based on slope, distance from roads, site productivity, and statutory restrictions. All forested areas of potentially affected IRAs were grouped into the following three categories:

- **Likely Operable:** These are areas where ground-based systems (mechanized tree cutting, including feller bunchers or harvesters, log skidding, and/or log forwarding) could feasibly be used. They are potentially forest areas that are less than 0.5 miles from a road, on slopes less than 40 percent, are productive for restocking (greater than 20 cubic feet per acre per year), and have no statutory restrictions from timber harvest.

- **Likely operable, but complex:** These are areas that require more complex or costly harvesting systems (for example, high-cost permanent or temporary road construction, 0.5 to 1 mile skidding or forwarding, and/or complex steep slope harvesting systems including cable and/or tether). They are potentially forested areas that are less than 0.5 miles from a road and on slopes between 40 percent and 70 percent or between 0.5 miles and 1 mile from a road and on slopes less than 70 percent. These areas have the same productivity for restocking as likely operable areas and have no statutory restrictions from timber harvest. Some national forests may have the ability to operate in these areas, while other national forests may not have the ability to harvest timber in these areas due to local economics of these systems. However, they could incur additional costs or accept lower rate timber sales to utilize these systems.

- **Likely not operable:** There are areas on steep slopes or far from existing transportation systems where potential forest management would require supplemental funding (for example, high-cost permanent or temporary road construction over 1 mile, skidding or forwarding over 1 mile, and/or complex steep slope harvesting systems including cable, tether, and/or helicopter logging). Complex harvesting systems in these areas would have to be available and local timber markets would have to be stronger for these areas to be managed, or large amounts of funding would have to become available where local timber markets do not exist. Non-commercial treatments would also be high cost due to more difficult access. These are areas that are neither likely operable nor operable but complex (further than 0.5 miles from a road, greater than 70 percent slope, etc.)

In addition, current land management plan components, including areas suitable for timber production, were evaluated to determine if timber harvest is allowed, generally allowed, generally not allowed, or not allowed (Appendix 1 – IRA Acreage Summarized by Region). Plan components that restrict or limit timber harvest may be included in land management plans for a variety of purposes, including, but not limited to, recreation, scenery, watershed, wildlife habitat, and special area designation (such as recommended wilderness area) purposes (see Potentially Affected Environment).

> 37 Idaho is excluded from the Northern and Intermountain Regions, Colorado is excluded from the Rocky Mountain Region, The El Yunque National Forest is excluded from the Southern Region, and the Alaska Region includes both the Chugach and Tongass National Forests.

While the physical conditions (slopes, productivity of the land) do not change, over time, accessibility of these areas and land management plans could change. In the long term, permanent road construction over 0.5 miles from existing roads could occur and could increase forest management opportunities and change operability categories. Similarly, it is possible that land management plan amendments and revisions could change plan components that apply to these areas over time. Some forest plan limitations could be removed if forest plans were revised or amended to allow for increased forest management activities. In other cases, existing plan components that limit certain activities in these areas may remain in place. All plan amendments and revisions would comply with the planning rule requirements relative to timber which include plan components, sustainability of ecosystems, multiple use, areas suitable and not suitable for timber production, sustained yield in areas suitable for timber production which would be within forest or grassland authority, inherent capability, and fiscal capacity of the unit (36 C.F.R. 219.1(g)).

In the short and long term, it would be more likely that local land managers would propose projects with commercial and non-commercial activities in the likely operable areas where timber harvest is consistent with land management plans. It is likely that alternative 2 would result in an increase in treatment area (acres) and volume harvested in these areas. If local harvest capabilities are available and it is economical, it is possible that alternative 2 could result in an increase in treatment area and volume harvested in the operable, but complex areas in both the short and long term. Non-commercial hand treatments would be more feasible in these areas than in the not operable areas, but also at higher costs than the likely operable areas due to steep terrain or more difficult accessibility.

Any forest management activity, including timber harvest, in areas that are likely not operable would present the lowest likelihood compared to the other two operability categories where consistent with land management plans. In the short term, it is not reasonably foreseeable to see large increases in forest management activities in these not likely operable areas due to high costs and complexities unless permanent or temporary road construction, skidding, or forwarding over 1 mile would be feasible within stronger local timber markets. In the long term, there could be a slight level of increased harvest if technology or market conditions improve, or future land management plan changes drove these increases.

To aid in comparison of alternatives, based on the assumptions above, estimates of potential treatment acres and volumes, by operability, were derived from Forest Service data, using models developed. Acreage and sawtimber volume estimates were derived from Forest Service Cut and Sold Reports (2014-2024), Timber Information Manager, Forest Inventory and Analysis data, and Forest Service Activity Tracking System. These estimates are for analysis and comparison purposes only to illustrate the differences between alternatives because it is not possible to fully predict actual areas where harvest would be proposed in projects and annual harvest levels given varying land management plan direction, local decision-making authority and local market conditions.

###### **Potential Timber Harvest Areas**

Timber harvest is reasonably foreseeable within likely operable and likely operable, but complex areas consistent with land management plans. Overall, 4.8 million acres (16 percent of forested areas in the potentially affected IRAs) are likely operable or likely operable but complex in areas where timber harvest is allowed or generally allowed under current land management plans, though there are differences between regions. The Intermountain Region (Region 4) presents the most potential area for timber harvest with 1.5 million acres, followed by the Northern Region (Region 1) at approximately 841,000 acres, and the Pacific Southwest Region (Region 5) at approximately 608,000 acres. Much less area would have potential timber harvest opportunity in the Southwestern Region (Region 3) at approximately 200,000 acres and the Southern Region (Region 8) at approximately 148,000 acres (Figure 10). Some of the variation between regions shown is due to regional variation in land management plan components which differ based on local conditions, the age of the plan, and also the policy under which the plans were developed (see Appendix 1 – IRA Acreage Summarized by Region). In the long term, land management plan amendments and revisions could increase the area where timber harvest would be allowed.

**Figure 10. Alternative 2 land management plan operability – likely operable and likely operable, but complex areas applying current land management plan direction (acres)**

###### **Potential annual harvest levels**

Under alternative 2, it is reasonably foreseeable that an increasing proportion of timber volume sold (sawtimber) would come from within the potentially affected area. The estimated potential volume available for timber harvest by region is based on volume by species sold that exist within areas of potential timber harvest (above) compared to the average annual harvest rates and amounts of those species types on neighboring forest lands. All quantifiable operability data is presented as a range of hundred cubic feet (ccf) volume representing minimum and maximum potential annual harvest levels. Volume is presented in broad ranges and is best interpreted at broad scales due to the limited statistical reliability of Forest Inventory and Analysis data.

Applying current land management plan direction, national annual increases in sawtimber harvest in likely operable areas could be approximately 106,000 to 254,000 ccf per year and 147,000 to 303,000 ccf per year within likely operable but complex areas, a total of 253,000 to 557,000 ccf. If all of this annual harvest occurred, it could represent a potential 5 to 10 percent increase in total sawtimber harvest on National Forest System lands compared to current harvest levels (which are 5.5 million ccf per year nationally). The Intermountain Region followed by the Rocky Mountain Region and the Northern Region have the greatest potential timber volume output while the Southwestern, Southern, and Alaska Regions present the least (see Figure 11). In the long-term land management plan amendments and revisions could increase the area where timber harvest would be allowed and potentially increase the potential timber harvest volumes.

**Figure 11. Alternative 2 potential merchantable sawtimber volume (ccf) opportunity per year with current national forest harvest levels applying land management plan direction**

###### Implications for Forest Vegetation, Health, and Carbon

Under alternative 2, additional active forest management, including some level of road construction or reconstruction, could occur and would be more or less likely in certain areas as described above. Where site-specific vegetation management occurs, it would be designed to move vegetation conditions toward desired conditions and objectives identified in land management plans, consistent with the 2012 planning rule requirements for ecosystem integrity (36 CFR 219.8(a)) or, for older plans, prior planning requirements or ecosystem diversity under the 1982 Planning Rule (36 CFR 219.27(a)(1982)). Changes to vegetation structure, function, age class, and/or composition would be more likely in the operable areas and where land management plans allow timber harvest or road construction. Active forest management effects would generally be short term while road construction and associated infrastructure requiring permanent removal would result in long-term loss of forest canopy, but these levels would be small compared to the overall forested landscape.

More common forest types within inventoried roadless areas include the fir/spruce/hemlock, Douglas-fir, and aspen/birch groups (see Table 13). For common forest types, long-term effects would be mitigated by the relative abundance of the tree species and forest types across the landscape and appropriate reforestation management actions. Less common forest types within the affected environment include many of the eastern hardwood forest types, the longleaf/slash pine group, and the oak/gum/cypress group forest types (see Table 13). Less common forest types within inventoried roadless areas would likely be less affected by road construction or timber harvest because management activities would likely be avoided or they would be designed to improve these forest types.

Within areas that are potentially operable, and where forest plans generally allow for timber harvest, approximately 11-16 percent is old-growth forest, 54-63 percent is mature forest, and 26-31 percent is young forest. Potentially operable areas where land management plan allowances generally allow timber harvest exclude forest types with little to no merchantable sawtimber. However, not all remaining areas have potential for commercial timber harvest. For example, approximately 50 percent of the old-growth forest in potentially operable areas where plan allowances generally allow timber harvest are composed of non-commercial forest types such as aspen/birch, pinyon/juniper, and fir/spruce/mountain hemlock.

Alternative 2 would increase the opportunity to conduct management activities that mitigate insect and disease risks within the potentially affected IRAs, particularly on operable and operable but complex areas. Depending on the region, between 10-40 percent of acres within the operable and operable but complex categories are at risk for insect and disease related mortality (the low end of this range is found in Pacific Southwest Region IRAs that are operable but complex, whereas the upper end of this range is found in Northern Region IRAs, where 40 percent of operable but complex lands are at risk) (Table 16). Forest management activities that include practices that reduce risk to forest insect and disease or reduce impact of abiotic stressors in those local environments could reduce insect or disease caused tree mortality. Changes in risk cannot be estimated nationally and would be determined by local conditions, management activities, and pest conditions on the surrounding landscape.
