Under alternative 2, where future road construction, timber harvest, or non-commercial forest management remove vegetation, there would be a loss of stored carbon. Areas affected by permanent tree removal activities, such as permanent road construction, would lose capacity for carbon sequestration. Given forest management objectives, these areas would be small compared to the overall carbon within the forested landscape within roadless areas. Effects of timber harvest on carbon storage would be proportional to the level of harvest intensity. Affected areas following harvest treatments would retain capacity for carbon sequestration, and by including required reforestation efforts, can be expected to maintain stored carbon levels over the long term. Depending on stand age and the intensity of harvest, areas where timber was harvested may take several decades to return to similar levels of live biomass (Woodall et al. 2023). Carbon storage in wood products could offset some carbon loss through harvest. Active management, as guided by land management plans, could increase productivity and carbon sequestration in the long term through selective logging, thinning to allow for continued growth of large trees, timber salvage and carbon storage in wood products, and reforestation in the areas where forest management activities occur (Bettinger et al. 2016, Wadsworth 1997).

###### Non-Forested Vegetation

Alternative 2 would create potential opportunities for project vegetation treatments that could increase herbaceous and shrub production over alternative 1 because project activities would create canopy openings, altering the composition of the plant communities in the short term. As the canopy cover is removed, these treatments could allow more sunlight to reach the ground and temporarily increase production of grasses and browse for wildlife use. Large areas would be opened up to sunlight, and a variety of plants would generally appear, including forbs, grasses, trees, and shrubs. Depending on the scope, extent, and timing of the future active management, effects could also include long-term impacts such as invasive plant establishment and/or spread, a disruption in water relationships as would occur through diverting of surface or subsurface flow from road construction, or increased erosion potential through ground disturbance, and habitat fragmentation. If new roads are constructed or reconstructed in potentially affected IRAs, and if vegetation management or other project activities occur in those areas, best management practices would be implemented and much of the impact would be prevented through project design and consistency with land management plans.

##### **Effects of Alternative 3 – Modified Rule**

###### Timber and Forest Management

This alternative would, to a lesser extent than alternative 2, allow local land managers to tailor forest management, as appropriate, to local land conditions within roaded areas that are currently inventoried roadless areas and the wildland-urban interface. Boundary adjustments would increase areas where timber could be harvested in areas outside of roaded roadless areas and the wildland-urban interface. Similar to alternative 2, commercial timber harvest is reasonably foreseeable to increase, though the area where this is likely to occur is smaller (see below) and the total potential harvest volumes are less than alternative 2.

Alternative 3 also does not compel or authorize timber harvest – any harvest that occurs would be the result of future site-specific decisions and local market conditions and the specific locations or amounts of activity are not feasible to predict. Increased timber harvest and noncommercial forest management activities are reasonably foreseeable to occur in the alternative 3 analysis area. However, these activities would not be equally reasonably foreseeable across all portions of the alternative 3 potentially affected IRAs. The likelihood of timber harvest occurring in any given area is analyzed based on operability categories combined with current land management plan direction (see Potential Forest Management Opportunity below). Timber harvest would be more likely to occur (more reasonably foreseeable) in the likely operable and likely operable but complex areas where the cost of implementing treatments is lowest through traditional timber sales, where current land management plans allow it, and where natural resource objectives align. Natural resource objectives include, but are not limited to, fuels reduction in moderate and high risk areas and addressing insect and disease epidemics.

Similar to alternative 2, non-commercial treatment and incidental and permitted tree cutting activities is reasonably foreseeable and could occur at increased levels in likely operable areas than likely operable but complex or likely not operable areas compared to the existing condition. The level of this use would be less than the proposed action because roads could not be constructed outside of roaded roadless or wildland-urban interface areas. Non-commercial forest management completed by hand (without mechanized/logging equipment) could be feasible in any operability category but may be limited by land management plans, capacity, and funding. Within the wildland-urban interface and roaded roadless areas, activities could proceed without the 2001 Roadless Rule’s exception process. Outside these areas, the activities would remain subject to the exception process. Incidental and permitted tree cutting activities would continue to occur and may occur at increased levels if public access is increased with opened reconstructed roads or newly opened constructed roads.

###### _Potential Forest Management Opportunity_

Alternative 3 was analyzed using the same considerations as alternative 2 including operability categories and land management plan direction.

###### **Potential Timber Harvest Areas**

Likely operable areas would vary by region in alternative 3. Timber harvest is reasonably foreseeable within likely operable and likely operable but complex areas coupled in areas where it is allowed or generally allowed by land management plans. Overall, about 4.3 million acres (21 percent of the forested portions of the potentially affected IRAs for alternative 3) is within likely operable areas or likely operable but complex areas where timber harvest is allowed or generally allowed by current land management plans, though there are differences between regions. The Intermountain Region presents the most area potentially available for timber harvest with approximately 1.2 million acres, followed by the Northern Region with approximately 700,000 acres and the Alaska Region with approximately 575,000 acres. The Southwestern and Southern Regions would have the least area that could be potentially available.

**Figure 12. Alternative 3 operability – acres of likely operable and likely operable, but complex areas applying land management plan direction**

###### **Potential annual harvest levels within roadless areas**

Under alternative 3, it is reasonably foreseeable that an increased proportion of timber volume sold would come from the potentially affected environment. We estimated the potential volume available for timber harvest based on operable areas and average annual harvest rates and amounts on National Forest System lands. All quantifiable operability data is displayed in acres and hundred cubic feet (ccf) volume. Volume is presented in broad ranges and is best interpreted at broad scales due to the limited statistical reliability of Forest Inventory and Analysis data. Applying land management plan direction to national annual increases in sawtimber harvest in likely operable areas could be approximately 112,000 to 264,000 ccf per year and 106,000 to 237,000 ccf per year within likely operable but complex areas, a total of 219,000 to 501,000 ccf per year. It could represent a potential 4-9 percent annual increase in total sawtimber harvest on National Forest System lands. In the long term, land management plan amendments and revisions could increase the area where timber harvest would be allowed as well as increase potential timber harvest levels.

**Figure 13. Alternative 3 potential merchantable sawtimber volume opportunity (ccf) per year with current national forest harvest levels applying land management plan direction**

###### Implications for Forest Vegetation, Health, and Carbon

Under alternative 3, potential forest management activities would be more likely to be concentrated in areas near existing roads and within wildland-urban interface zones. These areas are generally easier to access. Operable ground within these areas are more likely to experience forest management activities which could include shifts in forest cover types aligned with land management plan desired conditions. Over the short term, this could result in a reduction in canopy cover, improvement of growing conditions of residual stands, and a shift toward younger age classes in stands treated with regeneration harvest. Over the longer term, how forest vegetation could be managed would continue to be dictated by land management plans or changed should land management plan amendment or revisions be undertaken. In contrast to alternative 2, alternative 3 would limit these changes to a smaller subset of the IRA footprint, leaving areas outside the wildland-urban interface or more than 0.5 miles from roads restricted from forest management.

Under alternative 3, carbon dynamics would be similar to alternative 2. Alternative 3 would increase the opportunity to conduct management activities that mitigate insect and disease risks within the potentially affected IRAs, particularly on operable and operable but complex areas. Depending on the region, between 9-43 percent of acres within the operable and operable but complex categories are at risk for insect and disease related mortality (the low end of this range is found in Pacific Southwest Region IRAs that are operable but complex, whereas the upper end of this range is found in Northern Region IRAs, where 43 percent of operable but complex lands are at risk) (Table 17). Forest management activities that include practices that reduce risk to forest insect and disease or reduce impact of abiotic stressors in those local environments could reduce insect or disease caused tree mortality. Changes in risk cannot be estimated nationally and would be determined by local conditions, management activities, and pest conditions on the surrounding landscape.

###### Non-forested Vegetation

Similar to alternative 2, alternative 3 could create opportunities for project vegetation treatments that would increase herbaceous and shrub production although long-term impacts such as invasive plant establishment and/or spread and a disruption in water relationships could also occur through diverting of surface or subsurface flow from road construction, or increased erosion potential through ground disturbance, and habitat fragmentation. The area of operability and the miles of new roads anticipated to be constructed are not expected to be substantially different under alternatives 2 and 3. As a result, these alternatives are not expected to differ significantly in regard to their contributions to effects of non-forested vegetation. If new roads are constructed or reconstructed in roadless areas, and if vegetation management or other project activities occur in those areas, best management practices would be implemented and much of the impact would be prevented through project design and consistency with land management plans.

##### **Conclusion – Vegetation and Management**

Inventoried roadless areas are shaped by natural forces like wildfire, drought, insects, and disease, with limited management under the 2001 Roadless Rule. Under alternative 1, only small amounts of timber harvest would occur, and vegetation trends would continue to be driven mostly by natural disturbances. Alternative 2 would expand active forest management opportunities the most, with about 4.8 million acres (16 percent of forested areas within potentially affected IRAs) where timber harvest could occur on operable areas and is allowed by current land management plans. If all potential annual timber harvest occurred, it could represent a potential 5 to 10 percent increase in total sawtimber harvest on National Forest System lands compared to current harvest levels. This option also provides the greatest ability to promote forest health by reducing insect and disease risks, especially in western conifer forests. Alternative 3 offers a more moderate increase than alternative 2, with about 4.3 million acres available under current plans. Potential harvest levels would be lower, but it would still promote forest health through targeted management in operable areas, though at a smaller scale than alternative 2. Across all alternatives, natural disturbances remain a force shaping vegetation, but increased active management, as proposed in alternatives 2 and 3, could promote increased timber production, forest health, and resilience to insects and disease.

#### Fire and Fuels Management

##### **Background**

In the right place at the right time, wildland fire can create many environmental benefits, such as reducing fuel loads, improving wildlife habitat, and increasing ecological resilience to future disturbances. In the wrong place at the wrong time, wildfires can threaten lives, homes, infrastructure, and important natural and cultural resources. Adaptive management principles involve continual assessment of how proposed management alternatives will affect wildfire hazards and the potential for risk mitigation. This analysis assumes consistency with The Federal Wildland Fire Management Policy, as outlined in the Interagency Standards for Fire and Fire Aviation Operations, and the location-specific land management plan objectives. The overarching goal remains to balance fire suppression with the strategic use of fire, resource management, and national adaptation to fire presence.

Forest Service objectives for fire management include minimizing the risk to people, communities, and natural and cultural resources by assessing the likelihood of occurrence, severity of potential impacts, and the possible advantages of actions to reduce risk. Wildfire risk is a measure of the probability and consequences of uncertain future fire events. The Forest Service uses a risk management framework to make decisions and investments focused on protecting people and the built environment, conserving natural and cultural resources, and restoring and maintaining ecosystem health and services. This system considers risk as a function of wildfire hazard (likelihood and intensity) and the vulnerability (exposure and susceptibility) of resources and assets. Risk mitigation focuses on actions, including fuel treatments and fire response, that reduce the likelihood of negative consequences from wildfire.

Fuel treatments can change the likelihood and intensity (hazard) of wildfire by altering the amount, type, and/or configuration of live and dead vegetation available to burn. Treating hazardous fuels on NFS land, especially within the wildland-urban interface (areas adjacent to communities) and municipal watersheds (sources of public drinking water), is a key priority under several national initiatives, including the National Fire Plan, the Healthy Forests Restoration Act (HFRA), the Healthy Forests Initiative, the National Cohesive Wildland Fire Management Strategy, and Congressional budget directives. Local land management plans set specific management objectives and the allowable management activities for different units of the landscape, including for fuels and fire response.

Hazardous fuels reduction work generally falls into three categories:

- Landscape fuel treatments designed to alter fire behavior and intensity principally to strengthen ecological resilience and protect natural resources,

- Point protection zones of defensible space to reduce potential for loss around firesensitive assets (such as housing or infrastructure), and

- Linear fuel breaks designed to improve the safety, efficiency, and effectiveness of fire firefighting efforts at strategic locations.

The agency uses a variety of treatment types to reduce landscape-specific wildfire risks and support fire control activities, including commercial and non-commercial forest thinning, biomass removal, and prescribed fire (broadcast or pile burning). Prescribed fire is an effective risk mitigation tool, but it requires extensive planning and approval, as well as timely repeated application. Integrated thinning and prescribed burning treatments are often most effective for achieving restoration and hazard-reduction outcomes (Stephens et al. 2009, Hood et al. 2022).

When a wildfire occurs, the Forest Service follows the Federal Wildland Fire Management Policy and similar agency program directives to determine appropriate management response considering applicable land management plan objectives, protection concerns, and firefighting safety, efficiency, and effectiveness. Typically, land management plans emphasize ecological values within IRAs, which are often promoted by fire, and there are few key issues within IRAs beyond drinking water source watersheds. Thus, naturally ignited fires (unplanned ignitions) are sometimes managed for resource objectives, including wildfire risk reduction and the restoration of forests negatively impacted by decades of aggressive fire suppression that built up fuels (North et al. 2021). However, a range of stakeholders are concerned about the prospect of notable, high-severity fires within drinking water source watersheds and large cross-boundary fires that impact adjacent communities. They argue that increased roads and hazardous fuels reduction treatments have the potential to reduce these effects by improving firefighting safety, efficiency, and effectiveness in IRAs, thereby increasing initial attack success and opportunities for large fire containment.

Due to the broad nature of the proposed rule change and uncertainties about the type, magnitude, intensity, and location of foreseeable activities, potential changes in wildfire risk and mitigation opportunities are evaluated relative to one another. These uncertainties include seasonal climate variability, which can affect fire behavior and frequency; funding constraints that may limit the scope and implementation of fire management efforts; and operational challenges, such as access restrictions in remote areas. By acknowledging these factors, planners can better anticipate potential challenges and develop more effective strategies for wildfire risk evaluation.

##### **Potentially Affected Environment**

###### Wildfire Occurrence

Wildfire occurrence refers to an instance of a wildfire event (a specific incident) or a collection of cases. Past research and new analyses presented here indicate that more fires, both in absolute numbers and density, originate on other NFS lands compared to IRAs and wilderness areas (Healey 2020; Johnston et al. 2021, see more detail below). Still, the percentage of burned area is highest in wilderness, followed by other NFS lands and IRAs. In other words, while there are fewer fires per land area (lower densities) in roadless and wilderness areas, the fires that do burn tend to reach larger sizes. The impacts to vegetation in terms of fire severity are broadly similar across the NFS-wide land designations, with only slightly greater proportions of roadless areas burning at moderate and high severity at the national scale.

Roadless and wilderness areas also experience a higher proportion of lightning-caused ignitions than other NFS lands, reflecting both reduced human access and biophysical conditions that support natural ignitions. The proportion of human-caused fires in IRAs and wilderness is less than half that on other NFS lands, which may be due in part to public access limitations. The incidence of human-caused fires generally increases with proximity to roads.

The tables and figures that follow summarize wildfire counts (human vs. natural), area burned, and severity information from recent decades using official agency incident records (InFORM) and satellite observations of burned areas and fire severity. These data are summarized across three land designations - wilderness, IRAs, and other NFS lands - to characterize baseline wildfire hazard in IRAs and consider how it might change if managed more like other Forest Service lands.

The InFORM Fire Occurrence Data Record (FODR)<sup>38</sup> was used to examine characteristics of wildfires that start on NFS lands by land management designation, including ignition density, cause, size, and total area burned, which may include area burned beyond NFS lands. The interagency, satellite-based Monitoring Trends in Burn Severity (MTBS)<sup>39</sup> dataset was used to characterize the total area burned on NFS lands by large wildfires and their severity (as reflected in vegetation effects). Both datasets were used because each has its benefits and drawbacks<sup>40</sup> . Differences in area burned estimates by land designation between these two datasets are to be expected. To ensure comparability across datasets and in accordance with current management conditions, primary comparisons of fire activity across land designations focus on 2014–2024, representing the most recent full decade of Roadless Rule implementation.

> 38 The FODR provides a near‑complete census of wildfire activity on Federal lands, with the most reliable data available from 1992 onward (Short 2014). These summaries do not include fires that start on adjacent ownerships and later burn onto NFS lands. This distinction is important because cross‑boundary fires originating off NFS lands cause a disproportionate share of community destruction (Downing et al. 2022).

> 39 MTBS considers fires large enough to map using satellite imagery if they exceed 1,000 acres in the western US or 500 acres in the East. These summaries include wildfires that have burned NFS lands, regardless of their origin. 40 The FODR includes data for fires of all sizes and provides reported points of origin and fire ignition causes. The most consistent and reliable subsets of the data are considered to date back to 1992 (Short 2014). The MTBS dataset includes mapped perimeters and severity information for large fires from 1984 to 2024 but lacks ignition location and cause information. The FODR data were used to present summaries of fire ignitions (counts, densities, average fire size) by cause, while the MTBS dataset was used to characterize fire severity. Both datasets were used to estimate area burned, but the FODR summaries are based on the land designations where fires (of all sizes) originated. In contrast, the MTBS summaries are based on intersections of large fire perimeters with land designation maps.

The total number of wildfires originating on NFS lands from 2014 to 2024 is lowest within wilderness, followed by potentially affected IRAs, and highest on other NFS lands (Table 18). However, when converted to a density estimate (number of fires per million acres per year) to account for differences in each land base, the IRA estimates are essentially equivalent to those from of wilderness (12 fires per million acres per year) and represent less than one‑third (29 percent) of the ignition density on other NFS lands (42 fires per million acres per year).

Fire occurrence data were also used to examine trends over a longer time period, back to 1992 (FODR) and 1984 (MTBS). Area burned has trended upward over time for all NFS lands and within the potentially affected IRAs, causing a rise in total area burned at moderate and high severity in recent decades (Figure 14). In some dry conifer forests, the increased area burned at high intensity and severity due to decades of fire suppression (exclusion) efforts is a problematic departure from historical conditions (Parks et al. 2023, McClure et al. 2024). Many of these lowelevation, dry pine forests exist near communities in the western U.S. High severity fires in pine forests that were historically burned by frequent low- to moderate- severity fires, may result in conversions to non-forested conditions and increased sedimentation and water quality impacts to municipal watersheds (Hohner et al. 2019, Coop et al. 2020).

These findings are similar to those of other recent studies that quantified fire occurrence within and outside Forest Service Roadless Areas. Healey (2020) found that a similar proportion of area burned on NFS lands, within and outside of IRAs, using MTBS data from 1984-2016. Additionally, Healey (2020) found that fire severity within the forested portion of IRAs was only slightly different than for other NFS lands, with the largest difference being proportionally more area burned at high severity area in IRAs (5 percent within IRAs, 3.2 percent outside of IRAs). Johnston et al. (2021) investigated the influence of roaded and roadless land use designations on fire ignitions, fire extent, and fire severity on NFS lands in the western U.S. In this study, “roadless” included both IRAs and wilderness. Using point-based agency fire occurrence records from 1992-2017 (like the FODR), they found that most fires (74 percent of total) started in roaded areas, but fires in “roadless” settings were more likely to exceed 300 acres (4 percent in roadless versus 1.4 percent in roaded areas). Johnston et al. (2021) also quantified Forest Service area burned by large fires (originating on any ownership) within the same categories, using MTBS data from 1984-2017, and found that a greater proportion of “roadless” area burned (30 percent) compared to the roaded setting (18 percent). Their analysis of burn severity for large fires from 1984-2017 found no significant difference across land designations when accounting for biophysical differences (for example, elevation, terrain, vegetation type, wetness).

As indicated below, ignition density is approximately four times greater on other NFS lands compared to the potentially affected IRAs and wilderness. Most of this difference is due to the greater density of human-caused ignitions within other NFS lands (Table 21). Among fires with causes determined, 77 percent and 74 percent were reported as naturally ignited in wilderness areas and potentially affected IRAs, respectively. In contrast, the majority (57 percent) of fires with cause determinations on other NFS lands were reported as human caused. Other NFS lands typically have more land management activities, infrastructure (such as powerlines), and public recreation that can lead to human caused ignitions. The new analysis presented in this DEIS indicates that other NFS lands also have about twice the density of natural ignitions than IRAs or wilderness, potentially due to biophysical differences or higher detection rates. The number of “actionable” and reportable wildfires may also be generally greater in areas outside of roadless or wilderness areas. Fires within the potentially affected environment are more often caused by natural (lightning) ignitions, and some may extinguish without being detected or requiring a management (suppression) response. Consistent with other research findings, these analyses indicate that human caused ignitions increase in abundance with proximity to roads, supporting the notion that roads facilitate human access, which can in turn result in more human caused fires (Narayanaraj and Wimberly 2012, Parisien et al., 2016, Balch et al., 2017, Chen and Jin, 2022).

###### Wildfire Risk

Wildfire risk is a function of the likelihood of wildfire and the potential consequences to highly valued resources and assets (HVRAs). The Forest Service uses a nationally consistent wildfire risk assessment framework to evaluate risk across landscapes and to inform planning, prioritization, and investments. This framework merges wildfire hazard (likelihood and intensity), exposure (the spatial coincidence of wildfire and HVRAs), and susceptibility (the degree to which HVRAs are harmed or benefit from fire). Together, these components provide a quantitative and qualitative understanding of wildfire risk and opportunities for mitigation.

The potentially affected IRAs span a wide range of vegetation types, climate regimes, and fire regimes. As a result, wildfire risk varies substantially across the IRA network. Some IRAs occur in ecological systems where fire is infrequent but severe when it happens; others occur in fireadapted landscapes where frequent, low- to moderate-severity fire traditionally sustained ecosystem structure and function. Understanding this variation is fundamental to examining how the proposed alternatives may influence wildfire risk.

Nationally, wildfire hazards are commonly characterized using the Wildfire Hazard Potential (WHP) index, which integrates burn probability and potential fire intensity. WHP provides a relative measure of wildfire hazard across the United States. Areas with high or very high WHP are disproportionately located in the western United States, particularly in fire-adapted conifer forests, where decades of fire exclusion have increased the likelihood of high-severity fire.

Exposure of HVRAs within IRAs varies by region and by the type of resource or asset considered. Many IRAs overlap with watersheds that supply drinking water to downstream communities. Others contain habitats for threatened, endangered, or sensitive species that may be vulnerable to high-severity fire. In some cases, fire can provide ecological benefits by reducing fuel loads, promoting regeneration, or maintaining habitat structure. In other instances, highseverity fire can result in long-term loss of forest cover, increased sedimentation, and degradation of aquatic habitat.

Susceptibility reflects the degree to which HVRAs are harmed or benefit from wildfire. For example, municipal watersheds are generally highly susceptible to negative impacts from highseverity fire, including increased erosion and sedimentation. In contrast, some fire-adapted ecosystems benefit from low- to moderate-severity fire, which can reduce fuel loads and promote ecological resilience. The susceptibility of HVRAs within IRAs, therefore, depends on both the environmental context and the potential fire behavior.

The Forest Service’s national risk assessment identifies priority landscapes for hazardous fuels reduction and wildfire risk mitigation. These areas represent opportunities to reduce wildfire risk through strategically placed fuel treatments and improved fire response. However, the extent to which such treatments can be implemented within IRAs depends on management direction, access, and operational feasibility. Key operational barriers to these implementations include restricted access due to road infrastructure limitations, which can impede the timely deployment of firefighting resources and equipment. The high cost of conducting treatments in remote or difficult-to-access areas further limits feasibility. Additionally, environmental reviews and regulatory compliance can delay or restrict treatment activities. These constraints highlight the need for realistic planning that accounts for these operational challenges while optimizing risk mitigation strategies.

To evaluate wildfire risk within the potentially affected IRAs, this analysis summarizes Wildfire Hazard Potential, HVRA exposure, and susceptibility across the IRA network (Table 22 and more details provided in the sections that follow). Across potentially affected IRAs outside of Alaska, 42 percent of lands are classified as high or very high Wildfire Hazard Potential, indicating a high potential for intense wildfire under severe weather conditions. Approximately 26 percent overlap with high-priority firesheds identified in the Wildfire Crisis Strategy (WCS).

These firesheds are areas where wildfire poses the most significant risk to communities, infrastructure, and natural resources. Within these firesheds, opportunities for risk mitigation may be substantial. In addition, approximately 15 percent of the potentially affected IRAs overlap with Community Wildfire Risk Reduction Zones (CWRRZ), which represent areas where wildfire poses elevated risk to communities. These overlaps highlight the importance of considering how IRA management may influence wildfire risk to adjacent neighborhoods.

The susceptibility of HVRAs within IRAs varies by region and by resource type. For example, IRAs in the Pacific Northwest and Northern Rockies often contain habitat for species sensitive to high-severity fire, such as the northern spotted owl and bull trout. In contrast, IRAs in the Southwest and southern Rockies often occur in fire-adapted ponderosa pine and mixed-conifer forests where low- to moderate-severity fire can provide ecological benefits.

Overall, wildfire risk within the potentially affected IRAs is influenced by wildfire hazards, HVRA exposure, and susceptibility. The proposed alternatives may influence wildfire risk by affecting the ability to implement fuel treatments, manage wildfire for resource objectives, and respond to unplanned ignitions. Evaluating these potential changes requires understanding the baseline risk conditions and the opportunities for risk mitigation within IRAs.

###### _Wildfire Hazard Potential_

The Forest Service Wildfire Hazard Potential (WHP) map products<sup>41</sup> provide nationally consistent information about the likelihood that vegetation will burn under high intensity

> 41 <u>https://research.fs.usda.gov/firelab/products/dataandtools/wildfire-hazard-potential</u>

conditions that are difficult to control (Dillon et al. 2014; Dillon 2023). Across potentially affected IRAs outside of Alaska, 42 percent of lands are classified as high or very high Wildfire Hazard Potential, indicating a high potential for intense wildfire under severe weather conditions. Regional values range from 5 percent in the Eastern Region to 60 percent in the Pacific Southwest. WHP is a measure of hazard, not full risk, because it does not incorporate the presence of communities, infrastructure, or other highly valued resources and assets. High and very high WHP ratings generally correspond to conditions where suppression operations are more challenging due to expected flame lengths, fireline intensity, and resistance to control. In IRAs, these challenges may be compounded by limited road access, steep terrain, and a greater reliance on indirect tactics or aerial resources. Landscapes with both elevated WHP and restricted access can therefore present higher suppression difficulty, influencing potential fire growth and the likelihood of impacts to nearby communities or infrastructure where those values occur (for example, Thompson et al. 2016; Calkin et al. 2015). Because WHP alone does not characterize exposure to people or critical resources, this analysis incorporates additional national datasets that delineate Community Wildfire Risk Reduction Zones (CWRRZs) and high‑priority firesheds, which are the primary indicators used by the Forest Service to understand and prioritize wildfire risk reduction (USDA FS 2022a; USDA FS 2022b).

###### _Community Wildfire Risk Reduction Zone_

A CWRRZ is a spatially defined area where mitigation activities are focused on reducing wildfire exposure to communities. These zones integrate wildfire likelihood with the location of populated areas to identify where ignitions are most likely to affect people and structures (USDA Forest Service 2022a). CWRRZs map where buildings exist and apply a 1.5‑mile buffer around building clusters, producing a more spatially refined representation of communities and adjacent wildlands that most strongly influence their wildfire risk. CWRRZs are categorized into minimal exposure, indirect exposure, direct exposure, and the transmission zone. Within the potentially affected environment 5.9 million acres (14.8 percent) overlap CWRRZs (Table 23). Nearly all CWRRZ within the potentially affected environment occurs within the transition zone (within the 1.5‑mile buffer surrounding building clusters).

###### _Priority Firesheds_

Firesheds represent areas where wildfire ignitions are likely to spread to communities and expose buildings or other highly valued resources and assets (USDA Forest Service 2022b). The Forest Service’s Fireshed Registry tracks past, current, and planned investments in forest and fuel management in relation to historical and predicted fire activity. Firesheds identified as high risk for building and drinking‑water‑source exposure are prioritized for hazardous fuels reduction (USDA Forest Service 2022b). Across the conterminous United States, over 7 million acres (26 percent) of potentially affected IRAs intersect with current high‑priority firesheds (Table 22). Alaska currently has no mapped high‑priority firesheds on NFS lands. This evidence demonstrates that, although less than one‑quarter of IRA acreage is closely associated with community protection priorities, there are specific landscapes where IRAs may contribute to elevated wildfire risk for nearby communities.

###### _Wildland-Urban Interface_

The Healthy Forests Restoration Act (HFRA) definition of the Wildland–Urban Interface (WUI), includes: at‑risk community boundaries; a 1.5‑mile buffer around at‑risk communities unless modified by a Community Wildfire Protection Plan (CWPP); and areas surrounding evacuation routes for at‑risk communities (see Alternative 3 – Modified Roadless Rule/IRA Boundaries). The HFRA WUI is used by the Forest Service to identify areas where hazardous fuels reduction may provide the greatest benefit in reducing wildfire exposure to people and structures (USDA Forest Service 2022). When combined with other national risk‑based datasets—such as Community Wildfire Risk Reduction Zones (CWRRZs) and high‑priority firesheds—the HFRA WUI helps clarify where IRAs may intersect with broader community protection priorities. Within potentially affected IRAs 9.8 million acres (24 percent) overlap HFRA WUI.

###### Wildfire Risk Mitigation Opportunities

Wildfire risk mitigation refers to activities designed to reduce the likelihood of wildfire occurrence and lessen the potential for losses to highly valued resources and assets. Aspects of risk mitigation include reducing hazardous fuels on the landscape—through mechanical treatments and prescribed burning—and strategically investing in control features, such as roads or other linear fuel breaks, to help manage fires when they do occur and to support prescribed fire activities.

###### _Hazardous Fuel Reduction_

The 2001 Roadless Rule EIS stated that “the majority of fuel management work is expected to occur outside inventoried roadless areas” (USDA FS 2000). However, recent assessments, including the wildfire risk analyses presented here, indicate that substantial acreage within potentially affected IRAs has a relatively high likelihood of burning under high‑intensity conditions that are difficult to manage or that could expose communities, infrastructure, or drinking‑water sources. These areas may therefore represent priority opportunities for hazardous fuel reduction. Despite concerns about recent increases in large‑fire frequency, size, and impacts to communities, many forested landscapes across the western United States remain in a deficit of fire relative to pre‑settlement conditions (Parks et al. 2015, Parks et al. 2025).

The 2001 Roadless Rule does not prohibit and has not entirely prevented hazardous fuels reduction in IRAs. For example, there are no prohibitions on the use of prescribed fire.  The 2001 Roadless Rule also provides exceptions for activities, including cutting and removing small diameter timber, to reduce wildfire risk. Table 23 describes the general opportunities across NFS land designations. Forest Service management activity reporting (Forest Activity Tracking System, or FACTS database) indicates that hazardous fuels reduction has been completed on about 5 percent of the potentially affected IRA area over the most recent decade (fiscal years 2014-2024).

###### **Managing Unplanned Wildfire and Prescribed Burning**

Of the reported accomplishments within the IRA, 55 percent involve managing unplanned ignitions to achieve land management plan objectives. When a wildfire is allowed to burn for resource benefit, the acres burned are recorded as a treatment accomplishment if post-fire assessments indicate the fire effects are consistent with the desired land management plan objectives identified for that location. The next most common activities are prescribed burning treatments (25 percent), including broadcast burning, jackpot burning, and under burning for fuel reduction or resource management. The frequent use of unplanned wildfires in activity reporting highlights its key role in ecosystem restoration and wildfire risk reduction.

These patterns highlight a fundamental trade‑off in wildfire management. Immediate suppression can reduce short‑term risks to communities, infrastructure, and other highly valued resources and assets. However, aggressive suppression across many decades has contributed to fuel accumulation and increased the likelihood that future fires, particularly those that escape initial attack, will burn under more extreme conditions and be more difficult to control – a dynamic often referred to as the wildfire paradox (Arno and Brown 1991; Calkin et al. 2015; Kreider et al. 2024). Allowing some naturally ignited fires (unplanned ignitions) to burn under conditions that align with land management objectives can help reduce fuels, restore ecological processes, and moderate long‑term risk, but doing so requires confidence that fire behavior will remain within acceptable limits.

The difficulty is in determining when and where unplanned ignitions can safely provide ecological and risk‑reduction benefits without exceeding control or threatening critical values. Fires in remote, unroaded areas can present opportunities to achieve resource objectives, particularly when spatial fire management planning, such as the use of Potential Operational Delineations (PODs) or similar tools, has identified likely fire pathways, potential control features, and areas where fire can be managed with lower risk (O’Connor and Calkin 2019; Thompson et al. 2022). These planning frameworks help clarify the operational environment and support decision‑making that balances the immediate benefits of suppression with the long‑term benefits of restoring fire as an ecological process.

###### **Mechanical Treatments**

Mechanical treatments within potentially affected IRAs have been more limited, focused on mechanical rearrangement (11 percent of reported activities) or mechanical removal (6 percent). Mechanical rearrangement includes movement or breaking down of woody fuels to reduce fuel bed depth or to speed up decomposition (such as piling, chipping, compacting). Mechanical removal occurs through a variety of means, including yarding and removal of woody fuels, burning of piled material, or even targeted grazing. Responsible Officials must grant exceptions for some of these activities (such as activities involving timber cutting), but others (targeted grazing, burning of piled material for example) are not prohibited (see 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule Implementation).

The analysis presented here suggests that hazardous fuels reduction treatment occurred on about 5 percent of the potentially affected inventoried roadless areas over the most recent decade. Previous research using similar agency data concluded that the 2001 Roadless Rule did not meaningfully constrain hazardous fuel treatment activities when considering the number and area of treatments as a share of total forested land (Healey 2020). But the limited total area treated within IRAs using mechanical methods is aligned with the general sentiment in planning studies that roadless designation is a barrier to mechanical treatment (North et al. 2015; Ager et al. 2021, Woolsey et al. 2024). There are also likely other inherent limitations for mechanical activities related to some combination of operability, lack of access for equipment, cost constraints, or consideration of public sentiment related to these activities in IRAs (for example, to avoid potential litigation).

###### _Fire Control_

Roads have potential to influence fire control opportunities through several mechanisms, featuring reduced decreased ground transportation response times to recently discovered ignitions, the types of resources that may be deployed, enhanced firefighter safety, and potential use as fire breaks during initial or extended attack (Thompson et al. 2021). Data from the FODR for fires occurring between 2014-2024 indicate that initial attack success rates<sup>42</sup> are high (more than 90 percent) for all land designations. Still, initial attack success rates were highest within the other NFS land class versus IRAs and wilderness (Table 24). There is a trend of decreasing initial attack success from other NFS lands to inventoried roadless areas to wilderness. Mean

> 42 Initial attack was considered successful if the final fire size was less than 300 acres, and the success rate was calculated from the population of fires with sizes attributed, which is most but not all wildfires.

time to containment of escaped fires (greater than or equal to 300 acres) increases considerably from other NFS to roadless to wilderness. However, this analysis does not account for fire management objectives and strategies (opportunities that may include less-than-full-suppression tactics). Management strategies other than complete suppression are more likely to be employed in IRAs and wilderness areas because land management plans emphasize ecological objectives and because critical resources and assets that can be harmed by fires are relatively limited (Daniels et al. 2024).

|---|---|---|---|
|Initial Attack Success Rate|97.2%|94.4%|92.3%|

Ground transport is the most widely used and cost-effective way for firefighters to access fires. The fire suppression effort needed to achieve containment typically scales with fire size and perimeter length (Fried and Fried 1996), which incentivizes rapid initial response to fires that threaten critical resources and assets. Protection priorities and lengthy travel times to reach fires in IRAs could be one factor influencing the lower observed initial attack success rate as compared to other NFS lands (Table 24). Fires that occur far from roads usually require firefighters to either hike long distances, which increases response time and firefighter fatigue, or to use air transport for access, which can be costly and introduces different safety risks (Belval et al. 2025). One study on fireline construction productivity found that hand crews spent 18 percent of their time driving and hiking (Broyles 2011). It is reasonable to assume that the proportion of time spent on travel versus suppression activities is higher in IRAs than other NFS lands, thereby decreasing the overall efficiency of fire control, whether in an initial or extended attack.

The presence of roads and their maintenance levels influence the types of firefighting resources that can be feasibly deployed to suppress a fire. Fires in locations far from roads generally limit the response options to hand crews or specialized aviation resources like helitack and smokejumper crews, and their efficiency and effectiveness may be limited by what materials and equipment they can bring with them or be provisioned with (such as water, fuel, pumps, food, and camping gear). Roads facilitate use of additional firefighting resources such as wildland fire engines, water tenders, bulldozers, material and equipment transport vehicles, and forestry equipment (such as fellers, skidders, masticators, and chippers). The road design and maintenance requirements for these resources vary, but they are best supported by roads with smooth surfaces, low grades, and wide corners, which generally correspond to permanent Forest Service roads with maintenance levels 3-5. Temporary roads and roads with lower maintenance levels may sometimes accommodate these firefighting resources depending on site specific factors and the ability to make emergency improvements.

Roads contribute to several aspects of firefighter safety described in the Lookouts, Communications, Escape Routes, and Safety Zones (LCES) framework, the 10 Standard Firefighting Orders, and the 18 Watch‑Out Situations (Gleason 1991; NWCG 2025a). Roadbeds are typically devoid of fuels, and roadside vegetation is often managed to reduce hazards and improve sightlines. These conditions can enhance visibility for observing fire behavior, locating personnel, and communicating through visual signals. Roads can also serve as pre‑existing escape routes, reducing the need to construct new ones and allowing firefighters to focus on other operational tasks.

Although roads can provide opportunities for containment, incident management teams do not automatically select every available road for suppression operations. Roads may be avoided when they are poorly aligned with expected fire spread, located in hazardous terrain, or positioned in areas where fire behavior is likely to overwhelm containment efforts. Some roads lack safe access or egress, have limited turnouts, or require extensive preparation before they can function as control features. In other cases, using a road may expose firefighters to unacceptable risks due to steep slopes, narrow canyons, or limited visibility. Strategic decisions also consider resource availability, operational priorities, and the potential for more effective containment opportunities elsewhere. As a result, only a subset of roads interacting with a wildfire are actively used in suppression, and containment outcomes reflect both the physical characteristics of the road and the tactical choices made by fire managers.

Roads are most frequently used as containment features when indirect tactics are safer, more efficient, or more effective than direct tactics, although few studies quantify their occurrence rate. One study in the Southwest found that 27.5 percent of large fire perimeters in recent decades aligned with roads (Yocom et al. 2019). This analysis was extended and updated for NFS lands (excluding Colorado and Idaho) using the most recent complete decade of large fire extents from Monitoring Trends in Burn Severity (2014–2023) and the current National Forest System Roads (as described in National Forest System Roads)<sup>43</sup> . Nationally, 23.5 percent of NFS road length that interacted with wildfires was associated with successful containment. Success rates varied by operational maintenance level: the highest rates occurred on level 3–5 roads (40.9–51 percent), moderate rates on level 2 roads (20.8 percent), and the lowest rates on level 0–1 roads (12.7–13.4 percent). Regional patterns also differed, with the highest containment success in Regions 8 and 9 (59.3–66.6 percent) and lower success rates across the Western Regions (14.6–39.7 percent).

Although there is limited research specifically evaluating the use of level 3–5 Forest Service roads for wildfire containment, several studies highlight the operational value of higher‑standard roads in suppression efforts. Higher‑maintenance roads generally provide safer access, more reliable egress, and better opportunities for anchoring indirect line construction or burnout operations (Thompson et al. 2021; Campbell et al. 2024). These roads are more likely to meet width, surface, and visibility requirements that support safe firefighter movement and equipment deployment. They also tend to be better aligned with terrain features that facilitate containment, such as ridgelines or flatter ground. They are more likely to be part of connected networks that offer multiple egress routes, an important consideration under the LCES framework. Although empirical studies quantifying containment success by road class are sparse, operational guidance and case‑study analyses consistently emphasize that higher‑standard roads are among the most reliable and frequently used containment features when indirect tactics are selected. The elevated containment success rates observed for level 3–5 roads in this analysis are consistent with these operational expectations.

##### **Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action**

The occurrence and severity of wildfires would likely remain similar to recent decades, with variation expected related to localized changes in environmental conditions and population levels. The area burned by wildfire is likely to remain relatively high in IRAs and wilderness, which could be beneficial in certain areas of high ecological resilience. While some hazardous fuels activities could continue, alternative 1 would allow for the least discretion for local land managers to modify dangerous fuels. Activities such as prescribed burning, or those not requiring mechanical thinning and road construction would likely continue at similar to recent rates (see Hazardous Fuel Reduction). Activities requiring tree cutting would require exceptions under the 2001 Roadless Rule exceptions, which allow for activities to reduce the risk of uncharacteristic wildfire effects (36 CFR 284.13(b)). The use of these exceptions would continue to be infrequent. The pace and scale of hazardous fuel treatments would likely be limited to that of recent decades, due to ongoing challenges to implementation, including litigation in some cases. Road construction or reconstruction would also continue to be an exception only when “a road is needed to protect public health and safety in cases of an imminent threat of flood, fire, or other catastrophic event that, without intervention, would cause the loss of life or property” (36 CFR 293.12(b)).

Overall, the prohibition on road construction would likely limit opportunities for mechanical thinning and fire control. Reliance on prescribed fire would continue, but its use is constrained by access, smoke, and public tolerance. Long‑term effectiveness of fuel treatments requires maintenance; single entries lose effectiveness over decades. The 2001 Roadless Rule prohibitions and the existing condition of IRAs limit both access and the ability to sustain treatment cycles.

There would be no changes to how the Forest Service can respond to fires within IRAs. Incidentspecific risk analyses that consider land management objectives, protection concerns, and firefighting safety, efficiency, and effectiveness will continue to inform response strategies. Challenges related to initial attack and large-fire containment in unroaded areas would persist. In portions with limited existing road access, initial attack success rates would continue to be slightly lower than on other NFS lands. Without roads to support large-scale fire containment, firefighters would continue to be challenged to identify safe, effective, and efficient places to contain fires before they spread to more accessible parts of the landscape, continuing the trend of larger and longer-duration fires in IRA.

##### **Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action**

Under alternative 2, additional active forest management, including some level of road construction, would likely occur with the extent varying by location (see National Forest Transportation System and Vegetation and Forest Management sections). With increased road construction, especially open-access roads, and other human activity, there could be increased hazard from human-caused ignitions. However, alternative 2 would provide increased opportunities for wildfire risk mitigation, especially within higher risk firesheds.

###### Wildfire Occurrence and Risk

Factors such as road type, pattern, and placement on the landscape may affect fire occurrence and the opportunities to mitigate wildfire risks with hazardous fuels reduction and fire response. As discussed in Wildfire Occurrence, past fire occurrence information on NFS lands, inventoried roadless areas, and wilderness can be used to evaluate the potential effects of new road construction. Road density is linked to human-caused wildfires, and as the density of roads increases so does the probability, number, and frequency of wildfire ignitions (Cardille et al. 2001). However, not all roads constructed under alternative 2 would be open for public access. Therefore, increased human ignitions do not necessarily follow from increased road construction. While human ignitions are a typical driver of wildfire occurrence, they can be targeted in wildfire prevention programs, including public education efforts and road-access restrictions (Devenport & Edgeley, 2025).

###### Wildfire Risk Mitigation Opportunities

Alternative 2 would provide the most local management flexibility for wildfire risk mitigation. This flexibility is essential for identifying, prioritizing, implementing, and maintaining treatments that address key drivers of wildfire risk, as well as for adapting strategies to changing conditions. Implementation of the National Active Forest Management Strategy goals would likely expand the area of timber harvest and hazardous fuels reduction treatments in potentially affected IRAs and shift some proportion of active management toward landscapes with the greatest potential to reduce wildfire risk to communities, infrastructure, and drinking‑water‑source watersheds.

###### _Hazardous Fuels Reduction_

Alternative 2 could present opportunities to implement activities that reduce wildfire risk in the potentially affected IRAs, compared to alternative 1. Expanded operational access via roadbuilding in high-priority areas may be used for increased active fuels management. Additional activities, including prescribed burning and wildfires with hazardous fuel reduction benefits, could also be influenced. Broadening the land base available for mechanical treatment allows for flexibility to develop treatment plans that optimize across the multiple dimensions of effective landscape-scale fuel treatment design and restore fire as a key ecosystem process (Woolsey et. al. 2024).

Over 7 million acres (nearly 26 percent) of all inventoried roadless areas outside of Alaska intersect with high priority firesheds (there are no high priority firesheds in Alaska). Our assessment of the high priority fireshed area within IRAs was further refined based on timber operability, which approximates the land available to combined timber and fuels projects. Approximately 3.6 million acres (52 percent) of the high priority firesheds in the potentially affected IRAs are in the likely operable or operable-but-complex condition, meaning that mechanical fuels reduction activity could be feasible. About 16 percent fall within the likely operable category, likely providing the greatest opportunities for integration between hazardous fuels reduction and economical timber harvest (see Table 28), as consistent with land management plans. Local project-specific design would determine which part of these areas would be strategic for treatment.

The Pacific Southwest and Pacific Northwest Regions contain the greatest extent of high‑priority fireshed overlap with IRAs, followed by the Northern Rockies, Intermountain, and Southern Regions (Table 28).

###### _Fire Control_

Alternative 2 would allow both temporary and permanent road construction where not otherwise restricted by land management plans. However, the prevalence of temporary, spur road construction within the operable areas around existing roads is likely, assuming that timber projects are the primary motivation and funding source for new roads (see National Forest System Roads section). New permanent road construction, strategically planned to support fuels and fire objectives, is less likely to be widespread within potentially affected IRAs, given the costs of road construction costs, maintenance funding limitations, constraints on placement of roads, and current land management plan restrictions. In high-risk landscapes, protection concerns could be great enough to motivate new permanent roads for fuels and fire suppression purposes. In such cases, they would likely be strategically placed along the edges or through the core of IRA to mitigate wildfire transmission to adjacent communities or to suppress fires at smaller sizes to reduce impacts to resources and assets within IRAs.

**Figure 16. Hypothetical depiction of potential road scenarios under different primary objectives of commercial timber harvest and wildfire risk reduction. Timber-centric investments are likely to result in additional temporary, spur roads within the operable areas around existing roads. Roads constructed for strategic wildfire risk reduction may be prioritized differently within operable areas based on their potential to provide effective fire control opportunities near the WUI (Fuels and Fire Edge) or hazardous fuel reduction opportunities further within the core of IRAs (Fuels and Fire Core).**

With increased road access, initial attack success rates would be expected to increase. Additional roads would provide safer, more effective, and more efficient containment opportunities, potentially reducing fire sizes and durations. The magnitude of these beneficial effects would depend on site‑specific decisions regarding the amount and layout of the expanded road network, its strategic placement for fire control, and its maintenance level. Benefits would increase if road access were paired with hazardous fuel treatments.

##### **Effects of Alternative 3 – Modified Rule**

The effects of alternative 3 would be similar to those of alternative 2 - the types of wildfire risk reduction opportunities (hazardous fuels reduction and fire response) are not expected to differ meaningfully between alternatives 2 and 3 in the areas where prohibitions are removed. Alternative 3 would provide less flexibility than alternative 2 to target wildfire risk in large patches retained as IRAs. Hazardous fuel reduction treatments would continue to be similar to alternative 1 for areas that remain subject to the rule prohibitions.

Under alternative 3 opportunities for mechanical hazardous fuels reduction and fire response would be concentrated in the WUI and roaded roadless, nearer to the communities they are intended to protect. Alternative 3 has a similar proportion of area mapped as high or very high WHP at 42.7 percent, excluding Alaska (Table 29). Compared to the potentially affected IRAs, alternative 3 has a greater proportion of potentially affected area overlapping high priority firesheds, CWRRZs, and HFRA WUI (Table 29) – especially when excluding Alaska.
