Alternative 3 has the same concern about new roads increasing ignitions, and these ignitions would be closer to communities, where they could potentially cause more damage. However, the net impact of new roads on fire occurrence is likely small or insignificant, given the potential for improved response times and increased initial attack success. Alternative 3 would provide less flexibility than alternative 2 to target wildfire risk in remaining IRAs. The increased fire control opportunities under alternative 3 would be concentrated closer to community protection areas than under alternative 2.

##### **Conclusion**

Currently the number, frequency, and density of wildfire ignitions are lower in designated wilderness and potentially affected IRAs compared to other NFS lands. Under alternatives 2 and 3, if there is an increase in public road access, the number and frequency of wildfires could rise. However, public access to roads constructed or improved under alternatives 2 and 3 can be limited to minimize fire hazard in high-risk areas.

Under alternative 1, hazardous fuels treatments can occur within inventoried roadless areas if they do not include road construction or tree cutting, or if exceptions are granted by the Responsible Official. Agency management records show that a lack of roads has not prevented fire prevention or protection measures, especially wildland fire use and prescribed fire. However, road restrictions limit opportunities for mechanical thinning and fire control.

Alternatives 2 and 3 would provide additional opportunities for hazardous fuel treatments in priority firesheds and other high-risk areas, when compared with alternative 1. Substantial acreage within potentially affected IRAs has a relatively high likelihood of burning under high‑intensity conditions that are difficult to manage or that could expose communities, infrastructure, or drinking‑water sources. Between 15-29% of the alternative 2 affected environment has at least one indicator of wildfire risk that would elevate it as a priority for mitigation. These areas may therefore represent priority opportunities for hazardous fuel reduction. The alternative 3 affected environment is similar to alternative 2 in terms of wildfire hazard potential, but has proportionately more high priority fireshed, community wildfire risk reduction zone, and WUI overlap. Under alternatives 2 and 3, certain strategically placed roads may confer fire control benefits not currently attainable under the no-action alternative. Opportunities under alternative 3 would be more concentrated in the WUI and roaded roadless, nearer to the communities they are intended to protect.

The Forest Service would use tools already in existence to help prioritize where site-specific treatments occur and to make hazardous fuels program investments with emphasis on existing Forest priorities, integration with the timber/vegetation management program, and an outcomebased assessment framework.

#### Non-Native Invasive Plant Species

##### **Introduction**

Executive Order 13751 defines an “invasive species” as a non-native organism whose introduction causes or is likely to cause economic or environmental harm or harm to human health, animal, or plant health. This executive order directs all Federal agencies to address invasive species concerns and refrain from actions likely to increase invasive species problems. Invasive plants can negatively affect habitat by competing with native plants for resources such as water and light, establishing and changing the community composition, eliminating or reducing native plants, or changing the vegetation structure. The changes in community composition or vegetation structure can reduce native plant populations as well as negatively affect habitat for wildlife and fish.

Policy and guidance for managing invasive plants are provided by the Forest Service Manuals and Handbooks and Forest Service policy, including the Invasive Species Management Policy (Forest Service Manual [FSM] 2900) and the National Strategic Framework for Invasive Species Management (USDA Forest Service 2013), and individual land management plans and invasive plant management plans. FSM 2900 and Forest-wide standards and guidelines include direction to review proposed projects to determine the risk of introduction or spread of invasive plants and implement appropriate mitigation measures. They also include direction to control existing invasions and rehabilitate habitats impacted by invasive species.

##### **Potentially Affected Environment**

Invasive plants are ranked and identified as being invasive or noxious in the state in which they occur. Invasive plants can spread rapidly and reproduce in high numbers, which enables them to effectively crowd out native plant populations. Some can pose serious threats to the composition, structure, and function of native plant communities and ecosystems. Ground-disturbing activities from machinery can transport seeds and reproductive plant parts of invasive plants and can create openings for existing or new populations to expand. Large, severe fires can also create openings and invasive plants may have competitive advantages over native plants that allow them to spread into recently disturbed sites. Forest Service policy and guidance provides direction to address invasive plant species establishment and spread by implementing land management plan standards and guidelines as well as measures in project planning and design such as equipment cleaning, herbicide treatments, and monitoring. Within the potentially affected IRAs, multiple invasive plant species were mapped<sup>44</sup> and cover approximately 309,000 acres (270,787 for alternative 3), less than 1 percent of the potentially affected environment area. It is likely that there are populations that have been undetected and have not been mapped. It should also be

> 44 The Natural Resource Manager within the Forest Service Threatened, Endangered, and Sensitive Plant–Invasive Species (TESP-IS) database was used to derive GIS polygon layers used in the analysis. Only existing mapped invasive plant occurrences within the inventoried roadless area were analyzed and obtained from the TESP-IS. These polygons represent mapped estimates of invasive plant infestations.

noted that of the 438 invasive plant species recorded in the affected environment area, their status and conservation rankings differ from state to state because each jurisdiction uses its own management priorities and evaluation methods.

##### **Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action**

Implementation of the no-action alternative would result in no direct increase of existing and new invasive plants in inventoried roadless areas in the short term because no new activities would occur. However, existing vectors and corridors for invasive plant spread, such as livestock, wildlife, motorized vehicles, roads, and trails would continue to contribute to a gradual steady encroachment of newly established invasive plant populations over the long term, particularly if resources are not available or there is lack of access to conduct ongoing detection and treatment of new populations. Where populations of invasives currently exist, population expansion by way of existing vectors and corridors would likely continue at current rates, depending on species, site characteristics, and other variables. The costs of treating existing invasive plants could also slightly increase due to potential population expansion. Under the noaction alternative, there would be fewer opportunities for active management in inventoried areas where fuels treatments and other management is needed. Wildfires and other disturbances create conditions that are conducive to invasive plant establishment, such as large areas of burned ground. Other factors that contribute to the introduction and establishment of invasive plants include transport of seeds and reproductive plant fragments on vehicles traveling on existing open roads, spread of existing roadside invasive plants, and potential wildfires that are likely to continue under this alternative.

##### **Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action**

In addition to the ongoing impacts of invasives (as described in alternative 1), under alternative 2, road construction and timber harvest activities, and other development, are likely to increase in some areas. Where these activities occur, they have the potential to introduce and spread invasive plant species due to ground disturbance. Effects from invasive plants may be minimal where the intensity of ground disturbance is low or where there are no existing invasive plant infestations. In the short term, ground-disturbing activities within the affected environment area could potentially lead to the introduction of invasive species where these species do not currently exist, especially where these activities are more likely to occur (see above). In the long term, increased disturbance could increase the spread risk of existing invasive plant populations. In addition, Forest Service policy and land management plan direction for invasive plants, best management practices, and future project design features reduce the likelihood of increasing or spreading invasive plant infestations (such as by treatment or removal). Alongside these measures, there would also likely be an increased cost associated with detecting and treating new invasive plant populations.

##### **Effects of Alternative 3 – Modified Rule**

Alternative 3 has fewer acres in the potentially affected area than alternative 2 and fewer acres of invasive plants (270,787 acres) within the affected area, though still less than 1 percent of the area is affected. The potential for the introduction and spread of invasive plant species due to ground disturbance associated with timber harvest, road construction, and other development or management activities would be similar to alternative 2. It is assumed that the majority of proposed projects in the affected area would be forest management activities. The areas of likely operability for these activities are not expected to be significantly different under alternatives 2 and 3. As a result, the alternatives are not expected to differ significantly in regard to their contributions to the introduction and spread of invasive species.

##### **Conclusion**

The no-action alternative would result in no direct increase in existing and new invasive plant species in the affected area because no project actions would occur. Conversely, alternatives 2 and 3 may provide opportunities for activities that could potentially increase the extent or the number of invasive plant species in the affected environment area. Invasive plant establishment or spread would most likely be in areas that would create ground disturbance. However, alternative 2 and 3 would also provide opportunities to reduce fuel loads in the affected environment area, therefore reducing the potential for wildfire that could create large openings for the establishment of invasive plant species. With compliance of applicable laws and implementation of the forest plan’s standard and guidelines for invasives species in addition to future project design features and management requirements to mitigate invasives species introduction and spread, the effects from activities resulting in the implementation of alternatives 2 and 3 are not expected to greatly increase.

#### Soil, Water, and Air Resources

Soil productivity, erosion, water quality, water yield and timing, and air quality can vary widely, even in undisturbed situations. Road construction, timber harvest, prescribed burning, and similar activities, can affect these values, and their variability. Sometimes the effects are within natural ranges; sometimes they are not. The most common effects of road construction and timber harvest activities on soil, water, and air resources are loss of vegetation, soil erosion and compaction, loss of soil productivity, increased potential for landslides, reduced transpiration (use of water by plants), increased water runoff, reduced water quality, and periodically reduced air quality.

Roads have long been recognized as a substantial human-caused source of soil and water disturbances in forested environments. Most impacts occur during initial road construction and then gradually decrease as roadside vegetation is reestablished and disturbed soil surfaces stabilize. Effects such as landslides persist when a road permanently undercuts unstable soils or landforms, or when roads are continually disturbed by road maintenance. Periodic maintenance activities can cause some of the impacts to reoccur. Areas of particular concern are the road surface and associated drainage structures such as ditches and water crossings (bridges, culverts, and fords). Poorly maintained roads can result in greater impacts as surface water is diverted, culverts plug, and other road design characteristics are compromised. Lack of maintenance commonly has detrimental effects on water quality. Insufficient maintenance funding is a key reason for the lack of adequate road maintenance (USDA Forest Service 2001). Temporary road construction has most of the same effects as permanent road construction, but generally for a shorter term and for a more limited physical extent. Long-term effects can occur if temporary roads receive extended use and are not decommissioned.

The National Forest Management Act requires land management plans to have assurances that soil, slope, or other watershed conditions will not be irreversibly damaged (16 USC 1604(g)(3)(E). This is reflected in both the 2012 planning regulations at 36 CFR 219.8(a)(2) and the previous 1982 planning regulations (now replaced) that were at 36 CFR 219.23.

##### **Soils**

###### Background

###### _Soil Compaction, Displacement, and Site Productivity_

Timber harvests and road construction can negatively impact soil and affect long-term productivity due to heavy machinery use and timber harvest operations which can 1) compress the soil (compaction), 2) displace or mix the surface organic matter with the mineral soil, or 3) expose low-productivity subsoil. These soil disturbances have the potential to affect site productivity. The degree and extent of soil disturbance are often site-specific and the distribution across a site depends on the type of logging system (such as ground-based operations, skyline or helicopter harvest systems) and silvicultural prescription (Crawford et al. 2021).

Soil disturbance most commonly occurs as compaction resulting from ground-based harvesting and slash disposal equipment. All soils are highly susceptible to compaction except for a few unique, strongly cemented soils. Soil compaction is defined as the “densification of an unsaturated soil body by the reduction of its air-filled porosity, generally resulting from the application of compressive forces (pressure)” (Hillel 2008: 265). Forest soils are prone to compaction due to their loose, friable structure and high porosity. The most common cause of soil compaction is human activities, such as timber harvesting, that increase the amount of traffic (such as off-road vehicles, harvest equipment; Cambi et al. 2015). The degree of soil compaction is related to soil texture (Page-Dumroese et al. 2009), soil moisture (Froehlich and McNabb 1983), harvesting system (Adams and Froehlich 1981), amount of slash on the soil surface (Han et al. 2006; McMahon and Evanson 1994), and the number of machine passes (Han et al. 2006; McDonald and Seixas 1997).

Concerns about the effects of compaction extend to reductions in vegetation and root growth, nutrient cycling, and microbial functions (Bodelier et al. 1996; Cambi et al. 2015; Frey et al. 2009; Startsev and McNabb 2000). Harvest equipment traffic leads to an increased bulk density, a measure of soil compaction (Han et al. 2006). The extent of impact on skid trails can be variable with bulk density increases in the skid tracks (if present), throughout the skid trail width (up to 5 meters in width), and up to 2 meters outside the trail (Solgi et al. 2020). In addition to changing soil physical properties, increased compaction can lead to substantial alterations of soil biological and chemical properties (Cambi et al. 2015), such as reduced gas exchange and water infiltration, reduced root and tree growth, lowered decomposition, altered nutrient cycling, and a general decline in health of the microbial community. These properties are critical for maintaining vegetation growth, and range from short- to long-term effects, depending on the degree of soil compaction.

Soil displacement and erosion, rutting and puddling, and platy structure are some other common forms of soil disturbance. Once soils reach saturation, soil displacement is often more detrimental than compaction (Crawford et al. 2021). Soil displacement is the movement of soil from one place to another by harvest equipment and is sometimes confused with soil erosion which refers to the physical wearing away of the Earth’s surface by wind, water, or other natural geologic forces. Displacement occurs when equipment turns on soft soil or when equipment moves topsoil from one location to another. Displacement can negatively affect numerous site and soil characteristics by altering short- or long-term soil quality or site productivity. For example, organic horizons improve site productivity because they act as a mulch, drive nutrient cycling, and are home to many macro- and microorganisms (Martin 1988), but they can easily be displaced during harvest operations. Perhaps the most important attribute of surface organic horizons and organic matter within the soil profile in seasonally dry forest soils is that they hold a great deal of water; 1 to 6 percent organic matter can hold 5 to 25 percent available water (Hudson 1994).

The effects of soil compaction and displacement have the potential to affect site productivity<sup>45</sup> . The effects of soil disturbance on site productivity or hydrologic function are related to the degree, extent, distribution, and duration of impact (Craigg and Howes 2007). Forest soil surface horizons are more fertile than deeper soil horizons, and a compacted or displaced surface can lead to reduced plant growth. Severe compaction in the surface mineral soil can negatively impact forest regeneration and root development when the soil penetration resistance exceeds 2.5 Megapascal, which is commonly reached in skid trails (Cambi et al. 2015; Williamson and Neilsen 2000). Seedling roots have difficulty penetrating compacted mineral soil, leading to seedling death or growth deformities (Greacen and Sands 1980). Seedling survival declines when emerging root radicles from seeds cannot penetrate dense soil caused by soil compaction (Froehlich et al. 1986). If seedlings do establish on compacted soils, they may have lower growth rates due to the alteration of soil physical properties that influence root penetration, aeration, moisture, and nutrient uptake (Brais 2001; Conlin and van den Driessche 1996; Demir et al. 2010; Froehlich et al. 1986; Gomez et al. 2002; Pinard et al. 2000; Smith 2003; Williamson and Neilsen 2000). The greatest losses during seedling establishment occur when the surface structure is destroyed by puddling (smeared mineral soil particles that reduce infiltration; Binkley and Fisher 2013).

###### _Soil Loss and Sedimentation_

Erosion is defined as the detachment and removal of soil particles from the soil surface by running water or wind (Hillel 2008). It can be exacerbated by displacement because of the piling of loose soil on the soil surface. On slopes, removal of overstory trees can lead to erosion where ground cover or surface organic horizons are removed due to traffic. Soil disturbance and erosion can further be increased if stumps are pulled for bioenergy or to reduce a threat of disease (PageDumroese et al. 1999). Over time all roots decompose and this loss of root structure within the

> 45 Site productivity is the capacity of a site to grow biomass such as trees.

soil profile can exacerbate soil erosion, particularly shallow landslides on steep slopes (Amman et al. 2009).

Sediment within timber harvest units is most strongly related to road construction and associated facilities (skid roads and trails, log landings, etc.) that are needed to remove logs (Anderson et al. 1976). Skid roads and trails, log landings, and similar disturbances within the sale area are the main cause of soil erosion and can contribute up to 90 percent of the sediment generated by timber sale activity (Patric 1976; Swift 1988). Historically, poorly managed timber harvest activities have been a major source of sediment from a timber sale area (Martin and Hornbeck 1994). However, implementation of Best Management Practices may significantly reduce sedimentation. Generally, monitoring has shown compliance rates for implementing best management practices to be between 85 percent and 98 percent,<sup>46</sup> , with compliance rates increasing over time as awareness and training programs take effect (Edwards 2000). Although best management practices do not completely eliminate water quality impacts, they do reduce impacts to acceptable levels. “Best management practices may not be completely effective, but they do provide a level of protection that the states and the Environmental Protection Agency judged sufficient to meet the goals of the Clean Water Act” (Cristan et al. 2018). All Forest Service permanent and temporary roads needed for timber sales are designed and constructed using water, soil, and air best management practices<sup>47</sup> that meet or exceed those required by individual States under Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) direction. Current road design and management criteria incorporate the latest knowledge and experience, resulting in fewer effects such as surface erosion, landslides, sedimentation, and dust emissions, on water, soil, and air resources. Proper design and construction of new roads and maintenance of existing and new roads can limit but not eliminate these effects (USDA Forest Service 2000b).

###### _Landslides and Debris Flows_

Forest harvesting and road construction may significantly increase the frequency and severity of debris flows by altering the landscape's hydrology and soil stability. Harvesting and roads disturb the natural cycle of soil reinforcement and drainage, making slopes more susceptible to failure, especially in mountainous regions with heavy rainfall (Goetz et al. 2015).

Effects of forest harvesting activities increase the likelihood of debris flows through reduced soil reinforcement and altered hydrology. The decay of tree root networks can dramatically increase the risk of shallow landslides, which can then evolve into debris flows. Tree roots act as a natural binding agent, reinforcing soil and increasing its shear strength. After logging, roots decay over a period of 5 to 20 years, causing a critical drop in soil strength. This makes slopes more vulnerable to landslides, with the highest risk occurring 5 to 10 years after harvesting. The removal of forest canopy and undergrowth reduces the amount of rainfall intercepted and transpired by plants. This increases the amount of water reaching the forest floor, elevating soil moisture levels and raising pore water pressure within the soil. Saturated soils are more prone to failure, particularly on steep slopes. Logging and mechanical harvesting can compact soils and

> 46 Results vary between States and ownerships, with Federal lands and large forest industry entities showing highest compliance.

> 47 National Best Management Practices for Water Quality Management on National Forest System Lands, 2012

create pathways like skid trails and landings. This decreases the soil's infiltration capacity, causing more rainwater to run off the surface rather than soak into the ground. This increased runoff can contribute to triggering debris flows. Landslides that occur in harvested areas can deliver large amounts of sediment directly into stream channels. This added material can accumulate and later be mobilized by rainfall, initiating in-channel debris flows.

Forest roads may be more of a trigger for landslides and debris flows than logging alone, particularly when improperly located in unsuitable terrain. Roads destabilize slopes and alter drainage networks in several ways. Road construction involves creating artificial cuts and fills that can alter the natural slope profile. Road fills are highly prone to failure because they are composed of loose, uncompacted material placed on the original, often steep, hillslope. Steepened road cuts can also fail, especially if subsurface water flow is concentrated behind them. Roads act as effective drainage systems, redirecting and concentrating surface and subsurface water flow. Road surfaces are highly compacted, which prevents infiltration and forces water to run off the surface. Poorly designed culverts or drainage ditches can become overwhelmed during intense rainstorms. When blocked by sediment and woody debris, water can overtop the road and lead to erosion and roadbed failure. Roads create a "hydrologically connected" network that quickly routes water and sediment into stream channels, bypassing natural infiltration processes. This leads to flashy and increased streamflows, which can initiate in-channel debris flows. Many landslides are triggered at road sites, and research has shown that landslides and debris flows are 6 to 9 times more likely adjacent to forest roads (Goetz et al. 2015).

###### Potentially Affected Environment

In general, soil conditions within the potentially affected environment are satisfactory. Potentially affected inventoried roadless areas have few large-scale disturbances related to management activities.

###### _Soil Compaction, Displacement, and Site Productivity_

Data collected as part of the Watershed Condition Classification<sup>48</sup> indicates that soil conditions in HUC<sup>49</sup> -12 watersheds (watersheds) which contain the potentially affected inventoried roadless areas across the Eastern, Southern, Southwestern, and Intermountain regions have soil conditions that are ranked as 43 percent, 28 percent, 31 percent and 13 percent as “Poor” which indicates

> 48 Watershed Condition Classification (WCC) system: The WCC system is a national forest-based, reconnaissance level evaluation of watershed condition that can be aggregated for a national assessment of watershed conditions. The WCC system offers a systematic, flexible means of classifying watersheds based on a core set of national watershed condition indicators. The system relies on professional judgment, GIS data, and available national databases, and on written rule sets and criteria for indicators that describe three watershed condition classes (functioning properly, functioning at risk, and impaired function). The WCC system is a first approximation of watershed conditions, and WCC information is employed to establish priorities, evaluate program performance, and communicate watershed restoration successes to interested stakeholders and Congress (FS-978, 2011). For more information, see: https://www.fs.usda.gov/naturalresources/watershed/condition_framework.shtml

The least altered soil conditions are currently found across watersheds in the Northern, Rocky Mountain, Pacific Southwest, and Pacific Northwest regions, where more than 75 percent of soils rank as being “Good” condition with “minor or no alteration of soil condition”.

###### _Soil Loss and Sedimentation_

Soil loss and erosion from forest harvest operations is typically highest from native surface or gravel roads. The GRAIP Road and Trail Erosion<sup>50</sup> metric is a spatial modeling approach to estimate sediment delivery to waterbodies from road and trail surfaces within a watershed. Within watersheds that contain potentially affected inventoried roadless areas, the best conditions are found in the Eastern and Pacific Northwest regions where more than 50 percent of the watersheds are rated “Good” which corresponds to a low sediment delivery to waterbodies (Table 31). All other Regions have more than 50 percent of watersheds classified as “Fair or Poor” with the highest percentages found in the Southwestern, Southern, Pacific Southwestern, and Intermountain regions.

The Southwest and Intermountain regions are characterized by arid or semi-arid climate with high intensity monsoon rainfall that leads to high erosion rates. The Pacific Southwest region is a Mediterranean climate that experiences high intensity and prolonged duration storm events

> 50 Geomorphic Roads Analysis and Inventory Package (GRAIP): Uses Digital Elevation Maps (DEMs), existing road GIS layers with surfacing type information, and field calibration datasets to determine subwatershed scale road sediment production and delivery. Specifically, we use two metrics derived in GRAIP: Slope Stability (Cmin) and Erosion Base Rate from Roads (Luce et al. 2025). Sediment production from roads is calculated, and delivery is based on the flow distance from the road to the stream and the calibrated probability of delivery at that distance. Slope stability ratings are based on the infinite-slope Factor of Safety equation (such as O’Loughlin 1974, Gray and Megahan 1981, Hammond et al. 1992, Pack et al. 1998), which describes the force balance of soil on a hillside. For more information, see: https://research.fs.usda.gov/rmrs/projects/graip

during winter months; while the Southern region is more temperate, it experiences very high intensity and often long duration rainstorms from tropical disturbances and convective storms.

###### _Landslides and Debris Flows_

Hillslope stability was modeled in GRAIP and results suggest that the highest risk for landslide potential within potentially affected inventoried roadless areas is found in the Pacific Northwest and Pacific Southwest regions where 69 percent and 56 percent of watersheds containing potentially affected inventoried roadless areas are rated as having “Poor” Slope Stability (Table 32). ###### Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action

Alternative 1 would have little potential for altering current soil and water conditions in the potentially affected IRAs because of the general prohibitions on road construction, reconstruction, and tree cutting activities. Few forest roads or major facilities are expected to be constructed in the potentially affected inventoried roadless areas. Thus, there would be little to no additional permanent loss of the productive capacity of the land. Likewise, the limited miles of new roads constructed or reconstructed relying on exceptions, would be scattered among many different potentially affected inventoried roadless areas so the likelihood would be low that projected road construction would occur on highly sensitive soils and result in a substantial increase in soil erosion. There would also be little additional risk of significant increase of landslides or debris flows because the likelihood would be low that projected road construction would occur on highly unstable slopes and result in a substantial increase in landslides or debris flows.

###### Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action

Under alternative 2, additional road construction, reconstruction, temporary roads, timber harvest, and other activities are reasonably foreseeable within the potentially affected IRAs. Impacts to soil resources are more likely where activities are more likely to occur (see National Forest System Roads, Vegetation and Forest Management, and Fire and Fuels Management sections) and where more sensitive conditions exist within the affected environment. Those impacts would be reduced using mandatory National best management practices and local land management plan compliance.

###### _Soil Compaction, Displacement, and Site Productivity_

Roadbuilding is a dedicated land use, and any additional permanent roads would remove the impacted area from forest production. Even temporary roads, which are required to be revegetated after use, and skid trails are likely to reduce productivity for several decades. Timber harvest and some non-commercial fuel treatments typically involve tracked or wheeled vehicles to cut and remove trees from the harvest area which may displace and compact soils under certain environmental conditions and if multiple passes are made over the same piece of ground. The potentially affected inventoried roadless areas within these watersheds are relatively undisturbed compared to other lands. The IRAs in the Intermountain, Southwestern, Southern, and Eastern regions are at higher risk for impairment due to additional activities because more of these areas are currently classified as ‘fair’ to ‘poor’ existing soil conditions (Table 30). Additional road building and harvesting in IRAs could increase soil disturbance and reduce productivity across the affected environment. However, operational restrictions would limit the potential for future soil disturbance within potentially affected roadless areas. For example, only 12 percent of the potentially affected inventoried roadless areas in the Intermountain region are classified as “likely operable” (see Vegetation and Forest Management and Appendix 1 – IRA Acreage Summarized by Region). Similarly, only 6 percent of potentially affected inventoried roadless areas in the Southwestern region are classified as “likely operable.”

###### _Soil Loss and Sedimentation_

Under alternative 2, future road building and timber harvest could disproportionately contribute additional sediment delivery where erosion rates are greatest. The GRAIP Roads and Trails Erosion model indicates that erosion rates from roads and trails rates are greatest in the Southwest, Southeast, Pacific Southwest, and Intermountain regions (Table 31). If additional road building occurs in areas with erosive soils and high intensity and/or high duration precipitation events, it could lead to additional sediment delivery to surface waters. Within the regions’ most sensitive to soil loss and erosion, only 6 percent of the Southwestern region, 12 percent of the Intermountain region, and 15 percent of the Pacific Southwestern region are classified as “likely operable”. However, 40 percent of the potentially affected inventoried roadless area in the Southeastern region is “likely operable”.

###### _Landslides and Debris Flows_

Vegetation removal and road construction may lead to landslides and debris flows where long steep hillslopes and intense precipitation coincide. Roads can facilitate landslides and debris flows by undercutting hillslope stability, adding mass to the slope, and channeling water downslope of the road surface. The GRAIP Slope Stability model indicates that mass wasting events are most likely in the Pacific Northwest and Pacific Southwest regions where long steep slopes and high intensity precipitation events, and saturated soils co-occur, particularly in winter months when atmospheric rivers frequently bring abundant moisture (Table 32). If additional road building activities occur in these unstable areas, it would likely lead to additional mass wasting events. There is also significant risk in the Southern and Northern Regions (Table 3). Although mass wasting events are less common in more arid regions and regions with lower relief, landslides and debris flows are still possible with additional road construction in potentially affected inventoried roadless areas.

As with other soil metrics not all of the potentially affected environment is likely operable. Only 15 percent of the potentially affected inventoried roadless areas in the Pacific Southwest and 21 percent of potentially affected areas in the Pacific Northwest are considered likely operable with another 16 percent in the Rocky Mountain and 40 percent in the Southern regions. Across the Roadless Affected Environment, land management plan components generally allow timber harvest across 13.4 million acres (33.4 percent of NFS land within the Roadless Affected Environment). Plans do not allow timber harvest on 36 percent and generally do not allow timber harvest on another 26.8 percent (with some exceptions). Across the Roadless Affected Environment, land management plan components generally allow road construction across 18.2 million acres (45.5 percent of NFS land within the Roadless Affected Environment).

###### Effects of Alternative 3 – Modified Rule

Soil conditions within roadless areas identified under alternative 3 do not differ greatly from those identified under alternative 2. Therefore, the distribution of areas of concern is consistent with alternative 2, but the affected area in alternative 3 is smaller than the affected area in alternative 2. Areas with the least amount of existing disturbance (further than ½ mile from existing roads) would continue to have roadless prohibitions with effects similar to alternative 1.

##### **Water Resources**

###### Background

###### _Water Quality_

The Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) delegates the responsibility to implement the Clean Water Act to the States and Tribes. The Forest Service works closely with States and Tribes to assure Agency management practices comply with their requirements. Per agreements with many States, the Forest Service is the designated water-quality management agency for NFS lands. These agreements include specific procedures to apply if water quality problems are discovered.

Section 303(d) of the Clean Water Act requires States to evaluate water quality in light of State water-quality standards, report those stream segments that are impaired, and require development of a total maximum daily load of pollutants. Many States have identified impaired stream segments on NFS lands, and they are working with the Forest Service to determine how to reduce pollutant impacts and meet total maximum daily load requirements. On NFS lands, many of the recognized impairments are from sediment, temperature, nutrients, and similar pollutants (U.S. EPA 1997).

Since 2012, the Forest Service has used National Core Best Management Practices to aid in meeting CWA requirements. The National Best Management Practices Program was developed to improve agency performance and accountability in managing water quality consistent with the Federal Clean Water Act (CWA) and State water quality programs. Current Forest Service policy directs compliance with required CWA permits and State regulations and requires the use of best management practices to control nonpoint source pollution to meet applicable water quality standards and other CWA requirements.

Roading and timber harvest may indirectly affect water quality by increasing the release of certain nutrients from the decomposition of timber harvest byproducts (leaves, branches, and other organic matter) and reduced nutrient uptake from harvested trees. Nutrients, such as nitrogen, phosphorous, potassium, and calcium may increase in stream water following timber management activities. Nitrogen generally shows the most abrupt changes. Tree cutting has less effect than subsequent site preparation activities that are used to expedite regeneration (Hornbeck and Leak 1992). Elevated nutrient levels in streamflow usually return to normal in 1 to 4 years (Chamberlin et al. 1991).

Road construction and timber harvest may cause water temperature to change where groundwater is intercepted and brought to the surface, where the stream channel shape is wider or shallower, or where loss of tree cover in riparian areas reduces shading (Hornbeck and Leak 1992). Temperatures may rise sharply in exposed areas and some of those elevated temperatures may then return to normal levels as water re-enters shaded areas downstream or receives cool inflow from other streams or groundwater (Pierce et al. 1993). Smaller or shallower streams are generally more susceptible to temperature fluctuations than larger or deeper streams (Chamberlin et al. 1991).

###### _Water Yield and Runoff Timing_

Roads affect the quantity and timing of stream flow by intercepting, concentrating, and diverting runoff (Furniss et al. 1991; Sedell et al. 2000). They can indirectly affect annual flow volume because they replace trees that use water. Water otherwise used by trees would run off or enter into the soil.

###### **Water Yield**

The relative effects of individual timber harvesting and roading activities on flooding decreases as watershed size increases. The extra flow generated in smaller watersheds becomes less evident as it joins flows from other watersheds and continues downstream (Anderson et al. 1976; Stone et al. 1979; Hewlett and Doss 1984; Thomas and Megahan 1998; Ziemer 1998). Similarly, numerous harvest units and roads in multiple sub-watersheds of a larger watershed generally do not yield proportional increases in floods. Additional water from smaller units enters the main stream at different times. This action desynchronizes the flows, moderating net flow increases. Effects of land uses, such as timber harvest and roading, are more evident during small and moderate storm events but are less important in large storm events (Bosch and Hewlett 1982). Large runoff events are generally the result of large volume or extended periods of precipitation or snowmelt runoff that exceed the capacity of the soil to hold additional water. This is true regardless of land use practices. Timber harvests can cause an increase in total annual water yield, whereas roads are unlikely to have a similar effect, mainly because harvests tend to cover more area than roads (Sedell et al. 2000).

Changes in total annual water yield would most likely be detected where there is abundant moisture to begin with, and where the soil has less ability to absorb additional water such as in the coastal forests of California, Oregon, and Washington (Harr 1983; Kattelmann et al. 1983; Ziemer 1987). Studies in Eastern forests indicate that at least 20 percent to 25 percent of the basal area in a given watershed must be removed to produce detectable increases in annual flow (Hornbeck et al. 1993). Changes in total annual water yield are generally less detectable in the drier climates of the Intermountain West and Southwest where additional water is quickly used by the remaining plants or is lost through evaporation (Sedell et al. 2000). Harvest levels on NFS lands in the Southern and Eastern regions are generally too small to generate measurable change (Hornbeck et al. 1993). Water-yield returning to normal levels is in direct proportion to how quickly the site revegetates. Regrowth in the East and in humid parts of the West is rapid, and flows return to normal levels in 6 to 10 years after harvest. Slower growth in drier parts of the country may extend the recovery period to at least twice as long (Douglas and Swank 1972; Sedell et al. 2000).

###### **Runoff Timing**

Timing of water runoff (how quickly a watershed generates runoff and the time it takes for that water to work its way downstream) can change as roads and related drainage structures intercept, collect, and divert water. This accelerates water delivery to the stream, more water becomes storm runoff, which increases the potential for runoff peaks to occur earlier, be of greater magnitude, and recede more quickly than in unroaded watersheds (Wemple et al. 1996).

Vegetation cover removal through timber harvest can also change flow timing. In conifer forests where the majority of precipitation is in the form of snowfall, such as in the Intermountain West, openings in the forest canopy can capture more snow and deliver it earlier during spring runoff (Leaf 1975; Troendle and King 1985; Troendle and King 1987). In rain-dominated Western conifer forests, flows from harvested areas are greater toward the end of the summer dry period than are flows from uncut forests, but the flow difference is minimal once soils are re-saturated by fall rains (Ziemer 1998). Harvesting hardwood forests and areas that receive the majority of precipitation from rainfall delivers more water in the late summer or early fall. This pattern can supplement low flows during these times and can be beneficial to fish and other aquatic organisms during water-stress periods (Anderson et al. 1976; Kochenderfer and Hornbeck 1999; Elliot and Vose 2011). Changes in water timing are most likely to occur in areas with large amounts of timber harvest and roading because these activities have the highest potential to alter natural hydrologic processes. Areas with greater variability in seasonal precipitation and runoff, such as the arid and semi-arid portions of the West, would be more sensitive to changes in flow timing than areas with more even rates of precipitation and runoff such as the humid portions of California, Oregon, and Washington, and the Eastern United States. Changes in the magnitude of flood peaks and seasonal low flows are more evident in drier climates (Sedell et al. 2000). The USDA publication, “Forest Service Roads: A Synthesis of Scientific Information,” (2000) summarizes most of the effects of roading and timber harvests on hydrologic regimes.

Collectively, these studies suggest that the effect of roads on basin stream flow is generally smaller than the effect of forest cutting, primarily because the area occupied by roads is much smaller than that occupied by harvest operations. Generally, hydrologic recovery after road building takes much longer than after forest harvest because roads modify physical hydrologic pathways but harvesting principally affects evapotranspiration processes.

###### _Municipal Water Use_

Forests are an important source of clean drinking water for over 150 million people in the United States (Caldwell et al. 2014; Liu et al. 2021). The 749 million acres of forested lands provide more than half of the national water yield from lands in the lower 48 States (Liu et al. 2021; Sun et al. 2016). Approximately 74 percent of total drinking water withdrawals originate from surface water sources, such as streams, ponds, and reservoirs, and national forests provide about 15 percent of the Nation’s runoff (Dieter et al. 2018; Liu et al. 2021). Population and water demands are projected to increase in the western U.S., including communities adjacent to IRAs (Sedell et al. 2000; Theobald 2005). At the same time temperatures are increasing, snowpack is declining and melting sooner, and drought and summer water deficits are more frequent and longer (Barnett et al. 2008; Mohammed and Tarboton 2008; Miller et al. 2021). Streamflow reductions ranging from 10 to 45 percent are likely for the western states over the next half century (Barnett and Pierce 2009; Miller et al. 2021). A 10 percent drop in streamflow is considered calamitous by municipal water districts. More frequent and intense flood events are also likely in places (Raff et al. 2009), despite drying conditions. Costs for flood control, repair and reconstruction, and insurance rates will also increase (GAO 2007). Because inventoried roadless areas represent roughly a third of national forestland, by inference they contribute significantly to the overall runoff volume and value estimated in billions of dollars annually (DellaSala et al. 2011). The Forest Service is required to manage National Forest watersheds that supply municipal water under multiple use prescriptions in land management plans (36 CFR part 219).

###### Potentially Affected Environment

###### _Water Quality_

There are watersheds which contain potentially affected inventoried roadless areas and 303d impaired waters within the affected environment. Water bodies on the 303d list are considered “Impaired” and require the development of a Total Maximum Daily Load (TMDL). A TMDL includes a calculation of the maximum amount of a pollutant that can be present in a waterbody and still meet water quality standards. Derived Road and Trail Erosion Indicator is a rating for watersheds containing potentially affected inventoried roadless areas. This indicator addresses the propensity for existing road and trail networks to deliver sediment to a water body based on local climate, topography, and soils. Nearly 40 percent of these watersheds with 303d listed streams are found in the Pacific Northwest region with another 13 percent in the Northern and Southern region, and 10 percent or less in all other regions (Table 33).

Within the potentially affected inventoried roadless areas, water quality parameters assessed in the Watershed Condition Classification are rated highest in the Rocky Mountain region, where more than 90 percent of watersheds containing IRAs are rated as having “Good” water quality. Followed by the Southwestern, Intermountain, Pacific Northwest, Pacific Southwest, and Northern Rockies, which all have more than 60 percent of watersheds rated as having “Good” water quality. The lowest rankings occur in the Southern and Eastern regions where long legacies of industrialization and agriculture have impacted water quality. Although water quality conditions are generally considered better in the western US, it should be noted that in the Pacific Northwestern region, nearly 40 percent of watersheds containing IRAs also have 303d listed streams.

###### _Water yields and run-off_

Water yields and hydrographs within the watersheds containing potentially affected inventoried roadless areas are generally in “Good” condition (Table 34). The Southwestern and Intermountain regions have a disproportionately high number of watersheds where water yields and hydrographs have been altered within the affected environment. Only 51 percent of watersheds considered in the Intermountain region are rated as “Good” and only 43 percent of watersheds considered in the Southwestern are rated as “Good.” The Southwestern and Intermountain are two of the most arid regions, and many streams have been altered for water delivery and are utilized for irrigation.

###### _Municipal Watersheds_

According to the more recent data in the Forest to Faucets 2.0 Assessment<sup>51</sup> , there are more than 7,000 municipal water intakes within watersheds containing potentially affected inventoried roadless areas (Table 35). The largest number of intakes occur in the Pacific Northwest and Intermountain Regions, but the largest populations served by these water supplies occur in the Pacific Southwest Region where nearly 5 million people receive drinking water from municipal intakes within watersheds containing potentially affected inventoried roadless areas. This is largely driven by the proximity of major population centers to water supplies in California. In other regions water from the potentially affected inventoried roadless areas flows downstream and collects in major rivers before being utilized by municipalities. This is particularly evident in the Northern, Rocky Mountain, and Intermountain Regions where population centers are further from sources. Approximately 24 million people utilize water which originates within potentially affected inventoried roadless areas (Table 35) outside of Alaska<sup>52</sup> .

###### Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action

###### _Water Quality_

Potentially affected inventoried roadless areas typically have good water quality due to limited disturbance. Less than 12 percent of watersheds with potentially affected inventoried roadless areas have “impaired” streams on the 303d list. Activities that could be allowed to occur under the exceptions of the 2001 Roadless Rule are unlikely to contribute to further impairment of streams currently listed on the 303(d) list or degrade water quality in a significant way as widespread road building and timber harvest are generally prohibited.

###### _Water Yield and Runoff Timing_

Water yields and runoff patterns within potentially affected inventoried roadless areas are generally unaltered from natural conditions. The extent and magnitude of road construction or vegetation removals under alternative 1 would not be expected to cause a major change in water yield from the potentially affected inventoried roadless areas. The aerial extent of the tree cutting in the roadless areas would not be great enough to exceed 20 percent basal area within any watershed. (Research indicates 20 to 30 percent of a watershed needs to be disturbed to generate a measurable increase in water yield); Douglas and Swank 1972, Hornbeck et al. 1993).

###### _Municipal Water Use_

Alternative 1, in general, has little risk of potential adverse effects and provides the greatest protection of water quality.

Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action

###### _Water Quality_

Road construction and native surface forest roads are the largest source of sediment related to timber harvest operations, and sediment delivered to surface waters is a major source of water quality degradation. Additional road construction and timber harvest is reasonably foreseeable in potentially affected inventoried roadless areas and has the potential to impact water quality through increased sediment delivery. However, using Best Management Practices during road maintenance and construction can reduce these impacts to levels congruent with the Clean Water Act. The Eastern and Southern Regions are the most susceptible to additional impacts due to long histories of land use, agriculture, and industrial pollution surface waters in (Table 33). The Eastern and Southern Regions also tend to have the largest percentage of potentially affected inventoried roadless areas considered likely operable with greater than 40 percent across both regions. Although water quality in the western United States is generally considered better due to larger protected areas, more than 20 percent of watersheds containing potentially affected inventoried roadless areas in the Northern Rockies region are rated as having “Poor” water quality, and 40 percent of surface waters in the Pacific Northwest are listed as “impaired” (Table 34). Within these regions, only 16 percent and 21 percent of potentially affected roadless areas are classified as “Likely operable” within the Northern and Pacific Northwest Regions (Potential Forest Management Opportunity, Figure 10).

###### _Water Yield and Runoff Timing_

The presence of roads can alter the timing and magnitude of peak flows because they can “short circuit” the natural water flow paths in a watershed through ditches and culverts, delivering water more quickly to the watershed outlet. Timber harvests and road construction have the potential to alter water yields and runoff timing if implemented over large areas, and regions with more arid climates are most likely to see altered runoff after timber harvests. Although harvested areas may accumulate more snowpack during winter, the snowpack tends to melt earlier in spring which potentially limits water yields later in the season. Water yields and runoff timing are already significantly altered in the Southwestern and Intermountain region due to water diversions for irrigation and municipal water use. Additional road construction and timber harvests in these regions could further alter water yields and runoff. Similar effects are possible in more arid portions of the Northern, Rocky Mountain, Pacific Northwest, and Pacific Southwest Regions. In all but the Rocky Mountain and Pacific Northwest regions the percentage of potentially affected inventoried roadless areas considered likely operable is less than 20 percent.

###### _Municipal Water Use_

If additional road construction or timber harvest result in a decrease in water quality, and or reduce the efficacy of water treatment. The Pacific Southwest Region is particularly vulnerable because nearly 5 million people draw water directly from watersheds containing inventoried roadless areas (Table 35). The Intermountain Region is also affected in that over 1 million people receive water directly from watersheds containing inventoried roadless areas (Table 35). This does not infer that other regions are not vulnerable to the effects of increased road and timber harvests. More than 4 million people receive water from inventoried roadless areas in the Northern Rockies, Rocky Mountain, and Intermountain Regions, another 2-3 million people receive water roadless areas in the Southwestern, Eastern, and Southern Regions; however, these users tend to be further downstream. Overall, the largest percentage of potentially affected inventoried roadless areas deemed “likely operable” occurs in the Eastern and Southern regions where the topography is less steep compared to the western US and harvest operations and road construction are more feasible in inventoried roadless areas. Most forest plans acknowledge the need for protections for municipal watersheds with plan components that have an overall management objective to maintain and improve watershed status.

###### Effects of Alternative 3 – Modified Rule

For the areas that remain with Roadless Rule designation and prohibitions, the effects would be similar to alternative 1.

For those areas where the 2001 Roadless Rule would no longer be in place, effects to water quality, water yield and runoff timing, and municipal water use would be similar to alternative 2, however, the distribution of effects would be in a smaller area.

###### _Water Quality_

Analyses of water quality within potentially affected roadless areas identified under alternative 3 do not differ from those identified under alternative 2. Therefore, the distribution of areas of concern are consistent with alternative 2, but the affected area in alternative 3 is less than the affected area in alternative 2.

###### _Water Yield and Runoff Timing_

Analyses of water yields and runoff timing within potentially affected roadless areas identified under alternative 3 do not differ from those identified under alternative 2. Therefore, the distribution of areas of concern are consistent with alternative 2, but the affected area in alternative 3 is less than the affected area in alternative 2.

###### _Water Use_

Under alternative 3 there are more than 5,000 municipal water intakes within watersheds containing potentially affected roadless areas, outside of Alaska. The largest number of intakes occur in the Intermountain and Northern Rocky Mountain regions, but the largest populations served by these water supplies occur in the Pacific Southwest Region where nearly 4 million people receive drinking water from municipal intakes within watersheds containing the potentially affected roadless areas identified under alternative 3 (Table 36). This is largely driven by the proximity of major population centers to water supplies in California. In other Regions water from the affected environment flows downstream and collects in major rivers before being utilized by municipalities. This is particularly evident in the Northern, Rocky Mountain, and Intermountain Regions where population centers are further from sources.

##### **Air**

###### Background

Air quality related values are defined as resources that may be negatively affected by air pollutants, including visibility, soil and water quality, and vegetation. Through the Clean Air Act<sup>53</sup> (CAA) the EPA identifies geographic areas of the country that do not meet the primary ambient air quality standards as being in “nonattainment” for that air quality standard. Individual states are responsible for compliance with the NAAQS and those containing non-attainment areas are required to develop State Implementation Plans (SIPs) that outline emission control strategies and other activities to demonstrate how they will address non-attainment. Primary NAAQS and EPA standards for the air pollutants included in this analysis are summarized in .<sup>54</sup>

|---|---|---|---|

|Ozone (O3)|70 ppb|8 hours|Annual fourth-highest daily maximum<br>8-hour concentration, averaged over 3<br>years|
|Particulate Matter 2.5<br>micrometers (PM2.5)|9.0 µg/m<sup>3</sup>|1 year|Annual mean averaged over 3 years|
||35 µg/m3|24 hours|98th percentile averaged over 3 years|
|Particulate Matter 10<br>micrometers (PM10)|150 µg/m3|24 hours|Not to be exceeded more than once<br>per year on average over 3 years|

53 The Clean Air Act, as amended (42 U.S.C. 7401 et seq.), significantly broadened the authority and responsibility of the Forest Service by:

- a. Complying with all Federal, State, or local air control rules, regulations, and directives.

- b. Requiring compliance with substantive and procedural requirements imposed by a Federal, State, interstate, or local administrative authority or court.

- c. Requiring consultation with each State having delegated authority on all matters concerning the prevention of significant deterioration of air quality, visibility, air quality maintenance plan requirements, and nonattainment requirements.

- d. Giving Federal land managers an affirmative responsibility to:

- (1) Protect the air quality related values on any lands managed by them within a class I area.

- (2) Consider, in consultation with the Administrator of the Environmental Protection Agency, whether a proposed major emitting facility will have an adverse impact on air quality related values.

54 The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency Green Book was used to evaluate the current state of air quality in potentially affected roadless areas. The EPA Green Book: The Green Book (https://www.epa.gov/green-book) provides information about area National Ambient Air Quality Standards (NAAQS) designations, classifications and nonattainment status. Information is current as of the Green Book posted date and is available in reports, maps and data downloads.

Prevention of Significant Deterioration is a state-permitting process that requires emission limitations for certain new or modified sources of air pollution, mandated by the Clean Air Act. Under this program, attainment areas are categorized as either Class I, Class II, or Class III based on their levels of air quality protection. Class I allows very little deterioration of air quality. The Clean Air Act established Class I areas in each national park over 6,000 acres and each national wilderness larger than 5,000 acres that existed when the 1977 Amendments were passed. The CAA also established the Regional Haze Rule (EPA 2011) to remedy existing, and prevent future, impairment to atmospheric visibility caused by pollution in selected Class I areas (42 USC 7491). This rule requires states to develop plans to reduce emissions that affect Class I areas in order to achieve natural visibility conditions.

###### Potentially affected environment

Current maps of air quality nonattainment and maintenance for O3, PM2.5, PM10, and NO2 were analyzed to identify areas that overlap with the potentially affected IRAs (Table 38). Nonattainment areas have not met the NAAQS and require a SIP to address air quality. Maintenance areas have a history of nonattainment but now meet the NAAQS under a maintenance plan. Affected inventoried roadless areas overlapping non-attainment and maintenance are areas where new emissions may be most important with respect to effects on air quality. State and local managers and experts would be best positioned to conduct the analyses needed to inform specific emission scenarios and potential air quality outcomes at the appropriate scale. Within non-attainment areas, General Conformity of new projects would apply. General Conformity is the pollution prevention and control program that requires a federal agency to assure any activity the agency proposes will conform to the purpose of applicable implementation plans and will not interfere with a State or Tribe's ability to attain and maintain the NAAQS for air quality, as required under the CAA section 176(c). A substantial portion of inventoried roadless areas are near or adjacent to wilderness areas so air quality in wilderness areas could be affected (see Recreation and Scenery).

Less than 5 percent of inventoried roadless area overlaps Primary NAAQS nonattainment or maintenance for O3, PM or NO2 in all regions other than the Pacific Southwest, consistent with generally good air quality across national forests and Class I areas nationally. The Pacific Southwest Region, however, contains much larger areas of inventoried roadless area overlapping nonattainment and maintenance: 40 percent of roadless area for ozone (nonattainment), 19 percent for hourly and annual PM2.5 (nonattainment), 27 percent for annual PM10 (nonattainment), and 7 percent for NO2 (maintenance). Overlap does not imply that nonattainment or maintenance in these areas is the result of emissions on National Forest System lands. Rather, these are areas where new emissions on National Forest System lands may contribute to an existing air quality issue.

###### Effects of Alternative 1 – No action

The vast majority of inventoried roadless areas are currently in attainment for Primary NAAQS. Maintaining current activity levels within inventoried roadless areas means that air emissions in these areas would not be expected to change significantly and that future trends in air quality will be the result of changes in emission sources outside inventoried roadless areas. In this analysis, the Pacific Southwest Region differs from other Regions in the extent to which current inventoried roadless areas overlap with non-attainment and maintenance for ozone, particulate matter, and nitrogen oxides. Improvements in air quality in these areas will be subject to effectiveness of State Implementation Plans and other emission reduction measures.

###### Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action and Alternative 3 – Modified Rule

###### _Smoke_

Fire (wildfire, prescribed burning, slash burning) are sources of ozone precursors, particulate matter, carbon dioxide, carbon monoxide and other trace gases. Fuel removal in newly accessible areas may reduce wildfire intensity but may also lead to more frequent prescribed burning. These emissions could impact nearby communities, especially in valleys and basins with poor dispersion. Most states have a smoke management program to minimize impacts to air quality, which may be part of a State Implementation Plan. Additionally, FSM 5140 policy requires coordination of prescribed fire program activities with Regional air quality specialists and Federal, State, Tribal, air pollution control district or county regulatory authorities to ensure compliance with air regulations and use of Basic Smoke Management Practices (USDA 2011) to minimize impacts to downwind communities.

Greater access to current inventoried roadless areas could affect fire and subsequent emissions in several ways. Use of prescribed fire could increase, potentially impacting nearby communities seasonally for relatively short periods of time. As noted previously, prescribed fires are carefully planned and managed under state and local smoke management programs and use of Basic Smoke Management Practices (USDA 2011) to minimize impacts to downwind populations. Expanded use of prescribed fire reduces wildfire risk, thereby increasing air quality resilience longer-term. Greater access leads to a higher potential for fire ignitions from recreational activities (campfires, vehicles) and forest management (vehicles, equipment). Expanded treatment of fuels (mechanical, prescribed fire, pile burning) would increase the frequency of short-term managed smoke events but reduce risk to wildfire over the longer-term. Greater access also allows for faster response to wildfires, resulting in smaller burned areas and shorter duration smoke events. The net effect of the alternatives would be more frequent short-duration smoke events from prescribed fire and fuels treatment but greater air-quality resilience due to reduced risk of catastrophic wildfire.

Potential expansion of the road system under the alternatives, and therefore access for fuel treatment, human wildfire ignition, and firefighting, will likely follow the operability designations for currently inventoried roadless areas. The total acreage of areas where the road system may be most likely to expand (likely operable) is nearly the same between alternatives. Thus, the potential for changes in fire emissions, both positive and negative, may be similar between the alternatives. Overall, resulting changes in wildfire will likely drive changes in total emissions from fire and subsequent air quality impacts over the long-term. A more detailed analysis of the potential net result is not possible at this time.

###### _Road Construction and Use_

Elimination of the national prohibitions in the 2001 Roadless Rule would allow for road construction and reconstruction within potentially affected inventoried roadless areas. This would likely increase permanent road miles, especially further than 0.5 miles from existing roads, resulting in more miles traveled on Forest Service roads (see National Forest System Roads). Road construction and maintenance results in emissions of particulate matter (dust) from ground disturbance and application of construction materials such as crushed rock, sand, and gravel. Construction equipment and associated vehicle traffic are sources of ozone precursors, particulate matter, carbon dioxide, carbon monoxide, sulfur dioxide and other trace gases. Postconstruction travel is also a source of these pollutants including emissions of dust on unpaved roads. Dust emissions from construction activities and travel of vehicles and heavy equipment on unpaved roads are episodic and do not typically involve long distances. Dust emissions are related to traffic volume and speed, vehicle characteristics, properties of the road surface (such as silt and moisture content), and frequency and amount of precipitation. Trackout of dust and dirt on vehicles onto paved roads is an additional source of PM emissions. Mitigation measures exist for control of emissions from the unpaved road surface and trackout. Disturbed natural soils are, in general, prone to wind-blown dust emission, the impacts of which will be highly dependent on meteorological conditions, direction and distance from non-attainment areas.

Increases in miles traveled will result in increased emissions. It is difficult to assess potential differences in effects between the alternatives at the national scale. Assuming new road construction and reconstruction is most likely to occur in “likely operable” areas (reference Timber operability), potential changes in emissions between the alternatives may be similar.

###### _Tree Cutting and Removal_

Increases in timber harvest could result in increased emissions from tree cutting and removal activities. Gas- and diesel-powered equipment used to cut and remove trees are also sources of ozone precursors, particulate matter, carbon dioxide, carbon monoxide, sulfur dioxide and other trace gases. Slash pile burning and prescribed fire are short-duration sources of these pollutants, as well. Tree cutting and removal also disturb natural soils, which may become a source of windblown dust.

Given the similarities in “likely operable” acreage noted above, it is reasonably foreseeable that emissions from tree cutting and removal between the alternatives would be similar.

###### _Mineral Development_

Under alternatives 2 and 3 across potentially affected inventoried roadless areas, any new or expanded authorizations for oil and gas or coal exploration and development would be subject to review and include surface use restrictions, including limiting road construction, consistent with the roadless rule prohibitions (see Locatable and Leasable Minerals Section). It is reasonably foreseeable that new mineral development is mostly likely to occur near existing roads and within the “likely operable” areas. Development of locatable (such as gold, silver, lead, gypsum), leasable (oil, gas, coal) and material (sand, stone, gravel) minerals would result in emissions associated with drilling, production, processing, storage, and transport of products. These emissions would include ozone precursors, particulate matter, carbon dioxide, carbon monoxide, sulfur dioxide and other trace gases. The sources of these emissions could include drilling equipment, venting and flaring of gas, storage tanks, pipe fittings and valves, pumps, dehydrators, compression engines, and heavy equipment and vehicles associated with extraction and transport activities. Particulate matter in the form of dust would also be emitted as a result of vehicle travel and ground-disturbing activities. Drilling, blasting, loading and hauling of mining products and waste are also sources of ozone precursors, particulate matter, carbon dioxide, carbon monoxide, sulfur dioxide and other trace gases. Oil and gas extraction, as well as mining, is also a source of methane, a greenhouse gas, and some hazardous air pollutants.

The potential for increased emissions from new mineral development would be greater for alternatives 2 and 3, compared to no action, but likely similar between the two alternatives.

**Table 39. Relative potential for air emissions differs among categories and alternatives. + represents lowest potential for emissions and ++++ represents the highest potential for emissions.**

|**Activity**|**Alternative 1**|**Alternative2**|**Alternative 3**|
|---|---|---|---|
|Road construction and use|+|++|++|
|Tree cutting and removal|+|++|++|
|Prescribed fire|+|++|++|
|Wildland fire|++++|+++|+++|
|Mineral development|+|++|++|

##### **Conclusion – Soil, Water, and Air Resources**

Alternative 2 and 3 would have variable effects on soil and water resources across Forest Service Regions and individual national forests depending on the direction in existing land management plans. Many of these plans already have protections in place for potentially affected IRAs. However, it is reasonably foreseeable that additional road construction and timber harvest eventually could occur, and these activities could contribute to reducing forest soil productivity and water quality. Impacts are more likely in the ‘operable’ areas and where existing land management plans allow them. Any specific activities that are eventually implemented would be decided by local and regional managers based on local conditions and priorities. Any future sitespecific projects or changes to land management plans would need to follow all Federal, State and local laws and agency policy and regulations, including but not limited to the Multiple Use, Sustained-Yield Act, National Forest Management Act, National Environmental Policy Act, and the Clean Water Act. Plan components ensure that management not lead to permanent impairment of the productivity of the land (16 U.S.C. 1604 Sec. 2(g)(3)(C).

Alternatives 2 and 3 would have minimal immediate effects on air resources. However, it is reasonably foreseeable that new activities that could affect emission of air pollutants could eventually occur. It is likely that changes in wildfire emissions resulting from the proposed alternatives will have the largest potential effect on air resources at the national scale, compared to emissions associated with prescribed fire, mineral development, road use and construction, and timber harvest and removal. The relative importance of changes in prescribed versus wildfire will vary Regionally. Any future site-specific projects or changes to Land Management Plans would need to follow all Federal State and Local laws and agency policy and regulations, including but not limited to requirements of the Clean Air Act. When relevant or required, project level analyses would determine potential changes in emissions more quantitatively to assess effects on air resources, taking into account local conditions that reflect contributions from influential sources outside inventoried roadless areas. This analysis revealed that the Pacific Southwest differs substantially from other Regions in the extent that current inventoried roadless areas overlap with current NAAQS non-attainment and maintenance areas for ozone, PM and nitrogen oxides. These represent areas where air resource objectives may be more sensitive to future changes.

#### Biodiversity

##### **Plant and Animal Communities**

###### Background

Biodiversity measures the variety of organisms and life forms in a specific area and is a key attribute of healthy ecosystems. Declining biodiversity is strongly linked to the human actions that have modified ecosystems leading to declines in habitat quality, quantity, and connectivity in marine, freshwater, and terrestrial settings (USDA 2023).

The following sections provide a detailed discussion of the reasonably foreseeable short- and long-term effects of the alternatives on biodiversity of plant and animal communities:

- Terrestrial Animal Species and Habitats

- Aquatic Animal Species and Habitats

###### • Terrestrial and Aquatic Plant Species and Habitats

In this analysis, reasonably foreseeable effects to biodiversity were evaluated by considering the kinds of species potentially affected, identifying the important characteristics of inventoried roadless areas that foster biodiversity, and evaluating the potential adverse and beneficial effects of road construction and timber harvest on those characteristics. These effects are discussed for plant and animal communities including terrestrial animal habitats and species, aquatic animal habitats and species, and terrestrial and aquatic plant species. These are broad groups for discussion and are intended to encompass all species within these groups that might occur on NFS lands, including but not limited to species of concern (such as species listed under the Endangered Species Act, Regional Forester’s Sensitive Species<sup>55</sup> , Species of Conservation Concern<sup>56</sup> ), migratory birds, game species, and pollinators. The potential effects of the alternatives were addressed by considering land use; regional distribution and abundance of inventoried roadless areas; relationship of species to habitats present in potentially affected inventoried roadless areas; fragmentation and connectivity; laws, regulations, and policies that affect biodiversity; operability; and invasion of non-native species.

###### Assumptions Common to All Alternatives

In all alternatives, biodiversity of plant and animal communities on NFS Lands, including inventoried roadless areas, would continue to be planned for and managed according to the requirements of law, regulation, and policy. All current Forest Service land management plans, through the National Forest Management Act (NFMA) and planning regulations (1982 Planning Rule or 2012 Planning Rule), are required to maintain the diversity of plant and animal communities and support the persistence of native species in the plan area (39 CFR 219.9, 2012 Planning Rule; Sec. 219.19, 1982 Planning Rule). Specifically, under the 2012 Planning Rule, biodiversity is maintained in the plan area by providing the ecological conditions necessary to support the persistence of most native species. These ecological conditions may include standards or guidelines related to habitat features, condition, or connectivity. Additionally, all land management plans, through NFMA and planning regulations, are also required to maintain and contribute to the recovery of threatened and endangered species and conserve proposed species in the plan area (39 CFR 219.9, 2012 Planning Rule; Sec. 219.19, 1982 Planning Rule).
