55 Regional Forester Sensitive Species (RFSS; sensitive species) – Policy developed under the 1982 Planning Rule at Forest Service Manual (FSM) 2670 requires identification of sensitive species and project-level analysis of effects on viability of sensitive species in a Biological Evaluation (BE). Although a BE was prepared for the proposed rule, under all alternatives project-level BEs will still be required for administrative units with land management plans under the 1982 Planning Rule.

> 56 **_Species of Conservation Concern (SCC)_** – The 2012 Planning Rule at 36 CFR 219 adopted a complementary ecosystem and species-specific approach to provide for the diversity of plant and animal communities and the longterm persistence of native species. This approach requires the identification of SCC and development of plan components to maintain or contribute to maintaining a viable population of each SCC. For all land management plans that have been or are being revised under the 2012 Planning Rule, the appropriate regional forester identified SCC. An SCC is a species – other than federally recognized threatened, endangered, proposed, or candidate species – that is known to occur in the plan area and for which the regional forester has determined that the best available scientific information indicates substantial concern about the species’ capability to persist over the long-term in the plan area (36 CFR 219.9(c)).

It is assumed that under all alternatives, future land management plan revisions or amendments would comply with NFMA and planning regulations to meet these requirements to the extent feasible within Forest Service authority and consistent with the inherent capability of the plan area (36 CFR 219.9 and FSH 1909.12). Future projects in current inventoried roadless areas will require compliance with environmental laws and regulations, including the Endangered Species Act, Migratory Bird Treaty Act, and Bald and Golden Eagle Protection Act. As is typical, future project designs would avoid and minimize adverse effects to ESA-listed resources to the greatest extent feasible.

###### Potentially Affected Environment

###### _Biodiversity_

Biodiversity conservation depends on preventing habitat loss and degradation, limiting competition and predation by non-native species, and preventing pollution and overexploitation (Wilcove et al. 2000, as cited in USDA FS 2000a). The 2001 Roadless Rule FEIS, Volume 1, contains a comprehensive review of biodiversity in national forests and grasslands, as well as its roadless area context (USDA FS 2001). Biodiversity in the potentially affected environment occurs in many types of landcover, from evergreen and deciduous forest to non-forest vegetation types such as shrub/scrub, grasslands, alpine meadows, and wetlands, as well as areas of barren land and open water (for example, see in the Vegetation and Forest Management section). Both the amount and configuration of these cover types, and key features within them like older forest patches, riparian areas, snags, and downed wood, provide habitat for numerous terrestrial and aquatic animal and plant species. In turn, the biodiversity found within the potentially affected environment supports highly valued ecosystem services such as hunting and fishing, wildlife viewing, and activities such as outdoor recreation in large blocks of contiguous older forest. In accordance with the requirements of statute, The U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) and the National Marine Fisheries Service (NMFS) evaluate the risk of extinction of species and have designated many plant and animal species as threatened, endangered, or proposed for listing (“threatened, endangered, or proposed”).

Patterns of biodiversity vary across landscapes in accord with the spatial patterns of vegetation and ecosystem processes over short and long time scales. Five coniferous forest group types comprise about 75 percent of the forest cover in the potentially affected environment, occur primarily in the western U.S. including Alaska, and support a host of threatened, endangered, and proposed species, as well as numerous species of regional conservation concern, and many other valued fish and game species. Additional forest types such as mixed hardwoods, longleaf/slash pines, and oak/gum/cypress forests, do not cover as wide an area within the potentially affected environment (see Vegetation and Forest Management), but also serve key functions, supporting high levels of biodiversity in the Appalachians, the Midwest, and the southeastern U.S., along with related ecosystem services. Across the U.S., the approximately 37 percent of the potentially affected environment in non-forest vegetation cover also supports a host of threatened, endangered, and proposed native plant and animal species including species only found in these areas (endemics), as well as migratory game and non-game species.

###### _Habitat_

The potentially affected environment provides habitat for a host both common and rare animal and plant species, both terrestrial and aquatic. Numerous threatened and endangered species call these areas home. The most recent USFWS and NMFS data indicate that the potentially affected environment of inventoried roadless areas provides habitat for more than 300 threatened, endangered, and proposed species. Additionally, the potentially affected environment provides habitat for thousands of Regional Forester Sensitive Species (RFSS) and Species of Conservation Concern (SCC).

Within the potentially affected environment there are 79 final or proposed critical habitats designated under the Endangered Species Act for species managed by USFWS and 19 critical habitats designated for species evolutionarily significant units or distinct population segments managed by NMFS. The potentially affected environment also includes areas designated by NMFS as Essential Fish Habitat under the Magnuson-Stevens Act.

See the ESA-Listed Species and Regional Forester Sensitive Species section of this document for more information on those species

###### _Fragmentation_

The spatial and temporal attributes of the potentially affected environment affect biodiversity and the persistence and abundance of threatened, endangered, and proposed species there (Wilson et al. 2015). A synthesis of fragmentation experiments spanning five continents and 35 years demonstrates that habitat fragmentation reduces biodiversity by 13 to 75 percent (Haddad et al. 2015). Effects tend to be greatest in the smallest and most isolated fragments, increasing over time (Haddad et al. 2015). Decreased habitat patch sizes, increased patch isolation, and increased edge area can affect ecosystem processes that result in generalizable changes at population, community, and ecosystem levels, such as decreased residency within and movement among habitat patches, reduced species richness, decreased nutrient retention, and shifts in microclimate. Thus, shifts in spatial attributes of habitat over time, and the nature and timing of those shifts relative to the life history requirements of a species (Wan 2025), may have profound effects on its abundance and persistence.

The effects of fragmentation do vary depending on the species being considered. Forestdependent birds, for example, are generally more sensitive to habitat fragmentation than birds adapted to more open habitats (Timmers et al. 2022). Males and females of the same species may also have different spatial requirements. Northern long-eared bats, for instance, roost and forage in the forest interior, but female roosting and foraging areas may be roughly six and three times larger, respectively, than those for males (Broders et al. 2006). Aquatic species sensitive to sedimentation or increased temperatures may be adversely affected by activities that fragment landscapes, such as those increasing runoff to aquatic ecosystems, reducing the delivery or persistence of large dead wood to streams, or decreasing the amount of closed canopy forest in riparian areas.

Connectivity, the degree to which a landscape facilitates or impedes the movement of organisms and the flow of natural processes, is crucial for maintaining biodiversity and allowing species to access essential resources and new or discontiguous habitats in dynamic landscapes.

Connectivity is not an inherent property of the landscape, but a function of the physical landscape structure (“structural connectivity”) and the specific behavioral responses and movement capabilities of a particular species (“functional connectivity”). Connectivity, therefore, is relevant at the scale at which a given species interacts with its landscape. For example, wide-ranging mammals such as the federally threatened grizzly bear, have suffered habitat loss and the extirpation or fragmentation of their populations, and require connected landscapes across broad geographic extents, to facilitate movements of individuals and promote the conservation and recovery of the species (Sells et al. 2023).

It is widely understood that increases in forest edge can have detrimental and beneficial effects on threatened, endangered, and proposed species. For example, both northern spotted owls and California spotted owls nest sites tend to be located away from forest edges (Phillips et al. 2010), but edges and heterogeneity have been shown to be important for foraging and overall fitness for the California spotted owl (Zulla et al. 2022, Zulla et al. 2023, Kuntze et al. 2023, Jones et al. 2024) Some bat and bird species use forest edges extensively for foraging, while other related species do not (for example, Brand and George 2001; Morris et al. 2010, Blakey et al. 2019). Some species such as the California spotted owl use habitat containing a large proportion of mature conifer forest at the home range scale, but at finer scales may use other vegetation classes interspersed with mature conifer for foraging (Williams et al. 2011). Similarly, the amount of connected mature forests in the home ranges of Canada lynx increase the proclivity of females to produce kittens (Kosterman et al. 2018, Holbrook et al. 2019). While lynx select mature forests at broad spatial scales, they use a mosaic of forest-structure classes, avoiding open initiating stands and preferring young (20-30 year) stands of advanced regenerating forests (Holbrook et al. 2017). Thus, relationships between forest patch sizes, configuration, and connectivity among patches are complex and species-specific. One generally detrimental effect of forest edges involves non-native invasive species. Non-native invasive species may outcompete and adversely affect the persistence of native species. Invasive species may have increased opportunity to establish and increase their ranges in increasingly fragmented landscapes, especially those with roads for ingress. Non-native plants, for example, are twice as common within 500 feet of a road (Healey 2020).

The potentially affected environment consists of inventoried roadless areas of various sizes, shapes, and configurations. Patches of different kinds of vegetation also have different characteristic shapes and sizes, and they vary in how they are laid out across a landscape in terms of what different types of vegetation are nearby, and how far apart patches of the same kind of vegetation are from one another. This variety results from where roads exist, where the inventoried roadless area boundaries are, and what are the biophysical features of the given landscapes. For detailed information about spatial characteristics of the affected environment, please see Appendix 5 – Biodiversity Analysis Supplemental Information, where you can find tabular results for fragmentation analyses for both land cover patches and forest vegetation patches in roadless areas.

In general, in the northeastern U.S., contiguous inventoried roadless areas tend to be the largest. In the southeast they tend to be the smallest. The western (Intermountain, Northern, and Pacific Southwestern Region) inventoried roadless area patches tend to be closer to each other than those in the eastern U.S. These larger western inventoried roadless area patches also have more linear edge per unit area, which might also be described as more irregular, “wiggly” edges, for patches of a given size. The Eastern and Southern Regions (8 and 9) tend to have more fragmented, intricately shaped landscapes.

For vegetation types, differences in sizes, shapes, and the distribution of patches of a given vegetation type across a landscape result from differences in land use history, road occurrence, limited land management activities, vegetation ecology, and the spatial pattern and frequency of disturbances there. A range of sizes and shapes, and spatial distribution of patches would be expected to occur across the various forest vegetation types, at local to regional scales, as well as among Forest Service regions.

The spatial components of inventoried roadless area lands, just described, have been fairly static, affected only by the introduction of new roads or land allocation changes approved under exceptions. However, forest vegetation patch sizes, along with other spatial characteristics key to some species such as snag occurrence and longevity, may also vary over short to long time periods given the wide variety of forest vegetation types, their life history traits, and the frequency and magnitude of disturbances such as fires, insect outbreaks, and windthrow and ice storm events. The pace and pattern of these dynamic landscape vegetation attributes may dramatically affect the spatial distribution and abundance of native species.

Effects to species resulting from changes to the amount and configuration of landscape features also vary among species given their habitat requirements, life history, and population characteristics. In forested ecosystems, habitat specialists may require specific forest vegetation types, and some species, such as cavity-nesting birds and snag-dependent species, or those relying on downed dead wood, may even require specific features within those types. Habitat generalists, on the other hand, may be less sensitive to the spatial components of landscape vegetation. Sensitivity to fragmentation may be influenced by habitat type and the degree of specialization of the species, as well as life history traits such as fecundity, body mass, dispersal ability, and lifespan (Keinath et al. 2017; Suarez-Castro et al. 2025). Further fragmentation of forest types may have more negative impacts in forest types and for associated wildlife where that forest type is already rare, disconnected, and/or fragmented, depending on the needs of the individual wildlife species. In other words, where patches of forest vegetation are more intact and wildlife populations that are sensitive to fragmentation are more robust, fragmentation might have fewer negative impacts.

Understanding how various forest vegetation types are spatially distributed and shaped in a landscape can help us to assess potential habitat for wildlife. Across regions, as well as within an individual region, forest type groups have some generalizable differences in their sizes, shapes, and spacing. These probably come from differences in ecological processes across these complex landscapes, as well as from the characteristic temporal and spatial patterns of land use history and the history of disturbance, including fire sizes and severities there (please see the Fire and Fuels Management section for further description of fire sizes and severities in inventoried roadless areas). Indeed, patch sizes across regions seem fairly consistent with which forest types prevail in those areas and related history: patches of Douglas-fir in the Pacific Northwest, Northern, and Rocky Mountain Regions (Regions 6, 1, and 2), California mixed conifer in the Pacific Southwest Region (5), maple/beech/birch in the Southern and Eastern Regions (8 and 9), and loblolly/shortleaf and longleaf/slash pine in the Southern Region tend to be larger. Patches of tanoak/laurel in the Pacific Southwest and Pacific Northwest Regions (5 and 6), oak/pine in the Southern and Eastern Regions, and aspen/birch in the Rocky Mountain and Southwestern Regions (2 and 3) tend to be smaller on average than other vegetation types. In the Southwestern Region (3), ponderosa pine patches are highly fragmented, as are aspen/birch in the Intermountain Region (4), loblolly/shortleaf pine in parts of the Southern Region (8), and maple/beech/birch in the Eastern Region (9) around the Great Lakes. Distance to the nearest patch of the same type for many forest vegetation types does not currently seem to depend on the size of the patches: both large and small patches of various types have high distances to the nearest patch of the same vegetation type.

###### Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action

Under alternative 1, continued management pursuant to the 2001 Roadless Rule would prohibit road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest within inventoried roadless areas, with limited activities occurring based on exceptions, similar to historic levels. Trends in the affected environment would be expected to continue under alternative 1. Refer to the effects analyses for alternative 1 in the National Forest System Roads; Vegetation and Forest Management; Fire and Fuels Management; Non-Native Invasive Plant Species; and Water Resources sections. Since 2018, records indicate that regional foresters reviewed and approved 168 exceptions approved tree cutting (2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule Implementation). The majority were related to maintaining and restoring ecosystem characteristics. Eight exceptions approved tree cutting for the explicit purpose of “improving threatened, endangered, or sensitive species habitat,” although some of the exceptions outside of this category may have served multiple purposes, such as providing benefits to species habitat and additional exceptions may have been by responsible officials below the regional forester level.

With fewer disturbances caused by roads and associated activities, stream channel characteristics, such as channel and floodplain configuration, substrate embeddedness, riparian condition, amount and distribution of woody debris, stream flows, and temperature regime, are less likely to be altered (Furniss et al. 1991 as cited in USDA FS 2000). Illegal introduction and harvest of fish species would be less likely to occur under alternative 1 due to limited existing motorized public access in recreation settings within the affected environment and negligible changes to current non-motorized recreation settings anticipated (USDA FS 2000). With relatively few roads and low exposure to the disturbances associated with them, inventoried roadless areas are less likely to experience problems with non-native invasive species and are more likely to be able to maintain intact native plant communities (USDA FS 2000). Although exceptions may occur that allow road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest in inventoried roadless areas, potential adverse effects to terrestrial and aquatic species and habitats from fragmentation, loss of connectivity, edge effects, and human disturbance are much less likely under alternative 1 than under alternatives 2 or 3. These potential effects to species across taxa groups are discussed in more detail under alternative 2. Beyond these potential adverse effects being less likely under alternative 1, the potential beneficial effect of alternative 1 to species and habitats would be that large contiguous tracts of land are more likely to be maintained because the potential for roads and timber harvest are less likely to occur under this alternative than alternative 2 or alternative 3.

Ongoing natural processes in inventoried roadless areas would differ across a wide range of forest types, but generally would include continued forest biomass accumulation, succession, disturbance and tree mortality related to wildfire, drought, windfall, insects and disease (Vegetation and Forest Management section). Further, while the 2001 Roadless Rule specifically includes exceptions to improve threatened, endangered, proposed, or sensitive species habitat or to maintain or restore the characteristics of ecosystem composition and structure, exceptions overall are intended to be infrequent, and these activities would be much less likely to occur under alternative 1 than alternative 2 or alternative 3. Therefore, potential adverse effects to terrestrial and aquatic species and habitats under alternative 1 could include very limited to no additional access created for some species and habitat management; loss or degradation of habitats due to vegetation encroachment, high severity wildfire, insect and disease impacts; or reduced ability for habitat enhancement through stand manipulation.

Areas of non-forest vegetation account for 37.2 percent of the total potentially affected environment, with 30.1 percent of the potentially affected environment being shrub/scrub/dwarf scrub (Table 11). Vegetation succession and woody encroachment in non-forested areas of the potentially affected environment could have adverse effects on some terrestrial animal species that depend on open or semi-open habitats for safety from predators while foraging or reproducing; species that require diverse herbaceous forage; or species that forage exclusively on plants sensitive to shading and competition. For example, greater sage-grouse (of the bi-state distinct population segment) exhibit strong site fidelity, limiting the degree to which they can adapt when their habitat changes due to pinyon-juniper encroachment, urbanization and infrastructure development, fire-invasive species cycles, or local increases in predation (Coates et al. 2024, USFWS 2020). Additionally, vegetation succession and woody encroachment could lead to over-shading and competition of some groundcover and midstory plant species that require a degree of open canopy, resulting in reduced growth and productivity of those plants. In some cases, it has been observed that herbaceous plant species richness and diversity decline in grasslands and savannas as woody species increase (Van Auken 2009, Ratajczak et al. 2012). Under alternative 1, prohibition on road construction and reconstruction could make accessing and managing these habitats in inventoried roadless areas less likely than under alternative 2 or alternative 3. As discussed in the Vegetation and Forest Management section, under alternative 1, change in non-forested vegetation would not be expected in the short term. In the long term, meadows and hardwood stands would be subject to conifer encroachment which reduces their individual size and distribution across the landscape. Non-forested vegetation structure, such as shrub density, would continue to move away from objectives making it susceptible to future natural disturbances, such as insect infestations or large wildfires. Although expected to be infrequent, approved tree cutting exceptions may complement natural processes affecting vegetation structure and composition to improve habitat or maintain or restore ecosystem composition and structure. Additionally, fuels management activity that does not involve road construction or tree cutting (such as prescribed fire, or wildfire use) would continue to be allowed and may offset some of the potential effects of vegetation succession and woody encroachment; however, for prescribe fire, current IRA constraints limit both access and the ability to sustain treatment cycles (see the Vegetation and Forest Management and Fire and Fuels Management sections).

Many species evolved under the influence of recurrent fire, including stand replacing events, and their long-term persistence relies heavily on the maintenance of important habitat components by these disturbance events. However, high severity wildfires in old growth forests, or other forested ecosystems which are not adapted to high severity fire, could have adverse effects on some terrestrial animal species that depend on mature, structurally complex forests, dense canopy cover, or specific resources found in unburned habitats. For example, a major threat to mature forest specialist species such as the California and Mexican spotted owl, and Sierra Nevada Fisher is an increased risk of wildfires that kill a majority of the canopy layer, which could result in direct mortality or local loss of suitable mature and structurally complex forest habitat (Jones et al. 2016, Steel et al. 2023, Jones et al. 2025, USFWS 2023a). High severity wildfires could have an adverse effect on specialist species without suitable habitat to shift to nearby, species that lack mobility to quickly shift their occupied area, or species with low population numbers that can’t recover from a sudden loss of a portion of their population (Jager et al. 2021, van Mantgem et al. 2015). Even among mobile animals such as birds, growing size of high severity patches can lead to reduced species richness within patch interiors (Steel et al. 2022). For plant species, high severity wildfires could result in direct mortality of plant species lacking fire-adapted traits. For plants that survive high severity fires, there could be increased competition with fast-spreading non-native invasive species that become established quicker in the post-fire environment. Overall, the threat of high severity wildfires to forested habitat availability is the risk of large-scale tree regeneration failure and persistent type-conversion (Stevens et al. 2017; Coop et al. 2020). For example, high severity fires in pine forests that were historically burned frequently, but by low to moderate severity fires, may result in conversions to non-forested conditions. In forested riparian areas, the loss of trees and other overhanging vegetation along waterbodies due to high severity wildfires could result in a loss of cover habitat for aquatic animal species, reduced bank stability, and increased sedimentation.

Aggressive suppression across many decades has contributed to fuel accumulation and increased the likelihood that future fires, particularly those that escape initial attack, will burn under more extreme conditions and be more difficult to control (Arno and Brown 1991; Calkin et al. 2015; Kreider et al. 2024). Area burned has trended up through time for all NFS lands and potentially affected IRAs, leading to more total area burned at moderate and high severity in recent decades (Figure 14). The mean fire size within inventoried roadless areas from 2014 to 2024 was greater than other NFS lands but less than Wilderness areas (Table 18), and percent of area burned that was high severity within inventoried roadless areas during that same period was greater than other NFS lands and Wilderness areas (Table 19). Within inventoried roadless areas opportunities for mechanical treatments, prescribed fire, and wildland fire use are more limited compared to other NFS lands due in part to greater restrictions on road construction, access constraints, terrain, high operational costs, narrow burn windows for prescribed fire, and ignition timing of wildfire (Fire and Fuels Management). While wildfires could occur under all alternatives, under alternative 1 the prohibition on road construction and reconstruction could make accessing areas to perform fuels management activities in inventoried roadless areas less likely than under alternative 2 or alternative 3. Under alternative 1, fuels management activities can occur within inventoried roadless areas if they do not include road construction or tree cutting, or if they are reviewed and approved by the regional forester under an exception. Agency management records show that a lack of roads has not prevented fire prevention or protection measures; however, road restrictions limit opportunities for mechanical thinning and fire control (see the Fire and Fuels Management section). High severity wildfires could result in mortality of dispersal limited species, displacement of species due to habitat loss or competition, or loss of old growth and other forested habitats in both the short term and long term; however, as hazardous fuel loads continue to accumulate in these forested areas the risk to some species and habitats from high severity fires would increase.

Insect and disease outbreaks can affect species and habitats by directly harming native species, disrupting ecosystems, and altering the physical landscape. For example, outbreaks of bark beetles can kill trees over large areas which can reduce biodiversity and alter habitat structure (USDA FS 2025). Under alternative 1, trends in insect and disease activity would continue; these vary across forest type and are influenced by abiotic and biotic conditions. While the risk of insects and disease is similar in potentially affected IRAs relative to all NFS lands, under alternative 1 prohibition on road construction and reconstruction can make accessing and managing insect and disease outbreaks in inventoried roadless areas less likely under this alternative than alternative 2 or alternative 3 (see the Vegetation and Forest Management section for more detailed discussion on insects and disease). However, infrequent activities permitted under the existing exceptions may support localized management action to mitigate insect and disease caused mortality or damage at minor levels. In the short-term insects and disease outbreaks may stress populations or kill individuals of a species, but in the long term, if not managed, these outbreaks could lead to widespread tree mortality and habitat loss for some species.

Reduced ability for habitat restoration and enhancement for some species through stand manipulation in inventoried roadless areas may be a potential adverse effect of alternative 1, because under the prohibitions and exceptions, tree cutting, of generally small diameter timber, is expected to be infrequent and must approved by a regional forester (36 CFR 294.13(b)). For example, waiting for natural succession to reach the late-seral stage is not ideal when managing for at-risk species like the cerulean warbler in some regions; forest management can be used to provide the necessary structural diversity in a short period of time (Nareff et al. 2019). Under alternative 1, activities related to improving habitat for ESA-listed and sensitive species would continue to be limited to approved exceptions and would be more likely to occur primarily in areas where roaded access already exists. Although permitted activities or approved tree cutting exceptions under alternative 1 can be implemented to complement natural processes affecting vegetation structure, composition, and forest health to improve habitat or maintain or restore ecosystem composition and structure, these activities could be less likely under alternative 1 than under alternative 2 or alternative 3 where the prohibitions on road construction and timber harvest would be rescinded.

Current status of and trends in biodiversity are likely to continue under alternative 1 in the shortterm because no increase in timber harvest within the IRAs is expected and new road construction would be based on existing exceptions. The level of assumed forest management activity would continue at approximately the same as the recent levels. In the long term, the potential adverse effects to biodiversity from lack of management would be more likely than in the short-term under alternative 1, and could lead to habitat alteration or loss, species displacement, or direct mortality to plant and animal species. However, these potential adverse effects are applicable to specific conditions which may be less certain to occur relative to the potential adverse effects from a potential increase in road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest under alternatives 2 and 3.

###### Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action

Although alternative 2 does not compel or authorize site-specific activities in potentially affected inventoried roadless areas, an increase in site-specific road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest is reasonably foreseeable. Road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest could increase due to the removal of the prohibitions. However, these activities would only occur in areas where allowed under the direction of land management plans, NEPA, agency policy, and areas where mechanized equipment can safely operate. In the long term, biodiversity is more likely to be affected by road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest which could occur in more areas of the affected environment if land management plans are revised or amended, though timber activities would still be unlikely in the “not operable” forested areas (see the alternative 2 summary tables in Appendix 1 – IRA Acreage Summarized by Region). Refer to Vegetation and Forest Management and National Forest System Roades sections for a full discussion on potential road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest activities.

Future proposals for road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest, where allowed by current land management plans and where operable, would be considered on a case-by-case basis at the project level (see Common to All Alternatives). The potential effects to biodiversity which could occur in the short term and long term are discussed in more detail below for terrestrial animal species and habitats, aquatic species and habitats, and terrestrial and aquatic plant species and habitats. Overall, potential impacts to species and habitats from these effects would be expected to vary depending on the timing, location, and scale of activities. The discussion in this section captures the principal effects that have been associated with roads and timber harvest, but these are potential effects, and not every activity associated with roads and timber harvest would necessarily exhibit each of these effects.

_Terrestrial Animal Species and Habitats_

###### **Potential Adverse Effects from Road Construction or Road Reconstruction**

Inventoried roadless areas may function to provide some terrestrial animal species with refugia from potential adverse human-related activities that are prevalent in roaded areas. Almost all roads present some level of benefits and risks. These effects can vary greatly in degree and can shift over time (USDA FS 2000, Findlay and Bourdages 2000). Some effects are immediately apparent, but others may require external events, such as a large storm, to become visible. Other effects may be subtle, such as pathogens noticed only when they become widespread in the landscape, or with increased road use as recreation styles and motor vehicles change (USDA Forest Service 2000h, as cited in USDA FS 2000). Some of the potential adverse effects described below may already be mitigated through land management plans, particularly standards and guidelines determined during the land management planning process to provide the ecological conditions necessary to maintain or restore ecological integrity and diversity. Therefore, in the short-term these potential adverse effects to terrestrial species and habitats are less likely, but in the long-term these effects could be more likely to occur if there is an increase in road-related activities in the affected environment. In both the short term and long term, in compliance with land management plan requirements and through project-specific planning, future activities would be designed to avoid or minimize adverse impacts from road construction or reconstruction to biodiversity and in some cases could incorporate design features beneficial to terrestrial animal species and habitats, however, complete avoidance of adverse effects may not be possible.

Some of the potential adverse effects of road construction and road reconstruction activities on terrestrial animal species and habitats are summarized below with examples.

- Habitat loss and degradation

- ♦ Smaller habitat patch size and loss of interior forest habitat have adverse effects on numerous species dependent on such habitat including some neotropical migratory bird species such as the cerulean warbler, hooded warbler, and wood thrush (Southern Appalachian Man and the Biosphere 1996a as cited in USDA FS 2000).

- Habitat fragmentation and edge effects

- ♦ Numerous species inhabiting landscapes affected by the rescission of the 2001 Roadless Rule could be negatively affected by habitat fragmentation and the creation of additional edge habitat along roads if new roads are constructed (Haddad et al. 2015). Bird richness tends to decline with road presence in habitats with dense tree cover (Kroeger et al. 2022). At a population level, more mobile species and those with larger home ranges tend to respond more negatively to road presence (Rytwinski and Fahrig 2012). Populations of species with high reproductive rates and high flight altitudes, on the other hand tend to be less vulnerable to roads (Kociolek et al. 2015).

- ♦ Roads and forest cover can affect the behavior, health, and survival of recreationally harvested species like elk. Elk avoid areas near roads and select for habitat away from roads (Rowland et al. 2004, Rowland et al. 2000, Rowland et al. 2018). Ideal summer elk habitat, as described by one study conducted in northern Idaho, consists of unroaded land with at least 40 percent canopy cover and forage. The same study noted that in areas with roads, elk select areas with greater canopy cover (Unsworth et al. 1998). A study comparing time periods before and during a period of road restriction on Bureau of Land Management lands found that during the road restriction period daily elk movement rates decreased, potentially conferring energetic benefits (Cole 1997). The same study also found that survival rates increased during the road closure period and decreased again when road closure gates were removed.

- ♦ Occupancy of endangered marbled murrelet habitat declined with greater fragmentation, with greater negative effects further from marine resources and closer to the species’ range edge (Valente et al. 2023).

- Introduction and spread of invasive species

- ♦ Road construction, routine roadside mowing, and the use of roads have been shown to facilitate the spread of non-native invasive plant species such as yellow starthistle, scotch broom, knapweed, small broomrape, gorse, and cheatgrass (Mortensen et al. 2009, Gelbard and Belnap 2003, Seipel et al. 2012). Areas infested with invasive species, such as knapweed and leafy spurge, can have low grass productivity affecting the quality and amount of forage available to many animal species (Stannard 2004, USDA 2017a, USDA 2017b, Ortega et al. 2006). Invasive species can also have extensive impacts on native plant abundance (Pearson et al. 2016a). Once established, many of these non-native species are extremely difficult or impossible to eradicate. Depending on the chemical used and the application methods, the use of herbicides in eradication or control efforts can have unintended adverse effects to populations of other terrestrial and aquatic species (Crone et al. 2009, USDA 2017a, Pearson et al. 2016b, Ortega and Pearson 2011).

- Encroachment of generalist, competitor species into novel ecosystems

- ♦ Roads and other linear features can cause woodland caribou, their competitors (moose, deer, and elk), and their shared predators to change their space use (Dickie et al. 2023) with negative implications for caribou demographic rates (Serrouya et al. 2017). Competitor ungulates and predators can use roads to access parts of caribou range that were previously inaccessible to them, leading to increased predation on caribou populations and depressed population growth (Wittmer et al. 2007).

- Recreational disturbance

- ♦ Wolverines appear to be sensitive to both motorized and non-motorized human recreation (Heinemeyer et al. 2019, Barrueto et al. 2022, Kortello et al. 2019). In some instances, these interactions may be caused by roads, as one study found that wolverine distribution is negatively correlated with forest roads used by winter snowmachines (Kortello et al. 2019). Females may be especially sensitive to recreational disturbance (Heinemeyer et al. 2019).

- ♦ Human recreation activities, especially winter activities such as backcountry skiing, cat-skiing, and snowmobiling, make use of existing roads and require ongoing road maintenance. These roads create more opportunities for more human disturbance in areas that were previously less accessible (84 FR 52598) which affects woodland caribou stress levels (Freeman 2008) and habitat use (in snowmobile areas, Seip et al. 2007).

- ♦ Sound disturbance from recreation causes behavior changes (increased fleeing and vigilance) and decline in presence of elk and other wildlife around areas with hiker and biker noise (Zeller et al. 2024). Long-term exposure can increase stress and avoidance of impacted areas (Piñeiro et al. 2012; Heinemeyer et al. 2019).

- ♦ While bald eagles may have active nests near roads, especially when the roads predate the eagles’ successful nesting activity in the area, they are less disturbed by a constant level of use than by intermittent, occasional, or irregular use of those roads (USFWS 2007). Generally, activities should be kept as far away from nest trees as possible (USFWS 2007).

- Increased human wildlife conflict

- ♦ According to the grizzly bear recovery plan, “the presence of open roads in grizzly habitat often leads to increased bear/human contact and conflict, and can ultimately end in grizzly mortality”. The recovery plan adds “accidental shooting... and habituation through direct human contact and/or food reward all increase with the use of even secondary, unpaved roads by humans” (USFWS 1993).

- ♦ A study in Canada found that some moose are attracted to roadsides where vegetation has higher concentrations of sodium (as a result of de-icing in winter) during periods of sodium deficiency, and therefore road construction could increase human-moose interactions (Laurian et al. 2010). Increased chances for interaction could lead to increased conflict because moose have been known to stand their ground or charge when they feel threatened or cornered by humans, particularly during breeding season and when protecting young (CPW 2025, Utah DWR 2025).

- Barriers to movement and dispersal

- ♦ Multiple studies found that roads often restrict the movements of small mammals and can function as barriers to population dispersal (McGregor et al. 2007, Brehme et al. 2013 Galantinho et al. 2022). These studies identify road design and vegetation management of the roadside as important factors contributing to roads acting as barriers for small mammals.

- ♦ Terrestrial migrations may be disrupted by roads. For example, the addition of roads associated with oil extraction in Wyoming may have altered the migration routes and increased the movement speed of mule deer (Sawyer et al. 2013).

- Increased disturbance from human interactions leading to physiological stress for some species and lowered success in reproduction and rearing of young

- ♦ An experimental study found that nestling tree swallows exposed to traffic noise had higher levels of stress response hormones and lower body condition (Injaian et al. 2018).

- ♦ Experimental application of road noise in a roadless area of southern Idaho showed over a quarter decline in bird abundance (McClure et al. 2013), 31 percent of species avoided “phantom roads” where noise was applied, and decreased body condition for those birds that remained (Ware et al. 2015), with especially negative impacts on hatch-year birds influencing community age structure (McClure et al. 2017).

- ♦ A review of research on the effects of noise (including road noise) on wildlife showed largely negative impacts, including reduced abundance, altered vocal, vigilance and foraging behavior, and impacts on individual fitness and community structure. These effects were especially prevalent among taxa such as songbirds that rely on vocal communication (Shannon et al. 2015).

- ♦ Many reptile species are sensitive to harassment or human presence during particular seasons, which could lead to reduced productivity, increases in energy expenditures, or displacements in population distribution or habitat use (Bennett 1991 and Mader 1984 as cited in USDA FS 2000; Beale et al. 2016)

- ♦ Bats are vulnerable to disturbances and displacement caused by human activities in caves and mines, and on rock faces (Taylor et al. 2020, USFWS 2023b). Human entry or other disturbance to hibernating bats can result in result in an increase in total energy expenditure at a time when food and water resources are scarce or unavailable (USFWS 2022a).

- Direct mortality

- ♦ In a meta-analysis of studies across 22 countries comprising 69 mammalian species, road mortality was the greatest source of mortality for approximately one-third of populations for which data were provided (N = 16 out of 57 populations). Large individual losses on roads can directly reduce the effective population size and/or increase mortality rates above recruitment rates, therefore making populations vulnerable to environmental and demographic stochasticity, as shown for many species (Moore et al. 2023).

- ♦ While highways are regularly implicated in wildlife mortality, wildlife can also be killed by vehicles on lower-trafficked roads. For example, between 2010 and 2015, 22 of 54 lynx road mortalities in Maine occurred on low traffic, low speed, dirt forestry roads and 2 of 9 mortalities in Minnesota occurred on unpaved forest roads (USFWS 2017).

- ♦ Amphibians may be especially vulnerable to roadkill because their life histories often involve migration between wetlands and uplands, and individuals are inconspicuous and sometimes slow moving (Fahrig and Rytwinski 2009). Roads can be demographic barriers that cause habitat and population fragmentation (Joly and Morand 1997 as cited in USDA FS 2000; Lesbarrères et al. 2006).

- ♦ Reptiles, such as snakes, seek roads for temperature regulation and are vulnerable to mortality from motorized vehicles (Fahrig and Rytwinski 2009, Andrews et al. 2015).

- ♦ Some research has shown that roads can cause mortality of low-flying species and disrupt bat travel, especially major roads with high associated noise (Bennett and Zurcher 2012, Fensome and Mathews 2016).

- ♦ Roads and road traffic are major sources of insect mortality, act as barriers to small or flightless species, and are sources of pollution that negatively impact insects (Munoz et al. 2014).

- Loss of genetic diversity and genetic connectivity within and between populations

- ♦ Early genetics work found that roads often, but not always, decrease genetic diversity (Holderegger and Di Guilio 2010). A recent analysis of over 1,400 mammal populations in North America found that the impact of roads on genetic diversity varied widely across genera (Habrich et al. 2021) and was moderated by some life history traits. For example, the genetic diversity of species with large home-ranges was more negatively affected by road density than species with small home-ranges (Habrich et al. 2021).

- ♦ In the endangered southern Sierra Nevada fisher population roads were found to have a negative influence on genetic connectivity for females in the south-central region of the population where even the most heavily used roads are primarily twolane mountain roads running through otherwise contiguous forested habitat (Tucker et al. 2017).

- Changes in behavior

- Roads with high traffic can alter grizzly bear habitat use, driving them to be largely nocturnal and to select for private lands with lower traffic levels (Northrup et al. 2012). Habitat use for both grizzly and black bears in northwest Montana was lower near roads and trails (Kasworm and Manley 1990). Negative impacts of roads can be moderated through managed access of motorized access (Proctor et al. 2020).

- Decline in species abundance

- ♦ Road disturbance is linked to a decrease in caribou abundance in occupied caribou habitat and the surrounding areas. Research suggests that for every 1 percent increase in roads (buffered by 50 meters) in the areas surrounding occupied caribou habitat, there would be a 42 percent decline in southern mountain caribou abundance (Lochhead et al. 2022).

- ♦ Reptile and amphibian population can decline due to roads and traffic. One review article spanning multiple countries found that populations of frogs, toads, salamanders, turtles, snakes and lizards are negatively affected by roads (Rytwinski and Fahrig 2015).

- ♦ A global meta-analysis indicated that the negative effect of roads (and other types of infrastructure) on bird abundance can extend up to one kilometer away, with much variation among species (Benitez-Lopez et al. 2010). Multiple studies generally see decreased species richness or community integrity with increasing density of roads (Glennon and Porter 2005), though there tends to be a lag effect after roads are developed (Findlay and Bourdages 2000).

- ♦ Grizzly bear densities decline with increased road density in British Columbia and Alberta, Canada, but reducing vehicle access would likely reduce impacts (Lamb et al. 2017, Boulanger and Stenhouse 2014).

###### **Potential Adverse Effects of Timber Harvest**

There are potential risks of adverse effects on terrestrial animal species and habitats associated with harvesting timber. Adverse effects of timber harvest can vary, depending on the amount, type and location of timber harvest, overall watershed condition, status of species within the affected area, and the mitigation measures applied. Some of the potential adverse effects described below may already be mitigated through land management plans, particularly standards and guidelines determined during the land management planning process to provide the ecological conditions necessary to maintain or restore ecological integrity and diversity. Therefore, in the short-term these potential adverse effects to terrestrial species and habitats are less likely, but in the long-term these effects could be more likely to occur if there is an increase in timber harvest in the affected environment. In both the short term and long term, in compliance with land management plan requirements and through project-specific planning, future activities would be designed to avoid or minimize adverse impacts from timber harvest to biodiversity and in some cases could incorporate design features beneficial to terrestrial animal species and habitats, however, complete avoidance of adverse effects may not be possible.

Some of the potential adverse effects of timber harvest on terrestrial animal species and habitats are summarized below (USDA FS 2000) with examples.

- Declines in abundance for some species

- ♦ A noticeable decline in marten habitat in Sagehen Experimental Forest (California) occurred between 1983 and 1990. During that period, 39 percent of the forested habitat in the Sagehen Experimental Forest experienced some form of timber harvest, including 11 percent harvested with clear-cut or shelterwood methods and 28 percent harvested with salvage sales. As a result, researchers estimated that the percent cover of high-quality marten habitat in the Sagehen Experimental Forest declined from approximately 27 percent in 1978 to 15 percent by 1990. A number of other studies suggest that martens tend to disappear from an area after the percent of total forest cover is reduced below 45–75 percent (Hargis et al. 1999, Potvin et al. 2000, Fuller 2006, Webb and Boyce 2009).

- ♦ Ash (1997), and Petranka et al. (1993) found that clearcut timber harvest eliminated some species of salamanders from the harvest area (as cited in USDA FS 2000). Mossman et al. (2019) studied the response of two salamander species in New England forests and found that both species populations were lowest immediately after timber harvest but that the long-term mechanism of effects from harvest on each species varied. For example, the red-backed salamander abundances were strongly tied to stand age and abundance of coarse woody debris, while the abundance of eastern newts were tied primarily to wood debris and vegetation characteristics and not directly related to stand age.

- Habitat loss, fragmentation, and negative edge effects

- ♦ “Intensive” forest harvesting such as “large scale clearcuts” can be detrimental to lynx by eliminating habitat associated with prey and lynx denning (65 FR 16053). While lynx occasionally use even recently harvested landscapes (Squires et al. 2010;

Holbrook et al. 2018), they do not prefer them. In winter, lynx in the Northern Rockies avoid clearcuts (Squires et al., 2010). Similarly, lynx in this region increasingly avoided forests 5 years or less post-disturbance as it became more available in home ranges (Holbrook et al. 2017). Work in Maine shows similar results with lynx avoiding recent clearcuts in winter (Fuller et al. 2007). Regardless of silvicultural treatment, lynx may avoid recently harvested landscapes. Work in the Northern Rockies showed that lynx avoided areas subjected to group selection cuts, liberation cuts, improvement cuts, pre-commercial thinning, regeneration cuts with natural regeneration, and regeneration cuts with planting up to approximately 10 years post-action (Holbrook et al., 2018). Lynx use of these silvicultural treatments increased with time in a manner dependent on treatment type and composition of the surrounding landscape (Holbrook et al., 2018).

- ♦ The northern spotted owl, a late successional forest species, continues to be threatened by habitat loss due to lag effects of past habitat loss, continued timber harvest, wildfire, and non-native barred owls (85 FR 81144). Timber harvest continues to be a stressor even though loss of habitat due to timber harvest has been greatly reduced on Federal lands over the past two decades (USFWS 2011). Previous studies found that negative effects from timber harvest were most likely associated with instances of higherintensity thinning, timber harvest close to the nest area, and when the affected owl site had low amounts of alternative habitat available (USFWS 2011).

- Loss of snags and downed logs

- ♦ Loss of large trees, snags and logs in areas adjacent to roads through commercial harvest or firewood cutting could have adverse effects on cavity-dependent birds and mammals (Hannet al. 1997 as cited in USDA FS 2000). However, potential impacts to species dependent on these features would be expected to vary depending on the timing, location, and scale of removal.

- Lowered success in reproduction and rearing of young

- ♦ Although forest edges, such as those created by timber harvest, may benefit some species, they also provide access to interior forest patches for opportunistic species, such as the brown-headed cowbird, a brood parasite that lays its eggs in the nests of other bird species, and which may adversely affect some bird populations (Cox et al. 2012). One study on the Swainson’s warbler, a neotropical migrant, in the St. Franics National Forest and White River National Wildlife Refuge found that the probability of brood parasitism was greater near forest edges and that number of Swainson’s warblers fledged was lower in parasitized nests (Benson et al. 2010).

- Increased physiological stress for some species

- ♦ A study on ovenbirds in Canada found that logging disturbs nestling ovenbirds resulting in elevated baseline corticosterone levels and a shift of offspring sex ratio towards males (Leshyk et al. 2012).

- Introduction and spread of non-native invasive species

- ♦ Ground disturbing activities from machinery can transport seeds and plant parts of invasive plants and can create openings for existing or new populations to expand.

- Altered trophic dynamics (for example, shifts in composition of prey base)

- ♦ Northern flying squirrels (Holloway and Smith 2011) and red tree voles (Forsman et al. 2016), prey species of the northern spotted owl, occurred at lower densities in some timber harvest areas than in unmanaged forests. Another study in Canada also found that red squirrels and northern flying squirrels occurred at lower densities in partially harvested stands of mature Douglas-fir relative to unharvested stands, while yellow-pine chipmunks increased with logging intensity (Herbers and Klenner 2010).

- Direct mortality; impacts to ground dwelling species (loss of burrows, soil compaction)

- ♦ Some native bee species nest in the ground and mechanical equipment associated with timber harvest could destroy their nests or result in direct mortality to bees if present. For example, the federally listed rusty patched bumble bee typically nests in abandoned rodent nests or similar cavities in well-drained soils and may inhabit forests or forest edges (USFWS 2021). The current recovery plan for this species identifies undisturbed nesting habitat as a key component to their survival and successful recruitment (USFWS 2021)

- Disruption of dispersal and species migration

- ♦ Many amphibian species have been found in lower densities in some timber harvest areas when compared to mature, unmanaged forests (deMaynadier and Hunter, Jr. 1998, Petranka et al. 1993, Ash 1997, and deMaynadier and Hunter, Jr. 1999 as cited in USDA FS 2000). One study found that seven out of nine pond-breeding amphibians consistently experienced negative effects from clearcut timber harvest, which exposed the forest floor to more sunlight and wind; although effects in partialharvest treatments were mixed, with some positive effects observed potentially due to enhanced productivity of the herbaceous and shrub layers (Semlitsch et al. 2009). Clearcuts can result in changes to microclimate and a loss of leaf litter used as cover, potentially disrupting the movement of some amphibian species (Semlitsch et al. 2009).

###### **Potential Beneficial Effects of Road Construction, Road Reconstruction, and Timber Harvest**

Some of the potential beneficial effects of road construction and timber harvest on terrestrial animal species and habitats could include:

- Enhanced access for some plant and wildlife management activities

- Easier access for habitat restoration and enhancement for some species through stand manipulation

- Creation of edge habitat and early successional habitat used by some species

- Increasing abundance, for some species

- Facilitation of movement, migration, and dispersal, for some species Some examples of potential beneficial effects on terrestrial animal species and habitats include the following:

- Timber harvest can be used to benefit species like the red-cockaded woodpecker, Florida scrub jay, and Kirtland’s warbler by creating and maintaining appropriate open forest or early seral conditions (Rudolph et al. 2004, 89 FR 85294, USFWS 2019a, USFWS 2019b).

- The response of California spotted owls and Mexican spotted owls to fuels treatments depends on the silviculture type, method, and scale of treatment; but long-term benefits to reduce the risk of severe large-scale wildlife could outweigh short-term negative impacts (88 FR 11600, USFWS 2012). Some studies in California found that strategic fuels management, depending on the methods, can both mitigate severe wildfire behavior and provide habitat structure beneficial to some species such as spotted owls and fisher (McGinn et al. 2025, Steel et al. 2023, Jones et al. 2025).

- Roads and associated infrastructure can provide important roosting habitat for bats. Gray bats roost under bridges when caves are unavailable (Etchison & Weber 2020, Sasse 2019). Similarly, tricolored bats roost in road-associated culverts in places where caves and mines are rare (USFWS 2022).

- Road corridors function as travel routes for a number of bat species including those in the _Myotis_ genus. Gray bats use roads as travel corridors during flight, so rural road development may aid bat movement to foraging areas (Jackson et al. 2023). However, as noted under the potential adverse effects above, other research has associated mortality and travel disruption with low-flying bats using roads as travel corridors (Bennett and Zurcher 2012, Fensome and Mathews 2016).

- Species adapted to open forest canopy, such as hoary bats and silver-haired bats, show increased activity in clearcuts and harvested areas while forest-interior bat species show more variable responses (Patriquin and Barclay 2003; Owen et al. 2004). The endangered northern long-eared bat and Indiana bat selected previously thinned areas and patch cuts (less than 4-ha) but avoided large clearcuts (greater than 4-ha; Divoll et al. 2022). Loeb and Waldrop (2008) also showed increased bat activity for some species following forest thinning treatments.

- Roads can provide access for invasive species monitoring and removal. For example, a combined monitoring and barred owl removal experiment relied on road accessible monitoring (generally less than 200 m from minor roads) to locate invasive barred owls and lethally remove them to understand the conservation benefits for spotted owls and ecological integrity (Hofstadter et al. 2022).

- Silvicultural practices that bring small gaps that effectively mimic natural disturbance processes can be neutral to forest birds and even enhance habitat (Forsman et al. 2010). Similarly, the National Fire and Fire Surrogate project showed general increase in small mammals following fuel reduction treatments (such as prescribed fire, thinning, mastication), but variable responses among species and taxa (Converse et al. 2006). In forests with relative homogeneity such as second-growth forest stands in eastern North America, moderate levels of canopy reduction can increase understory vegetation and subsequently foraging and nesting opportunities for certain birds (Akresh et al. 2023).

- Silvicultural practices such as thinning and burning that emulate historical disturbance regimes can also support the diversity and abundance of bees that require early successional resources including flowering understory plants (Chase et al. 2023). These positive impacts are conditional on season, soil conditions, and management type. For example, the combination of prescribed fire and mechanical treatments can increase bee abundance and occurrence of rare species, while the combination of herbicide application and plantings can negatively impact rare species (Cunningham-Minnick et al. 2024). Post-harvest bee diversity was high in a Douglas-fir stands but declined with stand age (Rivers and Betts 2021).

- Stand manipulation can be used to improve habitat for species like the cerulean warbler when used to create opening in the canopy while retaining large trees preferred by this species (Nareff et al. 2019). Further, managing breeding habitat for cerulean warblers can simultaneously improve habitat for game species such as wild turkey, ruffed grouse, and white-tailed deer that need complex forest structure with mast-producing trees, interspersed with fields and young forests to satisfy year-round habitat needs (Nareff et al. 2019).

A road- or timber harvest-related beneficial effect for one species, may, in fact, represent an adverse effect for another. Because alternative 2 would allow the full range of timber harvest to occur (in alignment with land management plans), some local negative impacts to these resources and to biodiversity could occur. For example, although forest edges, such as those created by road construction and timber harvest, may benefit some species, such as deer and bobwhite quail, they also provide access to interior forest patches for opportunistic or predator species (Norse et al. 1986, as cited in USDA FS 2000). These benefits would be weighed at the local project level against the risks of implementing these treatments during project-level environmental review and when assessing projects for consistency with land management plan requirements. All land management plans are required under the 2012 Planning Rule to maintain or restore ecological integrity (36 CFR 219.8(a)), and older plans under the 1982 Planning Rule to provide for or contribute to ecosystem diversity (36 CFR 219.27(a)(1982)). To meet these requirements, land management plans include plan components to provide the ecological conditions necessary to support diversity of communities and the persistence of most native species in the plan area. Therefore, future timber or road-related activities implemented consistently with land management plans, as required, would be designed to avoid or minimize adverse impacts to biodiversity and in some cases could incorporate design features beneficial to terrestrial species and habitats.

Beneficial effects to terrestrial species from timber harvest activities are often due to creating or maintaining some specific habitat condition. Timber harvest creates forest age-class diversity and mosaic habitats used by some species (USDA FS 2000, Nareff et al. 2019, Forsman et al. 2010). Some species require early seral or open-forest habitats that can be created and maintained by restorative timber harvest. Timber harvest activities may also reduce the risk of uncharacteristic large stand-replacing insect and disease outbreaks and severe wildfires. These disturbance events can present both benefits and risks to some species, at least at a local level.

###### _Aquatic Animal Species and Habitats_

Refer to the Water Resources section for a full discussion of the potential effects of alternative 2 on water resources. This section focuses on how potential effects to water resources under alternative 2 could impact aquatic animal species and habitats. In compliance with land management plan requirements and through project-specific planning, future activities would be designed to avoid or minimize adverse impacts from road construction or reconstruction to the biodiversity of aquatic animal species and habitats, however, complete elimination of adverse effects may not be possible.

###### **Potential Adverse Effects from Road Construction and Road Reconstruction**

Road construction, reconstruction, maintenance, use, and even the presence of roads in a watershed can have numerous adverse effects to aquatic habitats and the species they support. Road design and application of best management practices have been effective in some instances at moderating or avoiding many adverse effects (USDA FS 2000). The discussion in this section captures the principal effects that have been associated with roads, but these are potential effects, and not every road would necessarily exhibit each of these effects. Some of the potential adverse effects described below may already be mitigated through land management plans, particularly standards and guidelines determined during the land management planning process to provide the ecological conditions necessary to maintain or restore ecological integrity and diversity. Therefore, in the short-term these potential adverse effects to aquatic species and habitats are less likely, but in the long-term these effects could be more likely to occur if there is an increase in road-related activities in the affected environment. In both the short term and long term, in compliance with land management plan requirements and through project-specific planning, future activities would be designed to avoid or minimize adverse impacts from road construction or reconstruction to biodiversity and in some cases could incorporate design features beneficial to aquatic animal species and habitats, however, complete avoidance of effects may not be possible.

Adverse effects from roads on water resources could include (Furniss et al. 1991, Trombulak and Frissell 2000, and USDA Forest Service 2000h, as cited in USDA FS 2000, USDA 2023):

- Increasing habitat fragmentation and loss of connectivity.

- Increasing sediment loads in streams.

- Altering watershed hydrology, stream flows, water temperature regimes, and stream channel morphology.

- Degrading water quality.

These physical alterations to water resources can potentially result in a variety of adverse effects to aquatic species and habitats including:

- Increased vulnerability of subpopulations to catastrophic events and loss of genetic fitness, related to loss of habitat connectivity.

- Loss of spawning and rearing habitat, deep pools, protective cover, and resting habitat.

- Increased reproductive failure and mortality of eggs and young.

- Increased susceptibility to disease, predation, over harvest, and poaching.

- Shifts in macroinvertebrate communities to those tolerating diminished water quality.

- Competition from non-native species.

- Increasing barriers to passage, gradient, and temperatures.

Some examples of adverse effects from roads on aquatic animal species and habitats include the following:

- The primary threats to big sandy crayfish are land-disturbing activities that increase erosion and sedimentation leading to smothering of rocky substrates and suitable cavities this species requires for cover. Loss of cover and refugia can lead to reduced carrying capacity and density of subpopulations, increased predation risk, reduced foraging success, and increased competition with non-native crayfish. Within the range of the big sandy crayfish, sources of sedimentation have been identified as active surface coal mining, commercial forestry, unpaved roads, gas and oil development, road construction, and stream modifications associated with channel instability (USFWS 2025b).

- A controlled experimental study in northern California found that increasing concentrations of deposited fine sediment in streams decreased growth and survival of juvenile steelhead trout, increased steelhead activity and injury rates, and shifted invertebrate communities to be dominated by burrowing species not suitable as forage for steelhead (Suttle et al. 2004).

- Some studies suggest that spawning bull trout abundance is negatively correlated with road density in the northwest United States (Rieman et al. 1997, Baxter et al. 1999). The USFWS 2024 Species Status Assessment for bull trout notes forest management practices as one of the threats to bull trout stream habitats. The USFWS states that “forest management activities, including timber harvest and road construction, can affect bull trout stream habitats by altering recruitment of large woody debris, erosion and sedimentation rates, runoff patterns, the magnitude of peak and low flows, water temperature, and annual water yield” (USFWS 2024). However, the magnitude and potential synergistic effect of forest management activities with other threats would vary across recovery units, core areas, and among local populations.

###### **Potential Adverse Effects of Timber Harvest**

The effects of activities associated with timber harvesting (such as tree felling, yarding, landings, site preparation by burning or scarification, fuels reduction, brush removal and whip felling, and forest regeneration) are often difficult to separate from the effects of roads and road construction. The road systems developed to harvest timber are often a significant factor affecting aquatic habitats, as discussed above. Some of the potential adverse effects described below may already be mitigated through land management plans, particularly standards and guidelines determined during the land management planning process to provide the ecological conditions necessary to maintain or restore ecological integrity and diversity. Therefore, in the short-term these potential adverse effects to aquatic species and habitats are less likely, but in the long-term these effects could be more likely to occur if there is an increase in timber harvest in the affected environment. In both the short term and long term, in compliance with land management plan requirements and through project-specific planning, future activities would be designed to avoid or minimize adverse impacts from timber harvest to biodiversity and in some cases could incorporate design features beneficial to aquatic animal species and habitats, however, complete avoidance of adverse effects may not be possible.

Some of the potential adverse effects to aquatic habitat from timber harvest could include the following (Chamberlin et al. 1991, Hicks et al. 1991, and Beschta et al. 1987 as cited in USDA FS 2000):

- Increasing erosion and sedimentation from timber harvest, which can promote excessive substrate movement and negatively impact fish species such as the bull trout by causing egg and juvenile mortality and reduced suitable habitat (USFWS 2024). Sedimentation can also negatively impact aquatic plant species and habitat complexity by reducing sunlight penetration due to increased water turbidity and via smothering by deposited sediment.

- Removal of riparian vegetation, which can increase stream temperatures beyond what is suitable for cold-water species. For example, warmer temperatures can reduce egg survival, rearing densities, and growth of coho salmon (Carter 2005).

- Habitat loss and fragmentation; for example, the Van Dyke’s salamander, an aquatic and terrestrial species with 65 percent of known populations on federal lands, is primarily threatened by timber management practices, road construction, and climate change (NatureServe 2025). One of the most significant threats noted for this species is timber harvest (particularly clear cutting) which can damage microhabitat and alter moisture regimes (NatureServe 2025).

###### **Potential Beneficial Effects of Road Construction, Road Reconstruction, and Timber Harvest**

Some of the potential beneficial effects of road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest on aquatic animal species and habitats could include:

- Enhanced access for some plant and wildlife management activities.

- Easier access for habitat restoration and enhancement for some species.

- Facilitation of movement and dispersal for some aquatic species through road reconstruction or realignment.

- Improved aquatic habitat quality through road reconstruction or realignment.

Some examples of potential beneficial effects include the following:

- Roads in need of repair deteriorate and discharge sediment into streams that could contaminate water, block stream flow, and harm aquatic fish species. Road reconstruction could improve water quality, restore aquatic organism passages, and maintain forest access through the implementation of the Forest Service Best Management Practices for Water Quality Management on National Forest System Lands (USDA 2012).

- Increased or improved access to reduce hazardous fuels in forested riparian areas which could cause wildfires resulting in the loss of trees and other overhanging vegetation along waterbodies. Vegetation in forested riparian areas provides cover habitat for aquatic animal species and improves bank stability.

_Terrestrial and Aquatic Plant Species and Habitats_

###### **Potential Adverse Effects from Road Construction, Road Reconstruction, and Timber Harvest**

Potential effects to plants from road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest would vary by ecosystem type, road design, and traffic volume. Some of the potential adverse effects described below may already be mitigated through land management plans, particularly standards and guidelines determined during the land management planning process to provide the ecological conditions necessary to maintain or restore ecological integrity and diversity. Therefore, in the short-term these potential adverse effects to plant species and habitats are less likely, but in the long-term these effects could be more likely to occur if there is an increase in road-related activities and timber harvest in the affected environment. In both the short term and long term, in compliance with land management plan requirements and through project-specific planning, future activities would be designed to avoid or minimize adverse impacts from road construction, road reconstruction, or timber harvest to biodiversity and in some cases could incorporate design features beneficial to plant species and habitats, however, complete avoidance of adverse effects may not be possible.

Some of the potential adverse effects to plant species and habitats from road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest could include:

- Increased level of habitat disturbance from human activity.

- Habitat fragmentation.

- Introduction of non-native invasive plant species.

- Collection or trampling of individual rare plants.

Some examples of these potential adverse effects on terrestrial and aquatic plant species include:

- Isolation or severely restricted subpopulation size due to habitat fragmentation could lead to lack of genetic interchange that can lower a plant species ability to adapt or respond to changing environmental conditions (USDA FS 2000).

- Road construction, routine roadside mowing, and the use of roads have been shown to facilitate the spread of multiple non-native invasive plant species such as yellow starthistle, scotch broom, knapweed, small broomrape, gorse, and cheatgrass (USDA FS 2000). Invasion of non-native species can alter habitat composition and act as competition for native plant species.

- Road construction can create abrupt habitat edges that modify light, temperature, moisture, and wind conditions, fragmenting suitable habitat for plant species sensitive or intolerant to edge effects and altered microclimate (Hulting et al. 2024).

- Road construction can involve vegetation clearing and earthwork that directly destroys plant species and alters habitat structure in the project vicinity. Further, disturbance to topsoil during road construction or timber harvest activities could reduce seedbanks and directly harm fungi or disrupt mycorrhizal networks. Mycorrhizal networks are a beneficial relationship between fungi and trees, where underground fungi in contact with tree roots help those trees access nutrients and water for growth in some forested ecosystems (Smith et al. 2014).

Discussions on the potential adverse effects of road construction and timber harvest to habitats in the Terrestrial Animal Species and Habitats and Aquatic Animal Species and Habitats sections above are also relevant to plant species.

###### **Potential Beneficial Effects of Road Construction, Road Reconstruction, and Timber Harvest**

Some of the potential beneficial effects of road construction and timber harvest on terrestrial and aquatic plant species could include:

- Enhanced access for some plant and wildlife management activities.

- Easier access for habitat restoration and enhancement for some species through stand manipulation.

- Creation of edge habitat and early successional habitat used by some species.

Some examples of potential beneficial effects include the following:

- Easier access to manage vegetation succession and woody encroachment in non-forested habitats to improve habitat for plants sensitive to shading and competition.

- Improved access to manage fuel loads to reduce the likelihood of stand-replacing wildfires and mortality to trees in old growth forests and other forested ecosystems which are not adapted to fire.

- The Arizona sneezeweed, a Regional Forester’s Sensitive Species, inhabits roadsides and clearings in ponderosa forests (NatureServe 2025).
