Other examples of RFSS that may inhabit roadsides or open edge habitats include the broad-tooth monkeyflower, butterfly milkweed, closed gentian, Cooke’s phacelia, and Cooper's milkvetch (NatureServe 2025). Road construction could result an increase of edge habitat suitable to these species; conversely road maintenance where these species already occur could pose a threat these species in some cases.

Discussions on the potential beneficial effects of road construction and timber harvest on habitats in the Terrestrial Animal Species and Habitats and Aquatic Animal Species and Habitats sections above are also relevant to plant species.

###### Effects of Alternative 3 – Modified Rule

Road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest could increase due to the removal of the prohibitions from a portion of inventoried roadless areas. Within the affected environment, timber harvest and related road construction are more likely in operable areas and where allowed by current land management plan direction (see the Alternative 3 summary in Alternative 2 – Proposed Rule).

In the areas where 2001 Roadless Rule prohibitions would no longer apply under alternative 3, the potential adverse and beneficial effects to species and habitats from road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest are similar to what is described under alternative 2, but generally to a lesser extent as the potentially affected environment would be reduced to 17.9 million acres outside of the Tongass National Forest (versus about 30 million acres under alternative 2). More specifically, the likelihood of potential adverse effects due to road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest including landscape fragmentation and loss of connectivity, habitat degradation, increased levels of human activities, and non-native invasive species introductions as described under alternative 2 would be reduced under alternative 3. Where prohibitions would remain in approximately 13 million acres of inventoried roadless areas under alternative 3, the potential adverse and beneficial effects to species and habitats from retaining the 2001 Roadless Rule are similar to current conditions and what is described under alternative 1, but to a lesser extent.

Under alternative 3, most adverse effects are possible but less likely relative to alternative 1 and alternative 2. The most notable difference in likelihood of effects between alternative 3 and alternative 2 is that the potential for fragmentation of large contiguous tracts of undisturbed areas currently within inventoried roadless areas would be less likely to occur, because prohibitions in current inventoried roadless areas would only be rescinded in relatively close proximity to existing roads and within WUI areas. The size of a protected area has been positively related to biodiversity (MacArthur and Wilson 1967 as cited in USDA FS 2000a; Gray et al. 2016). Large unfragmented areas generally contain more species, more species with large home ranges, and more species sensitive to human activity. These areas can provide refuge for species sensitive to disturbance because there is sufficient space for the species to move away from the area of disturbance to reduce stress or to shift to suitable interior habitat away from edge effects as the environment changes. Species richness and native biodiversity are more likely to be conserved in areas large enough to offer a shifting mosaic of habitat patches in various stages of recovery from disturbance (Noss and Cooperider 1994 as cited in USDA FS 2000a). However, under alternative 3 the total size of some of these large contiguous areas could still be reduced overall under alternative 3 if they overlap with the areas where prohibitions would be rescinded. Further, exceptions may still occur that allow for activities related to roads and timber in these core inventoried roadless areas which could lead to additional fragmentation, but that could also be the case under alternative 1 where the 2001 Roadless Rule would be fully retained.

Overall, alternative 3 would be less likely to have adverse effects to animal and plant species sensitive to disturbance, fragmentation, and loss of connectivity than alternative 2. In particular, the potential effects to species with large home ranges or large patch size requirements such as game species, fisheries, large carnivorous mammals, and migratory birds would be expected to be less under alternative 3 than alternative 2.

###### Conclusion

###### _All Alternatives_

Under alternatives 1, 2, and 3 any road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest could potentially have long-term adverse effects on biodiversity by increasing habitat fragmentation, loss of connectivity, negative edge effects, and human disturbance. These impacts would likely be more frequent and broader in scale under alternative 2. Impacts under alternative 3 would likely have a lesser effect on biodiversity than alternative 2, because the prohibitions on these activities would continue in some areas. Impacts to biodiversity would likely be the least under alternative 1, because current prohibitions allow limited exceptions for these activities.

Under all alternatives, biodiversity of plant and animal communities on NFS Lands, including inventoried roadless areas, would continue to be planned for and managed according to the requirements of law, regulation, and policy. All current Forest Service land management plans, through NFMA and planning regulations (1982 Planning Rule or 2012 Planning Rule), are required to maintain the diversity of plant and animal communities and support the persistence of native species in the plan area (39 CFR 219.9, 2012 Planning Rule; Sec. 219.19, 1982 Planning Rule). Site-specific road construction or forest management activities would be implemented in compliance with laws, regulations, and relevant agency policy which provide for a sustained yield, multiple use, and protection, improvement, and enhancement of the environment.

###### _Alternative 1 – No Action_

Under alternative 1 the potential beneficial effect to biodiversity is a greater likelihood, relative to alternative 2 or alternative 3, that large tracts of contiguous land within inventoried roadless areas would be maintained and remain relatively undisturbed by human activities. However, under alternative 1 the continued prohibitions on road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest could potentially have adverse effects on species and habitats due to very limited to no additional access created for species and habitat management activities related to woody encroachment, vegetation succession, fuels reduction, and insects and disease outbreaks. Ability to manage these areas could be less likely under alternative 1 than alternative 2 or alternative 3. Further, habitat restoration and enhancement in inventoried roadless areas could be less likely under alternative 1 than the action alternatives due to limited or no access to remote areas along with stand manipulation being limited to small diameter trees.

In the short term the current status of and trends in biodiversity are likely to continue under alternative 1 because no increase in timber harvest within the IRAs is expected. Forest management activity would remain infrequent and include incidental harvest of small diameter timber, where approved by regional foresters through exceptions (36 CFR 294.13(2001). The level of assumed forest management activity would continue at approximately the same as the recent levels. New road construction would be based on existing exceptions. Agency management records show that a lack of roads has not prevented fire prevention or protection measures, especially wildland fire use and prescribed fire; however, road restrictions would continue limit opportunities for mechanical thinning and fire control. In the long term, the potential adverse effects to biodiversity from limited management (with the exception of continued fire-suppression) would be more likely and could lead to habitat alteration or loss, species displacement, or direct mortality to plant and animal species. However, these potential adverse effects are applicable to specific conditions which may be less certain to occur relative to the potential adverse effects from an increase in road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest under alternatives 2 and 3.

_Alternative 2 – Proposed Action and Alternative 3 – Modified Rule_

Under the action alternatives the potential beneficial effects to biodiversity include enhanced access for species and habitat management, easier access for habitat restoration and enhancement for some species through stand manipulation, creation of edge habitat and early successional habitat used by some species, and easier access for hunting and wildlife viewing activities. Under these alternatives the potential adverse impacts to biodiversity from increasing road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest in potentially affected inventoried roadless areas generally include habitat loss and degradation, habitat fragmentation and loss of connectivity, barriers to movements, dispersal, and migration, human disturbance and interactions. The effects could have negative impacts on both terrestrial and aquatic habitats, and species across all taxa groups, including migratory species and game species.

In the short term, biodiversity is less likely to be impacted. Although the area where road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest would increase, these activities would be likely only where existing land management plans allow. Further, timber harvest would be most likely to occur where it is allowed by land management plans and where “operable”. Noncommercial timber activities would be most likely where “operable/operable but complex”. In the long term, biodiversity is more likely to be impacted by road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest which could occur in more areas of the affected environment over time if land management plans are revised or amended, though timber activities would still be unlikely to occur in the “not operable” areas. Refer to the Vegetation and Forest Management and National Forest System Roads sections for full discussion on conclusions, expectations, and assumptions related to these activities.

Some of these activities can be designed to avoid or minimize impacts to species and habitats, and in other cases, complete avoidance may be impractical. Through compliance with applicable laws, regulations, and policy, it is assumed that under all alternatives, future land management plan revisions or amendments would meet the species diversity requirements of NFMA and planning regulations to the extent feasible within Forest Service authority and consistent with the inherent capability of the plan area (36 CFR 219.9 and FSH 1909.12). Further, future land management plan revisions or amendments would require plan-level NEPA and plan-level ESA consultation. Project activities under current or future land management plans would need to be consistent with their land management plan and perform site-specific consultation as appropriate.

##### **ESA-Listed Species and Regional Forester Sensitive Species**

Section 7(a)(2) of the Endangered Species Act (ESA) requires Federal agencies to ensure any actions they authorize, fund, or carry out do not jeopardize listed species or adversely modify designated critical habitats. The proposed rule is a Federal action and must comply with the ESA. Draft biological assessments have been prepared to assess federally listed species and designated critical habitat under the Endangered Species Act.

In addition, under the current Forest Service direction for management for sensitive plants and animals, contained in FSM 2670, a Biological Evaluation is required by the Agency to analyze and document any potential effects of a proposed project, activity, or program to threatened and endangered species, species proposed for listing, and sensitive species. A Biological Evaluation has been prepared to assess reasonably foreseeable effects to Regional Forester Sensitive Species.

The analysis and findings presented in the draft biological assessments and biological evaluation are incorporated into this DEIS by reference and summarized in the sections that follow.  These draft analyses are available with the supporting documents on the project website.

This Draft EIS presents preliminary findings. Based on these findings, we anticipate initiating consultation under Section 7(a)(2) of the ESA and that the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) and National Marine Fisheries Service (NMFS) will issue a Biological Opinion. Future actions in inventoried roadless areas are subject to ESA consultation if it is determined these projects “may affect” ESA-listed resources.

Because no alternatives would compel the amendment or revision of any land management plan, analysis of species of conservation concern at this stage is not required. It is assumed that under all alternatives, future land management plan revisions or amendments shall meet the requirement for evaluating SCC under the 2012 Planning Rule (36 CFR 219.9(b), 36 CFR 219.13(b)(6), and FSH 1909.12). Similarly, Management Indicator Species<sup>57</sup> , an element of the 1982 Planning Rule, are identified and analyzed during land management planning, and monitored during plan implementation (36 CFR 219.19(a)(1982), FSM 2620.5, FSM 2634.1). It is assumed that under all alternatives, land management plans still operating under the 1982 Planning Rule were developed and shall be implemented consistently with the planning rule requirements for maintaining and improving habitat for Management Indicator Species (36 CFR 219.19(a)(1982)). All future activities must be consistent with land management plans.

The Magnuson–Stevens Fishery Conservation and Management Act (MSA) mandates that agencies initiate consultation with the National Marine Fisheries Service for any activities that could affect essential fish habitat (EFH). This consultation is completed for site-specific projects with ground-disturbing activity. Because no alternatives would compel or authorize site-specific ground disturbing activities, analysis of EFH at this stage is not required. It is assumed that under all alternatives any action that would be taken that could affect EFH would undergo project-level consultation.

> 57 Management Indicator Species: “plant and animal species, communities, or special habitats selected for emphasis in planning, and which are monitored during forest plan implementation in order to assess the effects of management activities on their populations and the populations of other species with similar habitat needs which they may represent” (FSM 2620.5)

Under the 2001 Roadless Rule, management actions that do not require the construction of new roads are still allowed, including activities such as timber harvest for clearly defined, limited purposes, development of valid claims of locatable minerals, grazing of livestock, and offhighway vehicle use where specifically permitted. Forest health treatments for the purpose of improving threatened, endangered, proposed, or sensitive species habitat or maintaining or restoring the characteristics of ecosystem composition and structure, such as reducing the risk of uncharacteristic wildfire effects, are allowed where access can be gained through existing roads or by equipment not requiring roads.

Inventoried roadless areas under the 2001 Roadless Rule serve as the baseline action area, though wilderness, wilderness study areas, and wild portions of wild and scenic rivers were removed from the analysis because these designations provide protections beyond those afforded by the 2001 Roadless Rule. The resulting potentially affected environment overlaps the range of more than 300 threatened, endangered, and proposed species; 79 final or proposed critical habitats managed by USFWS; and 19 critical habitats for species evolutionarily significant units or distinct population segments managed by NMFS and listed under the ESA. These analyses are detailed in the USFWS and NMFS Biological Assessments.

Over 3,500 species (plants, wildlife, and fungi) are listed as Regional Forester Sensitive Species on Forest Service-managed lands. Each Region of the Forest Service compiles its own list of sensitive species and periodically updates them. This analysis uses the most recent approved list from each region. For the purposes of sensitive species, the word “species” can refer to a species, a subspecies, a botanical variety, or a distinct population or population segment. These analyses are provided in the Biological Evaluation.

###### _ESA-Listed Species and Designated Critical Habitat_

Interagency cooperation pursuant to section 7(a)(2) of the Endangered Species Act is ongoing and will be completed prior to a final decision. Based on the preliminary findings of the draft biological assessments, the Forest Service expects a majority of ESA-listed species in the affected environment could potentially be exposed to road construction and timber activities under the proposed rule; however, the magnitude, intensity, and timing of activities is not precisely known at this time.

The pertinent subject matter experts reviewed the proposed rule and documented determinations for threatened, endangered, and/or proposed (TEP) and/or critical habitat in a biological assessment. To facilitate analyzing effects to a large number of species, each ESA-listed species was assigned to one of the following taxonomic groups: mammals, birds, reptiles, amphibians, plants, insects and arachnids, bivalves, crustaceans, or fish.

The potential exposure of ESA-listed species to activities following rescission of the 2001 Roadless Rule is not well understood because rescinding the rule does not compel any future activities in areas previously identified as inventoried roadless areas. However, because the 2001 Roadless Rule specifically prohibited road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest those are the activities that are being analyzed in the Biological Assessments. Exposure was determined by comparing species occurrence data with inventoried roadless area operability and categorized as high, moderate, and low likelihood of exposure. Exposure estimates were performed in a manner to err on the side of likely exposure. Existing protections afforded by land management plans and other documents/decisions were also reviewed to determine how they may affect the determinations for each species, particularly if effects to the species were determined to be highly impactful. The response of species was derived from published scientific literature and previous consultations. Together, exposure and response were used to make the determinations: No effect (NE), Not likely to adversely affect (NLAA), and likely to adversely affect (LAA). Where information is lacking the Forest Service erred on the side of making the more impactful determination.

Given the lack of information regarding future projects in previously inventoried roadless areas, most of the 300+ ESA-listed species would likely have some level of expected exposure. Similarly, the overlap of designated critical habitat with the operability analysis will inform the likely exposure of critical habitat to future activities associated with rescission of the 2001 Roadless Rule.

Under alternative 2 (the proposed rule), preliminary findings resulted in a “may affect, likely to adversely affect” determination for 327 species and 71 designated critical habitats, as listed in the Appendix 5 – Biodiversity Analysis Supplemental Information and in the USFWS and NMFS Biological Assessments. Additionally, the Biological Assessments contain the full evaluations for the ESA-listed species and designated critical habitat, and also lists the species with “no effect” or “may affect, not likely to adversely affect” determinations.

###### _Regional Forester’s Sensitive Species_

The Forest Service policy for sensitive species is to conserve sensitive species so that they do not become ESA-listed species and their habitats remain well distributed throughout their geographic range on NFS lands (FSM 2670.22). The pertinent subject matter experts reviewed the proposed rule and documented determinations for sensitive species in a biological evaluation.

The potential exposure of sensitive species to activities following rescission of the 2001 Roadless Rule is not well understood because rescinding the rule does not compel any future activities in areas previously identified as inventoried roadless areas. However, because the 2001 Roadless Rule specifically prohibited road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest those are the activities that are being analyzed in the Biological Evaluation. Exposure was determined by comparing species occurrence data with inventoried roadless area operability and categorized as high, moderate, and low likelihood of exposure. Exposure estimates were performed in a manner to err on the side of likely exposure. Existing protections afforded by land management plans were also considered to determine how they may affect the determinations for each species. Given the lack of information regarding future projects in previously inventoried roadless areas, most of the sensitive species will likely have some level of expected exposure. The habitat use of species was derived by comparing occurrences to the National Land Cover Dataset, and when occurrence data was not available by reviewing NatureServe species accounts or other published scientific literature. Threats to sensitive species related to road construction, road reconstruction, and timber harvest were summarized from NatureServe species accounts or other published scientific literature, if available. Where information is lacking the Forest Service erred on the side of making the more impactful determination.

Under alternative 2 (the proposed rule), preliminary findings resulted a “may effect individuals or habitat” (but will not result in a loss of species viability or a trend toward federal listing) determination for 3,490 sensitive species as summarized in the table below (note that some species occur in more than one region and may be counted more than once in the table). The Biological Evaluation contains the full sensitive species evaluation and lists the species with “no effect” determinations. No determinations of a loss of species viability or trend toward federal listing were made.

###### Conclusion

Under all alternatives, activities on NFS lands must comply with laws, regulations, and policies. All projects on NFS lands must be consistent with land management plans. All future activities in potentially affected IRAs, regardless of the alternative selected, would require compliance with the ESA prior to implementation. As is typical, future project designs in these areas would avoid and minimize adverse effects to ESA-listed resources. All future activities in potentially affected IRAs, regardless of the alternative selected, would require identification of sensitive species and project-level analysis of effects on viability of sensitive species in a Biological Evaluation, for administrative units with land management plans under the 1982 Planning Rule.

ESA-listed species and critical habitats, Regional Forester’s Sensitive Species, and Essential Fish Habitat were analyzed under Alternative 2. There were 327 ESA-listed species and 71 designated critical habitats for which a determination of “may affect, likely to adversely affect” (LAA) was reached for the proposed rule, as described further in the NMFS and USFWS Biological Assessments. For sensitive species, there were 3,490 species for which it was determined that the impact of Alternative 2 would be “may effect individuals or habitat”, as described further in the Biological Evaluation. Use of this determination indicates that Alternative 2 would not cause a trend towards federal listing under ESA, nor cause a loss of viability in each planning unit.

Under alternative 3, effects determinations to Regional Forester’s Sensitive Species potentially within the affected environment would be expected to be similar or reduced under alternative 3 relative to alternative 2, depending on the species’ range, with the total number of “May Effect Individuals or Habitat” determinations likely to decrease. Effects to ESA-listed species and critical habitats would be expected to be similar or reduced under alternative 3 relative to alternative 2, depending on the species’ range and critical habitat locations, with the total number of “may affect, likely to adversely affect” determinations likely to decrease.

#### Recreation and Scenery

##### **Background**

Federally managed recreation resources provide unique and important recreation opportunities (White et al. 2023). About 13 to 16 percent of individuals over age 6 in the United States engage in each of the most popular nature-based recreation activities: day hiking, camping, and fishing (White et al. 2023). Federally managed recreation resources provide unique and important recreation opportunities (White et al. 2023), and 39 percent of outdoor recreation participants reported recreating on national forest land at least once a year (Outdoor Foundation 2023). Among the six Federal agencies providing recreation opportunities, the USDA Forest Service receives the third-highest number of recreation visits each year, behind the National Park Service and the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers (Hanson et al. 2025). Thirty-nine percent of outdoor recreation participants reported recreating on national forest land at least once a year (Outdoor Foundation 2023). The agency’s National Visitor Use Monitoring program does not track separately the number of recreation visits to IRAs. However, the national forests that include current inventoried roadless areas receive about 125 million visits annually or 76 percent of the 164 million visits each year to all NFS lands.

Recreation on National Forest System lands encompasses more than just the activities themselves. Outdoor recreation is generally described in terms of several integrated aspects – recreation opportunities, access, use, and settings. Specifically, the 2012 Planning Rule defines sustainable recreation as, “The set of recreation settings and opportunities on the National Forest System that is ecologically, economically, and socially sustainable for present and future generations” and requires that each Land Management Plan “provide for sustainable recreation; including recreation settings, opportunities, and access; and scenic character” (36 CFR 219.10(b)(1)(i)).

Effects related to recreation and scenery include: (1) recreation opportunities and settings, (2) scenery, (3) developed facility and road-based recreation, (4) trail-based and dispersed recreation, (5) special uses and outfitters and guides, and (6) wilderness and other designated areas.

##### **Recreation Opportunities and Setting**

A recreation setting is the social, managerial, and physical attributes of a place that, when combined, provide a distinct set of recreation opportunities. These characteristics represent how recreation resources are considered and managed within an overall spectrum of settings – ranging from little to no development with emphasis on resource protection, to developed settings focusing on user comfort. For example, camping in a remote undeveloped area for selfreliance or, in contrast, reserving a site within a popular campground for social interaction and comfort facilities like toilets and tables.

The basis for describing the affected environment for recreation opportunities and settings is the Recreation Opportunity Spectrum (ROS) which consists of six setting classes: Primitive (P), Semi-Primitive Non-Motorized (SPNM), Semi-Primitive Motorized (SPM), Roaded Natural (RN), Rural (R), and Urban (U) (USDA Forest Service 2024b, USDA Forest Service 1982). The 2012 Planning Rule requires land management plans to use ROS to define and categorize existing recreation settings, as well as include desired conditions for sustainable recreation using mapped desired ROS classes. Beyond the 2012 Planning Rule, the National Forest Management Act of 1976 requires that land management plans coordinate all multiple uses, including outdoor recreation. For a specific example, timber harvest activities intended to regenerate stands must be designed to protect recreation opportunities.

Accessibility considerations tied to statutory and regulatory requirements and universal design<sup>58</sup> principles are applied to address usability, safety, and availability of programs and facilities across recreation settings and facility types at the planning and implementation levels. Sitespecific accessibility reviews, determinations, and documentation occur during project-level planning and design.

###### Potentially Affected Environment

Existing ROS settings were mapped across all alternatives. This mapping exercise was conducted specifically to provide a comprehensive data layer of existing ROS settings based on current roads and trails data. These mapped existing ROS settings represent a snapshot of current ROS settings and do not convey desired conditions found in land management plans that guide units as to what ROS settings should be maintained or achieved over the life of a land management plan. The first step in mapping ROS is to categorize travel routes (roads and trails). As would be expected of roadless areas, consistent with the roadless characteristics defined in the 2001 Roadless Rule, the majority (90 percent) of the potentially affected IRAs are either primitive, semi-primitive non-motorized, or semi-primitive motorized recreation settings. Of that, 33 percent primitive and 49 percent semi-primitive non-motorized.

Given the nationwide scale of the analysis, mapping existing ROS settings across all National Forest System lands is not intended to be exact.<sup>59</sup> The purpose is to provide as accurate as possible representation of existing recreation settings across the affected environment that are consistent with existing travel data (roads and trails) to aid in analyzing potential effects. The output reflects the existing condition of primitive, semi-primitive non-motorized, semi-primitive motorized, and roaded natural settings. Mapping rural and urban settings relies on site-specific data and local knowledge at a further refined scale. For the sake of this analysis, the roaded natural setting was not parsed into either rural or urban settings. This approach is consistent with

> 58 Universal Design: The design of products and environments to be usable by all people, to the greatest extent possible, without need for adaptation or specialized design. <u>https://www.section508.gov/develop/universal-design/</u>

> 59 Finer-scale inaccuracies may exist due to data discrepancies or inadvertent errors in light of an all National Forest System lands scale. The national existing ROS product used for this analysis was not verified with local knowledge and informed professional judgement. As with other resources, recreation settings at the land management plan and project level are considered based on local unit specific ROS mapping and pertinent plan direction within each land management plan.

the scale of analysis and employs a simplified, conservative approach with the affected environment leaning more toward the less developed end of the recreation opportunity spectrum.

###### Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action

The characteristics of the existing recreation settings are consistent with overall characteristics of roadless areas and would be maintained due to the restrictions or limitations that would continue within the affected environment area under the 2001 Roadless Rule. The 2001 Roadless Rule allows road construction in limited situations. The amount of change from such new permanent or temporary road construction or reconstruction is anticipated to have a negligible change on the current non-motorized settings. In those limited areas where permanent roads are constructed or reconstructed, the setting could change from non-motorized to motorized if the road is designated as available for public motorized access on motor vehicle use maps.

###### Effects of Alternative 2 _–_ Proposed Action

Under alternative 2, road construction and reconstruction are reasonably foreseeable. Where and when these activities occur, they could have short duration effects on recreation use and settings from increased on-site infrastructure and noise during site specific project implementation. Generally, timber harvest would not alter ROS settings after project implementation in the long term. Road construction has more potential to impact ROS settings in the long term. If newly constructed or reconstructed roads are designated for public use on motor vehicle use maps, existing ROS settings could change to a more developed setting class. It is assumed that any potential effects to ROS settings or designated public motorized access would be consistent with recreation-related land management plan direction and analyzed following the procedural requirements of the National Environmental Policy Act (see Common to All Alternatives).

Road construction and timber harvest and associated effects are more likely to occur where existing land management plans allow it and where the areas are ‘operable’ for mechanical vegetation treatments (see National Forest System Roads and Vegetation and Forest Management sections).

Potential changes under alternative 2 are more likely to affect those portions of the affected environment currently in roaded natural, semi-primitive motorized, and semi-primitive non- motorized. The likely operable and likely operable but complex portions of the affected environment (collectively referred to in this section as “operable area”) are primarily comprised of semi-primitive non-motorized ROS settings (nearly 9 million acres, or about 70 percent of the operable areas). The semi-primitive motorized and roaded natural settings of the operable area comprise 48 percent and 62 percent, respectively, of those two motorized settings within the affected environment as a whole. However, the combined motorized setting acreage within the operable area (3.4 million acres) represents 5.5 million acres less than just the semi-primitive non-motorized setting acreage within the operable area. Primitive ROS settings comprise a much smaller share (4 percent) of the operable area compared to the potentially affected environment as a whole.

Given that operability is based on the ability to reach and operate within a location proximate to roads and infrastructure, these ROS setting breakdowns within the operable area are reasonable. It is much less probable that primitive recreation settings would be impacted by road construction than any of the other three ROS settings. An average of about 52 percent of semiprimitive non-motorized, semi-primitive motorized, and roaded natural ROS settings within the alternative 2 affected area are more likely to be impacted. However, under alternative 2 there would be more opportunities for implementing management approaches in these three ROS settings which could stabilize or improve the biophysical characteristics of the settings and/or user experience.

###### Effects of Alternative 3 _–_ Modified Rule

The 13 million acres that would remain inventoried roadless areas would not see changes in their ROS settings, as described for alternative 1. The effects of road construction and timber harvest would generally be the same as alternative 2, however, the potentially affected inventoried roadless areas are smaller, so fewer acres of all recreation settings could be impacted overall. Most notably, about 12 million fewer non-motorized acres could be potentially impacted (about 5 million less primitive and almost 7 million less semi-primitive non-motorized acres).
