About 4 percent of primitive ROS settings within the potentially affected environment fall within operable areas, while about 55 percent of semi-primitive non-motorized, 52 percent of semi- primitive motorized, and 63 percent of roaded natural settings fall within either operability type. Of the two non-motorized ROS setting classes within the operable area of alternative 3 (7.3 million acres), about 31 percent falls within likely operable and about 69 percent within likely operable, but complex. However, a total of about 21 million acres makes up the two nonmotorized ROS setting classes within alternative 3 versus about 33 million acres within alternative 2. Of the two motorized recreation ROS setting classes within the operable area of alternative 3 (3.3 million acres), about 80 percent falls within likely operable and about 20 percent within likely operable, but complex. This represents both a similar total motorized ROS setting acreage and operability as alternative 2 (about 76 percent within likely operable and about 24 percent within likely operable, but complex).

Similar to alternative 2, these ROS setting breakdowns are expected and similar across the two alternatives for three of the four settings – semi-primitive non-motorized, semi-primitive motorized, and roaded natural. About 5.3 million fewer primitive acres are potentially impacted in alternative 3 (8 million acres) than alternative 2 (13.3 million acres). Both action alternatives potentially impact about 4 percent of the total primitive settings under each scenario.

##### **Scenery**

Scenery, the general appearance or features of a place or landscape, contributes to recreation settings and influences visitors’ recreation experiences and tourism. Scenic conditions vary by location and depend on natural features like geology, vegetation, landforms, water features, and human developments. Scenery contributes to a community’s sense of place and quality of life. People value scenery because it is integral to community character and recreation experiences. Research shows that high-quality scenery, especially scenery with natural-appearing landscapes, enhances people’s lives and benefits society (USDA 1995). Scenery and natural settings provide the backdrop for homes and communities, and inspire people to visit, recreate in, and live near national forests and grasslands. More people are choosing to live and recreate in scenic environments, as evidenced by population increases in areas known for their recreational and natural amenities (English, Froemke, and Hawkos 2014; Johnson and Stewart 2007). Highquality scenery and access to recreational opportunities provide social and economic benefits locally, regionally, and nationally.

Observing scenic views for pleasure is the second most common activity associated with recreational visits to national forests, and it is the third most common primary activity, according to National Visitor Use Monitoring (USDA 2024). People visiting national forests expect to see “a naturally appearing character” on NFS lands (USDA 1974). The Forest Service manages scenery for the benefit of present and future generations because scenery has benefits, outcomes, and ecosystem services related to communities, recreation settings, and tourism (economic benefits). Forest Service scenery management identifies scenic character and scenic integrity as key indicators of scenic quality. Scenic character is a combination of the physical, biological, and cultural images that give an area its scenic identity and contribute to its sense of place. Scenic character provides a frame of reference from which to determine scenic attractiveness and to measure scenic integrity. Scenic integrity, specifically allocated scenic integrity objectives in unit land management plans, are the minimum degree to which desired scenic character attributes are to remain intact (USDA 1995, USDA 2025). Scenery management follows guidelines from the Forest Service Manual 2380 and land management plans, which use standards called Visual Quality Objectives (VQO)<sup>60</sup> or Scenic Integrity Objectives (SIO) to decide how much a landscape can be changed. These systems help staff analyze and share how projects might affect the scenery, using methods from the Visual Management System and the updated Scenery Management System<sup>61</sup> . There are four nationally defined scenic integrity objectives (SIOs) that can serve as desired conditions (very high (unaltered), high (appears unaltered), moderate (slightly altered), and low (appears altered). A landscape where management activities are consistent with desired scenic character is rated as very high or high scenic integrity. Those landscapes where scenic character appears altered have lower scenic integrity. National forests and grasslands staff apply SIOs to any management activity that has the potential to affect the landscape’s scenic character and design projects to meet land management plan direction (SIOs) in the long term.

Multiple legislative acts have scenery related requirements. In statutes scenery may be referred to as aesthetic values, esthetic resources, environmental design arts, visual resources, scenic values, or other related terms. Scenery as a part of recreation is a multiple use of NFS lands (MultipleUse Sustained-Yield Act of 1960). National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) and National Forest Management Act (NFMA) require all Federal land management agencies to consider scenic resources during land management planning, resource planning, and project design, implementation, and monitoring. Scenery is often a nature and purpose (meaning a basic, inherent feature) for which designating legislation and management plans establish and identify designated areas (such as scenic byways, nationally designated trails, wild and scenic rivers, national monuments, and more). Scenery is also embedded in the 2012 Planning Rule) and policy (Forest Service Manual 1920, 2310, and 2380 and Forest Service Handbook 1909.12).

Most noticeable changes to scenic conditions across landscapes occur through natural processes such as wildfires, insect and disease, hurricanes or flooding. These natural disturbances shape the vegetation and landform features of the landscape, affecting how landscapes change over time and, consequently, how scenery changes over time. Therefore, Forest Service scenery management considers the dynamic nature of landscapes so that ecological processes and disturbance patterns are part of a landscapes’ scenic character and consistent with high or very high levels of scenic integrity.

60 For older unit land management plans that use the visual management system the land management plan allocated visual quality objectives and other current Plan direction would be used to manage scenery and determine plan compliance of any site-specific projects. Visual quality objective definitions differ from the scenic integrity objective definitions provided in this analysis and can be found in a unit’s land management plan. To describe and compare consequences, this analysis uses SMS terminology (scenic integrity objectives) for all alternatives.

61 The Scenery Management System (SMS) guides how scenery is managed for current and future generations. SMS helps the Forest Service record scenery conditions and set goals for how landscapes should look (SIOs). These goals are mapped and described as the "desired scenic character," which is the look the landscape should keep or develop over time. Staff use SIOs to make sure any project fits the landscape’s scenic goals. SIOs range from "Very High" (almost untouched) to "Very Low" (heavily altered, only used to describe current conditions, not as a target). Each level shows how much change is acceptable, from landscapes that look natural to those with visible management activities but still blend with the surroundings.

Management actions, such as timber harvest, fuels management, minerals and energy development, range, recreation facilities, transportation infrastructure (roads and trails), and special uses also affect the scenic integrity of a landscape to varying degrees. Management impacts to scenery are often mitigated through design criteria identified during project level analysis under NEPA to comply with land management plan components and NFMA.

###### Potentially Affected Environment

The 2001 Roadless Rule identified “natural appearing landscapes with high scenic quality” as a roadless characteristic that is often present in and characterizes IRAs. The existing scenic integrity of landscapes in the potentially affected IRAs is generally high or very high, which indicates a low level of landscape modification due to a lack of high intensity management activities. However, altered landscapes with lower levels of existing scenic integrity may exist in some areas due to activities such as mining, grazing, and special uses. Because these areas have been managed under 2001 Roadless Rule prohibitions resulting in very limited road construction and commercial timber harvest (36 CFR 294.11), it is assumed that since 2001, management activities in these areas have maintained and enhanced high/very high scenic integrity and desired scenic character focused on natural appearing landscapes with high scenic quality. Exceptions to 2001 Roadless Rule prohibitions have been limited (see 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule Implementation) and it is assumed that associated management activities have been designed to maintain roadless characteristics and to meet land management plan scenery desired conditions in the long term.

###### Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action

All resource management activities in inventoried roadless areas strive to achieve long-term sustainable desired scenic character within the scenic integrity objectives identified in the land management planning process.

Under this alternative and the current 2001 Roadless Rule, the scenic quality would remain substantially unaltered by future management activities. Inventoried roadless areas would continue to be managed for roadless characteristics including natural appearing landscapes with high scenic quality. Scenic character maintained and enhanced under this alternative would primarily be naturally evolving, substantially unaltered, and natural appearing and consistent with high to very high scenic integrity objectives.

Individual land management plans also have allocated scenic integrity objectives that likely align with maintaining scenic quality in IRAs (though may be less restrictive, especially for plans that have not been revised under the 2012 Planning Rule).

Activities currently occurring or permitted to occur would continue and have scenic effects based on proposed locations at the project level. Scenery management techniques and principles would be used to mitigate any future site-specific land altering activity or introduced elements on the land, to achieve and maintain desired scenic integrity objectives and desired scenic character identified in land management plans in the long term. Tree cutting within the provisions (generally small-diameter material for specific purposes) would have localized effects to scenic integrity for a few years. However, the amount and types of tree cutting allowed in inventoried roadless areas would often result in positive effects to scenery over time, such as a more naturalappearing mosaic of vegetation; variety in species, composition, age classes, and structure of vegetation; and a landscape that is more resilient to uncharacteristic disturbances. Over time, scenic integrity could be maintained or enhanced.

The 2001 Roadless Rule allows road construction in limited situations. It is anticipated that the amount of change from such new permanent or temporary road construction or reconstruction would have a negligible change on the current high and very high scenic integrity in most inventoried roadless areas. In those limited areas where permanent roads are constructed or reconstructed, the scenic integrity could change from high-to-moderate or low. Alternative 1 also has universal design implications and would maintain visual coherence and predictability according to allocated scenic integrity objectives in affected areas, which reinforces orientation and perceptual clarity for all visitors.

###### Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action

Under alternative 2, there are potential impacts to scenery as a result of increased timber harvest and road construction and reconstruction management activities, depending on the intensity and location of treatments. Future site-specific design criteria would be identified in project level analysis based on the desired scenic character within the scenic integrity objectives and other plan components (desired conditions, objectives, standards or guidelines) to ensure consistency with the land management plan. Areas currently managed for high or very high scenic integrity consistent with “natural appearing landscapes with high scenic quality” of inventoried roadless areas may have scenic integrity objectives ranging from high to moderate to low in land management plans.

Management activities affect scenic resources by altering the appearance of the natural landscape and can create beneficial, neutral, or adverse changes to the scenic character in both the short and long term. When management activities blend with the line, form, color, and texture of the desired scenic character, the scenery effects are considered neutral or beneficial. When management activities strongly contrast with the line, form, color, and texture of the desired scenic character, the scenery effects are considered adverse or negative (figure 19). Management activities are usually noticeable for a few years after project completion and defined for scenery as contrasts or deviations to the surrounding landscape’s desired scenic character. This analysis focuses on the potential effects of increased timber harvest including hazardous fuels reduction treatments and road related construction and reconstruction management activities because these types of activities may impact landscape attributes and have the potential to thereby impact scenic resources. The goal of site-specific scenery design criteria identified in project level analysis is to reduce the contrast management activities create so that they blend with the desired scenic character and thereby have neutral or beneficial effects for scenery over time and meet the scenery management direction found in a unit’s land management plan.

**Figure 17. These images show the difference in form and line from geometric shapes that dominate the scenic character (left) to shaping units to look more natural and irregular, reducing impacts to scenic character (right). These images are from “Forest Landscape Management, Volume 2, Number 5—Timber.”**

###### _Timber Harvest and Hazardous Fuels Reduction_

Timber harvest and hazardous fuels reduction would temporarily impact scenic resources. A landscape’s desired scenic character is closely related to the appearance of a landscape in which species composition and stand structure are within natural range of variation. Conditions within an ecosystem’s natural range of variation generally create scenery desired conditions that maintain a natural-appearing scenic character and an environment in which scenery attributes are sustainable and resilient to uncharacteristic disturbance in the long term. Therefore, management activities that would move the vegetation toward the desired scenic character and include scenery design criteria to meet the land management plan scenic integrity objectives are beneficial to scenery resources in the long term. These beneficial effects are often realized over a long period of time but lead to the lasting sustainability of valued scenery attributes.

Activities including tree removal, depending on the intensity of the treatment, can have varying consequences on scenery. Scenery effects of timber harvest are often the most noticeable for a few years after implementation until the growth of new grasses, shrubs, and remaining trees begin to soften the effects of removal operations. These effects are especially noticeable when the viewer has an up-close view of the harvest site usually in the foreground viewing distance which is up to ½-mile from the viewer. Viewers may have a longer duration view of these effects when traveling at slower speeds along travelways such as using a non-motorized trail or using a developed recreation site, campground or scenic overlook. Viewer’s traveling at faster speeds on travelways such as using motorized trails or driving along roads, have shorter duration views of these effects for a few years. When viewed from further distances (middleground and background which are 1/2 mile to 4 miles or beyond the viewer), changes to the forested texture from thinning or introduced shapes, forms, or edges of treatment units, may be noticeable for several years or more especially along vegetated hillsides or mountain slopes. When site specific scenery design criteria are used to blend the form, shape, texture and edge of timber harvest management activities with the scenic character, the management activities are less noticeable in both short and longer duration viewing by recreationists (figure 19). Project level analysis should define short- and long-term timeframes based on the specific activities proposed and those timeframes may vary for each project depending on the site-specific conditions such as the goals and ecological conditions of the project area and desired conditions to be achieved.

**Figure 18. These images show scenery effects when the viewer has an up-close view of a selective timber harvest. Immediately after harvest scenery effects of slash, stumps, and removed trees are noticeable (left). As time passes, vegetation growth softens these effects and the harvest becomes less noticeable, reducing impacts to scenic character (right). (USDA Forest Service photos by Korey Morgan)**

The scenery effects of tree removal can vary depending on the prescription and intensity of the treatment and amount of trees removed. Removal operations and activities which create young forest or openings (regeneration prescriptions) tend to be more noticeable in the landscape directly affecting scenery and views from high concern level travel routes and use areas. Generally, the effects of these types of prescriptions change the forested structure and texture, creating a more open landscape. If many trees are removed, the tree removal may be quite noticeable due to the textural change in the forest canopy and introduced shape and form of treatment units. These effects may be reduced if some trees are left in clumps and groups, or younger, smaller-diameter trees are present in the stand and protected during removal operations (figure 19).

Stumps, slash, and edge effects of freshly logged areas or units, depending on the intensity of the treatment, can result in a forest that appears moderately altered for a few years (figure 20). The initial contrast between harvested and unharvested areas is often quite noticeable. Shaping and blending treatment unit edges, using naturally occurring unit boundaries and terrain breaks, and applying other common scenery design criteria reduces the effects to scenery and promotes a natural-appearing landscape over time. When viewed from the foreground and middleground distances, the transition zone of unit edges used in common scenery design criteria would reduce noticeable edges between treated and non-treated areas. Tying proposed unit boundaries into existing meadows and openings and using variable density (feathering) along the edges can also reduce noticeable edge effects between treated and untreated areas (figure 21).

**Figure 19. These images show the difference between not feathering (left) and feathering (right) timber unit edges to blend with surrounding openings (U.S. Department of Agriculture 1974). These illustrations are adapted from “National Forest Landscape Management, Volume 1—General Concepts.”**

Hazardous fuels reduction activities could change the character of the landscape where activities occur for a few years like effects described for timber harvest. Over time, with scenery design criteria applied, these activities may provide for some preferred scenic settings, such as visual access into a forested stand, retaining large trees, greater vegetative diversity, and herbaceous ground cover (Gobster 1994) (figure 22). The removal of some trees, dependent on scale and intensity of treatment, may improve scenic character over time and make scenic attributes more resilient to natural disturbance such as insect and disease outbreaks. Vegetation conditions allowing fires to move through the landscape at the appropriate scale, frequency, and intensity without doing major damage and recovering relatively quickly from fire is beneficial for longterm scenic quality and scenic character resilience. Healthy and resilient forest environments that recover relatively quickly would maintain or improve scenic character which maintains sense of place attachment and opportunities to connect with nature.

**Figure 20. These images show a coniferous forest with ponderosa pine and lodgepole pine. Before examples show pre-thinning and existing scenic character with a wall of small conifer trees with little to no spacing between the trees (left). After examples show after thinning and desired scenic character with large trees, green grass, and an “open” forested stand (right).**

Effects described for timber activities are more likely to occur where allowed and generally allowed by current land management plan direction and could expand to more areas over time as plans change. In alternative 2, the effects of these potential management activities would be most likely in the operable areas identified in alternative 2.

###### _Roads Related Activities_

Potential road construction can impact scenic resources by creating evident contrasts in color and texture and changing landforms for cut and fill slopes. New road construction is often noticeable by exposing light-colored soils on the new road surface and any cut and fill slopes and by potentially creating a break in the tree canopy. Effects of road construction may be dominant in foreground, middleground, and even background distance zones depending on location and design. Roads also serve as the viewer platform, offering opportunities and access to view scenery. The somewhat modified setting of a viewer platform (road or trail) is accepted as a necessary component allowing travelers to experience the greater landscape (USDA 1995, page D-1). Project specific design criteria can reduce effects, so road construction meets scenic integrity objectives.

Construction of temporary roads can affect scenery by exposing light-colored soils and creating noticeable color contrasts which may be seen in foreground and middleground views. Generally temporary roads, once rehabilitated, revegetate quickly and would visually recover within a few years once rehabilitation activities are complete.

Decommissioning roads is typically beneficial to scenery resources by recontouring slopes to mimic natural landforms and rehabilitating and revegetating exposed soils typically noticeable on cut and fill slopes created during road construction. Road maintenance is beneficial for scenic resource viewing by providing access and a platform for viewing scenery from motorized vehicles.

###### Effects of Alternative 3 _–_ Modified Rule

In affected environment locations where alternative 3 removes roadless rule prohibitions, the effects would be the same as those described for alternative 2. However, the potential geographic scope where these effects could occur would be less than the geographic scope of alternative 2. In alternative 3, the effects of management activities described would be most likely in operable areas identified in alternative 3. This alternative also includes provisions to make needed modifications to the applicability of prohibitions within inventoried roadless boundaries. Where boundaries are modified that remove prohibitions, the effects would be the same as those described above.

In affected environment locations that continue current management, pursuant to the 2001 Roadless Rule in remaining IRAs (prohibit road construction, reconstruction, and timber harvest with limited exceptions defined in the 2001 Roadless Rule), the effects would be same as those described for alternative 1. In these areas under this alternative, scenic character maintained and enhanced would primarily be naturally evolving, substantially unaltered, and natural appearing and consistent with high to very high scenic integrity objectives.

##### **Developed Facility and Road-Based Recreation**

###### Affected Environment

Developed recreation sites on National Forest System lands serve as concentrated areas for public use, offering amenities such as restrooms, potable water, picnic tables, designated parking, and other facilities for user comfort. These sites are designed to accommodate a wide range of recreational activities while minimizing environmental impacts through managed infrastructure and visitor guidance. Developed sites<sup>62</sup> host a broad range of visitors, including people with disabilities, families, and others who benefit from accessible infrastructure and safe, reliable facilities, and they play a critical role in reducing dispersed impacts on sensitive ecosystems. By centralizing use and providing clear signage, administrative presence, and emergency access and egress, developed recreation areas help protect natural resources while enhancing visitor experience.

> 62 FSH 2309.19, Chapter 50, Section 50.5, Exhibit 01

The Recreation Site Development Scale outlines a graduated system for classifying recreation sites based on the degree of human modification and facility development. The development scales range from 0 (Undeveloped), where sites retain a natural character with minimal or no constructed features, to 5 (Highly Developed), where extensive infrastructure such as paved roads, utilities, and large visitor facilities dominate. Each level reflects increasing investment in access, amenities, and visitor capacity, while correspondingly reducing naturalness and the sense of a largely unaltered environment. This framework provides a consistent way to evaluate recreation settings, anticipate environmental impacts, and guide management decisions. The scales typically correlate with the ROS, where lower levels align with primitive or semiprimitive classes and higher levels correspond to roaded natural, rural, or urban classes.

There are 10,755 developed recreation sites (development scale 3-5) and assets located on National Forest System lands nationally. These provide the infrastructure necessary for the access and enjoyment of a wide variety of recreation activities. Developed recreation sites include, but are not limited to, campgrounds, recreation rentals, mountain resorts, and trailheads. The estimated cost of deferred maintenance for these sites is approximately $1.28 billion. There are approximately 500 developed recreation sites within the potentially affected IRAs. Most (70 percent) of these are campgrounds, but they also include lookouts/cabins, and trailheads. Over the last ten years, nationally, the Forest Service has constructed an average of 4 new developed recreation sites each year. Additionally, there are 20,061 development scale 0-2 recreation sites on NFS lands nationally that provide limited amenities and typically focus on resource protection over visitor comfort. The estimated cost of deferred maintenance for these sites is approximately $58 million<sup>63</sup> . Over the last ten years, nationally, the Forest Service has constructed an average of 6 new development scale 0-2 recreation sites each year.

Developed recreation sites require regular maintenance to keep them safe, clean, and in good condition. New developments, replacement/relocation of existing infrastructure, and some existing developed sites will need to be updated and modernized for usability and accessibility, or to meet visitor demand.

As a whole, people use federal lands and roads to access areas and sites that will provide some form of leisure opportunities such as recreation, hunting, wood cutting, and sight-seeing. In general, higher standard roads provide access to more developed recreation sites, such as campgrounds and resorts, and serve as primary routes for large segments of the visiting public. Access to less developed recreation sites and primitive areas, such as inventoried roadless areas and wilderness entry points, is often made via lower-standard roads or trailheads on the peripheries of unroaded areas. These roads also provide access for individuals using Other Power-Driven Mobility Devices (OPDMDs) on routes that are designated for motor vehicle use. For inventoried roadless areas, those trailheads can provide motorized trail access into the inventoried roadless area. Lower standard roads typically lead to more dispersed types of

> 63 Official Forest Service records, as found in the Natural Resource Management (NRM) database were used to provide information on NFS recreation sites. Database The total amount of deferred maintenance backlog for these sites is approximately $1.28 billion. records are dynamic; therefore, a snapshot of the recreation site data is used for analyses.

recreation such as primitive camping, fishing, hiking, caving, collecting forest products, and motorized trail recreation, also provide opportunity for driving for pleasure and viewing nature.

There are approximately 368,000 miles of road across the National Forest System. Not all road miles are designated for public motor vehicle use and, in many cases, off highway vehicles are limited to roads with lower development levels while higher development level roads are open to passenger cars. Approximately 65,000 miles, or close to 18 percent, are maintained for standard passenger cars; 200,000 miles, or 54 percent, of NFS roads, are maintained for high-clearance vehicles; and the remaining 103,000 miles, or 28 percent, are currently in storage (see Roads section) The Travel Management Rule at 36 CFR 212.51 establishes the process for designating roads, trails, and areas by vehicle class. Wheelchairs and mobility devices as defined in 36 CFR 212.1 and FSM 2353.05, including those that are battery-powered, may be used anywhere on NFS lands where foot travel is permitted. OPDMDs are managed according to vehicle class and may be used on roads, trails, and areas where that class of vehicle is designated for use as displayed on a Motor Vehicle Use Map.

###### Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action

With the exception of existing facilities and roads that pre-date the 2001 Roadless Rule, developed facilities and road-based recreation would remain concentrated in areas outside inventoried roadless areas. Future development, modernization, and increased visitor capacity would be focused in non-roadless areas, where higher-standard roads support amenities designed to serve a broad range of users through application of accessibility standards and universal design principles. As recreation demand continues to grow, these areas are expected to face increasing pressure to sustain infrastructure and services, amplifying the need to prioritize ongoing operation and maintenance. Deferred maintenance costs are expected to rise as aging facilities and roads require more intensive upkeep, especially in high-use areas. While trailheads provide limited access into inventoried roadless areas, the inability to build new roads or upgrade existing ones due to multiple factors including terrain, cost, and roadless designation restricts the potential for new developed sites in those areas.

Concentrating use within existing developed areas promotes consistency in layout, amenities, and surface and design conditions, which support usability and orientation for a wide range of visitors. However, limited expansion capacity may constrain opportunities to apply universal design principles that enhance accessible recreation experiences.

###### Effects of Alternative 2 _–_ Proposed Action

Under this alternative, developed and road-based recreation opportunities could expand into potentially affected IRAs, especially where road construction is allowed by land management plans. New construction of developed recreation sites would likely remain limited, as the recreation setting is more suitable for lower development facilities. It is likely that most new roads within 0.5 miles of existing roads would be temporary construction and not designated as open for public motor vehicle use (see Roads analysis). Permanent roads that are constructed would support a range of uses, with associated facilities likely designed to serve backcountry recreation needs such as low development scale trailheads, primitive campsites, or staging areas, rather than large-scale infrastructure like new campgrounds.

Existing developed recreation sites outside roadless areas would continue to operate and be maintained. As demand for recreation grows, these sites would experience increasing pressure to sustain services and amenities, especially where accessibility improvements or modifications are needed to meet applicable standards. Deferred maintenance costs would continue to accumulate, particularly in high-use areas where aging facilities and roads require more intensive upkeep to meet visitor expectations and safety requirements.

Increased flexibility for development under this alternative could provide opportunities to apply current accessibility standards and integrate universal design principles that improve usability, safety, and availability for all visitors into existing or new developed sites. Implementation would depend on project-level prioritization of accessible recreation opportunity development, site-specific conditions, and feasibility during planning and design.

###### Effects of Alternative 3 _–_ Modified Rule

Developed and road-based recreation would remain largely concentrated outside inventoried roadless areas, similar to alternative 1, but with more flexibility than the 2001 Roadless Rule because under alternative 3 there would be less designated IRAs. Areas that are removed from the inventoried roadless area designation (WUI and roaded roadless areas), could potentially support limited development, such as trailheads or primitive campsites, where terrain and resource conditions permit.

While new large-scale infrastructure development is likely to remain restricted, this alternative allows for modest expansion of developed recreation opportunities compared to alternative 1, but less than alternative 2. Similar to alternative 2, most new roads would be temporary, with limited public motor vehicle access.

Existing developed sites would continue to face pressure from growing recreation demand, requiring sustained investment in operations and maintenance. Deferred maintenance costs would remain a concern, especially in high-use areas.

##### **Trail-Based and Dispersed Recreation**

More than 164,000 miles of National Forest System trails and 7,500 trail bridges provide access for trail-based recreation across 193 million acres of National Forest System lands. Of these trails, approximately 19.5 percent (32,109 miles) are within designated wilderness areas, 39 percent (69,389 miles) are designated open to motorized use, and 41 percent (67,819 miles) are restricted to nonmotorized use outside of wilderness. Each trail in the system is assigned one or more managed use types: hiker/pedestrian, pack and saddle, bicycle, motorcycle, all-terrain vehicle, four-wheel drive vehicle, vehicles exceeding 50 Inches in width, cross-country ski, snowshoe, snowmobile, motorized watercraft, and nonmotorized watercraft. The trail system has increased by an average of 540 miles per year each of the past three years, largely due to road to trail conversions.

The trail system includes National Scenic and Historic Trails, which are congressionally designated per the National Trails System Act of 1968 (P.L. 90-543). NSHTs are long-distance, continuous trails that provide for maximum outdoor recreation potential and for the conservation and enjoyment of the nationally significant scenic, historic, natural, or cultural qualities of the areas. The Forest Service is the lead administering agency for five National Scenic Trails and one National Historic Trail, totaling 9,972 miles. The agency also manages portions of 11 other National Scenic and Historic Trails. These trails are to be managed to preserve the nature and purposes for which they were established and in accordance with their Comprehensive Plan. These trails are predominantly managed as non-motorized and with high scenic integrity objectives. Additionally, the National Trails System Act of 1968 authorized the Secretary of Agriculture to designate National Recreation Trails. Today, the Forest Service administers 538 National Recreation Trails.

National Forest System lands are a popular destination for those seeking dispersed recreation experiences. Dispersed recreation activities occur outside of developed recreation areas, including in wilderness areas, along roads and trails, and general forest and water areas not managed as developed sites (campgrounds, picnic areas, or visitor centers). Activities can be motorized or non-motorized and include, but are not limited to, hunting, fishing, boating, hiking, cross-country skiing, horseback riding, mountain biking, off-highway vehicle riding, snowmobiling, and dispersed camping. Dispersed areas generally have few or no facilities such as toilets, tables, or garbage bins. People who enjoy dispersed recreation activities opt for areas allowing dispersed use over developed sites because they prefer less human disturbance and onsite controls, and/or want to avoid paying a fee to recreate on public lands. Dispersed recreation occurs within all ROS settings; however, any infrastructure installed in these settings emphasizes resource protection over user comfort.

###### Affected Environment

Within potentially affected IRAs, there are 21,808 miles of trails, including 1,127 miles of national scenic and historic trails, and 981 miles of national recreation trails. The majority of these trail miles are managed for non-motorized use, such as hiking, biking, equestrian use, or skiing/snowshoeing. The potentially affected area includes about 5,200 miles of motorized trails (581 miles of which are snow trails) and 16,208 miles of non-motorized trails (274 miles of which are snow trails). Trails within affected roadless areas are accessed by roadside pull outs and trailheads. The vast majority of trailheads are located either on the periphery of roadless areas or at farther distances from the roadless area in places accessed via higher standard roads.

Wheelchairs and mobility devices as defined in 36 CFR 212.1 and FSM 2353.05, including those that are battery-powered, may be used anywhere on NFS lands where foot travel is permitted. OPDMDs are managed according to vehicle class and may be used on roads, trails, and areas where that class of vehicle is designated for use as displayed on a Motor Vehicle Use Map.

Dispersed recreation activities are typified in Forest Service inventoried roadless areas, where minimal infrastructure, limited human disturbance, and a focus on resource protection over user comfort align with the preferences of visitors seeking quiet, challenge, and low-impact outdoor experiences.

The no-action alternative would sustain the availability of primarily unroaded settings that provide quiet, remoteness and self-reliance. These settings provide nature-based activities such as hiking, backpacking, horseback riding, mountain biking, hunting, fishing, wildlife viewing, dispersed camping, and off-highway vehicle (OHV) use on designated routes. While minor shifts and displacement in recreation patterns may result from ongoing activities or those approved by exceptions under the 2001 Roadless Rule, the overall availability of inventoried roadless areas for dispersed recreation would remain high.

The lower-development and primitive settings of the no-action alternative would provide more unroaded recreation opportunities compared to the action alternatives.

###### Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action

Under this alternative, there will be some reduction in areas that provide quiet, remote settings as compared to alternative 1. New road development is reasonably foreseeable. Where these roads are permanent and open to public access, this could provide increased public access to dispersed recreation opportunities due to more roads, but may result in reduced availability of recreation opportunities in quiet, remote settings. This alternative would provide fewer quiet, remote settings than alternatives 1 or 3. As a result, users seeking a more natural setting are more likely to experience decreased availability of their preferred recreation setting or be displaced to other areas. Consequently, existing recreation uses in these areas could experience more use conflicts than Alternative 1 and 3. Alternatively, motorized recreation opportunities may increase more than alternatives 1 or 3 due to a potential increase in road miles. This alternative could provide increased opportunities for dispersed camping, hunting, and climbing access, and could potentially lead to additional trailhead development for increased trail access where new roads are developed. Site specific designs would reduce adverse impacts to comply with land management plans.

Alternative 2 is not expected to directly change the total number of motorized or non-motorized trail miles, however, the national trend of adding approximately 540 miles annually<sup>64</sup> suggests additional trail mileage may occur over time, subject to capacity and funding and some temporary roads may be developed as trails once management activities are concluded. An increase in trail miles could potentially increase trail-based recreation opportunities, particularly for individuals with mobility disabilities.

Active management activities may temporarily affect access to dispersed recreation areas and trails due to equipment use and safety closures. Visual and noise impacts may also affect the quality of the recreation experience. These impacts are expected to be short term. This alternative would provide more short-term impacts to trail based and dispersed recreation visitors than alternatives 1 and 3.

Alternative 2 has the highest potential for active management activities and is most likely to result in negative effects to 920 miles of National Recreation Trail and 1,075 miles of National

> 64 Data derived from the Forest Service Enterprise Data Warehouse annual updates and averaged over time.

Scenic and Historic Trails within potentially affected IRAs. Active management could result in temporary closures to public access, short-term visual and noise impacts resulting from active management activities, and long-term visual and noise impacts from additional miles of permanent roads crossing or near NSHTs. These activities and impacts would be more likely to occur in areas that are potentially operable for timber harvest. Of the NSHTs within the Alternative 2 analysis area, 268 miles are in areas considered operable and 316 miles are in areas that identified as operable but complex for active management activities.

Increased flexibility for trail and dispersed recreation management under this alternative could allow for application of current accessibility standards and universal design principles in new or reconstructed trailheads or trails, access routes, and constructed features, improving usability and safety for a wide range of visitors where conditions permit. Expanded road and trail networks may also create additional opportunities for use of Other Power-Driven Mobility Devices (OPDMDs) where consistent with resource protection and visitor safety. However, temporary closures, shifting recreation settings, or changes in allowable uses could reduce predictability and limit accessible recreation opportunities until project-level design and designation decisions are implemented.

Overall, this alternative may increase opportunities for road-based recreation and increase availability of accessible infrastructure and amenities compared to alternatives 1 and 3.

###### Effects of Alternative 3 _–_ Modified Rule

Under alternative 3, there would be some reduction in areas that provide quiet, remote settings as compared to the no-action alternative, but this alternative would preserve more of those settings than alternative 2. Active management activities could result in increased permanent road development in some areas. This could provide increased public access to dispersed recreation opportunities due to more roads but may result in reduced availability of recreation opportunities in quiet, remote settings. Consequently, existing uses in the area could experience an increase in use conflicts over alternative 1 but less than Alternative 2. This alternative would provide for more quiet, remote settings than alternative 2. As a result, users seeking a more natural setting may experience decreased availability of their preferred recreation setting or be displaced to other areas compared to alternative 1. This alternative may provide more recreation opportunities than alternative 1 for motorized recreation due to an expected increase in road miles. Compared to alternative 1, this alternative would provide increased opportunities for dispersed camping, hunting, and climbing access, and could potentially lead to additional trailhead development for increased trail access.

This alternative would provide more short-term impacts to trail based and dispersed recreation visitors than alternative 1 but less than alternative 2.

Because this alternative would retain some roadless designations, it has lower potential to result in negative effects to NSHTs than alternative 2. There is a total of 680 miles of NHST’s affected by alternative 3, 28 miles of which are designated open to motorized use. Active management could result in temporary closures to public access, short-term visual and noise impacts resulting from active management activities, and long-term visual and noise impacts from additional miles of permanent roads crossing or near NSHTs. These activities and impacts would be more likely to occur in areas that are potentially operable for timber harvest. Of the NSHTs within the analysis area, 268 miles are considered operable and 122 miles are operable but complex for active management activities.

Overall, this alternative may increase opportunities for road-based recreation and increase availability of accessible infrastructure and amenities compared to alternative 1 and less than alternative 2.

##### **Recreation Special Uses**

Frequently, visitors to national forests turn to others to facilitate their recreation experience. Recreation special use authorizations, such as permits, are employed by Forest Service managers to allow others to provide desired recreation services. The agency has over 24,000 active Recreation Special Use (Rec SU) permits that vary in complexity and size, from which the agency collected approximately $150 million in fee revenue in fiscal year 2024. Recreation special use permits are issued to a variety of entities including governmental, for profit, and nonprofit. Permit holders are authorized to operate in specific locations, which can be as broad as an entire forest or as narrow as a specific campground. The types of uses include fixed footprint uses with developed sites and facilities such as ski areas, campgrounds, organizational camps, marinas, recreation residences, and resorts. Ski areas sometimes have large areas within their permit boundary that are unroaded and provide for similar recreational opportunities within their footprint. Permits can also be used for dispersed uses without developed sites and facilities such as outfitting and guiding services recreation events, and noncommercial group uses. Outfitters and guides for activities such as sightseeing, hunting, fishing, and rafting often provide recreation opportunities authorized by special use permits in semi-primitive areas, outside of wilderness which has less restrictions. These areas often falls within inventoried roadless areas. Recreation special use permitholders must ensure equal access to publicly available facilities, services, programs, and activities, such as websites, rentals, ski lessons, and restaurants, for individuals with disabilities.

###### Affected Environment

_Fixed Footprint Recreation Special Uses with developed sites and facilities such as ski areas, campgrounds, organizational camps, marinas, recreation residences, and resorts_

Ski Areas are identified in this analysis because they are a unique commercial use and occupancy of Forest Service lands. Downhill skiing, primarily done at permitted ski resorts, is the second most popular recreation activity on Forest Service lands (USDA Forest Service 2023c). Demand exceeds mountain resort capacity in many locations and the corresponding Recreation Opportunity Spectrum classification in the applicable land management plan tends to be “rural,” or “urban.” Other fixed footprint recreation special uses such as campgrounds, resorts, marinas and recreation residences have generally much smaller footprints, less potential for overlap with inventoried roadless areas and less frequent expansions into inventoried roadless areas. So, for the purpose of this analysis, ski areas will serve as the proxy for other fixed footprint recreation special uses which may be affected by changes in inventoried roadless areas but to a lesser extent.

Often some of the fixed footprint developed sites such as ski areas and resorts are overlapping or adjacent to inventoried roadless. There are approximately 70 developed sites, other than ski areas such as resorts and campgrounds, in inventoried roadless areas that are operated by permittees.

The Forest Service administers 127 ski area special use permits in the United States, which attracted approximately 26 million visitors to the National Forest landscape in FY2024. Fifteen of the 127 ski areas are in or adjacent to inventoried roadless areas. Through special uses permitting, ski area operations and developments are limited to the assigned ski resort boundary (2709.14_61.1). As ski area operations and developments respond to increases in recreation demand, the ski areas regularly submit proposals to change or expand ski area boundaries. Additionally, the Forest Service works with ski resort entities to develop Master Development Plans (MDPs) (FSH2709.11_52.3 Clause C-7 Master Development Plan) with complementing environmental analysis to make transparent plans for growth, development, and mitigations within the ski resort permit boundary. Effects of new developments, such as a new chair lift, are identified in the MDP and analyzed through project-level environmental analysis as they may require ground disturbance and tree removal. These types of developments are legacy installations that will last decades or longer. Some developments can be adjusted to minimize environmental disturbance. On the other hand, developments such as chair lifts must be installed using precise engineering specifications.

_Dispersed Recreation Special Uses without developed sites and facilities such as outfitting and guiding services, recreation events and non-commercial group uses._

Outfitters and guides for dispersed recreation activities such as sightseeing, hunting, fishing, and rafting, provide most recreation opportunities authorized by special use permits in roadless areas. Outfitting and guiding activities in inventoried roadless areas usually provide recreation opportunities for an unconfined type of outdoor recreation experience, free of the urban influence. Other common dispersed activities such as recreation events (trail running and mountain biking events) and non-commercial group uses are less likely to be affected because of their smaller scope and scale and often times they have the ability to change locations if conditions change or do not meet their needs. Long-distance events covering a hundred miles or more may be an exception, as they are more likely to encounter roadless areas and may find it difficult to choose another route.

###### Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action

A wide variety of recreation special uses, outfitting- and guiding-assisted dispersed recreation opportunities would continue, balanced with other key values such as, maintenance of high ecosystem and scenic integrity, clean water, wildlife viability and biodiversity, landscape character, research opportunities, traditional cultural properties, and sacred sites.

For the fifteen ski areas that are within or adjacent to inventoried roadless areas, it may be difficult for ski area expansion or new construction because of greater regulatory jurisdictions, complex procedures, and heightened public scrutiny. Impacts on categories (other than ski areas) of developed recreation special use permit holders would be minimal from a national perspective.

Proposed ski area, resort, or other recreation development expansion into inventoried roadless areas would be allowed to continue under existing Forest Service procedures if special use permits were in existence prior to 2001 and proposed activities take place within boundaries established by the special use permit before implementation of the 2001 Roadless Rule.

The no-action alternative would maintain existing levels of access to guided opportunities for dispersed recreation and no expansion of related accessible recreation opportunities where the permit boundaries at ski areas and resorts do not already encompass inventoried roadless areas.

###### Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action

Under alternative 2, there could be an increase in road construction, timber harvesting, and other resource management activities in areas that are currently inventoried roadless areas (where land management plan prescriptions allow it). This potential increase of management activities could reduce the supply of unroaded or backcountry areas available for outfitter and guide assisted dispersed recreation opportunities. Short-term shifts in guided recreation use may occur because of timber harvesting allowed in the proposed action. For instance, most outfitters and guides prefer natural-appearing landscapes, so cutover areas could be avoided until they grow back; or, on the other hand, timber harvested areas may attract use because of increased hunting, wildlife viewing, or berry picking opportunities.

Some road-based guided activities could potentially shift into the current roadless areas if roads or motorized trails are developed through management activities as a means to offer additional locations or opportunities to provide recreation services. Alternative 2 could introduce a new opportunity (such as building a new road in a resort complex or guiding activities on a newly created system road) that a permitee could propose commercial use for. However, evaluating permit requests through Agency screening criteria, permitting process, site-specific environmental review, where applicable, and land management plan requirements would still apply.

Under this alternative, projects associated with campgrounds, resorts, ski areas, or other entities that hold special use authorizations would proceed with planning under existing Forest Service procedures.

Expanded opportunities for activity within permit boundaries may create new opportunities for accessible participation, while increased development or terrain modification could trigger additional analysis, accessibility review, and documentation requirements consistent with permit terms and conditions.

###### Effects of Alternative 3 _–_ Modified Rule

This alternative would have similar effects as alternative 2 in the areas that would have a change in policy for the management. However, the scope would be reduced due to the reduced acreage that could be impacted. The areas that are not near roads or in WUI and would not have a change in policy for management from the existing 2001 Roadless Rule, would have similar effects as alternative 1.

Sixty-one of the 70 developed sites that are identified in the NRM database as operated by permitees would be in the affected area. This would allow these developed sites to potentially increase their development and operational footprint. The areas outside of the affected environment in this alternative, which would remain designated as IRAs, would allow the dispersed service providers, such as outfitters and guides, to operate in the backcountry, natural appearing landscapes that are desired by their clients.

##### **Wilderness and Other Designated Areas**

Congressional and administrative designations cover a large portion of the National Forest System, with each designation type directing the agency to manage the area for a particular purpose. The National Wilderness Preservation System, for example, encompasses nearly 112 million acres across the United States, with about 37 million acres located on National Forest System lands. These areas, established under the 1964 Wilderness Act, are meant to remain unimpaired for future generations, preserving qualities such as naturalness, solitude, and freedom from human control. Wilderness areas offer opportunities for primitive recreation and scientific study, while also serving as important ecological reserves. Although Congress alone can designate new wilderness, the Forest Service identifies recommended wilderness areas in its land management plans, maintaining their character until Congress acts. Roadless areas often lie adjacent to wilderness, providing transitional landscapes that blend natural processes with recreation opportunities not permitted in designated wilderness, such as certain motorized activities. Together, these lands receive millions of visits annually, reflecting their importance to the public.

Beyond wilderness, the Forest Service manages a wide array of other designated areas, each established to protect unique values such as scenic, geological, cultural, or recreational. These include national monuments, national recreation areas, wild and scenic rivers, and national scenic areas, among other. Management varies depending on whether the designation is prescriptive, with strict rules written into law, or descriptive, allowing more agency discretion. Regardless, the guiding principle is to emphasize the primary values for which the area was established while ensuring other uses remain compatible. Many of these areas are showcases for resource management and public recreation, though balancing recreation with protection of special attributes is often a challenge, particularly in places like scenic areas, recreation areas, and monuments where developed facilities are common.

###### Affected Environment

Inventoried roadless areas that overlap with other congressional or administrative designations are managed to meet all applicable requirements for both IRAs and the other designation.

As noted in Potentially Affected Environment, over 4 million acres designated as IRA managed under the 2001 Roadless Rule within wilderness, wilderness study areas, and wild and scenic river corridor segments classified as wild were removed from the potentially affected environment for this analysis but are displayed here because they are within IRAs designated under the 2001 Roadless Rule.
