###### _Wilderness_

The National Wilderness Preservation System includes approximately 111.9 million acres; of these, approximately 36.7 million acres are on National Forest System lands. Congressionally designated wilderness areas are required under Section 2(a) of the 1964 Wilderness Act “to be administered for the use and enjoyment of the American people in such manner as will leave them unimpaired for future use and enjoyment as wilderness, and so as to provide for the protection of these areas, the preservation of their wilderness character.” “Wilderness character” has been interpreted by the Forest Service and other federal wilderness administering agencies to include the following qualities: (1) “untrammeled” or free from intentional human control; (2) “natural;” (3) “undeveloped;” (4) “outstanding opportunities for solitude or a primitive and unconfined type of recreation;” and (5) “may also contain ecological, geological, or other features of scientific, educational, scenic, or historical value” (Landres et al. 2015). Congress has the sole authority for designating additions to the National Wilderness Preservation System. Congressionally designated wilderness within the National Forest System offers many similar recreation opportunities as roadless areas (except for motorized and mechanized trail activities) and receive about 13 million visits each year or 8 percent of the visits to all lands in national forests. Approximately 1.3 million acres of inventoried roadless area managed under the 2001 Roadless Rule (2.8 percent) are within congressionally-designated wilderness. Further, 2.9 million acres of IRAs under the 2001 Roadless Rule are within statutorily designated Wilderness Study Areas.

Recommended wilderness areas are administratively identified in Forest Service land management plans through an inventory and evaluation process (36 CFR 219.7(c)(2); FSH 1909.12, 70) and have plan components to maintain the characteristics that provide the basis for the agency’s wilderness recommendation. Congress may consider these recommendations for future additions to the National Wilderness Preservation System. There are 1.5 million acres (3.7 percent) of the affected environment that are managed as recommended wilderness.

In addition, a substantial portion of inventoried roadless areas are near or adjacent to wilderness areas. Inventoried roadless areas near or adjacent to wilderness serve as natural transitions between land with road-based resource managed activities and lands affected substantially by natural processes. Some of the key characteristics of inventoried roadless areas lie in their unique Primitive, Semi-Primitive Non-Motorized, and Semi-Primitive Motorized recreation opportunities (see Recreation Opportunities and Setting and; Trail-Based and Dispersed Recreation).These areas provide popular, appropriate alternatives to wilderness areas because, although they contain many attributes similar to wilderness, a wider range of recreation opportunities with fewer restrictions is available.

Wheelchairs and mobility devices as defined in 36 CFR 212.1 and FSM 2353.05, including those that are battery-powered, may be used anywhere on NFS lands where foot travel is permitted, including in designated wilderness areas.

###### _Other Designated Areas_

Certain areas of National Forest System lands (not designated as wilderness) that contain outstanding examples of plant and animal communities, geological features, scenic grandeur, or other special attributes merit special management. These areas are designated by law or may be designated administratively by executive order or action through Agency planning efforts as special areas. Areas so designated are managed to emphasize specific values identified in their enabling legislation or order such as recreation, geology, or history. Other uses are permitted in the areas to the extent that these uses are in harmony with the purpose for which the area was designated. Inventoried roadless areas managed under the 2001 Roadless Rule include congressionally and administratively designated areas such as National Recreation Areas, National Scenic Areas, and National Primitive Areas; Scenic Areas, and Recreation Areas; Game Refuges and Wildlife Preserves; National Monuments and Volcanic Monuments; Wild and Scenic Rivers; Wilderness Study Areas; Research Natural Areas; National Scenic and Historic Trail corridors; and others (Table 43).

Specific to designated wild and scenic rivers, 85,000 acres of inventoried roadless areas managed under the 2001 Roadless Rule are within designated river corridors classified as ‘wild’ segments, generally accessed only by trail. Nearly 83,000 additional acres are within river corridors classified as scenic or recreational. An additional 551,900 acres of inventoried roadless areas on NFS lands are within a quarter mile of river segments identified as eligible or suitable for future wild and scenic river designation.

The law, proclamation, order, or decision designating each area provides specific objectives and guidelines for management of the area. Some designations are prescriptive with management details written into law. Other designations are more descriptive, providing the Forest Service with more management discretion. Despite these differences, the Agency’s policies that guide management of most designated areas have some similarities. The Forest Service manages each designated area as an integral part of NFS lands with emphasis on the primary values and resources as directed by the law or order that established the area. Then the Forest Service manages values or resources not emphasized or prohibited by the establishing law or order in a manner that is complementary or enhances the primary values of the area and are compatible with overall NFS land management objectives. Further, the land management planning process requires certain steps to be taken during plan revision to inventory and evaluate areas for future designation and assign interim management protections to those areas identified, such as for recommended wilderness and wild and scenic river eligibility. This plan revision process also provides for the establishment of new administratively designated areas such as botanical areas, Forest Service recreation areas, historical areas, and paleontological areas, among others. Lastly, designated areas are managed as showcases to demonstrate resource management programs, services, and facilities.

With the exception of National Game Refuges and Wildlife Preserves, and Research Natural Areas, one of the objectives for special management involved providing for public outdoor recreation opportunities. However, the special values (that is, scenic, cultural, historic, wildlife, geologic, or other values) and attributes must be protected. Other resource values in the area are managed to enhance public recreation values or the special attributes of the area.

This situation is generally most acute in National Scenic Areas, National Recreation Areas, National Wild and Scenic Rivers classified as scenic or recreational, National Monuments, and National Volcanic Monuments because these areas are more likely than other categories of special designated areas to feature developed recreation.

Designated areas are characterized by the special values, attributes, or unique features for which they were established. As a result, the effects of the rulemaking on these areas will vary depending on each area’s management emphasis.

###### Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action

Under alternative 1, inventoried roadless areas in and around designated wilderness would continue to be managed under the 2001 Roadless Rule. Human use would continue to increase in inventoried roadless areas. Patterns of recreation and other uses would be managed to maintain or enhance roadless characteristics. Large tracts of land where natural processes occur uninterrupted would be maintained. Threat to wilderness character would be low overall. No short-term disturbances from commercial timber harvesting or long-term benefits from timber harvesting for stewardship purposes would accrue, as there would continue to be limited or infrequent tree cutting and limited road construction only allowed by the current exceptions under the 2001 Roadless Rule and when consistent with any other applicable designation’s management requirements. Other ongoing activities would continue in 2001 Rule IRAs.

Maintaining existing inventoried roadless areas under the 2001 Roadless Rule that are adjacent to designated wilderness would likely maintain existing levels of wilderness character protection. Inventoried roadless areas adjacent to or near wilderness areas can be more easily accessed by visitors than designated wilderness and provide an alternative location for certain recreation activities in unroaded areas, thereby relieving some recreational use pressure on designated wilderness. Any additional distance between wilderness areas and intense management activities also provides greater opportunities for natural processes to occur uninterrupted.

This alternative would continue to provide less flexibility than alternative 2 to local land managers of other designated areas to determine the long-term disposition of unroaded lands to meet developed and dispersed recreation needs within the context of the law, proclamation, order, or decision that established the area. Over the long term, this alternative would maintain the highest relative supply of lands with dispersed recreation potential in designated areas. Among all other designated areas, the availability of roadless areas for forest visitors seeking Primitive, Semi-Primitive Non-Motorized, and Semi-Primitive Motorized recreation opportunities would be highest in Wilderness Study Areas, National Recreation Areas, and National Wild and Scenic River corridors because they have the greatest number of inventoried roadless acres. Therefore, this alternative would provide more opportunities than in alternative 2 for resolving issues between motorized and non-motorized dispersed recreation activities.

###### Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action

Under alternative 2 the national prohibitions would no longer be applied to inventoried roadless areas. The proposed rulemaking would not change or affect any of the other designated area status of these lands, and where these designations occur, the lands would continue to be managed according to the requirements of the relevant laws, policies, or land management plan requirements.

Some designated areas would continue to prohibit or limit road construction and reconstruction or timber harvest. For example, these activities are not allowed in congressionally-designated wilderness, WSAs, and wild and scenic rivers segments classified as wild. Based on existing designations and land management plans, currently, road construction and timber harvest are generally not allowed in 55 percent and 66 percent of the potentially affected IRAs. Site-specific projects would be evaluated for consistency with applicable designated area plans, land management plans, and the relevant laws, regulations and policies for these areas.

Over time, if permanent roads are developed within IRAs, the supply of inventoried roadless areas available would decrease resulting in more developed recreation settings, increased resource management activity, and reductions in the size of lands available for uninterrupted natural processes. This trend of shifting human patterns, increased resource management activity, and reduced ecological integrity near designated wilderness could increase the threat to the qualities of wilderness character of these areas.

This alternative would provide slightly more flexibility to local land managers of designated areas to determine how to meet developed and dispersed recreation needs within the context of the law, proclamation, order, or decision that established the areas and consistent with the land management plan. Over time, the land management plan components could be amended or revised to permit road construction or timber harvest in these areas. However, any amendment or revisions would need to be consistent with the statute, proclamation, or order designating the area. These remaining stipulations, for example in the case of designated wild and scenic river corridors classified as scenic or recreational, may not include outright prohibitions but instead allow for activities that are consistent with the statutorily required comprehensive river management plan and satisfied management objectives that protect and/or enhance identified river values.

Road construction, timber harvesting, and other resource management activities are reasonably foreseeable in inventoried roadless area and would be most likely to occur where current land management plans allow it and where the areas are operable for mechanized equipment (see Roads and Timber sections). Existing recreation settings could become more developed settings over time depending on activity location, design, and implementation. Because about 6 million more acres of existing non-motorized recreation settings (located within either operability category) could be impacted, this difference suggests that it might be more probable for nonmotorized settings to be impacted in the short or long term from management activities. However, it is more likely for existing motorized settings to be impacted simply given that operability is based on the ability to reach and operate within a location proximate to existing roads and infrastructure.

Alternative 2 would have the least effect on the management of National Game Refuges/ Wildlife Preserves, National Scenic Research Areas, and National Volcanic Monuments categories of other designated areas. Nationally, these categories have the fewest acres of inventoried roadless area.

This alternative would not change management of areas where road construction or timber harvest is otherwise prohibited by land management plans. However, land management plans could be revised in the future to permit road construction or timber harvest, unless otherwise stipulated by the statute, proclamation, order, or decision designating the area. For example in the case of designated wild and scenic river corridors classified as scenic or recreational, land management plans may not include outright prohibitions, but instead allow for activities that are consistent with the statutorily required comprehensive river management plan and satisfied management objectives that protect and/or enhance identified river values.

With regard to accessibility and universal design implications, greater management flexibility could allow for selective improvements to access routes or points of entry adjacent to or within designated areas where consistent with land management plans. Such changes may improve usability and safety but could alter landscape conditions that support low-development or low- stimulus experiences. Evaluation of site-specific accessibility considerations would occur during project planning and implementation.

###### Effects of Alternative 3 _–_ Modified Rule

In affected environment locations where alternative 3 removes roadless rule prohibitions that are within ½ mile of existing roads or within WUI, and on 9.3 million acres on the Tongass National Forest, the effects would be the same as those described for alternative 2. However, the potential geographic scope where these effects could occur would be less than the geographic scope of alternative 2. Specifically, across the Roadless Alternative 3 Affected Environment, 1,087,115 acres (after the hierarchy of overlapping categories was considered) are categorized as a designated area or recommended designated area.

This alternative also includes provisions to make needed modifications to the applicability of prohibitions within inventoried roadless boundaries. Where boundaries are modified that remove prohibitions, the effects would be the same as those described above.

In affected environment locations that continue current management, pursuant to the 2001 Roadless Rule in core roadless areas (prohibit road construction, reconstruction, and timber harvest with limited exceptions defined in the 2001 Roadless Rule), the effects would be same as those described for alternative 1.

##### **Conclusion – Recreation and Scenery**

Alternative 1 maintains primitive, undeveloped settings with the most opportunities for quiet, remote and self-reliant recreation opportunities and least flexibility for new developed recreation opportunities. Recreation settings would remain unchanged, preserving current roadless characteristics with lower-development, primitive settings that offer the most unroaded and quiet recreation opportunities. These areas provide dispersed recreation and help reduce pressure on designated wilderness, though this approach limits flexibility for meeting local recreation needs.

Alternative 2 has the greatest potential impact on recreation settings and scenery. When and where future timber harvest or road construction occur, short-term effects could include noise, visible infrastructure, and temporary closures during active management activities. Long-term, if roads are designated for public motorized access, recreation settings could shift toward more developed conditions, especially in areas with existing semi-primitive and roaded natural settings. Approximately 12 million more non-motorized acres could be affected by alternative 2 compared to Alternative 3.

Future potential management activities may have some short- and long-term scenery effects under both alternative 2 and 3, the management activities would strive to achieve long-term sustainable desired scenic character within the scenic integrity objectives identified in the land management planning process and any scenery related direction outlined in other statutory and regulatory requirements, including the procedural requirements of the National Environmental Policy Act. Scenic integrity objectives in land management plans would vary from high-tomoderate to low. Alternative 2 has the greatest potential for negative effects to scenery. Alternative 3 would potentially impact fewer areas.

Under Alternative 2, developed and road-based recreation could expand into inventoried roadless areas where land management plans allow road construction. Most new roads would likely be temporary and closed to public motor vehicle use, while permanent roads would support backcountry recreation with low-development facilities such as trailheads, primitive campsites, or staging areas—not large-scale infrastructure like campgrounds. This expansion would increase road-based recreation and accessibility improvements but reduce opportunities for quiet, remote and self-reliant recreation opportunities and increase user conflicts. Alternative 3 has similar effects to Alternative 2 but affects fewer acres overall, especially non-motorized settings. It preserves quieter, remote areas than Alternative 2 but fewer than Alternative 1. Some permanent road development is expected, increasing motorized access and dispersed recreation opportunities, but to a lesser extent than Alternative 2. Short-term impacts and user conflicts occur but are less severe than under Alternative 2.

Alternative 2 also has the highest potential for negative effects on National Recreation Trails and National Scenic and Historic Trails (NSHTs), including temporary closures and visual/noise impacts. Overall, this alternative provides the most flexibility for accessibility improvements and universal design but also results in the greatest changes to remote recreation settings. Alternative 3 has a lower potential for negative effects on NSHTs compared to Alternative 2 and offers modest improvements in accessibility and recreation opportunities compared to Alternative 1, though less than Alternative 2.

#### Cultural and Historic Resources

##### **Introduction**

Cultural resources include, but are not limited to, objects and specific locations linked to human activity, occupation, or use that is identifiable through field survey, historical documentation, or oral evidence. They include pre-contact (indigenous) and post-contact historic districts, archaeological sites, buildings, structures, objects, artifacts, sacred sites, cultural landscapes, and traditional cultural places, and also include land, mineral, water, animal, and plant resources important to Tribes and other cultural groups with traditional and contemporary practices tied to these resources (Forest Service Manual 2360.5). Pre-contact indigenous cultural resources date to a time before Europeans arrived in North America, while post-contact cultural resources date to a time after Europeans began occupying North America. Cultural resources may have significant spiritual and ceremonial value to one or several federally recognized Tribes. For Tribes, natural resources are cultural resources, and therefore it is impossible to fully list every type of potential cultural resource in this analysis.

Historic properties are defined in the National Historic Preservation Act as “any prehistoric or historic district, site, building, structure, or object included on, or eligible for inclusion on, the National Register [of Historic Places], including artifacts, records, and material remains relating to the district, site, building, structure, or object” (54 U.S.C. 300308). The term includes properties of traditional religious and cultural importance to Indian Tribes. To be eligible for inclusion in the National Register, a cultural resource must meet at least one of four criteria and retain sufficient integrity to convey its significance. The four criteria are: A) resources that are “associated with events that have made a significant contribution to the broad patterns of our history;” B) resources “that are associated with the lives of significant persons in our past;” C) resources “that embody the distinctive characteristics of a type, period, or method of construction, or that represent the work of a master, or that possess high artistic values, or that represent a significant and distinguishable entity whose components may lack individual distinction;” and D) resources “that have yielded or may be likely to yield information important in [pre- or post-contact] history...” A historic property can be eligible under one or more of these criteria and usually is at least 50 years old (36 CFR 60.4). A traditional cultural place is one that is eligible for inclusion in the National Register of Historic Places because of its association with cultural practices or beliefs of a living community that are (1) rooted in that community's history and (2) important in maintaining the continuing cultural identity of the community. Because these sites are still in use and Tribes and Tribal members may exercise their rights to visit them under multiple laws and treaties, including but not limited to the American Indian Religious Freedom Act and Executive Order 13007 (sacred sites), traditional cultural places are an important part of the affected environment for cultural and historic resources.

Ground disturbance, including whether areas have previously been disturbed, is a key consideration when determining impacts to cultural resources. New ground disturbance may damage historic properties and other cultural resources. Activities that would not create new impacts beyond the extent of previous ground disturbance (from logging, natural disasters, and so forth) do not constitute new ground disturbance. Visual effects may also impact a historic property or other cultural resource, particularly if a visual change on the landscape affects culturally or historically significant features.

The rulemaking does not authorize any activities that can affect historic properties. In accordance with Section 106 of the National Historic preservation Act, future actions in inventoried roadless areas require project-level, site-specific consideration of effects to historic properties.

##### **Potentially Affected Environment**

Historic properties and cultural resources, including those important to Tribes, are found across the potentially affected environment and reflect both human activity and natural resource use. The most common types of historical and cultural resources on National Forest System lands may include those from pre-contact (indigenous) and post contact (historical).

The pre-contact period, before Europeans arrived in North America, is understood through a mix of Western science and Indigenous Knowledge, with evidence of indigenous habitation dating back at anywhere from 13,000 years (Surovell et al. 2022) to 23,000 years ago (Pigati et al. 2023) although most Tribes cite occupation since time immemorial. These Indigenous peoples used local plants, animals, water sources, and minerals, travel routes, occupation sites, and resource gathering areas. Pre-contact sites range from lithic quarries and habitation sites to sacred locations and burial grounds. National Forest System lands are Tribal ancestral lands, and many Tribes and Tribal members continue traditional cultural and religious uses of the land. Treaties, agreements, and laws have reserved certain lands and rights for Tribes within National Forest System lands (see Tribal Rights and Interests section for a list of various authorities).

Spanish exploration and colonization in the 16th century established European sites in the Southwest and California, followed by other European settlements, especially in the eastern U.S. The 1800s saw westward expansion of European, Euro-American and other non-indigenous peoples driven by legislation like the Indian Removal Act of 1830 and the Homestead Act of 1862. These types of laws led to displacement, including forced removal of Tribes from their homelands. As non-Indigenous peoples moved westward, this led to the advent of railroads, leading to new industries and settlement sites such as mining camps and homesteads, which turned into towns and increased the demand for lumber. Federal forest management officially began in 1876, evolving through the creation of forest reserves and the U.S. Forest Service in 1905. Depression-era structures built by the Civilian Conservation Corps, including guard stations and fire lookouts, remain significant cultural resources.

##### **Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action**

In general, activities that do not create new ground disturbance or do not introduce visual changes to the landscape usually have little to no negative impacts on cultural resources. Under alternative 1, timber harvest and road construction and reconstruction would continue to be limited by the 2001 Roadless Rule, though allowed through limited exceptions for these activities. Any ground disturbing activities that occur within the potentially affected environment could have long-term negative effects on cultural resources including historic properties, though under alternative 1 exceptions are expected to continue to be infrequent. Site-specific planning for any proposed activities would require review under Section 106 of the National Historic Preservation Act, including early consultation with Tribes and other consulting parties to identify and evaluate historic properties and assess effects to them and, if needed, resolve adverse effects. These projects would also need to comply with land management plans and be reviewed under the National Environmental Policy Act, both of which would include consideration of cultural resources and opportunities to mitigate effects.

##### **Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action and Alternative 3 – Modified Rule**

Under alternatives 2 and 3, ground disturbance or visual impacts from increased timber harvest and road construction and reconstruction could have long-term negative effects on cultural resources including historic properties. As site-specific projects are planned, the potential for impacts would be reduced through environmental review, compliance and consultation.

Across the potentially affected IRAs, effects would be more likely where land management plans allow these activities and on lands that are operable for mechanical equipment (see Roads and Timber analyses). Under alternative 3, the potentially affected environment would be limited to wildland urban interfaces and areas within ½ mile of roads, therefore the potential for impacts to cultural resources would be on less area.

Alternatives 2 and 3 do not authorize site-specific actions. Future proposals would require review under Section 106 of the National Historic Preservation Act and its implementing regulations. Future timber and road projects would require early consultation with Tribes and other consulting parties to identify and evaluate historic properties and assess effects to them and, if needed, resolve adverse effects. These activities would also require land management plan compliance and review under the National Environmental Policy Act, which would include consideration of cultural resources.

The Forest Service often avoids and minimizes impacts to archaeological sites and other cultural resources by flagging them for avoidance, but there are many other ways to avoid, minimize, and mitigate impacts to historic properties and other cultural resources based on site-specific and project-specific information. Some activities can be designed to minimize impacts to archaeological sites and other cultural resources. For example, using vehicles and machinery that create little to no new ground disturbance will likely not have significant impacts on archaeological sites. However, ground disturbance by heavy machinery and even smaller tools such as a come-along can cause substantial ground disturbance from the machines themselves and/or by dragging or skidding trees across the land. Indirect impacts of timber harvest can include post-harvest erosion impacts to cultural resources. Erosion can be mitigated using soil stabilization methods such as seeding and mulching. Timber harvest can also change the visual character of cultural landscapes, which may include historic properties. In roadless areas, early and regular Tribal consultation can inform project design to avoid, minimize, or mitigate potential visual impacts to Tribally important cultural landscapes, sacred sites, and traditional cultural places.

New road construction always has the potential to impact cultural resources when roads are built in undisturbed areas where cultural resources are present, because they create substantial new ground disturbance. Many roads are built on historic travel routes often dating to thousands of years before the present, and cultural resources are commonly found in proposed road construction areas. Some roads can be aligned to avoid cultural resources, but often complete avoidance is impractical. In undeveloped areas, new roads can sometimes create visual impacts to Tribally important sites or cultural landscapes, and only Tribes with ancestral ties to these areas can identify these types of impacts. Activities such as road reconstruction and repairs in the same footprint of previous road construction and maintenance will have little to no impact on cultural resources, while road widening, realignments, and new roads usually have negative long-term impacts on cultural resources, when present, and these impacts may meet the threshold of an “adverse effect” to historic properties under implementing regulations for Section 106 of the National Historic Preservation Act (36 CFR 800). When the agency makes a finding of “adverse effect,” it will follow the procedures at 36 CFR 800.6 to resolve adverse effects, which usually requires a memorandum of agreement. By making cultural resources sites more easily accessed by the public, roads provide increased opportunities for vandalism, looting, and inadvertent degradation of cultural resources from human foot and vehicle traffic.

##### **Conclusion**

Under alternatives 1, 2, and 3 any timber harvest and road construction and reconstruction could potentially have long-term negative impacts on cultural resources including historic properties. The impacts would likely be more frequent and broader in scale under alternative 2. Impacts to cultural resources would likely be the least under alternative 1, because current restrictions allow limited exceptions for these activities. Impacts under alternative 3 would likely impact fewer cultural resources than alternative 2, because additional activities would be permitted on less land area. Under all alternatives, future timber and road projects would require review under Section 106 of the National Historic Preservation Act including early consultation with Tribes, State Historic Preservation Officers, and other consulting parties to identify and evaluate historic properties and assess effects to them and, if needed, resolve adverse effects.

#### Tribal Rights and Interests

##### **Introduction**

The Forest Service has legal obligations under the United States Constitution, treaty rights, and Federal laws, regulations, and policy to protect and preserve the inherent rights of American Indians into perpetuity. These unique and expansive rights include access to lands, use of forest products, the freedom to practice traditional religions, ceremonial use of Indian sacred sites, and the avoidance of adverse impacts to such sites, cultural resources or products. National Forest System lands contribute to Tribal economic, social, and cultural sustainability by providing goods and services, including essential ecosystem services such as forest products, clean water, and biodiversity for subsistence and other traditional cultural practices. According to the U.S. Census Bureau, 9 million U.S. residents identify as American Indian and Alaska Native alone (2.7 million) or American Indian and Alaska Native in combination with one or more other races (6.3 million).

Each Tribe has slightly different rights according to treaties, executive orders, and various laws, regulations, and court orders. Ongoing and regular meaningful government to government consultation with Tribes on proposed Federal actions is legally mandated and provides an important opportunity for the Tribes to express information and concerns during evaluation of proposed actions.

Preliminary responses to the Notice of Intent, formal written consultation requests from Tribes, and documentation from consultations<sup>65</sup> indicate several important values, perspectives, and legal aspects to consider:

- 1) an intimate relationship with and broadscale reliance on forest resources for subsistence use, since time immemorial, to directly sustain community health and wellbeing in perpetuity;

- 2) the value of and legally binding requirements to shared decision-making between Tribes and federal agencies when proposed actions may impact trust assets;

> 65 (see also Consultation with Federally Recognized Tribes and Alaska Native Corporations) Information shared by Tribes through official consultation, correspondence, and conversations with individuals provides a rich source of information on Tribal perspectives, resource uses, topics of interest, areas of importance, and the unique relationships Tribes have with Federal agencies. Information provided by Tribes is often protected from disclosure in accordance with section 304 of the National Historic Preservation Act – Confidentiality of the location of sensitive historic resources (54 U.S.C. 307103), section 9 of the Archaeological Resource Protection Act – Disclosure of Information (16 U.S.C. 470hh(a)), section 8106 of the Food, Conservation, and Energy Act of 2008 - Prohibition on disclosure (25 U.S.C. 3056), section 552(b) of the Freedom of Information Act (5 U.S.C. 552), and the regulations at 36 CFR 296.18 – Confidentiality of Archaeological Resource Information.

- 3) an emphasis on the co-stewardship opportunities that may exist for tribally led forest restoration using authorities such as Indian Trust Asset Reform Act and 638 contracting;

- 4) including Traditional Ecological Knowledge via co-management agreements and other formal mechanisms is critical to secure tribal data and knowledge, and protect cultural resources for future generations of Native people;

- 5) net benefit to Tribal economics through the protection of vast, intact landscapes that support ecotourism and recreation; and

- 6) clear and consistent strong opposition to the Roadless Rule Rescission citing past lawsuits that resulted in favoring Tribal interests (with a few exceptions).

The following indicators are used to assess the extent that each alternative responds to particular Tribal rights and interests.

- **Access:** Tribes’ ability to access treaty resources, sacred sites, or other areas of importance to Tribes.

- **Resource Usability:** The quantity and quality are sufficient to meet Tribal needs.

- **Disturbance:** Any activity that could pose a potential threat to treaty resources, sacred sites, or other areas of importance to Tribes.

Together, these indicators give an overview of the major concerns and relative means of ranking alternatives. These are discussed qualitatively based on consistent feedback received from Tribes. However, both Tribes and the Forest Service have some quantitative information regarding the quantity and quality of resources in local, regional, and national databases. Site-specific Tribal consultation is essential to understanding what access, uses or resources may be affected, the extent it could affect Tribal rights and interests, and how to avoid, minimize, or mitigate disturbance.

##### **Potentially Affected Environment**

The United States has a unique, legally affirmed nation-to-nation relationship with American Indian and Alaska Native Tribal Nations, which is recognized under the U.S. Constitution, treaties, statutes, executive orders, and court decisions (See Consultation with Federally Recognized Tribes and Alaska Native Corporations). This section provides background information on current conditions as they relate to Tribal sovereignty, Trust Responsibility and Tribal Treaty Rights, Tribal Co-stewardship, Tribal Sacred Sites Indigenous Knowledge and Land Management.

###### Tribal Sovereignty

Tribal sovereignty refers to a Tribes’ original, inherent authority to govern themselves, their lands, and their resources. It is not a power delegated to Tribes by Congress but is instead an inherent power that has never been extinguished (Worcester v. Georgia). Due to their unique status as sovereign nations, federally recognized Tribes maintain a government-to-government relationship with the Federal government. The United States recognizes the right of Tribal governments to self-govern and supports Tribal sovereignty and self-determination.

###### Trust Responsibility and Tribal Treaty Rights

A trust relationship exists between the United States (including all Federal agencies) and Tribes, in which the government “has charged itself with moral obligations of the highest responsibility and trust” (Seminole Nation v. United States, 316 U.S. 286, 1942). The nature of the trust relationship is defined by federal law and includes particular duties or fiduciary obligations.

Indian treaties are government-to-government agreements between Tribes and the United States as sovereign nations. They are typically not grants of rights to Tribes but grants of rights from Tribes to the United States, with a reservation of those rights not granted, ceded, or relinquished. Under the U.S. Constitution, ratified treaties are the supreme law of the land, and their terms may be changed only by Congress. Treaties with Tribal Nations may explicitly secure rights for the Tribe, including rights to lands, fishing and hunting rights, water rights, and goods and services such as food, education, and healthcare. Rights not addressed by Tribal treaty provisions are presumptively reserved, so long as the rights retained are consistent with federal law.

###### Tribal Co-Stewardship

Federal lands are the original homelands of Tribes. Co-stewardship broadly refers to collaborative or cooperative agreements between Federal agencies and Tribes regarding the management, conservation, and protection of lands, waters, and associated flora, fauna, and resources. Co-stewardship can take many forms, including sharing technical expertise and Indigenous Knowledge, combining Federal and Tribal capabilities to improve resource management, as well as integrating Tribal views and values into land management.

In the 1970s, Congress enacted Public Law 93-638, also known as the Indian Self-Determination and Education Assistance Act (25 U.S.C. 5301). The Act initially established a contracting framework with federally recognized Indian tribes, authorizing agencies within the Department of the Interior and Department of Health to coordinate with tribal governments on planning and administrative programs. These contacts are often called “self-determination contracts,” as they enable federal agencies meet their trust responsibilities to tribes, and allows for better involvement of tribes in the stewardship of lands. The 2018 Farm Bill (Public Law 115-334) expanded ISDEAA funding to include approved USDA Forest Service Tribal Forest Protection Act (TFPA) projects. The USDA Forest Service therefore possesses discretionary authority to enter 638 “self-governance compacts” and funding agreements with Tribes to perform “administrative, management, and other functions” of TFPA<sup>66</sup> projects. “Forest Service officials

> 66 [1] There must be an approved Tribal Forest Protection Act project in order to utilize the Forest Service 638 authority. 638 agreements are for work on National Forest System (NFS) lands (while DOI forestry program funding is for work on tribal lands). Furthermore, 638 agreement projects must be in compliance with NEPA, ESA, and NHPA as applicable. 638 TFPA agreements are only available to Tribes and tribal organizations that can meet a reasonable standard of “adjacency” of tribal trust lands under TFPA. The first USDA 638 agreement was signed Sep 9, 2020, with the Tulalip Tribes. It consisted of a demonstration project to help ongoing work to restore beaver habitat and protect salmons. The 638 agreement supports the restoration of Nationals Forest Lands that were Tulalip’s ancestral and treaty protected lands, in a way consistent with the spirit and purpose of the ISDEAA.

may provide advice and information to Indian Tribes in advance of Tribes’ submitting proposals for stewardship contracts or agreements to assist Indian Tribes in developing proposals that are consistent with the criteria in the Tribal Forest Protection Act” (FSH 2409.16, chapter 60).

###### Tribal Sacred Sites

Executive Order 13007, Indian Sacred Sites, was issued in May 1996 to address the protection and preservation of Indian religious practices at sacred sites on federal lands. This order defines a sacred site as “...any specific, discrete, narrowly delineated location on Federal land that is identified by an Indian tribe, or Indian individual determined to be an appropriately authoritative representative of an Indian religion, as sacred by virtue of its established religious significance to, or ceremonial use by, an Indian religion; provided that the Tribe or appropriately authoritative representative of an Indian religion has informed the agency of the existence of such a site.”

###### Indigenous Knowledge and Land Management

There is no single definition or phrase that captures Indigenous Knowledge. Numerous phrases (Indigenous Knowledge, Indigenous Traditional Ecological Knowledge, Traditional Ecological Knowledge, Indigenous Science, or Native Science) have been used to describe knowledge that “includes Indigenous relationships to land, plants, animals, community, self, cosmos, spirit, and the creative animating processes of life (Cajete 2021:15).” Indigenous Knowledge incorporates direct experience, as well as long-term observations by individuals and across generations (Emery et al. 2014). For the purposes of this document, Indigenous Knowledge, as defined at 36 CFR 219.19, refers to the body of observations, oral and written knowledge, innovations, practices, and beliefs developed by Tribes and Indigenous Peoples through interaction and experience with the environment. Indigenous Knowledge is a holistic and ongoing accumulation of knowledge, practices, and beliefs about the relationships between living beings in a specific ecosystem, acquired by Indigenous people since time immemorial through direct contact with the environment, passed down through generations, and used for life-sustaining purposes. Tribes see themselves as inseparably intertwined with the land and natural world and have a responsibility to steward lands in perpetuity (Berkes 1999, Emery et al. 2014, Kimmerer 2000). Indigenous Knowledge research recognizes the deeply interwoven relationships between people and their environments. These relationships remain critical to sustaining Tribal lifeways (Asselin 2015, Burger et al. 2008, De Groot et al. 2002, Fisher 2017, Hoagland 2017, Tengberg et al. 2012, Long et al. 2018).

Tribes rely on forest products for fuel, food, medicines, and other materials for cultural purposes, and they desire to restore the land as well as Tribal relationships with the land (Rogers-Martinez 1992, Schroeder 1996). Travel routes that were carved out by Tribal use over thousands of years later, and many of these routes became Forest Service trails or roads. Benefits of these road systems to Tribal members include motorized access to areas once accessed only by foot or horse. Access by roads allows for trips to hunt, gather, or travel to religious sites.

##### **Effects of Alternative 1 – No Action**

In general, activities that do not impede Tribal access or use of National Forest System Lands, do not impact resource usability nor disturb resources reserved for Tribes and have little to no new negative impact on Tribal rights and interests. Under alternative 1, timber harvest and road construction and reconstruction would continue to be infrequent and only where Responsible Officials approve exceptions for these activities. When exceptions are granted, timber harvest and road construction could have long-term negative effects on tribal rights and interests, and these impacts would be similar to those for alternatives 2 and 3 but would likely happen less frequently and affect less land area. Future projects that propose activities currently prohibited by the roadless rule would require early and regular consultation with Tribes to identify potential impacts to Tribally important areas and resources and to ensure the agency meets its Tribal trust responsibilities and that projects do not conflict with reserved rights in treaties and other laws.

##### **Effects of Alternative 2 – Proposed Action and Alternative 3 – Modified Rule**

Under Alternatives 2 and 3, additional timber harvest and road construction and reconstruction are reasonably foreseeable. These activities could have long-term negative effects on Tribal rights and interests. However, these alternatives would not authorize site-specific actions. Future projects would require early and regular consultation with Tribes to identify potential impacts to Tribally important areas and resources and to ensure the agency meets its Tribal trust responsibilities and that projects do not conflict with reserved rights in treaties and other laws. In addition, future activities would be compliant with land management plans, which are developed in consultation with Tribes and often contain plan components addressing Tribal rights and uses.

Across the potentially affected IRAs, effects would be more likely where land management plans allow these activities and on lands that are operable for mechanical equipment (see National Forest System Roads and Vegetation and Forest Management). Under alternative 3, outside of the Tongass National Forest, the potentially affected environment is limited to wildland urban interfaces and areas within ½ mile of roads, therefore, potential impacts to Tribal rights and interests would be similar, but over less area than alternative 2.

The following are general considerations for types of impacts that may result from timber and road construction projects based on the agency’s experience and based on comments received from Tribes during the comment period for the notice of intent. Future projects that propose activities currently prohibited by the roadless rule would require early and regular consultation with Tribes to identify potential impacts to Tribally important areas and resources and to ensure the agency meets its Tribal trust responsibilities and that projects do not conflict with reserved rights in treaties and other laws.

Timber harvest and road construction in inventoried roadless areas could significantly alter Tribal access to treaty-reserved resources, sacred sites, and other culturally significant areas. These lands often encompass cultural forestscapes, sacred forests, ancestral trails, and Areas of Tribal Importance designated under 36 CFR §219.10(b)(1). Construction of new roads may fragment or obscure ancestral trails that hold deep religious, spiritual, and cultural significance. These trails are not merely transportation corridors but ceremonial pathways and links to oral histories and traditional knowledge. Increased road infrastructure may lead to unauthorized public access, vandalism, or desecration of sacred sites and traditional cultural places, especially if not adequately protected or monitored. This can erode the sanctity and privacy required for traditional practices. Many Tribes rely on undeveloped, roadless areas for quiet, solitude, and spiritual renewal. The presence of roads and logging operations can compromise the acoustic and visual integrity of these spaces, diminishing their suitability for ceremonies and prayer. For Tribes engaged in cultural tourism, road development may either enhance or hinder access to interpretive sites. However, unmanaged tourism can also lead to overuse or degradation of culturally sensitive areas. Roads and timber harvest may create barriers to treaty-reserved hunting, fishing, and gathering areas, especially if access is restricted due to safety or operational concerns.

The usability of treaty resources—such as fish, game, plants, and clean water—is directly tied to their quantity, quality, and ecological integrity. Timber harvest and road construction can degrade these resources, undermining subsistence practices, economic development, and Tribal restoration goals. Timber harvest and road-building activities can increase sedimentation, alter hydrology, and impair fish and aquatic organism passage, directly affecting fisheries that are central to Tribal diets, economies, and ceremonies. Roads can fragment wildlife corridors, (see Biodiversity) reducing populations of game species. This diminishes hunting opportunities and violates the spirit of reserved hunting rights. Roads act as vectors for invasive plant species (see Non-Native Invasive Plant Species), which can outcompete culturally significant native plants used for food, medicine, and ceremony. This reduces the availability and quality of traditional resources.

Timber harvest can alter forest composition and structure, disrupting cultural forestscapes managed by Tribes for generations. These landscapes are often cultivated for specific species (such as beargrass or huckleberries) and spiritual purposes. Timber harvest in sacred areas may remove trees considered spiritually sentient or essential for ceremonial materials, undermining cultural continuity and spiritual well-being. Tribal-led restoration and economic development projects—such as non-timber forest product harvesting or eco-cultural tourism—may be compromised by competing land uses that degrade the resource base.

Disturbance includes both direct and indirect threats to Tribal resources and values. These can be ecological, cultural, or spiritual in nature, and often have cumulative and intergenerational impacts. Timber harvest and road construction increase the risk of disturbance, looting, or destruction of archaeological and cultural sites, especially in the absence of robust monitoring and protection protocols. The sensory impacts of machinery and increased human presence can disrupt ceremonial practices, wildlife behavior, and the spiritual character of sacred landscapes. The transformation of inventoried roadless areas into managed landscapes alters their character, which can be essential for religious and spiritual practices that require isolation and natural soundscapes. Projects that fail to integrate Indigenous knowledge into planning and design risk overlooking cultural sensitivities, ecological insights, and sustainable practices that could mitigate disturbance.

##### **Conclusion**

Generally, Tribes have shared concerns about current and future adverse effects on resources including all physical resources such as access routes, air, groundwater and surface water, plant and animal life, and landscapes, as well as less tangible attributes such as sense of place; sense of historical, spiritual, religious, and Tribal identity; opportunities for solitude; and opportunities to continue traditional cultural practices and ceremonies. There would be the greatest potential for adverse effects under alternative 2 and slightly reduced potential under alternative 3. Under all alternatives, future projects that propose activities currently prohibited by the roadless rule would require early and regular consultation with Tribes to identify potential impacts to Tribally important areas and resources and to ensure the agency meets its Tribal trust responsibilities and that projects do not conflict with reserved rights in treaties and other laws.

#### Social and Economic Environment

##### **Background**

This section considers the social and economic conditions and effects of alternatives, including economic effects and effects on the quality of life of the American People. It focuses on the social and economic issues raised during the public comment period.

Activities on National Forest System lands affect the quality of life for many people, whether they live near or far from a national forest. Quality of life can be discussed as a positive or desirable existence, trait, action, or outcome based upon the interactions between social, economic, and environmental components influencing the human environment (Diener and Suh 1997; Reed and Brown 2003; Petrosillo et. al. 2013; Santacreu and Fernandez-Ballesteros 2023). Quality of life is multi-dimensional and comprised of interrelated subjective and objective indicators and measures that can be viewed from an individual, community, or population level (Diener and Suh 1997; Petrosillo et. al. 2013; Santacreu and Fernandez-Ballesteros 2023). The effects detailed below can collectively describe how peoples’ quality of life could be impacted by the action alternatives.

For more detailed economic analysis, see the Cost Benefit Analysis for the proposed rule, available on the project website

This analysis is a mix of qualitative and quantitative effects, depending on the availability of information to aid in predicting potential effects. See Scope of Analysis section for more information. Recreation and timber data were available to estimate quantitative economic effects of the alternatives. National Visitor Use Monitoring data was used for the economic analysis of recreation effects. Forest Service Forest Inventory and Analysis and Timber Information Manager data were used for estimating potential timber volumes in operable roadless areas and stumpage rates. These quantitative analyses are provided only for comparative purposes across alternatives and are estimates of possible future activities. Under all alternatives local land management plans and site-specific decision-making would dictate what activities actually occur on the ground and are difficult to predict. A qualitative analysis is provided for other social and economic effects analysis, such as minerals, wildfire, and social values of roadless areas, because the effects were not possible to predict at the programmatic level.

##### **Potentially Affected Environment**

###### Demographics and Communities

More than 95 percent of inventoried roadless area acres managed under the 2001 Roadless Rule (outside of Colorado and Idaho) are in ten Western states, including (from largest inventoried roadless area acreage to smallest): Alaska, Montana, California, Utah, Wyoming, Nevada, Washington, Oregon, New Mexico, and Arizona. People in these areas could be affected by the action alternatives because they are living in close proximity to areas that could experience increases in road building and timber harvest. People outside of these areas and across the country could also be affected as visitors to these areas for recreation and people find value in knowing the areas exist.

The population across the United States and Puerto Rico has increased 9.7 percent since 2010 (U.S. Department of Commerce, 2021, 2025). This analysis focuses on the counties that contain potentially affected IRAs, referred to here as IRA footprint counties. To understand geographic differences, population trends are provided by Resources Planning Act Assessment regions<sup>67</sup> . Overall, the populations in IRA footprint counties are growing at a rate (10.6 percent) greater than the national rate (9.7 percent), especially in the western states that contain the majority of roadless area (Great Plains, Intermountain, and Pacific Northwest RPA regions).

The U.S. population is projected to increase by 8.7 percent between 2022 and 2055 (U.S. Department of Commerce 2023). These population projections are nationwide and reflect continued urban, suburban, and ex-urban development. Many counties surrounding National Forest System lands are becoming more densely populated. Growth within these counties is unlikely to be distributed evenly among local communities and can cause some areas to become more urban while others become increasingly more decentralized. Natural amenities have been found to influence employment changes, rural population change, and the development of rural recreation and retirement destinations (Knapp and Graves 1989, Clark and Hunter 1992, Treyz et al. 1993, Mueser and Graves 1995, McGranahan 1999, Lewis et al. 2002). Growing populations and the encroachment of human development place greater demand on forest goods and services and can impact the range of values people hold for these landscapes, as well as sense of place and quality of life in communities surrounding National Forest System lands (Stedman 2003).

Management of National Forest System lands contributes to social and economic sustainability in rural economies across the Nation. As a result, rural ways-of-life are related to forest management and are potentially affected by road building and timber harvest activities. Of the 397 IRA footprint counties, almost half (191) are rural<sup>69</sup> (USDA Economic Research Service 2024). Because of their proximity to National Forest System lands and rural status, those counties may depend on the ecosystem services and multiple uses provided by National Forest System lands to a greater degree than other counties. As populations and subsequent housing

> 69Rural designation is based on the county’s assignment to the three rural-urban continuum codes. The Office of Management and Budget (OMB) identifies metropolitan statistical areas as U.S. counties and county equivalents with at least one urban area of 50,000 or more, plus adjacent territory that has a high degree of social and economic integration with that core due to commuting patterns. The USDA Economic Research Service further subdivides the OMB division of counties to create three metro categories, based on the population size of the metro area of those counties, and six nonmetropolitan (nonmetro) categories, based on their degree of urbanization and adjacency to a metro area. Of the six non-metro categories, three are characterized as rural for the purposes of this analysis: 1) Counties, or equivalent, with an urban population of 5,000 to 20,000, not adjacent to a metro area; 2) Counties, or equivalent, with an urban population of fewer than 5,000, adjacent to a metro area; and 3) Counties, or equivalent, with an urban population of fewer than 5,000, not adjacent to a metro area (USDA Economic Research Service 2024).

increase near public lands, federal managers must contend with the complexity of managing natural resources in a rapidly changing social context (Mockrin et al. 2018).

Timber Industry and Forest-Dependent Communities

Since 1980, the amount of timber sold from the NFS has declined by more than 80 percent. The decline is attributable to multiple factors, including (but not limited to) changing legislative directives and related forest management policies and practices, changing market dynamics for wood products, public preferences, and litigation (Congressional Research Service, 2022). From 1980 to 2023, Forest Service timber harvest peaked at 12.7 billion board feet in 1987, then declined to a low of 1.7 billion board feet by 2002 owing largely to steep declines in harvest from national forests in the Pacific Northwest and Pacific Southwest Regions (Washington, Oregon, and California). For the last 15 years, timber harvest volume remained relatively constant, averaging about 2.5 billion board feet. In 2023, 2.9 billion board feet of timber was harvested from NFS lands, at a value of approximately $163 million. In 2024, harvested timber value across all NFS lands was $151 million and sold was nearly $144 million (USDA Forest Service 2025b).

Declining timber supply from western national forests caused log shortages for many mills, contributing to changes in the structure of forest related industry nationally. Regardless of national trends, timber supply changes at smaller scales elicit a variety of responses from local industry. Typically, in response to short-term conditions, facility managers make curtailments, add or remove shifts, and schedule overtime or downtime. Longer-term decisions include building new mills, investing in capacity-boosting capital improvements in existing mills, or permanently closing facilities. Spelter (2002a) described the number of sawmills closed and opened and overall capacity changes that impacted the softwood lumber sector between 1995 and 2009. The number of softwood sawmills in the United States and Canada declined from 1,258 to 891, even as the combined sawmill processing capacity increased from 150 to 168 million cubic meters. In addition, the sawmill industry in the United States experienced a process of industrial concentration over this period. While the number of sawmills has decreased, larger sawmills have come to dominate the softwood industry (Spelter 2002a, 2009), a trend that continues to have implications for forest landowners, policymakers, and rural regions affected by sawmill closures.

Other than the drop during the recession of 2008, U.S. production and consumption of industrial roundwood, which comprises pulpwood, sawlogs and veneer logs, from 1961 to 2019 has remained largely unchanged, averaging around 250 million cubic meters (or 55.2 billion board feet applying the industry standard conversion factor for roundwood of 1 mbf = 4.53 cubic meters (Spelter 2002b)) per year (Johnston et al. 2023).

The reduction in timber harvest on national forests has resulted in increased harvests from private and state lands and increased lumber imports, mostly from Canada (MacCleery 2008). The shift towards state and private lands has impacted timber producing regions of the country differently. In the West, Northwest Forest Plan implementation drastically reduced Forest Service timber harvest in the Pacific Southwest and Pacific Northwest regions, contributing, along with market changes, mill efficiency improvements, and industry consolidation, to mill closures and consolidation (Helvoigt and Adams, 2009) shifting timber harvest to state and private forestlands (Gale et al. 2012). In Oregon and Washington, most of the mills able to process large diameter logs had retooled to handle smaller logs or closed by the early 2000s. With the closure of sawmills and shift to shorter rotation forestry, the forest industry had to retool to cut smaller, more uniform saw logs to remain competitive in the global marketplace (U.S. Government Publishing Office, 2012). Adams and Haynes (1996) found that industrial production shifted from the West to the South in the 1990s following reduction of harvest from public lands in the West. The U.S. South contains few national forests and wood supply already originated on private and industrial lands engaging in short rotation forestry. In the Northeast, the timber industry is comprised of specialty hardwood lumber and higher end products like furniture along with commodity production of softwood lumber from both federal and private sources.

Timber harvest contributes to local economies and rural community well-being by supplying raw materials, supporting local jobs and income, and stimulating economic activity in supporting sectors. Timber harvest activities make positive contributions to rural communities, especially if wood harvested is processed locally. Economic contributions can expand more widely, as the ripple effect of job creation and money spent circulates throughout the related industries and economic sectors. In 2023, harvest and processing of forest products from NFS lands contributed approximately $5.8 billion to the Gross Domestic Product of the U.S. economy, and supported about 51,200 jobs, both full-time and part-time (USDA Forest Service 2025a). Timber harvesting, whether for wood production or restoration objectives, provides employment and tax revenue in many counties throughout the Nation. These are especially important contributions to rural economies where there may not be other opportunities.

Direct employment in industries associated with timber and wood products include forestry, fishing and agriculture services and manufacturing, as displayed in Table 46 for North American Industrial Classification System (NAICS) for forestry and logging, support activities for forestry, wood product manufacturing, and paper manufacturing (U.S. Department of Labor 2024). Overall, timber sector employment has declined by 30.5 percent since 2001 with losses in paper manufacturing (37.3 percent), forestry and logging (33.1 percent), and wood product manufacturing (25.1 percent). On the other hand, employment in support services for forestry has grown by 25.5 percent. This category typically includes jobs associated with forest stand improvement and stewardship activities, such as pre- commercial tree thinning to reduce wildfire risk and ecological restoration to improve watersheds and fish and wildlife habitat. The expanding use of Forest Service authorities to engage in “goods for services” stewardship contracting and Good Neighbor Authority agreements to achieve ecological restoration goals is reflected in the support services employment trends. By providing opportunities for living wage jobs through local contracting and workforce development, Forest Service management activities contribute to economic opportunities in rural communities.

Forest dependence has been widely recognized as a valuable concept for understanding the wellbeing of human communities and their vulnerability to shocks in the forest sector and policy changes. Forest-dependence relates to communities’ abilities to cope with change, using concepts such as resiliency, stability, and adaptability. Frey et al. (2022) examined forest dependence by creating environmental, economic, and social criteria based on threshold levels for forest area, earnings, employment, and indigenous population. An environmentally forest-dependent community is one that relies on regulating and supporting ecosystem services to support basic community functions. These communities were defined by Frey et al. (2022) based on the percentage of forest land in a county (a baseline threshold of 75 percent forest land to be a forestdependent community), using data from the USDA Forest Service’s, Forest Inventory and Analysis program data.

Economically forest-dependent communities rely on provisioning ecosystem services, such as timber and clean water, to support livelihoods and subsistence, and Frey et al. (2022) used the sum of employment and earnings from North American Industry Classification (NAICS) industry classes for forestry and logging, hunting and trapping, support activities for forestry, wood products manufacturing, and paper manufacturing, divided by total employment and earnings to determine the proportion of economic activity related to forestry-based sectors<sup>70</sup> . A baseline threshold of 10 percent of workers employed in the forest sector or more than 15 percent of earnings from the forest sector determined whether a community met the economically forestdependent criterion. Forests can provide a deep connection between community and nature, a sense of place, and people derive meaning from forest-based recreation, rites, rituals, and other cultural practices (Frey et al. 2022).

Measuring social forest-dependency is challenging, and Frey et al. (2022) used indigenous populations as a proxy for social or cultural dependence on forests. The percent of indigenous peoples among the total population was calculated from American Community Survey data, and this proportion was combined with forest land use to develop the criterion. A threshold of 5 percent indigenous and 30 percent forest land use was required to meet the third criterion (Frey

> 70 No data source was found or provided for economic activity related to recreation that could be limited to forestbased recreation only and thus was not included (Frey et al. 2022).

et al. 2022). Meeting any one of the three criteria was sufficient for a community, characterized at the county-level, to be categorized as forest-dependent.

Forest-dependent communities are most likely to feel impacts from changing Forest Service policies. Using the criteria developed by Frey et al. (2022) and updated data, 158 of the 397 IRA footprint counties are considered forest-dependent (39.8 percent), with 21 of those counties (5.3 percent) meeting the thresholds for economic dependence. Over 23 percent of forest dependent counties (of any criterion) are rural, while nearly 80 percent of economically dependent counties are characterized as rural as defined by the USDA Economic Research Service’s rural-urban continuum code (USDA Economic Research Service 2024). As shown in , there is geographic variation in which RPA assessment regions meet the various thresholds and forest-dependency criteria. Over 90 percent of the IRA footprint counties in the Northeast RPA assessment region meet the threshold for environmentally forest-dependent, whereas zero percent of the IRA footprint counties in the Great Plains meet any of the thresholds. The Pacific Southwest and the state of Alaska have the highest proportion of socially forest-dependent counties (36.8 percent and 42.9 percent, respectively) while the Northeast and Pacific Northwest RPA assessment regions have the highest proportion of economically forest-dependent IRA footprint counties (18.2 percent and 12.0 percent, respectively). Given the reliance of the Frey et al. (2022) framework on forest land proportion (an environmental criterion) and forest sector employment and earnings (an economic criterion), dependence is likely to highlight certain regions more than others. Thus, connections between public lands and adjacent communities might be harder to detect for IRA footprint counties located in regions with higher proportions of grasslands or rangeland activities. However, IRA footprint counties in general are more likely to feel impacts from changing policies and management practices on National Forest System lands.

###### Minerals and Energy

In 2023, energy and mineral production from NFS lands contributed more than $7.6 billion to the Nation’s economic growth in terms of Gross Domestic Product and supported approximately 37,800 jobs (USDA Forest Service 2025a). Even where its share of total U.S. supplies is small, National Forest System production can be very important to local economies.

Locatable minerals, such as gold, silver, copper, and iron, may be located and removed from Federal lands open to location and entry under the authority of the Mining Law of 1872, as amended (“the mining laws”). The Forest Service estimates that revenue generated in 2024 from 11 large mines on NFS lands totaled about $1.4 billion (USDA Forest Service 2025c). Valuable deposits of locatable mineral resources may occur in inventoried roadless areas and exploration and development for these mineral resources is ongoing within IRAs (see Mineral Exploration and Development).

Leasable minerals are certain types of minerals, primarily energy resources (for example, oil, gas, coal, and geothermal resources) not subject to mining claim location but are available for exploration and development under provisions of the Mineral Leasing Act of 1920. Currently, 3.4 million acres of NFS lands are leased for oil, natural gas, coal, phosphates, other leasable mineral commodities, and geothermal resources. In 2024, energy and mineral development from Federal leases on NFS lands produced commodities valued at over $4.8 billion, which generated $533 million in revenue through royalties and other payments to the U.S. Treasury ($106 million from coal, $402 million from oil and gas, and $25 million from other leasable mineral commodities) (USDA Forest Service 2025c). Leases for oil and gas and coal that pre-date the 2001 Roadless Rule continue within the potentially affected IRAs (they are considered ‘valid existing rights’ under the rule). Federal review and approval of any new exploration, leasing, or development is evaluated relative to the prohibitions of the rule as well as local land management plan requirements (see Mineral Exploration and Development section)

###### Recreation

Slightly more than half of the U.S. population engages in some form of outdoor recreation, including exercise and outdoor sports (White et al. 2023, Outdoor Foundation 2021). About 13 to 16 percent of individuals over age 6 in the United States engage in each of the most popular nature-based recreation activities: day hiking, camping, and fishing (White et al. 2023). Other activities, such as mountain biking, rafting and kayaking, skiing and snowboarding, hunting, and wildlife viewing, attract between 1 and 10 percent of the U.S. population aged 6 and older (White et al. 2023, Outdoor Foundation 2021).

Recreation is the primary way most people engage with forests and other natural resources. Exercise and spending time with friends and family are the most common motivations for recreation (Outdoor Foundation 2018). Other leading motivations for recreating include spending time in nature and viewing scenery. The most popular outdoor recreation activities in the U.S., such as using developed recreation sites, outdoor swimming, and day hiking (White et al. 2023), provide opportunities for exercise, socializing, and engaging with nature in forested settings. See also the Recreation and Scenery section.

Recreation is a key engine of the U.S. economy. U.S. outdoor recreationists supported about $639 billion of U.S. gross domestic product (GDP)—about 2.3 percent of total U.S. GDP—with their expenditures on trips for outdoor recreation and purchases of gear and equipment to engage in recreation (U.S. Bureau of Economic Analysis 2024). The share of U.S. GDP associated with outdoor recreation is larger than the share of GDP associated with the mining (1.5 percent) or utility (1.6 percent) sectors, among others (U.S. Bureau of Economic Analysis 2025).

###### _Future Recreation Demand_

The share of the population engaging in outdoor recreation has remained steady to slightly increasing over the last nearly two decades (White et al. 2016, White et al. 2023, Outdoor Foundation 2023). Population increases in the United States over that time have led to sustained growth in the number of people participating in outdoor recreation and the aggregate number of days of outdoor recreation occurring each year. Recreation infrastructure—including trails, roads, picnic areas, nature viewing sites, and campgrounds—on public lands managed by all levels of government provides the primary means for meeting that recreation demand (White et al. 2023).

Demand for recreation activities common on national forests is projected to continue increasing, sometimes dramatically, by 2040 and 2070 (White et al. 2023) (Table 48). Local, state, federal and private lands will provide the settings to meet future recreation demand and recreation opportunities on NFS lands will certainly play a key role. By 2040, the number of days of recreation for most of the activities common on trails and in dispersed settings (such as day hiking, mountain biking, off-road driving) are projected to increase. For example, by 2040, those participating in off-road driving are projected to engage in at least 360 million days of recreation in that activity while day hikers are projected to engage in at least 500 million days of hiking in 2040. Demand for recreation at developed facilities, such as visiting natural and historic places, engaging in outdoor family gatherings, and picnicking, is also projected to increase across a wide range of potential futures with at least 1.2 million days of recreation engagement by 2040. Demand for camping in developed campgrounds is also projected to increase in the coming decades (at least 206 million days of developed-site camping by 2040). Demand for fishing and viewing nature (including viewing wildlife) are both projected to increase in the coming decades under a wide range of potential futures. Demand for hunting is projected to increase modestly (relative to fishing and viewing nature and other activities) in futures with moderate or high population and income growth but decline slightly in a future of low population and income growth.
