**Figure 22. Alternative 3 potential merchantable sawtimber harvest value per year in operable inventoried roadless areas applying land management plan direction**

The potential annual increase in sawtimber harvest on operable IRA areas of National Forest System lands under alternative 3 could have economic impacts. This harvest could replace or substitute existing harvest in non-IRA areas, which wouldn’t generate additional jobs. It is also possible that harvest in IRA operable areas is additional harvest which would generate new jobs. These economic impacts are not expected to be substantial overall given the harvest increase is potentially 4-9 percent across all forests nationwide within IRA operable areas, though impacts to local communities could be larger. Estimating the economic impacts is not possible given the unknown degree of substitution.

###### Minerals and Energy

Under alternative 3, while maintaining some roadless rule prohibitions on road construction, this alternative could influence exploration for minerals and energy activities in inventoried roadless areas where prohibitions are removed. Mineral and energy activity on National Forest System lands continue to depend upon such factors as market conditions, environmental regulations, tax policies, technological advances, and mineral and energy resource potential.

In the areas where the 2001 Roadless Rule continues to apply, the effects would be similar to the no-action alternative. In addition, there are no anticipated changes in locatable mineral production and associated social and economic effects under alternative 3.

Under alternative 3, in areas that are no longer designated IRAs, the effects would be the same as alternative 2, such as new authorizations and expanded authorizations could include road construction and reconstruction, depending on other legal requirements, such as land management plan components, and resource protection considerations. The removal of land within the roadless rule boundary could generate renewed interest in mineral leasing where road building was previously not allowed.

Under alternative 3 there is additional flexibility for potential future leasable mineral development opportunities and some development and associated social or economic effects could occur, though less than alternative 2. However, we anticipate that there would be no net change in domestic leasable production on NFS lands under the action alternatives relative to alternative 1 because leasable mineral development in IRAs could still occur based on technological methods of extraction without the use of roads. Or as stated previously, would replace or substitute existing development outside of IRAs on federal, state, or private lands resulting in no net change in domestic production.

###### Recreation

Under alternative 3, potential changes in recreation settings and conditions, owing to timber harvest and road construction, would be confined to a smaller area of potentially affected IRAs. Where the 2001 Roadless Rule remains in place, landscape conditions and recreation settings would likely be similar to those under alternative 1. In the portions of IRAs potentially affected under alternative 3, timber harvest and road construction could lead to losses in trail-based and dispersed recreation opportunities or changes in recreation settings.

###### _Future Recreation Demand_

In the short term, the ability of roadless areas to meet recreation demand under this alternative would be similar to alternative 1, because it is most likely that the bulk of management activity in the short term would be what is already being planned. In the long-term, loss of recreation opportunities because of timber harvest and road construction that changes recreation setting could make it harder to accommodate future demand for trail-based and dispersed recreation. That loss in capacity to meet future demand would conceivably be less under alternative 3, compared to alternative 2, because the spatial extent of where prohibitions would be removed is smaller in alternative 3. Given no expectation of funding for construction of new recreation sites and little expected increase in permanent roads open to public vehicular traffic, it is unlikely that alternative 3 would result in significant opportunities to meet future demand for developed site and road-based recreation.

###### _Economic Activity from Recreation in Roadless Areas_

In the short term, community economic activity resulting from recreation in roadless areas associated would be consistent with that in alternative 1. In the long term there may be a loss in visits (and visitor expenditures) from current roadless areas under this alternative. However, because the spatial extent of potential changes in landscape conditions is more limited in this alterative, these losses in expenditures would likely be less than those under alternative 2. Further, losses in visitor expenditures may be mitigated better under this alternative, because there would be larger areas of potentially affected IRAs left in place to receive any displaced visits. As in alternative 2, few new road-based and developed recreation opportunities are anticipated under alternative 3 and thus any increase in expenditures from those engaging in road-based and developed recreation is expected under alternative 3 is expected to be marginal nationally.

###### _Economic Benefits from Recreation in Roadless Area Forests_

In the short-term, the economic benefits to recreationists recreating in current roadless areas would likely be similar to those in alternative 1. In the long term, this alternative would likely yield losses in recreation visits (and aggregate economic benefits to visitors). However, the aggregate losses in recreation economic benefit would likely be less under alternative 3 than under alternative 2 because less of the present IRAs would be disturbed. Because the establishment of new developed recreation sites and permanent roads open to public vehicular traffic is anticipated to be minimal under this alternative, there would likely be little aggregate gain in economic benefit to visitors engaging in road-based and developed site recreation.

###### Fuels Management and Wildfire

Effects of alternative 3 would be similar to alternative 2 because more of the potential new road building and associated opportunities for mechanical hazardous fuels reduction and fire response are concentrated closer to the communities that they are intended to protect. With increased road construction, there could be increased hazard from human-caused ignitions and increased opportunities for wildfire risk mitigation (see Fire and Fuels Management section).

###### Non-Commodity Values for Roadless Areas

Similar to alternative 2, alternative 3 would remove 2001 Roadless Rule prohibitions, though on a smaller portion of the potentially affected IRAs. Where the 2001 Roadless Rule prohibitions are removed (in areas that are within ½ mile of existing roads or within WUI), further development of these areas could occur over time, and the effects would be similar to alternative 2. However, in contrast to alternative 2, alternative 3 would maintain and conserve roadless characteristics on a portion of inventoried roadless areas that are outside ½ mile of existing roads or within WUI, sheltering those more remote roadless areas from some forms of development and disturbance. Similar to alternative 1, this would support the social values and benefits associated with the roadless area characteristics.

##### **Conclusion**

Social and economic effects of the alternatives provide an understanding of the full realm and variety of influences on peoples’ quality of life. Alternative 2 enhances the quality of life for people that value timber harvest and potential mineral and energy development and the economic contributions that these provide to the community. Both action alternatives would similarly enhance the quality of life for people living in communities near the IRAs, as there are increased opportunities for wildfire risk mitigation. Alternative 2 would have the greatest negative effects to those who primarily use recreation opportunities located within IRAs, businesses and communities that depend on the spending of recreationists using IRAs, and those that hold intrinsic value for the existence of roadless areas and the ecosystem services they provide.

#### Environmental Impacts Within the Tongass National Forest

In 2001, the USDA determined that the Tongass National Forest deserved “special attention in formulating alternatives due to its unique social and economic conditions,” stating:

_“the agency has considered the alternatives of exempting and not exempting the Tongass National Forest, as well as deferring a decision per the proposed rule. Social and economic considerations were key factors in analyzing those alternatives, along with the unique and sensitive ecological character of the Tongass National Forest, the abundance of roadless areas where road construction and reconstruction are limited, and the high degree of ecological health. In developing the proposed action, the agency sought to balance the extraordinary ecological values of the Tongass National Forest against the needs of the local forest dependent communities in Southeast Alaska.” (66 FR 3254)._

Similarly, the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule Final Environmental Impact Statement (2020 FEIS) found that similar issues drove the Alaska-specific state rulemaking and analysis (page ES-3).

The effects associated with the potentially affected IRAs within the Tongass National Forest are fully disclosed in the 2020 FEIS for the Alaska Roadless Rule, analysis supporting the 2023 rulemaking, and associated record. This EIS uses those analyses as outlined in 7 CFR 1b.9 on efficient and effective environmental reviews (see Appendix 4 – Review of the Final Environmental Impact Statement for the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule). The environmental impacts presented in the other sections of the DEIS are programmatic and nationwide in nature. However, the Tongass is a unique environment relative to other IRAs, includes a large proportion of IRAs (approximately 9.3 million acres, nearly 1/4 of the potentially affected inventoried roadless area acreage), and has a long history of special treatment in roadless rulemaking. The 2020 FEIS provides more specific, localized effects analysis for the Tongass National Forest affected environment, which supplement the broader nation-wide analysis.

The following section is a summary of effects from the 2020 FEIS. All page numbers referenced in the following section refer to the 2020 FEIS, unless otherwise noted). Only the conclusions related to the potentially significant impacts of the alternatives have been summarized here. All the related supporting information and findings represented in those documents are incorporated by reference.

Six alternatives were analyzed in the 2020 FEIS. Alternative 1 analyzed no action in which the 2001 Roadless Rule would remain in place for Tongass IRAs. This is the same as alternative 1/no-action alternative for this DEIS because the 2001 Roadless Rule is currently in effect for the Tongass. Alternative 6 of the 2020 FEIS analyzed a full exemption from the 2001 Roadless Rule for the Tongass. This would have the same effects as alternatives 2 and 3 for this DEIS. To avoid confusion with the differences in the alternative numbering, this section simply refers to ‘2001 Roadless Rule in place’ and ‘removing roadless rule prohibitions.’

##### **Assumptions Common to All Alternatives**

The 2020 FEIS had very similar baseline assumptions across all alternatives, and these are consistent with the assumptions for this DEIS. These include that the actual location, design, and extent of future activities are not known and would depend on future project-level decisions (page 3-2). The 2016 Tongass Forest Plan was the basis for analysis in the 2020 FEIS.

##### **National Forest System Roads**

Impacts of the alternatives on National Forest System roads are discussed primarily in the section Transportation, Energy, Communications, and Infrastructure (pages 3-163-3-170) and under Key Issue 2; Infrastructure Development page 3-58). Estimated road miles under each alternative are summarized in Table 3.3-20 and 3.3-21 (pages 3-121 and 3-135). Note that the FEIS states that there is considerable uncertainty concerning the future development of Southeast Alaska’s road system.

The amount of new road construction would be similar under all alternatives (page 3-166); though removing roadless rule prohibitions would have slightly more road miles than if the 2001 Roadless Rule remained in place. Removing the Roadless Rule prohibitions results in an estimated 49 miles more of potential new road constriction within the Tongass compared to leaving the Roadless Rule in place (Table 3.3-21). The estimated average road density for all NFS lands is the same for whether the Roadless Rule is in place or not (0.23 miles/sq. mile). Removing the Roadless Rule prohibitions on all 9.3 million acres of roadless areas would allow the maximum level of road construction for timber harvest and all other uses. There would be a moderate beneficial effect for potential development of state roads and other transportation projects (pages 2-25, 3-58). However, the economics of building roads to access forest land farther from the forest transportation system would be a limiting factor for new road construction or project development.

##### **Access and Uses (Special Uses, Minerals)**

Effects to access for special uses is discussed in the section _Transportation, Energy, Communications, and Infrastructure_ (pages 3-163-3-170). In general, access would not be significantly affected whether the Roadless Rule is in place or not because the amount of road construction and road reconstruction would be similar under all alternatives (see the Roads discussion above). Removing the Roadless Rule prohibitions would allow for the maximum level of road construction. Communications and infrastructure projects that do not require tree cutting or road construction/reconstruction are not prohibited in IRAs. No significant adverse consequences to energy projects and related infrastructure are anticipated by removing the Roadless Rule prohibitions. It would simplify the process for approving projects but would not necessarily result in an increase in the number of projects developed.

Effects related to minerals exploration and development are covered in two separate sections: _Mining and Mineral Development_ under _Key Issue 2_ (pages 3-48-3-49 and pp. 3-57 through 3- 58) and _Minerals_ under _Other Important Issues_ (pages 3-177 to 3-180). The Tongass National Forest has three current geothermal leases where no development has occurred, and the probability of development is low, and the anticipated demand for leasable minerals elsewhere on the forest is expected to remain low. As a result, changes in designated roadless management are expected to have limited impacts on leasable mineral development. For locatable minerals, exploration, mining, and mineral processing activities, including road construction and reconstruction, are presently allowed to the extent provided by statute in IRAs and would continue to be allowed under all alternatives. Changes in roadless management are, therefore, not expected to affect existing or future locatable mineral exploration or mining activities on the Forest (p. 3-57).

##### **Forested and Non-Forested Ecosystems and Forest Management**

Many aspects of forested and non-forested ecosystems are covered throughout the FEIS:

- Timber harvest pages 3-147 through 3-148, and 3-171 through 3-176,

- Forest products pages 3-34 through 3-43 and 3-50 through 3-55,

- Biological diversity (cover types, old growth) pages 3-61 through 3-88

- Old-growth forests pages 3-17 through 3-26,

- Climate change and carbon storage pages 3-143 through 3-150

###### Potential Timber Resources and Forest Products

The PTSQ for the 2016 Forest Plan would not change under any of the alternatives, so overall timber harvest levels and general composition (old-growth versus young-growth) are expected to remain unaffected by rulemaking within the Tongass. Timber program output levels are expected to remain constant and involve a similar number of acres whether the Roadless Rule is in place or not, varying only by the location of timber harvest. The main effect of removing the Roadless Rule on timber resources would be the ability to develop economic sales (see page 3-175-76).

The Alaska Roadless Rule, as analyzed in the 2020 FEIS, would expand the suitable areas, due to inclusion of an administrative change to the Tongass Forest Plan. That analysis found that with the timber suitability change to the Plan, a greater area would be available for the development of timber sales, allowing more choices for the development of economic ones. The resultant effect would be the same harvest spread out over a larger area. Removing the Roadless Rule would result in a greater increase in harvest in remote areas; however, economic conditions suggest that economic sale requirements may limit the level of entry into remote areas, at least for the next 5 or 10 years. With the Roadless Rule in place, approximately 564,000 acres (227,000 acres of old growth and 334,000 acres of young growth) were mapped as suitable for timber production. With the Roadless Rule removed and the timber suitability change, approximately 749,000 acres (395,000 acres of old growth and 354,000 acres of young growth) would be suitable for timber production (Table 3.8-1, page 3-175).

With the Roadless Rule in place, there would be no changes in the availability of suitable old growth acres for harvest. With the Roadless Rule not in place and the timber suitability change, former inventoried roadless areas would be managed in accordance with the 2016 Forest Plan, with an estimated net gain of about 168,000 acres of suitable old growth, including 59,000 acres of high-volume suitable old growth (Table 3.2-8, Figure 3.2-12). This alternative would also result in an estimated net gain of about 20,000 acres of suitable young growth, including 8,000 acres more distant from roads. Removing the Roadless Rule would not result in significant changes to the Projected Timber Sale Quantity (PTSQ), and the timber objectives of the Forest Plan would continue to involve the transition to primarily young-growth harvest. Young-growth suitable acres would increase only slightly (3 to 6 percent) with the Roadless Rule removed (Table 3.2-8).

Timber harvest in productive old growth may reduce biological diversity by shifting the agestructure of the forest by replacing old growth trees with younger trees, changing the composition of understory vegetation, and removing key habitat features such as large decadent trees, snags, and downed logs (see page 3-73).

With the Roadless Rule in place, the current Forest Plan harvest levels would be maintained at about 42,500 acres of old growth and 284,000 acres of young growth over 100 years. Suitable high-volume POG and suitable large-tree POG would be about 96,000 acres and 31,000 acres, respectively. Overall impacts due to fragmentation and on the Old-growth Habitat Conservation Strategy are expected to be minor and consistent with the existing Forest Plan (see pages 3-74, 3- 75).

With the Roadless Rule removed and the timber suitability change, implementation of the Forest Plan estimates harvest levels similar to the level projected with the Roadless Rule in place, about 42,500 acres of old growth and 284,000 acres of young growth over 100 years. Additionally, with the timber suitability change, the total suitable acres of young growth would increase by about 20,000 acres or about 6 percent relative to estimates with the Roadless Rule in place and suitable old-growth acres would increase by about 168,000 acres or about 74 percent. By removing the Roadless Rule, roads and timber harvest are likely to penetrate much farther into roadless areas, resulting in a greater degree of fragmentation (see pages 3-83, 3-84).

With the removal of the Roadless Rule there is the potential for young-growth harvest within the reserve system, beach and estuary fringe, or riparian management areas to affect the integrity of the Old-growth Habitat Conservation Strategy under the Forest Plan. Effects can include reduced functionality of these areas, reduced or fragmented buffers, and increased edge effects. For the action alternatives, the acres of suitable young growth in these special areas would not increase that much because most young growth occurs outside of roadless areas (see page 3-110). Because overall harvest levels would not change relative to keeping the Roadless Rule in place and because the broader Old-growth Habitat Conservation Strategy for the Tongass was developed prior to the roadless rule and would be maintained under the Forest Plan, the general effects of removing the Roadless Rule on wildlife and the Conservation Strategy are expected to be relatively low but greater than projected for the no-action alternative (retaining the rule) (page 3-84).

###### Forest Carbon

This scope and degree of timber harvest effects on forest carbon would be similar and minor whether the Roadless Rule is in place or not, affecting an estimated 3.5 percent of the 9.7 million acres of forested land, 6 percent of all productive forest land, and less than 1 percent of productive old growth forests on the Tongass over 100 years (based on the assumption that harvest occurs at the Projected Timber Sale Quantity rate over the next 100 years). The average harvest rate over the next 100 years would be about 425 acres per year for old growth and 2,842 acres per year for young growth (with more old growth and less young growth in the early years and the opposite in later years). Because all of the alternatives would result in the same harvest volumes, effects are expected to be similar with or without the rule. Given the fact that the average harvest rate expected over the next 100 years is low by historical standards it is logical to assume that the rate of carbon loss due to disturbances would also be lower, because timber harvesting has been the dominant disturbance type.

##### **Fire and Fuels Management**

Not discussed in the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule FEIS.

##### **Invasive Species**

Invasive species are discussed on pages 3-157 through 3-158 and 3-160 through 3-162. The potential for the introduction and spread of invasive plant species due to ground disturbance associated with timber harvest, road construction, and other development or management activity exists whether the Roadless Rule is in place or not. Timber harvest and road construction could potentially lead to the introduction and spread of invasive species where these species do not currently exist.

##### **Soil, Water, and Air**

Physical values (soil, water, and air) as a roadless characteristic are discussed on page 3-15. Potential effects to water are discussed within the fish analysis on pages 3-129 through 3-136.

Removing the Roadless Rule would have a slightly larger potential for adverse effects to physical resources than keeping the Roadless Rule in place because it is anticipated that there would be slightly more road development. However, the differences would be minor because project-level analysis would use best management practices and land management plan standards and guidelines to lessen or mitigate effects. Most of the potential effects would be for a short duration, with disturbed soil areas rehabilitated after projects are completed in those areas.

Road construction, road drainage, level of road use, number of road stream crossings, watershed road density, and related actions in forested areas may all influence the amount of sediment to streams. Density of roads is also a major factor in considering watershed condition. New road construction and road density would be similar whether the Roadless Rule is in place or not. Under existing conditions (with the Roadless Rule in place), average road density is about 0.19 miles per mile square mile. With the Roadless Rule removed the average road density is anticipated to increase to about 0.23 mile per square mile (see Table 3.3-21). This would translate into about 90 percent of all subwatersheds on the Tongass National Forest with road densities in the “properly functioning” range (less than 1 mile of road per square mile).

##### **Biodiversity**

Biological Diversity is discussed on pages 3-61 through 3-88. Effects to wildlife are discussed on pages 3-89 through 3-218. Effects to Fish are discussed on pages 3-129 through 3-142. Sensitive plants pages 3-155 through 3-158, and 3-160 through 3-162.

###### Threatened, Endangered, and Sensitive Wildlife Species

On the Tongass National Forest all Endangered Species Act listed species are associated with the offshore marine environment adjacent to the National Forest. There are no listed threatened or endangered species that occupy the terrestrial or aquatic ecosystems systems managed by the Tongass National Forest except for a few isolated haul-out locations for the Stellar sea lions associated with the western distinct population segment (DPS). The review of listed species and habitats for the action alternatives associated with this analysis were found to have ‘no effect’ to any threatened or endangered species or designated critical habitat and therefore there is no need for consultation with either the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service or National Marine Fisheries Service. The National Marine Fisheries Service reviewed the biological assessment for threatened and endangered species under their regulatory jurisdiction and concluded that the 2016 Tongass Forest Plan Amendment EIS was “not likely to adversely affect” threatened or endangered species occurring on the Tongass (the 2020 Alaska FEIS summarizes the analysis on wildlife species and their habitat from the 2016 Forest Plan Amendment EIS).

###### Threatened, Endangered, and Sensitive Fish Species

No federally listed fish species or stocks originate from Alaska streams. However, some federally listed fish stocks may occur in marine waters within the boundary of the Tongass National Forest. There currently are no fish species designated as sensitive species in the Alaska Region (the Alaska Region Sensitive Species List was updated in 2009).

###### Effects to Non-TES Fish Species

Roads pose the greatest risk to fish resources on the Tongass, partly because they pose the largest risk of management-caused sediment input to streams. Density of roads is also a major factor in considering watershed condition as indicated by the Forest Service Watershed Conditions Framework. The amount of new road construction estimated in the 2020 Alaska FEIS would be similar under all alternatives. Overall, the potential effects to fish from road construction, reconstruction, and maintenance under projects that could be approved in the future would be similar among all alternatives and would be evaluated on a project-specific basis.

Overall effects to fish habitat are expected to be minimal whether the Roadless Rule is in place or not, because of the strong protections to fish habitats provided by Forest Plan direction and the riparian management strategy. Localized effects on fish habitat may occur, but these are expected to be minimal overall with low long-term risk. Any potential site-specific effects will be addressed under separate site-specific NEPA analysis, as this assessment will not authorize any site-specific actions.

###### Sensitive and Rare Plants

An increase in road miles that could result from removing the Roadless Rule could lead to indirect effects to sensitive plants such as increased off-road vehicle use, increased access, and increased use and associated trampling of plants.

##### **Recreation**

Recreation use and Tourism are discussed on pages 3-56 and 3-192 to 3-193. Under the 2001 Roadless Rule, most projected harvest is expected to occur in ROS settings where some modification of the natural environment is expected. Less than 1 percent of the acres currently allocated to Primitive (P), Semi-Primitive Non-Motorized (SPNM), and Semi-Primitive Motorized (SPM) ROS settings would be harvested after 100 years, assuming the maximum allowable levels of harvest were to occur. With removal of roadless prohibitions, recreational visitors with an expectation of a remote experience would be most affected by timber harvest in Primitive, SPNM, and SPM settings. With removing the roadless rule and changes in timber suitability, there would be an increase of SPNM acres, as a share of the estimated total potential timber harvest to 18 percent. With removal of the Roadless Rule, nearly all new roads constructed would be closed following timber harvest, so would not be available for highway or high-clearance vehicles, but may be available for access by other methods, though any potential increase in recreation access may be limited by road closures.

Impacts specific to outfitter/guide activities are on pages 3-56 through 3-57 and 3-181; also page 3-185, 3-193 through 3-220). Outfitter/guide activities have the potential to be affected by changes in future timber harvest allowed by removing the Roadless Rule. In most of the existing outfitter/guide use areas, harvest that could already occur in these areas (with the Roadless Rule in place) has the potential to conflict with existing outfitter/guide use. By removing the Roadless Rule, and thereby expanding the acres available for harvest, there is an increased potential for impacts to outfitter/guide use by increasing the number and geographic extent of the acres affected.

##### **Scenery**

Scenery is discussed under Key Issue 1 on page 3-11 (table 3.1-1) and page 3-16. Scenery is also discussed on pages 3-221 through 3-238. Effects to viewsheds are discussed by analyzing selected viewsheds on pages 3-224 through 3-237. Road construction and timber harvest can have varying degrees of adverse effects on the scenic integrity of a landscape. Removing the Roadless Rule would likely result in more road development to reach more remote places, which would have a greater adverse effect on scenery than with less road development with the Roadless Rule in place. There is not a big difference in the amount of estimated road mileage whether the Roadless Rule is in place or not.

No changes in the Scenic Integrity Objectives (SIOs) or related scenery standards and guidelines are proposed with the removal of the Roadless Rule. Harvest of old growth would follow the SIOs of the 2016 Forest Plan.

Removing the Roadless Rule would have the highest effects on scenery in most viewsheds. Viewsheds with the highest potential for negative effects from young-growth harvest include Chatham Strait, Duncan Canal, Frederick Sound, Lynn Canal, and Stikine Strait, based on both the percent increase and the increase in acreage of young growth suitable and projected harvest relative to keeping the Roadless Rule in place. Viewsheds with the highest potential for negative effects from old-growth harvest include Duncan Canal, Eastern Passage, Frederick Sound, Stikine Strait, Tenakee Inlet to Tenakee Springs, and Zimovia Strait, based on both the percent increase and the increase in acreage of old growth suitable and projected harvest relative to keeping the Roadless Rule in place.

##### **Cultural Resources, Tribal Rights and Interests**

Traditional cultural properties and sacred sites are discussed on page 3-16 and pages 3-254 through 3-255. There would be an increased risk to cultural resources with the removal of the Roadless Rule because of potentially greater road lengths. There would also be an increased risk from potential activity in areas currently and previously protected from development and harvest activities with the Roadless Rule.

Access to native cultural sites, for tree harvest for Alaska Native cultural purposes, and for rural subsistence activities is discussed on p. 3-58 through 3-60. Also see table 3.2-10. Subsistence resources provide the foundation for Native culture, forming the basis for different clans and potlatch ceremonies, as well as reinforcing basic values of respect for the earth and its resources. The Tongass, with nearly 17 million acres of largely undeveloped land, includes extensive subsistence resources.

Timber cutting, sale, or removal is prohibited in IRAs with the Roadless Rule in place, with some exceptions if the regional forester or designated forest officer determines that one of a limited number of circumstances exists (as specified in 66 FR 3244 § 294.13). These circumstances include the provision of trees, portions of trees, or forest products to Indian tribes free of charge for traditional and cultural purposes. The timber cutting, sale, or removal exception for the provision of trees to Indian tribes for traditional and cultural purposes would remain in place with the Roadless Rule removed.

Removing the roadless rule would not require specific exceptions for access to Alaska Native cultural sites or to harvest trees important for cultural purposes. With the Roadless Rule not in place there would be the opportunity for an increase in timber harvest in some places. Access for cultural purposes has benefits but may increase competition with the timber industry for trees to be used for traditional or cultural purposes, and competition is the greater concern from the Tribes. Competition is noted as a concern from the Tribes. The type of access allowed with the Roadless Rule in place is preferable to Tribes compared to the access created by removing the Roadless Rule.

##### **Economic Effects**

Local economic factors are discussed on pages 3-46 through 3-47. The proportion of timber harvest occurring in roadless areas would vary with the Roadless Rule in place or not, but overall regional economic impacts are assumed to remain constant. These impacts were estimated for the first decade following implementation in the 2016 Forest Plan Amendment EIS, with an estimated annual average harvest of 46 MMBF.

A similar range of direct jobs and income are assumed to be supported whether the Roadless Rule is in place or not. Based on the 2016 Forest Plan Amendment EIS assessment, all the alternatives would support an estimated 92 jobs in logging, 49 to 100 jobs in sawmilling, and 29 to 46 jobs related to transportation and other services, with direct income ranging from $9.8 million to $10.4 million.

Within the context of Southeast Alaska’s seasonal and cyclical resource-based employment, subsistence harvest of fish and wildlife resources takes on special importance. The use of these resources may play a major role in supplementing cash incomes during periods when the opportunity to participate in the wage economy is either marginal or nonexistent. Because of high prices of commercial products provided through the retail sector of the cash economy, especially in remote communities, the economic role of locally available fish and game takes on added importance. Subsistence use of fish and wildlife has been and continues to be an important component of the economies of Southeast Alaska communities. Removing the Roadless Rule could have impacts on the amount and distribution of subsistence resources.

##### **Subsistence Use and Alaska National Interest Lands Conservation Act (ANILCA)**

Pages 3-239 to 3-252 considered effects related to subsistence, as defined in the Alaska National Interest Lands Conservation Act of 1980 (ANILCA) Section 803. It includes a Forest-wide evaluation that assesses the three factors related to subsistence uses identified by ANILCA: abundance and distribution, access, and competition.

ANILCA requires the evaluation of the potential effects of certain actions on subsistence resources and uses and needs on federal lands in Alaska, related to three factors: abundance and distribution, access, and competition. Section 810 of ANILCA (16 U.S.C. 3120) provides that in determining whether to withdraw, reserve, lease, or otherwise permit the use, occupancy, or disposition of public lands under any provision of law authorizing such actions, the head of the Federal agency shall evaluate the effect of such use, occupancy, or disposition on subsistence uses and needs, the availability of other lands for the purposes sought to be achieved, and other alternatives which would reduce or eliminate the use, occupancy, or disposition of public lands needed for subsistence purposes. Section 810 also specifies that if the “withdrawal, reservation, lease, permit, or other use, occupancy or disposition” of Federal lands “would significantly restrict subsistence uses,” the agency must take certain additional steps. Specifically, the agency must give notice to the appropriate State agency and the appropriate local committees and regional councils and give notice of, and hold, a hearing in the vicinity of the area involved, and determine that (1) such a significant restriction of subsistence uses is necessary, consistent with sound management principles for the utilization of the public lands, (2) the proposed activity will involve the minimal amount of public lands necessary to accomplish the purposes of such use, occupancy, or other disposition, and (3) reasonable steps will be taken to minimize adverse impacts upon subsistence uses and resources resulting from such actions.

An ANILCA Section 810 (16 U.S.C. § 3120) evaluation and determination is not required for approval of the proposed rule, which is a rulemaking process and a programmatic level decision and not a determination whether to “withdraw, reserve, lease, or otherwise permit the use, occupancy, or disposition” of National Forest land. Therefore, this EIS, as part of that rulemaking process, does not require an ANILCA Section 810 evaluation and determination. However, the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule FEIS (pages 3-239 to 3-252) and 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule (85 FR 68692) voluntarily considered a forest-wide evaluation in accordance with ANILCA Section 810. ANILCA subsistence hearings were conducted for the DEIS and proposed rule, consistent with Section 810 in 2019.

Alternatives 2 and 3 in this DEIS propose the same action, relative to the inventoried roadless areas on the Tongass National Forest, as the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule. The previous analyses were reviewed and are still relevant (see Appendix 4 – Review of the Final Environmental Impact Statement for the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule).

### **Appendix 1 – IRA Acreage Summarized by Region**

This appendix provides more detail about the potentially affected inventoried roadless areas. For each Forest Service Region, the tables show the 2001 Roadless Rule acres (based on the official national maps maintained by Forest Service headquarters and posted on the Forest Service Roadless Area website (https://www.fs.usda.gov/managing-land/planning/roadless). The tables also show the potentially affected acres – this is smaller than the total IRA because it removes the non-NFS lands as well as Congressionally designated areas, as described in Potentially Affected Environment section.

As stated in the Scope of the Analysis section, the analysis in the EIS focuses on the difference where reasonably foreseeable activities, particularly road construction and timber harvest, **are more or less likely to occur** within inventoried roadless areas under the alternatives, and the resulting differences in ecological and social effects. Estimates of areas where activities are more likely, were prepared for effects analysis purposes (see National Forest System Roads, Vegetation and Forest Management and other sections) – they do not reflect proposed or planned activity areas. The tables display two key factors evaluated: areas where plans currently allow these activities and areas with potential timber harvest operability.

#### Current Land Management Plan Direction

Land management plans were analyzed to better understand where geographically the applicable plan direction applies and what timber management and road construction uses may or may not be allowed according to those plans. Land management plans include plan direction that may apply to the entire national forest or grassland in the form of a Forest-wide plan component. They may also include plan direction that only apply to a specific management area, geographic area, and designated area. Plan components include desired conditions, standards, guidelines, goals, and objectives, which may constrain certain uses or activities to meet the management intent for that area to varying degrees (see Relationship of the 2001 Roadless Rule to Land Management Planning).

Some examples of more recently revised plans under the 2012 Planning Rule are included in the table below – because the Plans are variable due to differences in local conditions and interest, the age of the plan, or the policy in which the plan is developed under, not all plans contain language similar to these examples.

For both timber harvest and road construction, the forestwide, management area, geographic area, and designated area plan components were reviewed. Across the potentially affected IRAs, the lands were categorized in the following four categories. Where multiple plan components apply to a given area, the most restrictive category was applied.

- Not allowed: Use is prohibited, except where needed for reserved and outstanding rights or other exemptions mandated by law, regulation, or policy. These include plan standards that are mandatory. For example, timber harvest and road construction is prohibited in these types of areas:

- ‘key watersheds’ under the Northwest Forest Plan

- designated and recommended wilderness areas, research natural areas, and backcountry non-motorized areas

- Generally not allowed: Use is generally restricted based on a desired condition or guideline and not a mandatory restriction. For example, timber harvest and road construction is generally not allowed in these types of areas:

- denning habitat for species

- areas that protect soil and watershed conditions

- areas that avoid interfering with natural processes in certain ecological areas

- areas that protect recreation experiences, facilities, and scenic resources

- Generally allowed: Use is generally not restricted, except under some specific exceptions based on the purpose of and need for the use, or use limitations, or protection of natural resource values. For example, these types of areas generally allow timber harvest and road construction:

- grazing, hunting, big game, and wild horse and burro areas

- winter ranges

- early successional areas

- adaptive management areas

- Allowed. Use is allowed for any multiple-use management need, where consistent with law, regulations, or policy. For example, these types of areas allow for timber harvest and road construction:

- General forest and active management areas

- Areas designated for multiple uses

This is a national review at a programmatic level to help compare alternatives by looking at plan direction specific to road construction and timber harvest. Land management plans do not compel action or authorize projects and future decisions would be subject to local interpretation of the plan and site-specific project analysis. It is important to note that the Potentially Affected Environment and the discussion of ‘areas where plans allow’ activities across the Environmental Impacts do not include the consideration of all of the individual land management plan direction that is oftentimes unique to local conditions which may not directly limit road construction or timber harvest but may still influence planning and design of future site-specific projects. For example, the relationship of how forests manage habitat conditions for spotted owl or grizzly bear are not reflected in this analysis.

#### Potential Timber Harvest Operability

Operability indicates the potential timber harvest opportunity (or likelihood) in the potentially affected environment. A nationwide geospatial analysis was used to estimate potential operability, or the capability of mechanized treatment or activities, in each area based on slope, distance from roads, site productivity, and statutory restrictions – see Potential Timber Harvest Opportunity.

- Likely Operable: Potentially forest areas that are less than 0.5 miles from a road, on slopes less than 40 percent, are productive for restocking (greater than 20 cubic feet/acre/year), and have no statutory restrictions from timber harvest.

- Likely Operable but Complex: Potentially forested areas that are less than 0.5 miles from a road and on slopes between 40 percent and 70 percent or between 0.5 miles and one mile from a road and on slopes less than 70 percent. These areas have the same productivity for restocking as likely operable areas, and have no statutory restrictions from timber harvest.

- Likely Not Operable: Potentially forested areas that are further than 1 mile from a road, within one mile that exceed 70 percent slope, are not productive for restocking, or include statutory restrictions from timber harvest.

The potential operability is for analysis purposes only, actual feasibility and need for timber harvest would be determined locally based on many factors.

### **Appendix 2 – Rule Language by Alternative**

The following provides representational rule language for all alternatives. Final rule language could vary from what is presented in this appendix based on comments received and other considerations.

#### Alternative 1 – 2001 Roadless Rule (Existing Rule)

Refer to the 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule, available at: <u>https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2001/01/12/01-726/special-areas-roadless-areaconservation.</u>

##### **Subpart B—Protection of Inventoried Roadless Areas**

294.10 Purpose.

294.11 Definitions.

294.12 Prohibition on road construction and road reconstruction in inventoried roadless areas.

294.13 Prohibition on timber cutting, sale, or removal in inventoried roadless areas.

294.14 Scope and applicability.

###### Authority

16 U.S.C. 472, 529, 551, 1608, 1613; 23 U.S.C. 201, 205. § 294.10 Purpose

The purpose of this subpart is to provide, within the context of multiple-use management, lasting protection for inventoried roadless areas within the National Forest System.

###### § 294.11 Definitions

The following terms and definitions apply to this subpart:

**Inventoried roadless areas:** Areas identified in a set of inventoried roadless area maps, contained in Forest Service Roadless Area Conservation, Final Environmental Impact Statement, Volume 2, dated November 2000, which are held at the National headquarters office of the Forest Service, or any subsequent update or revision of those maps.

**Responsible official:** The Forest Service line officer with the authority and responsibility to make decisions regarding protection and management of inventoried roadless areas pursuant to this subpart.

**Road:** A motor vehicle travelway over 50 inches wide, unless designated and managed as a trail. A road may be classified, unclassified, or temporary.

(1) **Classified road:** A road wholly or partially within or adjacent to National Forest System lands that is determined to be needed for long-term motor vehicle access, including State roads, county roads, privately owned roads, National Forest System roads, and other roads authorized by the Forest Service.

(2) **Unclassified road:** A road on National Forest System lands that is not managed as part of the forest transportation system, such as unplanned roads, abandoned travelways, and off-road vehicle tracks that have not been designated and managed as a trail; and those roads that were once under permit or other authorization and were not decommissioned upon the termination of the authorization.

(3) **Temporary road.** A road authorized by contract, permit, lease, other written authorization, or emergency operation, not intended to be part of the forest transportation system and not necessary for long-term resource management.

**Road construction:** Activity that results in the addition of forest classified or temporary road miles.

**Road maintenance:** The ongoing upkeep of a road necessary to retain or restore the road to the approved road management objective.

**Road reconstruction:** Activity that results in improvement or realignment of an existing classified road defined as follows:

- (1) **Road improvement:** Activity that results in an increase of an existing road's traffic service level, expansion of its capacity, or a change in its original design function.

- (2) **Road realignment:** Activity that results in a new location of an existing road or portions of an existing road, and treatment of the old roadway.

**Roadless area characteristics:** Resources or features that are often present in and characterize inventoried roadless areas, including:

- (1) High-quality or undisturbed soil, water, and air;

- (2) Sources of public drinking water;

- (3) Diversity of plant and animal communities;

- (4) Habitat for threatened, endangered, proposed, candidate, and sensitive species and for those species dependent on large, undisturbed areas of land;

- (5) Primitive, semi-primitive non-motorized and semi-primitive motorized classes of dispersed recreation;

- (6) Reference landscapes;

- (7) Natural appearing landscapes with high scenic quality;

- (8) Traditional cultural properties and sacred sites; and

- (9) Other locally identified unique characteristics.

§ 294.12 Prohibition on road construction and road reconstruction in inventoried roadless areas

(a) A road may not be constructed or reconstructed in inventoried roadless areas of the National Forest System, except as provided in paragraph (b) of this section.

(b) Notwithstanding the prohibition in paragraph (a) of this section, a road may be constructed or reconstructed in an inventoried roadless area if the responsible official determines that one of the following circumstances exists:

(1) A road is needed to protect public health and safety in cases of an imminent threat of flood, fire, or other catastrophic event that, without intervention, would cause the loss of life or property;

(2) A road is needed to conduct a response action under the Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability Act (CERCLA) or to conduct a natural resource restoration action under CERCLA, Section 311 of the Clean Water Act, or the Oil Pollution Act;

(3) A road is needed pursuant to reserved or outstanding rights, or as provided for by statute or treaty;

(4) Road realignment is needed to prevent irreparable resource damage that arises from the design, location, use, or deterioration of a classified road and that cannot be mitigated by road maintenance. Road realignment may occur under this paragraph only if the road is deemed essential for public or private access, natural resource management, or public health and safety;

(5) Road reconstruction is needed to implement a road safety improvement project on a classified road determined to be hazardous on the basis of accident experience or accident potential on that road;

(6) The Secretary of Agriculture determines that a Federal Aid Highway project, authorized pursuant to Title 23 of the United States Code, is in the public interest or is consistent with the purposes for which the land was reserved or acquired and no other reasonable and prudent alternative exists; or

(7) A road is needed in conjunction with the continuation, extension, or renewal of a mineral lease on lands that are under lease by the Secretary of the Interior as of January 12, 2001, or for a new lease issued immediately upon expiration of an existing lease. Such road construction or reconstruction must be conducted in a manner that minimizes effects on surface resources, prevents unnecessary or unreasonable surface disturbance, and complies with all applicable lease requirements, land and resource management plan direction, regulations, and laws. Roads constructed or reconstructed pursuant to this paragraph must be obliterated when no longer needed for the purposes of the lease or upon termination or expiration of the lease, whichever is sooner.

(c) Maintenance of classified roads is permissible in inventoried roadless areas.

§ 294.13 Prohibition on timber cutting, sale, or removal in inventoried roadless areas (a) Timber may not be cut, sold, or removed in inventoried roadless areas of the National Forest System, except as provided in paragraph (b) of this section.

(b) Notwithstanding the prohibition in paragraph (a) of this section, timber may be cut, sold, or removed in inventoried roadless areas if the responsible official determines that one of the following circumstances exists. The cutting, sale, or removal of timber in these areas is expected to be infrequent.

(1) The cutting, sale, or removal of generally small diameter timber is needed for one of the following purposes and will maintain or improve one or more of the roadless area characteristics as defined in § 294.11.

(i) To improve threatened, endangered, proposed, or sensitive species habitat; or

(ii) To maintain or restore the characteristics of ecosystem composition and structure, such as to reduce the risk of uncharacteristic wildfire effects, within the range of variability that would be expected to occur under natural disturbance regimes of the current climatic period;

(2) The cutting, sale, or removal of timber is incidental to the implementation of a management activity not otherwise prohibited by this subpart;

(3) The cutting, sale, or removal of timber is needed and appropriate for personal or administrative use, as provided for in 36 CFR part 223; or

(4) Roadless characteristics have been substantially altered in a portion of an inventoried roadless area due to the construction of a classified road and subsequent timber harvest. Both the road construction and subsequent timber harvest must have occurred after the area was designated an inventoried roadless area and prior to January 12, 2001. Timber may be cut, sold, or removed only in the substantially altered portion of the inventoried roadless area.

§ 294.14 Scope and applicability

(a) This subpart does not revoke, suspend, or modify any permit, contract, or other legal instrument authorizing the occupancy and use of National Forest System land issued prior to January 12, 2001.

(b) This subpart does not compel the amendment or revision of any land and resource management plan.

(c) This subpart does not revoke, suspend, or modify any project or activity decision made prior to January 12, 2001.

(d) This subpart does not apply to road construction, reconstruction, or the cutting, sale, or removal of timber in inventoried roadless areas on the Tongass National Forest if a notice of availability of a draft environmental impact statement for such activities has been published in the Federal Register prior to January 12, 2001.

(e) The prohibitions and restrictions established in this subpart are not subject to reconsideration, revision, or rescission in subsequent project decisions or land and resource management plan amendments or revisions undertaken pursuant to 36 CFR part 219.

(f) If any provision of the rules in this subpart or its application to any person or to certain circumstances is held invalid, the remainder of the regulations in this subpart and their application remain in force.

#### Alternative 2 – Proposed Rule

PART 294—SPECIAL AREAS

The authority citation for part 294 continues to read as follows:

Authority: 16 U.S.C. 472, 529, 551, 1608, 1613; 23 U.S.C. 201, 205.

Subpart B—[Removed and Reserved]

Remove and reserve subpart B, consisting of §§ 294.10 through 294.18.

294.13 Prohibition on timber cutting, sale, or removal in inventoried roadless areas.
