###### **(g) [ADDITION] Boundary modifications**

a. Modifications to boundaries: Responsible officials, working with local government, Tribes, and interested parties, may propose boundary modification to the Chief. Modifications and additions approved by the Chief will be reflected in the set of maps maintained at the national headquarters office of the Forest Service. Modifications of inventoried roadless area boundaries may be made to correspond with geographic features, land management plan management areas, or for other reasons. A boundary modification is not required to perform work done under an exception pursuant to §§ 294.12(b) or 294.13(b). Public notice with a minimum 30-day comment period will be provided for any proposed boundary modifications.

b. Administrative corrections to boundaries: The Chief of the Forest Service may issue administrative corrections. Administrative corrections to the maps of any inventoried roadless area are adjustments to correct clerical errors, to conform with new statutory or regulatory requirements, or to correspond with changes based on improvements in mapping technology.

### **Appendix 3 – Notice of Intent Comment Summary**

#### Introduction

This appendix provides a summary of written comments received in response to the publication of the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) notice of intent (NOI) to prepare an Environmental Impact Statement (EIS) for the proposed 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule Rescission. It captures concerns identified in the written comments received during the comment period and the range of perspectives but is not exhaustive of all written comments.

#### Public Comment Period Overview

The Department published a NOI to inform the public that it would prepare an EIS on August 29, 2025, initiating a 21-day comment period. The NOI requested public comments on alternatives or effects and on relevant information, studies, or analyses with respect to the proposal (7 CFR 1.7(b)). Comments received in response to the NOI were used to inform the development of a range of alternatives and the analysis of environmental impacts.

Over 220,000 comment letters were received during the NOI comment period. These comments came from Federally Recognized Tribes and Alaska Native Corporations and Tribal members; members of Congress and other elected officials; Federal, State, and local governments; and businesses, organizations, and individuals. Throughout this report, these commenters and the comments are generally referred to as “commenters” or “public comments”, without identifying the commenter type. In addition to sending letters directly requesting government to government consultation, some Tribes and Tribal organizations submitted comments during the comment period via the online portal. Any letters received from Tribes that were submitted via the online comment portal are also summarized here. Letters received through other methods and information received in consultation are not summarized (see Tribal section of DEIS). Counting all signatures submitted with the petitions as individual submissions, the letters represent over 625,000 submissions.

Of the comment letters received, approximately 99 percent were generally opposed to the proposed rule recission, while approximately 1 percent were generally in support. Those containing substantive<sup>72</sup> comments related to the NEPA process, potential alternatives, and/or specific issues<sup>73</sup> to be addressed in the EIS are summarized below. Similar issues and recommendations are grouped or paraphrased.

72 Substantive – as defined in the USDA NEPA regulations 7 CFR 1b.11 (a)(53) “Substantive means information that meaningfully informs the consideration of reasonably foreseeable impacts on the human environment and the resulting significance determination or decisions on how to proceed (i.e., alternatives to be considered or analyzed or the alternative selected for implementation).”

73 Issue definition from the USDA NEPA regulations 7 CFR 1b.11(a)(23) “Issue means a logical cause-effect relationship between the actions proposed (cause) and the reasonably foreseeable impacts (effect) on resources found in the affected environment. An issue may be addressed by modifying the proposed action, developing an action alternative, or supplementing, improving, or modifying the analysis to better understand the effects.”

All NOI comments were reviewed, coded, and analyzed to develop the below overarching themes, concerns, and recommendations. The agency responded by:

- Modifying the proposed action, developing action alternatives, or considering additional alternatives;

- Using information to inform/conduct the analysis (including consideration of various literature/references cited);

- Making factual corrections or updating content to read more clearly and be more readily understood; or

- Considering but making no change because the concern/recommendation was already accounted for, outside the scope of the rulemaking, and/or outside the authority of the Department/agency.

#### Comment Themes

##### **Legal, Process, and Other Environmental Review and Compliance Concerns**

Commenters raise numerous concerns about compliance with NEPA and other Federal legal requirements.

Commenters raised concerns that the public involvement process and comment period duration for the Notice of Intent restricted meaningful input in comparison to the extensive process used for the original rule. Commenters sought a longer comment period, public meetings, government-to-government consultation, and inclusion of Tribes, states, and local governments as cooperating agencies.

Commenters were concerned about compliance with the Administrative Procedure Act, Endangered Species Act, National Forest Management Act, Clean Water Act, and the agency’s multiple-use mandate. Some commenters said that the 2001 rule itself exceeds statutory authority by functioning as de facto wilderness designation. Commenters also expressed concern about potential conflicts with Alaska National Interest Lands Conservation Act.

The NRPM and DEIS include description of the Agencies and Persons Consulted.

The NRPM and DEIS provide preliminary findings regarding compliance with various relevant laws and policies (Other Environmental Review and Consultation Requirements). The FEIS and Final Rule will include final determinations.

##### **Purpose and Need**

Commenters expressed concern that the stated purpose and need are inadequately justified, noting that the existing rule already allows exceptions for fire suppression, fuel reduction, and habitat improvement. Commenters question the scientific basis for rescission, asserting that longterm monitoring supports retaining the rule.

Local Involvement and Decision-Making vs. National Consistency

There were conflicting views about whether management of inventoried roadless areas should remain governed by a national standard or be shifted to local decision-makers. Commenters wrote that the 2001 Roadless Rule provides a consistent, science-based framework that ensures stable, nationwide conservation protections. They expressed concern that recission would create a fragmented patchwork of policies vulnerable to local political and economic pressures and potential favoring of short-term extractive interests over long-term national environmental priorities. Commenters also expressed concern that many local offices lack the staffing and resources to carry out the additional environmental reviews required, making decentralized management burdensome and potentially less effective.

Others commented that local or state authorities are better positioned to tailor management to site-specific conditions through the forest planning process. They contend that returning authority to local Forest Supervisors would improve responsiveness and align decisions with local needs.

The Notice of Proposed Rulemaking and DEIS include minor clarifications to the purpose and need for the proposed rulemaking. The DEIS provides additional information regarding IRA management (Background) transportation system costs (National Forest System Roads), management needs and flexibility (Vegetation and Forest Management).

##### **Range of Alternatives and Recommendations**

Commenters expressed concern that the NOI described only two alternatives: full rescission of the 2001 Roadless Rule (proposed action) and retaining the rule (no-action alternative). They suggested the Department evaluate a broader range of alternatives, including variations of the noaction alternative, alternative forest management strategies to achieve the purpose and need, and various action alternatives.

###### Variations of the No-action Alternative

Commenters proposed several variations on the no-action alternative. There were recommendations for a strengthened rule alternative that would expand the scope of the rule by removing exceptions and prohibiting additional activities, including mining, grazing, oil and gas development, and motorized use. Some commenters sought permanent protective designations similar to the National Wilderness Preservation System.

There were comments suggesting alternatives focused on addressing the existing road maintenance backlog, prioritizing road repair and decommissioning unsafe or failing roads. Some commenters proposed revising Forest Service Manuals, Handbooks and other policies to improve oversight and increases in active management using existing exceptions.

Alternative Forest Management Strategies (non-rulemaking) to Achieve the Purpose and Need

Commenters recommended non-road-dependent management strategies to meet forest health and fire safety goals including using aircraft, drones, or other technologies. Additional alternatives include a no-net-roads policy requiring decommissioning equivalent or greater road mileage for any new construction and limiting development to existing transportation corridors. Some commenters sought prioritizing resource extraction only in areas already roaded or previously developed to avoid new ecological disturbance in roadless landscapes.

Suggested Rulemaking Alternatives to meet the Purpose and Need

Many alternatives suggested modifying the rule’s prohibitions, exceptions, or application to provide opportunity for local decision-makers to address pressing management issues, especially wildfire risk mitigation.

**Modified Rule for Targeted Access** – Commenters proposed a hybrid alternative designed to address management concerns (like wildfire risk) without sacrificing roadless protections. These suggested alternatives involve retaining the roadless character but modifying the rule to allow specific, limited actions under strict environmental oversight.

**Time-Bounded, Decommissioned Access for Fuels Treatments** – Comments suggest the Department analyze an alternative that retains the Roadless rule while enabling timebounded, decommission-on-completion access for hazardous fuel reduction near communities.

**Wildland Urban-Interface-Limited Alternative** – Comments suggest the Department analyze an alternative strictly limiting changes to the wildland-urban interface (WUI) for fuels management.

**Refine Existing Exceptions** – Comments suggest the Department analyze an alternative modifying the existing 2001 Roadless Rule by refining or expanding its current exceptions for specific management needs, such as allowing for ecologically based restoration needs or forest thinning in the WUI.

**Modified Boundaries** – Comments suggest the Department analyze an alternative redrawing roadless boundaries to exclude existing roads, or to exclude roaded areas and note the inability to have boundaries modified under the existing rule. Commenters suggest the Department consider alternatives involving partial rescission or boundary modification to remove the land base with high timber production values and/or areas near at-risk communities while retaining boundaries that protect high-value watersheds and biodiversity.

###### **Regional or State-Specific Rules**

Commenters suggested the Department analyze an alternative allowing regional, Tribal, or state-specific rules, such as those modeled after the Idaho and Colorado Roadless Rules, which involve tiered protections or a petition process.

The Proposed Action and Alternatives section describes the alternatives considered in detail, as well as those not considered in detail with a brief rationale.

##### **Road Maintenance and Transportation System Costs**

Commenters expressed concerns about the financial burden of expanding the road network into inventoried roadless areas, emphasizing that the Department already struggles to maintain its vast existing road system. Commenters note that the current deferred maintenance backlog totals billions of dollars, leaving the agency able to maintain only a fraction of its roads each year and argue that building new roads would add to that burden.

Conversely, some commenters contend that the 2001 Roadless Rule contributes to maintenance challenges by restricting reconstruction or repairs in certain areas, leading to higher costs for access using specialized means, such as helicopters. These commenters argue that rescinding the rule could support more efficient maintenance.

The National Forest System Roads section includes road construction and maintenance financial impacts.

##### **Tribal and Cultural Resources**

Commenters were concerned that rescinding the 2001 Roadless Rule could harm Indigenous rights, cultural heritage and subsistence practices by allowing road construction in culturally significant or traditionally used areas. Commenters emphasized the importance of upholding Federal Tribal trust responsibilities and conducting meaningful and early government-togovernment consultation, noting concerns about compliance with the National Historic Preservation Act, Executive Order 13175, and subsistence protections under the Alaska National Interest Lands Conservation Act.

Commenters were concerned that new development and increased access could infringe on treaty-protected rights to hunt, fish, and gather; undermine Tribal sovereignty and food security by degrading habitats essential to subsistence species such as salmon, game animals, and culturally important plants; and damage or destroy sacred sites, archeological resources, ancestral homelands, and other cultural properties, causing irreversible loss to indigenous cultural and spiritual heritage.

The Consultation with Federally Recognized Tribes and Alaska Native Corporations section describes Tribal consultation. The Tribal Rights and Interests subsection includes effects on Indigenous people and various interests as well as treaty-protected rights. The Cultural and Historic Resources section includes impacts to archeological and cultural resources.

##### **Fire Risk and Management**

Commenters expressed conflicting views about how rescinding the 2001 Roadless Rule would affect wildfire risk and management. There is concern that building new roads would increase human-caused ignitions such as those from campfires, cigarettes, and vehicles, thereby raising wildfire risk rather than reducing it. Commenters contend that logging can worsen fire severity by removing fire-resistant old-growth trees, increasing fine fuels, drying forest conditions, and promoting invasive, highly flammable vegetation. They also argue that the existing rule already contains exceptions that allow road construction for timber harvest, fire management and public safety.

Conversely, commenters wrote that the 2001 Roadless Rule has limited access for fuel reduction, salvage logging, and suppression operations, contributing to dense, overgrown forests with high fuel loads and heightened vulnerability to catastrophic wildfires. Commenters wrote that access roads are needed for thinning, treating diseased and dead trees, safe firefighting operations, and providing evacuation routes. Some commenters also note that nearly half of roadless acres are rated at high or very high wildfire risk and that millions of acres have burned since the rule’s enactment.

The Fire and Fuels Management section includes effects on wildfire risk in IRAs and the Vegetation and Forest Management section includes effects on forest health.

##### **Biodiversity and Wildlife**

Commenters were concerned that road construction and logging would fragment habitat, disrupt migration corridors, and degrade large, intact ecosystems that support sensitive, threatened, endangered, and wide-ranging species, including impacts to old-growth forests. There is concern that roads create edge habitat, increase human disturbance and contribute to vehicle strikes, poaching, chemical contamination, and aquatic habitat degradation, particularly through sedimentation and altered stream conditions that harm aquatic species.

In contrast, some commenters wrote that the rule’s passive management has allowed fuels to accumulate and contributed to severe wildfires, insect outbreaks, and habitat decline. Commenters said that active management, such as thinning, prescribed fire, and forest restoration, requires road access and is necessary to maintain resilient and diverse wildlife habitat.

The Biodiversity section includes effects on wildlife habitat, including effects on sensitive, threatened, and endangered species. The Vegetation and Forest Management and Fire and Fuels Management sections include impacts due to accumulated fuels and insect and disease infestations.

##### **Invasive Species, Pests, and Pathogens**

Commenters expressed concern that road construction and increased human access into inventoried roadless areas may introduce and spread invasive species resulting in and exacerbating adverse ecological impacts and potentially increasing fire risk. Commenters identify road construction and resulting vehicle traffic as the leading cause of invasive species spread within national forests. This occurs because seeds and pathogens are often transported via vehicle tires, disturbed soil, and construction materials. The spread of invasive species is cited as a significant threat to native ecosystems, biodiversity, and forest health, because invasive species may outcompete and displace native plants.

The Non-Native Invasive Plant Species section includes effects on invasive species and the Vegetation and Forest Management section includes effect implications for vegetation and forest health.

##### **Water Quality and Watershed**

Commenters noted that roadless areas provide critical drinking water for hundreds of municipal watersheds and function as natural infrastructure that maintains clean, reliable water supplies. Commenters wrote that allowing road construction and access is needed to perform fuels reduction activities to protect water infrastructure. Others expressed concern that introducing roads and development into currently intact roadless areas would harm water quality and watershed health. There is concern that road construction and logging increase erosion, sedimentation and chemical runoff, raise water treatment costs, degrade aquatic ecosystems and destabilize steep terrain. Concerns also include contamination from vehicle pollutants, mining activity enabled by new access, and waste from unauthorized camps. Commenters note that roads can alter surface and groundwater hydrology by redirecting drainage, increasing flood risks, and reducing groundwater recharge. There were conflicting comments about improvements in road design and maintenance mitigating these effects.

The Soil, Water, and Air Resources section includes impacts to water quality and watershed health.

##### **Geology and Soils**

Commenter concerns related to geology and soils focus on the impacts associated with road construction and logging within roadless areas and potential to cause erosion, sedimentation, and slope instability. Commenters also expressed concern that motor vehicle damage can destroy biological soil crusts, compact soils, and alter drainage and they contend that reversing these ecological impacts is far more difficult and costly than maintaining the roadless status. There is concern related to the physical structure and geological stability of the landscape because increased ground disturbance, road construction, logging, and high precipitation are factors that lead to increased slope instability and the likelihood of landslides.

The Soil, Water, and Air Resources section includes impacts to soils, including the likelihood of landslides.

##### **Forest Carbon**

Commenters were concerned that rescinding the 2001 Roadless rule would allow logging, road construction and resource extraction that release stored carbon, create long-lasting “carbon debt”, and diminish the landscape’s ability to absorb future emissions, thereby accelerating climate change. Commenters argue that intact forests can store more carbon than managed or degraded forests, and that inventoried roadless areas collectively hold billions of metric tons of carbon. Conversely, other commenters wrote that passive management under the existing rule increases forest mortality from insects, disease, and severe wildfire, which can reduce carbon storage capacity and, in some cases, turn forests into net carbon emitters.

The Vegetation and Forest Management section includes impacts to carbon.

##### **Air Quality**

Commenters raised concerns that rescinding the 2001 Roadless Rule would degrade air quality by increasing both direct and indirect sources of pollution. There is concern that road construction, logging operations, and added vehicle and truck traffic would introduce dust, noise, and a range of pollutants, including particulate matter, diesel emissions, oil, heavy metals, carbon dioxide, and carbon monoxide, into areas currently valued for their clean air. Commenters were also concerned that rescinding the 2001 Roadless Rule could lead to a potential increase in wildfire frequency or severity, resulting in more smoke impacting nearby communities and elevating associated public health risks.

The Soil, Water, and Air Resources section includes effects on air quality, including the effects of smoke due to wildfire (also see the Fire and Fuels Management section).

##### **Recreation and Access**

Commenters raised competing concerns about how rescinding the 2001 Roadless Rule would affect recreation quality and access. Some emphasize that road construction in inventoried roadless areas would permanently degrade the quiet, remote, wilderness-like experiences that visitors value. There is concern that roads would introduce noise, light pollution, and visual impacts that diminish backcountry recreation experiences such as hiking, hunting, fishing, climbing, and backcountry skiing. Commenters also expressed concern about risks to protected trails including sections of the Continental Divide, Pacific Crest, and Appalachian National Trails which depend on adjacent roadless buffers to maintain their character and scenic value.

Conversely, commenters wrote that current roadless designations limit public and motorized access, creating de facto wilderness areas that disadvantage motorized users, campers, hunters, snowmobilers, and people with disabilities or limited mobility. Commenters expressed interest in expanded access to disperse recreation pressure and ensure equitable opportunities for all user groups. There was concern that recreation access being concentrated in smaller areas leads to overcrowding and greater potential for user conflict and resource damage.

The Recreation and Scenery section describes recreation quality and access effects.

##### **Socioeconomic**

Commenters highlight competing socioeconomic concerns. Supporters of rescinding the 2001 Roadless rule write that roadless protections restrict logging, mining, energy development and road construction, leading to job losses, mill closures and economic instability in rural, resourcedependent communities. They contend that the rule limits traditional economic opportunities and reduces revenue for local infrastructure and public services. Some recreation industries, including ski areas, further note that unmanaged wildfire risks in adjacent roadless areas may threaten their economic viability.

Conversely, other commenters said that intact roadless areas serve as powerful long-term economic assets, driving sustainable tourism and outdoor recreation industries that generate substantial economic output and support millions of jobs. They emphasize that businesses, guides, outfitters, and gateway communities rely on undeveloped landscapes.

The Social and Economic Environment section describes the economic effects.

##### **Intrinsic Value of Roadless Areas**

Commenters expressed concern that road construction would cause irreversible ecological harm and permanently diminish landscapes that hold unique recreational, cultural, and spiritual value. They view road construction as a “forever decision” that trades long term environmental health for short term benefits, negatively impacting resources meant to be preserved for future generations.

Quality of life concerns focus on the loss of the quiet, remote character that roadless areas provide. Commenters emphasize that these undeveloped spaces support mental and physical well-being by offering rare refuge from stress, noise, and modern life. They argue that roads would introduce traffic, noise, light pollution, and other disturbances that erase the solitude and wilderness experience visitors seek, undermining what they consider an essential public health asset.

The Biodiversity section describes ecological effects, the Tribal Rights and Interests sections describe cultural and spiritual effects, the Recreation and Scenery section and the NonCommodity Values for Roadless Areas subsection of the Social and Economic Environment section describe recreation experience and social impacts associated with roadless areas qualities.

##### **Public Health and Safety**

Commenters expressed concern about expanding road access into currently roadless areas, emphasizing safety risks, environmental degradation, and increased vulnerability to illegal or harmful activities. There is concern that new roads would enable illegal dumping, drug cultivation, poaching, unauthorized motorized use, and other behaviors that threaten wildlife, water quality and overall ecosystem health. Commenters indicated concern that added industrial traffic from logging would increase hazards on rural routes.

Additional concerns include the strain on forest personnel and infrastructure, with commenters concerned that greater access may lead to more vandalism and more incidents requiring search and rescue. Some, however, expressed the opposite concern, writing that the existing Roadless Rule has sometimes limited access for property owners, the Department, and Search and Rescue Teams, creating its own safety challenges. Commenters also highlight public health risks, noting that increased human presence may elevate the likelihood of zoonotic disease transmission and the spread of tick-borne and mosquito-borne diseases.

Alternatives 1 and 3 provide for limited exceptions to prohibitions on road construction and timber harvest for public health and safety. The Fire and Fuels Management and National Forest System Roads sections disclose human safety effects. The Tribal Rights and Interests section disclose effects such as unauthorized public access, vandalism, or desecration of sacred sites and traditional cultural places that could erode the sanctity and privacy required for traditional practices, quiet, and spiritual renewal.

##### **Minerals and Mining**

Commenters note that the 2001 Roadless Rule is a barrier to accessing critical mineral resources by prohibiting necessary road construction for exploration and development, particularly in Alaska. Others note that mineral exploration is allowed pursuant to the General Mining Law of 1872.

The Social and Economic Environment section describes effects on accessing mineral resources.

##### **Impacts of the Proposed Action on Specific Locations**

Comments received related to specific geographic locations primarily focused on the irreplaceable ecological value of unique regional ecosystems, the necessity of clean drinking water protection, and the potential impacts on Indigenous cultural and subsistence resources.

The Soil, Water, and Air Resources section describes the effects on water resources, including municipal water supplies. The Tribal Rights and Interests subsection section includes effects on Indigenous cultural and subsistence resources.

### **Appendix 4 – Review of the Final Environmental Impact Statement for the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule**

In response to a petition from the State of Alaska and after extensive engagement with the public, Alaska Native Tribes, Alaska Native Corporations, and others, in 2020, the USDA issued the final rule entitled “Special Areas; Roadless Area Conservation; National Forest System Lands in Alaska,” (2020 Alaska Roadless Rule) (85 FR 68688). A Final Environmental Impact Statement (hereinafter “2020 Final EIS”) was prepared to analyze the effects of the proposed Alaska Roadless Rule and several alternatives. In 2021, the Department initiated a new rulemaking process to reconsider the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule, and it was subsequently repealed in 2023 (2023 Repeal) (88 FR 5252). The 2020 Final EIS that was issued in association with the promulgation of Subpart E (85 FR 68688) and the supporting analysis, including a Determination of NEPA Adequacy, relative to the 2023 Repeal, may be found on the <u>project website</u> (see project documents).

This 2020 Final FEIS analysis, 2023 Determination of NEPA Adequacy, and other supporting information and analyses provide information necessary for findings and conclusions required for this proposed national rulemaking EIS and has been relied upon and incorporated by reference for a portion of the effects analysis, specific to effects to resources within the Tongass National Forest. This appendix documents review and re-evaluation of the 2020 FEIS (7 CFR 1.9(e)(7 and 8) and (q)).

#### Similarity Between the Proposed Action and Alternatives

Both rulemakings expressly addressed application of the 2001 Roadless Rule to the Tongass National Forest.

The current no action alternative (continuation of the 2001 Roadless Rule) was also analyzed as alternative 1 of the 2020 Final EIS, because the 2001 Roadless Rule was in effect on the Tongass at the initiation of the 2020 rulemaking effort. Following promulgation of the Subpart E exemption in October 2020, the directed administrative change to the 2016 Tongass Land and Resource Management Plan (hereinafter “2016 Forest Plan”) was not issued and no timber harvest or roadbuilding in roadless areas that would have been prohibited by the 2001 Roadless Rule occurred. Effectively, roadless management on the Tongass National Forest has been consistent with the 2001 Roadless Rule for over a decade. In addition, as a result of the 2023 repeal of the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule, the 2001 Roadless Rule is in effect today and serves as the ‘no action’ alternative.

The current proposed action (alternative 2) would rescind the 2001 Roadless Rule on all national forest system lands, including within the Tongass National Forest. Alternative 6 of the 2020 Final EIS analyzed a full exemption of the Tongass National Forest through an Alaska Roadless Rule, which meant that the 2001 Roadless Rule would not apply to the Tongass National Forest under that alternative. Alternative 6 was selected by then-Secretary Purdue and is also referred to as the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule. The current Alternative 3 would exempt the Tongass National Forest. This is the same as the alternative analyzed in detail as Alternative 6 of the 2020 Final EIS (full exemption of the Tongass National Forest through an Alaska Roadless Rule) and the effects would be the same as current Alternative 2.

Four additional alternatives for managing roadless area conservation specific to the Tongass National Forest were considered in the 2020 Final EIS, an appropriate range of alternatives for that rulemaking. The following is a brief description of the alternatives evaluated in the 2020 Final EIS (Chapter 2 of the Final EIS contains a complete description of the alternatives):

- Alternative 2 provided limited additional timber harvest opportunity by making timber harvest, road construction, and road reconstruction permissible in areas where roadless characteristics have already been substantially altered, while maximizing Inventoried Roadless Area (IRA) designations where roadless characteristics have not been altered.

- Alternative 3 increased the available land base from which timber harvest opportunities could occur by making timber harvest, road construction, and road reconstruction permissible in areas where roadless characteristics have already been substantially altered and in areas immediately adjacent to existing roads and past harvest areas. Alternative 3 also established a Community Priority category to allow for small-scale timber harvest and associated road construction and reconstruction.

- Alternative 4 provided additional available land base from which timber harvest opportunities could occur while maintaining IRA designations for areas defined in the 2016 Forest Plan as Scenic Viewsheds, T77 Watersheds, and The Nature Conservancy/Audubon Conservation Priority Areas.

- Alternative 5 maximized the land base from which timber harvest opportunities could occur by removing 2.32 million acres from IRA designation.

Taken together, the six alternatives represented the spectrum of management regimes identified to the Forest Service through interdisciplinary review, public comments, public meetings, Tribal and Alaska Native corporation consultations, and cooperating agency input. Alternatives 1 through 5 would not meet the purpose and need for the current rulemaking, as they would not comply with Executive Order 14153. See also Alternatives Considered but Eliminated from Detailed Study.

#### Similarity Between the Potentially Affected Environment

While the 2020 Final EIS addresses only the Tongass National Forest, the current proposed action would also apply to areas outside the Tongass National Forest. This current EIS considers roadless area conservation and the effects of various alternatives nationwide, including on the Tongass National Forest. The geographic area within the Tongass National Forest relevant to the current proposal and the previously analyzed are the same. Both pertain to the inventoried roadless areas on National Forest System lands within the Tongass National Forest.

#### New Circumstances or Information

##### **2023 Review of New Information/Changed Circumstances**

The agency conducted a review of new information or circumstances relevant to the 2023 Recission/Repeal of the 2020 Alaska Roadless Rule. This review considered (1) if there is new information or circumstances since publication of the 2020 FEIS that would be relevant to environmental concerns and (2) if new information or circumstances would substantially change the analysis in the 2020 FEIS. New information or changed circumstances considered included: the release of the USDA Southeast Alaska Sustainability Strategy (SASS) in July 2021, Sealaska Corporation’s announced plan to transition away from logging its lands in January 2021, introduction of the Unrecognized Southeast Alaska Native Communities Recognition and Compensation Act in November 2021 by Senator Lisa Murkowski (this was introduced again most recently in 2025)<sup>74</sup> , and completion of the Alaska Mental Health Trust Land Exchange in October 2021. As explained in greater detail in the 2023 Determination of NEPA Adequacy, the agency concluded that the existing analysis remained valid and adequate in light of the new information and potential changed circumstances identified by the public.

##### **Additional New Information/Changed Circumstances**

The following new information or changed circumstances were reviewed to see if they presented anything that may substantially change the effects from the 2020 and 2023 analyses and findings.

###### Executive Orders and SASS

Changes since 2023 include executive orders including the 2025 Executive Order 14153, _Unleashing Alaska's Extraordinary Resource Potential_ and Executive Order 14225, _Immediate Expansion of American Timber Production_ . These orders state the policy of the United States to maximize the development and production of natural resources in Alaska and to increase timber production to protect national and economic security.

SASS was a press release that did not represent law, regulation, directive, or an agency action. SASS no longer provides context for project development and priority use of discretionary funds.

Accordingly, although the Tongass has renewed its focus on timber production, including that associated with old growth, circumstances relevant to the effects analysis have not changed. Projected forest management on the Tongass continues to be in alignment with the existing (2016) forest plan, which includes an annual Projected Timber Sale Quantity of 46 MMBF oldand young-growth timber during the first decade, and approximately 72 MMBF during the

> 74 In 2025, the Alaska Congressional delegation introduced the “Unrecognized Southeast Alaska Native Communities Recognition and Compensation Act” (H.R. 41) and the “Alaska Native Landless Equity Act (S.2554). Both bills proposed to amend the Alaska Native Claims Settlement Act of 1971 (43 U.S.C. 1601 et seq.) (ANCSA) to form 5 new Urban Corporations for the communities of Haines, Ketchikan, Petersburg, Tenakee, and Wrangell, and convey 23,040 acres of National Forest System lands to each. The legislation identifies a proposed total conveyance of approximately 115,000 acres of National Forest System land. Approximately 50 percent of the lands included in the proposed legislation are within the inventory for the 2001 Roadless Rule.

second decade as more young growth reaches harvestable age. This is consistent with the 2020 FEIS, for which the analysis was based on the PTSQ reflected in the 2016 Forest Plan.

Tongass National Forest Plan Assessment

In 2025, the full Assessment for the Tongass Forest Plan Revision, including 22 individual assessment reports, was published. https://www.fs.usda.gov/r10/tongass/planning/forest- <u>plan/tongass-national-forest-plan-revision.</u> The Tongass Forest Plan assessment was reviewed to determine if it may present ‘new’ conditions relative to the 2020 EIS. One circumstance that was noted as a change since the 2023 DNA is that Gulf of Alaska Chinook salmon was identified as a candidate species for listing under the Endangered Species Act<sup>75</sup> . However, more recently, the NOAA National Marine Fisheries Service determined that listing of the Gulf of Alaska Chinook salmon under the Endangered Species Act is not warranted.  The 2020 FEIS evaluated effects to fish habitat and found that there would be overall effects to fish habitat are expected to be minimal whether the Roadless Rule is in place or not, because of the strong protections to fish habitats provided by Forest Plan standards and guidelines and the riparian management strategy (page 3-138).

In addition, in accordance with the 1990 Tongass Timber Reform Act (TTRA), a new long-term timber demand analysis is underway at the USDA Forest Service Pacific Northwest Research Station to inform the revised plan. This information is not yet available for consideration.

Tongass National Forest Plan Revision

On February 18, 2026, the Forest Service published a <u>Notice of Initiation of the Development of a Proposed Plan Revision and Notice of Intent to Prepare an Environmental Impact Statement.</u> The preliminary need to change is focused on updating and modernize the plan consistent with the 2012 Planning Rule, prioritizing local and regional prosperity of Southeast Alaska, incorporating strategies to address significant changes in recreation and tourism, including plan content that encourages collaboration and shared stewardship with a variety of partners, considering needs for subsistence uses such as hunting, fishing, and gathering, and considering indigenous knowledge related to land stewardship, cultural issues, and culturally-significant sites.

This NOI acknowledged this proposed rulemaking to rescind the 2001 Roadless Area Conservation Rule and indicated that the EIS for the revised plan will analyze one or more alternatives that do not include IRA designations so final decision on the revised plan can align with the outcome of this rulemaking process.

In March 2026, the Tongass National Forest posted preliminary draft plan content and requested public comment and input received.

Neither the notice of intent to prepare a draft plan and EIS nor the preliminary draft plan content result in any changed effects and at this stage it would be speculative to consider what potential changes could be. Effects of any changes from the Revision relative to the uses of existing inventoried roadless areas, including timber suitability, would be analyzed in the EIS for the Plan Revision.

75National Marine Fisheries Service (NMFS) found that the Gulf of Alaska Chinook salmon (Oncorhynchus tshawytscha) may be warranted for listing as threatened or endangered on May 24, 2024 (89 FR 45815), after being petitioned. Following on the positive 90-day finding, in May 2026, NOAA Fisheries completed a status review and <u>12-month finding for Gulf of Alaska Chinook salmon in response to a January 2024 petition. NOAA determined that</u> listing any of the three evolutionarily significant units of Gulf of Alaska Chinook salmon under the Endangered Species Act is not warranted.

###### Greens Creek North Extension Project

This decision was signed in November 2024 and authorized mine expansion in an inventoried roadless area. This project approval is consistent with the analysis in the 2020 FEIS, which recognized that “mining activities are expected to expand at existing sites, including Greens Creek on Admiralty Island”.

###### Cruise Ship Tourism

Cruise ship tourism and associated investments have increased, with associated attention on visual priorities along the cruise ship routes. This trend is consistent with the analysis in the 2020 FEIS, which recognized that “Over the long-term, recreation and tourism are expected to continue and increase in the future” and also recognized the uncertainty regarding levels of recreation and tourism in the near-term due to the ongoing coronavirus pandemic.

###### Recent Defoliator Outbreaks

The Tongass NF has experienced recent defoliator outbreaks across the landscape, including Western black headed budworm ( _Acleris gloverana_ ) and hemlock sawfly ( _Neodiprion tsugae_ ) causing mortality and decline of forested stands. The 2020 FEIS recognizes these common insects and more generally that they are an integral and natural part of forest ecosystems.

#### Adequacy of Effects Analysis for the Proposed Action

- The potential new information and changed circumstances have been found to result in no substantial changes to the analysis and the analysis remains adequate. The environmental effects of the alternatives would be similar to those analyzed in the 2020 FEIS. Effects of the 2020 FEIS are summarized (see Environmental Impacts Within the Tongass National Forest section and incorporated by reference in the sections of the current DEIS, where relevant, to support findings.

- After an assessment of the subsistence evaluations in the 2020 FEIS and 2020 Final Rule, those evaluations have also been found to remain valid and adequate for consideration under Section 810 of ANILCA to support a new decision by the Department (see Subsistence Use and Alaska National Interest Lands Conservation Act (ANILCA)).

##### **Disclosure of Ongoing Judicial Action**

The 2023 decision to repeal the Alaska Roadless Rule is subject to an ongoing judicial action. Judicial review of the complaint, 2023 decision, and the relevant environmental review documents, is stayed pending the proposed 2001 Roadless Rule Rescission.

### **Appendix 5 – Biodiversity Analysis Supplemental Information**

The following tables provide supplemental information for the Plant and Animal Communities Affected Environment discussion (Table 56 and Table 57) and the ESA-Listed Species and Designated Critical Habitat discussion (Table 58) under the Biodiversity section of the EIS.

#### Affected Environment – Fragmentation

We assessed fragmentation of the land area within the affected environment minus a 100-foot buffer for roads for each Forest Service region (Table 56). We divided the two Eastern regions, which have very wide extent across the eastern U.S., into geographically-based subregions for processing purposes. The Northern region and Northeastern subregion had the largest patches on average, while patches tended to be smallest (MPA, LPI) in the Southern Region (8), in the southeastern U.S. Intermountain, Northern, and Pacific Southwestern Region patches were relatively close to one another (NN) but also had the greatest amount of edge (ED) relative to other regions. The two Eastern Regions (8 and 9) had more fragmented, intricate shapes relative to area (ED, LSI).

Largest patch index reflects habitat continuity and approaches 0 for highly fragmented landscapes. Landscape shape index higher values indicate more fragmented or intricate shapes relative to area.

We also assessed fragmentation-related metrics of forest type groups (Table 57). For this analysis, we also divided the two eastern regions, which have very wide extent across the eastern U.S., into geographically-based subregions for processing purposes.

Forest patches in the affected environment varied in size by vegetation type, from smaller aspen/birch patches in the Rocky Mountain Region (2) and the Southwestern Region (3), tanoak/laurel in the Pacific Southwest and Pacific Northwest Regions (5 and 6), and oak/pine in the Southern and Eastern Regions (8 and 9); to larger patches of forest types such as pinyon/juniper in the Southwestern and Intermountain Region (3 and 4), California mixed conifer in the Pacific Southwest Region, Douglas-fir in the Northern, Rocky Mountain, and Pacific Southwest Regions (1, 2, and 6), fir/spruce/mountain hemlock in the Northern, Rocky Mountain, Intermountain, and Pacific Northwest Regions (1, 2, 4, and 6), spruce/fir, maple/beech/birch in the Southern and Eastern Regions, and longleaf/slash pine and loblolly/shortleaf pine in the Southern Region.

Common vegetation types with high fragmentation (more than 10 percent incidence and <= 0.05 LPI) included ponderosa pine in the Southern Region, aspen/birch in the Intermountain Region, loblolly/shortleaf pine in the Southern Region Gulf, and maple/beech/birch in the Eastern Region Great Lakes. Distance to the nearest neighbor patch of the same type (NN) was relatively high for some cover types regardless of patch size; for example, oak/hickory in the Southern Region East was 92 percent of landscape with 125 ha average size, and pinyon/juniper in the Pacific Southwest Region was 10 percent of the landscape with 2ha average size. Most cover types were highly aggregated (CI), with none resembling a checkerboard pattern.

Table notes: Eastern regions were split into subregions of grouped National Forests. Puerto Rico was omitted from Region 8 due to technical constraints. Analysis area was affected environment minus the area within 100 feet of roads. Results are shown for the top 3 softwood and top 3 hardwood groups if >1% of the affected environment. Largest patch index (LPI) reflects habitat continuity and approaches 0 for highly fragmented vegetation type patches. Mean shape index (MSI) averages patch shape complexity (perimeter vs. area) across all patches of the vegetation type. Clumpiness index (CI) measures how aggregated or dispersed a specific land cover type is in a landscape, and approaches 1 for maximally clumped patches, with 0 indicating a random pattern and -1 indicating maximally dispersed (e.g. a checkerboard pattern).

#### ESA-Listed Species - “May Affect, Likely To Adversely Affect” Determinations

Under alternative 2 (the proposed rule), preliminary findings resulted in a “may affect, likely to adversely affect” determination for the threatened, endangered, and proposed species listed in the table below. Note that a likely to adversely affect determination for the species does not always indicate a likely to adversely affect determination for that species’ designated critical habitat; the table below notes in parentheses when the designated critical habitat determination was not likely to adversely affect (NLAA). The U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) and National Marine Fisheries Service (NMFS) Biological Assessments contain the full evaluations for ESAlisted species and designated critical habitat, including the rational for the determinations listed below. The biological assessments also list the species with “no effect” or “may affect, not likely to adversely affect” determinations.
